Every call from a speech-disabled owner ends with no service, and the logs label each one an "honest safe exit". Release strata cover dialect and accent, not speech disability, so no per-group bound blocks release. Koenecke et al. (2024) found that hallucination rates rise for speakers with aphasia. A vulnerability heuristic based on how speech sounds treats disability as incapacity, which risks Unruh Act and ADA claims of a disability-based denial of service, and none of it appears in harm metrics that count exits as successes.
- Defense: The vulnerability counter is semantic, not acoustic. R15 feeds it from Needle signals (confused(), wrong_party_reference(), defers_decision) plus unclear/contradictions. Slurred audio alone does not determine it. Fallback is "callback or human" (R56 warm transfer), not denial, and RELAY_MODE (R42) already treats accessibility as a Tier A metric. The design never meant fallback to count as success; only NOT_SUITABLE does. Still, speech disability is missing as a release stratum, and unintelligible counts can reach the counter.
- Verdict: R15's vulnerability counter explicitly counts unclear(), so atypical speech that Needle cannot parse can drive NO_SALE_SAFE_EXIT, which also suppresses redial. The design's strata (R26/R93) cover dialect and accent only, so no release blocker measures disability-correlated denial. The Breaker is wrong that such exits count as successes (only NOT_SUITABLE does). The disparate-denial gap stands on existing elements, and the defense concedes it.
- Fix: Add speech-disability strata (dysarthria, hypophonia, stuttering, electrolarynx, aphasia) to the gold sets, using consented data. Make per-group false-unintelligible, false-vulnerability and fallback rates release blockers. Exclude unintelligible, low-ASR-confidence and pure-ASR-quality unclear events from the vulnerability counter. After repeated unintelligible re-asks, route to an ACCESS_PATH (DTMF answers, SMS/web self-serve, or a human with extra time) that is counted as an access failure, never a safe exit, and never suppresses redial.

### Round 112. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: A party who joins mid-call (extension pickup, three-way merge) never hears the preamble, and there is no telephony event to say so
- Attack: After the PREAMBLE, the wife picks up the bedroom extension on the same landline, or the husband merges his brother in by three-way calling. Media just mixes, and no SIP or telephony event fires. The new listener talks, hears the price, and later answers the commit question. Nobody told them that the voice is an AI, that the call is recorded, or how to opt out. Mandatory segments are "delivered" once per call, not once per listener. A shared landline line or carrier conference bridge produces no event at the StateSmith root. CIPA (Penal Code 632) needs consent from all parties to a confidential communication, and the AI designation must reach the person deciding. The speaker-change detector, if it fires at all, relabels a turn but does not replay the PREAMBLE.
- Defense: A new voice cannot make the decision unnoticed. R9's speaker_change() "resets identity", so VERIFIED is lost, and personalized fragments and bookings are guarded by identity, owner and adult. A new party who speaks goes back through IDENTITY. The contract binds only after OTP e-signature by every record owner (R88), and a co-owner notice precedes the read-back. In California, NO_RETAIN is the default without affirmative retention consent (R40). Silent listeners remain the real gap, as does the missing replay of the disclosure.
- Verdict: R9's speaker_change() only resets identity. A second adult at the same address can pass the IDENTITY challenge and reach READ_BACK and the commit without ever hearing the AI designation, recording notice or opt-out. Disclosures are delivered once per call (R31/R39), and silent listeners are never addressed. NO_RETAIN covers retention in all-party states but not the AI or opt-out disclosures, and the defense concedes the missing replay.
- Fix: Make speaker_change() enter a NEW_PARTY state that replays the compiled disclosure block (AI designation, seller, purpose, recording, opt-out) as mandatory segments before any further pitch, and revert recording to NO_RETAIN until the new party consents. Play a short compiled re-disclosure at the start of READ_BACK to cover silent listeners. Require the affirming speaker's diarization id to match the VERIFIED speaker at the commit gate.

### Round 113. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: No-retain and recording-failure calls blind the post-call DNC sweep and drop out of the audited harm measure
- Attack: A Californian says "Don't record me." The agent enters no-retain mode, or the SIPREC recording fork fails silently on one SBC. Later the caller says "just stop calling" in a mumble, and live Needle misses it, which the design lists as an open item. The post-call DNC sweep has no audio or transcript to scan, so the number is redialed. These calls are also never in the human-audio audit sample. The design's backstop for missed voice stops, "post-call DNC sweep of every transcript", assumes retention. Privacy-asserting callers and silent recording faults are exactly the calls it cannot sweep. Certification measures harm "only on real calls labeled by humans listening to audio", so these calls are excluded from the gate. That is selection bias toward callers who stay calm and agree to recording.
- Defense: The sweep does not read the recording. It reads the live ASR transcript, which the pipeline already produces. A SIPREC fork failure therefore does not blind it. NO_RETAIN is "in-memory only" (R40), so the sweep runs at teardown on the in-memory transcript before it is purged. It keeps only a DNC flag, not content. The mid-call stop is the union of the lexicon and Needle GLOBAL, and DTMF opt-out still works. The attack's real point is the audit's selection bias.
- Verdict: The design's "post-call DNC sweep of every transcript" (R66) can run on the live in-memory ASR transcript, so that half of the attack mostly fails. But the sweep re-runs the same deterministic lexicon and GLOBAL on the same text that live Needle missed, so it adds little for these calls. Human audio audit and the certification gate exclude every NO_RETAIN call, and that is the default for all-party-consent states without affirmative consent (R40). A large, non-random share of production traffic is therefore outside the per-call harm bound and the drift alarms, and the existing design neither handles this nor reports it.
- Fix: Make the teardown sweep mandatory before purge, with an attestation log, and suppress the number if the sweep fails or no transcript exists (fail closed, including for recording or transcript fork faults). Treat NO_RETAIN as its own stratum with a conservative profile: any unresolved unclear in the closing turns or any Tier-2 stop suppresses redial. Bound its harm with outcome proxies (later complaints, repeat DNC requests, rescissions) compared against the retained stratum, and state the coverage gap explicitly in the certification report.


### Round 114. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: State do-not-call registries (Indiana, Texas, Colorado, Pennsylvania, Missouri) missing from the pre-dial gate
- Attack: 1) Indiana homeowner is on Indiana's state DNC list, not the national one. 2) A lead vendor form gave "prior express written consent" naming the seller and AI voice. 3) Pre-dial gate checks national + internal DNC, consent, RND, hours: all green. 4) Agent dials and closes a sale. 5) Homeowner complains to the Indiana Attorney General, who enforces its own list and its own, narrower exemptions. 6) Audit finds thousands of dials to state-list numbers, every one "certified compliant." The gate names only "national/internal DNC." Several states run separate registries with their own exemption wording that does not map onto federal PEWC (Indiana Code 24-4.7). "Most restrictive jurisdiction" is applied to hours and frequency, not to list scrubbing, so the model-checked invariant proves the wrong property. Source: in.gov/attorneygeneral do-not-call telephone-solicitors page.
- Defense: The design never limited list scrubbing to federal law. R8 puts "state mini-TCPA rules as facts rows" in the pre-dial gate. R70 builds a "pre-dial candidate jurisdiction set with most-restrictive-wins" and checks "invariants across all profiles". The Compliance gates are a "per-jurisdiction regulation profile, most restrictive wins". A state registry is a state mini-TCPA rule, so it belongs in those rows. The attacker is right on one point. The final plan's summary line says only "national/internal DNC" and never names state registries or their separate exemption wording. We close that gap explicitly below.
- Verdict: The design's pre-dial gate (R8) lists only "National + internal DNC scrub". "State mini-TCPA rules as facts rows" (R8) and most-restrictive-wins (R70) are general hooks, but no step ever names scrubbing against state registries or their own exemption tests. The Designer agrees the gap exists, and the defense depends on the NEW state_dnc_registry rows.
- Fix: Add a state_dnc_registry row for each state that runs a list (IN, TX, CO, PA, MO and others). Each row carries its own exemption predicate and is never mapped to federal PEWC. Scrub against every candidate jurisdiction's list before each dial, and do not dial when a list is stale or unavailable or its exemption can't be proven. Add a model-checked invariant: no DIAL edge unless every candidate state's registry check passes.

### Round 115. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Consent revoked through another channel (STOP reply to the e-sign SMS, email, installer, website) never reaching the dialer
- Attack: 1) Customer gets the e-sign envelope SMS, replies "STOP, no more calls." 2) The reply lands at the e-sign vendor's number, which only emits signature webhooks. 3) Customer also emails "remove me." 4) Neither is a voice transcript, so the post-call DNC sweep never sees them. 5) Eleven business days later the callback scheduler (WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE follow-up) redials with the AI voice. 6) Customer sues for each call after revocation. Since April 11, 2025, 47 CFR 64.1200(a)(10) lets consumers revoke by any reasonable means, including replying to a text, and requires honoring it within 10 business days. The design's stop handling covers only voice: lexicon, Needle GLOBAL and the transcript sweep. Texts, emails and vendor channels it opens are not wired in. Source: FCC 24-24; Kelley Drye, Apr. 2025.
- Defense: Every redial already passes the per-number gate. R74 puts a "per-number consent gate in deterministic dialer", so the WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE callback is not a free pass. Consent has one ledger, and revocation only has to reach it. Many voice paths already feed it: the stop lexicon, GLOBAL, DTMF opt-out (R75), request_dnc_policy (R84) and the post-call sweep. The gap is real, though. Texts and emails sent to channels the outbox opens (the e-sign SMS, the R89 short code, the written DNC-policy mail) are not wired into that ledger. Under 64.1200(a)(10) those are "reasonable means".
- Verdict: Every revocation path in the design is voice or DTMF: the lexicon, GLOBAL, the R66 transcript sweep and R75 DTMF. The design itself opens text channels (the e-sign SMS, the R89 short code, the R84 outbox mail), and nothing routes replies on those channels to the consent ledger. Under 64.1200(a)(10), revocation by any reasonable means, such as a text reply, has to be honored within 10 business days. The R74 gate rechecks the ledger before every dial, but that does not help when the revocation never reaches the ledger.
- Fix: Build a revocation intake bus. The e-sign vendor, SMS short codes, email, the web form, installers and CRM notes all feed the stop lexicon plus the GLOBAL stop classifier. A hit writes the DNC ledger within 1 business day, unmatched replies go to human review, the ledger is read at every dial (scheduled callbacks included), and vendor contracts require replies and STOP events to be forwarded. Add Tier A fault-injection tests for each channel.

### Round 116. Winner: Designer
- Topic: TSR 310.5 five-year recordkeeping vs "no-retain mode" and combinatorial fragment wordings
- Attack: 1) Privacy profile switches a California call to no-retain mode with PII surrogates. 2) Each utterance is built from fragments with "several wordings, chosen deterministically" plus slot fills. 3) FTC sends a civil investigative demand: produce the call detail record and the exact message played on call #8,413,227, plus the consent record. 4) Company has surrogate IDs, no audio, and must regenerate the message from a bundle hash that has since rolled back. 5) No authentic copy of what was actually played exists. The 2024 TSR amendment (effective Oct. 2024) requires sellers/telemarketers to keep for five years call detail records (number, time, duration, disposition, caller ID) and a copy of each unique prerecorded message, plus consent records. No-retain mode and per-call fragment variants break this. Source: 89 Fed. Reg. 26760 (Apr. 16, 2024); 16 CFR 310.5.
- Defense: The design already keeps exactly the material needed to rebuild what was played. R67 says "immutable retained bundles", and every session is stamped with its bundle content hash. A rollback changes which bundle new calls use, not whether old bundles exist. R59 says the wording is "chosen deterministically and logged". R39 writes "per-segment playout marks". R74 keeps the PEWC artifact with timestamp, seller and disclosure version. So the exact message can be rebuilt bit-identically, and the consent record is kept. No-retain (R40) covers the caller's speech, not the agent's own output or call metadata. The gap is that retention periods and storage for these records are never stated.
- Verdict: The scenario depends on not being able to rebuild the played message after a rollback. The design rules this out: bundles are immutable and kept, each session is stamped with its bundle hash (R67), the deterministic wording choice is logged (R59), playout marks are recorded (R39), and the PEWC artifact is stored (R74). With all of that, the exact unique message can be reproduced bit for bit. NO_RETAIN (R40) covers caller content, not the agent's own render log or the consent ledger. The 5-year WORM retention is only polish.

### Round 117. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Litigation hold triggered mid-call ("my lawyer is recording this") while automated deletion keeps running
- Attack: 1) Callee says: "I've sued you people before, my attorney will hear this call." 2) No GLOBAL event for a legal threat, so Needle labels it out_of_scope or objection, and the call continues. 3) Standard retention jobs run: no-retain audio dropped, PII swapped for surrogates, the transcript scrubbed for training. 4) Three weeks later a TCPA class action is filed citing this call. 5) The company can't produce the original audio or unredacted transcript, only a "bit-identical replay" made from fragments. The duty to preserve starts when litigation is reasonably expected. Losing electronically stored information after that invites FRCP 37(e) sanctions, up to an adverse-inference instruction. A deterministic replay is a reconstruction, not the original record, so authentication (FRE 901) and the best-evidence rule (FRE 1002) can be attacked. The graph has no event or hold path for legal threats.
- Defense: Two things limit the exposure. In NO_RETAIN calls (R40) no recording was ever lawfully made. There is no duty to create evidence, so nothing was spoiled. For retained calls, R7 makes "deletion = data operation", and A3's WORM render log keeps the agent side. But the attacker is right on two points. No event detects a legal threat. And surrogate replacement and scrubbing run blind to any preservation duty. A bit-identical replay is not the original record.
- Verdict: The design has no event for a legal threat and no preservation hold. Routine surrogate replacement, scrubbing and gold-set deletion (R7, "deletion = data operation") therefore keep running after litigation becomes reasonably foreseeable. The Designer agrees on both gaps. NO_RETAIN only protects calls that were never recorded, and the defense rests on the NEW legal_threat event and hold flag.
- Fix: Add a legal_threat GLOBAL event, fired by a lexicon or by Needle. It plays a fixed neutral fragment, offers a human, ends the pitch and sets legal_hold. The hold freezes every deletion, surrogate and scrub job for that session and number, keeps the original audio and transcript, and alerts legal. Inbound complaint and demand channels and the post-call sweep set the same hold. Add Tier A tests of hold versus deletion.

### Round 118. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Illinois BIPA: speaker-change detection, self-echo filtering and prosody features as voiceprint collection without a written release
- Attack: 1) The agent calls an Illinois homeowner. 2) To run IDENTITY "speaker change" handling and the self-echo filter, the front end computes per-speaker voice embeddings and pitch features. 3) Its notices cover recording and AI, nothing more. 4) Embeddings are logged with the trace for audit and bit-identical replay. 5) A plaintiff firm subpoenas the pipeline spec and files a class action: voiceprints were collected from every Illinois callee without a signed written release or a published retention schedule. BIPA (740 ILCS 14/15(a)-(b)) requires a public retention policy and an informed written release before collecting a "voiceprint." Damages are $1,000 for negligent and $5,000 for reckless violations, with a private right of action. A spoken recording notice is not a written release. Texas CUBI imposes similar duties. The design never classifies its speaker-tracking features as biometric.
- Defense: The scenario's premise is not in the design. The self-echo filter (R38) matches the "incoming transcript vs agent's rendered text in a time window". It compares text, not voices. speaker_change() (R9) is a Needle event over words ("this is his wife"), and identity is "challenge-response (caller states name/address, deterministic match)" (R73), so it is not voice-based. Prosody (R80) is an "F0 final-rise detector -> <rise> token". That is a single per-utterance pitch slope, which can't identify anyone. Replay (R67) uses the bundle and transcripts, not embeddings. The design computes no voiceprint. Still, nothing forbids one, so we lock that down.
- Verdict: The attack's premise is false. The self-echo filter compares text (R38), speaker_change is a Needle event over words (R9), identity is a spoken challenge-response (R73), and prosody is a single per-utterance F0 final-rise token (R80). Nothing in the design computes or keeps a speaker embedding or voiceprint.

### Round 119. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Washington My Health My Data Act: health inferences from sensitive_event and the vulnerability counter
- Attack: 1) A Washington callee says: "My wife's on home oxygen, we can't lose power." 2) The sensitive_event fires, the vulnerability counter goes up, and the trace is labeled "medical_dependency" for exit routing. 3) That labeled transcript goes into the audit sample and the per-release Needle finetune union. 4) No separate health-data consent was collected. 5) A consumer sues under the Washington Consumer Protection Act over the collection, the inference and the use in model training. Washington's MHMDA (RCW 19.373, in force since 2024) covers "consumer health data," including inferences from non-health data. It requires separate opt-in consent to collect, separate consent to share, and a health-data privacy policy, with a private right of action. The design labels, keeps and trains on exactly these inferences. Its PII surrogates hide names, not the health label.
- Defense: There is no "medical_dependency" label in the design. sensitive_event types are coarse routing classes (R19). Their only purpose is a scripted exit with "no pitch", which in itself serves the consumer. R7 limits data collection, and the vulnerability counter (R15) only routes the call. The gap is real, though. Retained transcripts and gold sets (R7, R40) keep the spoken health content and the event label, and the per-release finetune union trains on them without MHMDA consent.
- Verdict: sensitive_event(medical_emergency) exists (R19), and wherever retention consent is given (R40), its label and the spoken health content stay in retained transcripts, audit samples and the per-release training union. Retention consent is not the separate MHMDA opt-in. "Medical_dependency" is not the design's label, but medical_emergency serves the same purpose. PII surrogates (R7) remove names, not health content linked to a phone number and call record. The Designer agrees on this gap.
- Fix: Add a health-data quarantine. When a medical sensitive_event or a health lexicon hit fires, redact those spans from every retained transcript, audit sample and training set, and persist only a type-less sensitive_exit label. In MHMDA (and NV/CT) profiles, keep no health-derived data beyond the in-memory routing decision. Train the medical classes only on synthetic or consented data, have the build pipeline reject health-tagged real spans, and publish a consumer health data policy.

### Round 120. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: ESIGN consumer-consent disclosure and inaccessible e-sign for a blind customer
- Attack: 1) A blind homeowner agrees by voice and gets the SMS envelope and OTP. 2) The vendor's consent-to-electronic-records screen and the contract PDF don't work with the phone's screen reader. 3) A sighted relative taps through "I agree to use electronic records" and signs. 4) The agent never confirmed the customer could open electronic records. 5) Customer later disputes the contract. The seller can't show valid electronic delivery of the required California solar disclosures or the senior cancellation notice. To satisfy a law requiring written disclosures, 15 USC 7001(c) needs the consumer's affirmative consent after hardware and software disclosures, given in a way that "reasonably demonstrates" they can access the records. If that fails, the disclosures were never legally given and cancellation windows may never start. Inaccessible signing also invites Unruh and ADA claims.
- Defense: Several parts are already in place. The contract is only binding after the vendor reports a completed signature (R60). Install waits out "the longest cancellation window" (R17). The design already lists a landline/mailed fallback as an open item (R22). But the attacker is right that no step ensures ESIGN 7001(c) consent "reasonably demonstrates" access, and that no accessible alternative is offered.
- Verdict: Nothing in the design checks for ESIGN 7001(c) consent that "reasonably demonstrates" the consumer can access the records, and it offers no accessible or paper alternative. The landline/mailed fallback is still an open item (R22). Waiting on webhooks (R60) and on the cancellation window (R17) does not help if the required disclosures were never validly delivered, because then the window may never start.
- Fix: Before CONTRACT_SENT, ask a fixed question: electronic documents, or paper or an in-person signing? Paper, or any stated access need, routes to a mailed or consultant-assisted signing. The envelope runs a 7001(c) demonstration step (a code visible only inside a sample record in the contract's format). The vendor must meet WCAG 2.1 AA with tagged PDFs. Install waits until delivery of the disclosures is verified.

### Round 121. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Disability discrimination against direct callers with speech disabilities (dysarthria, stuttering, laryngectomy)
- Attack: 1) A homeowner with Parkinson's dysarthria answers without a relay service. 2) The ASR-confidence pre-gate scores turns low and sends them to unintelligible re-ask. 3) The design is "clarify once, then fallback": after two turns the call ends with a callback that gets the same result. 4) Across the campaign, people with speech disabilities never reach QUOTE, while everyone else does. 5) A California consumer sues under the Unruh Act for being denied equal access to the offer. Unruh (Civ. Code 51, 52) covers business establishments, includes online and remote ones, and sets $4,000 minimum statutory damages per violation. The audit is split by dialect and accent, not disability. Relay handling covers TRS users only. The fixed clarify budget systematically excludes a protected class, with no accommodation path such as slower pacing, DTMF or text.
- Defense: The fallback is not a dead end. "Clarify once, then fallback" leads to "deterministic fallback (callback or human)". R56 request_human means a "warm transfer to staffed queue ... else book consultant call", and a human can quote. Endpointing already lengthens for incomplete turns (R25). Rising confusion lengthens thresholds (R15). RELAY_MODE (R42) covers relay users. Still, two things are true. The fallback could pick an AI callback that ends the same way. And audits are stratified by dialect, not by disability.
- Verdict: The existing fallback is "callback or human". R1 books a consult, and R56 gives a warm transfer to staffed humans or a booked consultant call, and a human can quote. So the design is not a structural dead end, and R25/R15 already lengthen thresholds. The Unruh theory is also weak. Without an ADA violation, Unruh requires intentional discrimination (disparate impact is not enough), and the ADA's reach to remote, non-physical sales is limited in the Ninth Circuit.

### Round 122. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: State telemarketer registration and bonding (Texas ch. 302 as amended by SB 140) as an unmodeled pre-dial requirement
- Attack: 1) A Texas campaign launches after September 1, 2025. 2) The seller holds no Texas Secretary of State telephone-solicitation registration or security deposit and relies on federal TCPA consent. 3) The SMS e-sign envelope and appointment texts now count as telephone solicitation under Texas law. 4) A recipient sues under the DTPA for each unregistered call and text. 5) The regulation profile has hours, DNC, consent and HIS rows, but no row requiring "registered and bonded in the callee's state." Texas SB 140 (effective September 1, 2025) extends ch. 302 to texts and makes violations actionable under the DTPA, adding private suits. Ch. 302 already required registration ($200 fee, $10,000 security). Other states, such as Utah and Washington, also require telemarketer registration. The gate never checks seller licensure status per state. Sources: Morgan Lewis (Sept. 2025); Kelley Drye (Sept. 2025).
- Defense: The design already gates on per-state licensing. R47 says "where salesperson registration applies (e.g. CA HIS), call runs lead-gen mode", and the regulation profile is "most restrictive wins". The outbox texts (CONTRACT_SENT SMS, appointment texts) leave only through deterministic gated effects (R13, R55). Any per-state row therefore covers them automatically. Fair point: that row is not there yet. There is a contractor-registration row (HIS), but no telemarketer registration and bond row, and no rule that texts count.
- Verdict: R47 covers contractor or salesperson licensing (CA HIS), not telemarketer registration and bonding. No row in the design checks Texas ch. 302 registration, which after SB 140 also covers texts, or registration in other states. The Designer agrees the row is missing, and the defense depends on the NEW seller_registration rows.
- Fix: Add a seller_registration row per state (TX ch. 302 as amended by SB 140, UT, WA and others) with registration id, bond or security, and expiry. Use it as a pre-dial and outbox guard, with texts counted as solicitation. Add a model-checked invariant: no DIAL and no SMS effect without valid registration in every candidate state. Raise an alert 30 days before expiry.

### Round 123. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: E-signature attribution: OTP proves control of the phone, not identity, so one spouse signs for all record owners
- Attack: 1) Title lists two co-owners. 2) The agent captures the second owner's contact by asking the caller for it, and the caller gives their own second line. 3) Both OTP envelopes reach the same person, who signs as both. 4) Webhooks report "completed," the lien and UCC filing are recorded, and installation proceeds. 5) The non-signing spouse sues to void the contract and remove the lien, citing forgery. 6) The seller's only proof that the spouse signed is an OTP sent to a number the other spouse supplied. Under UETA §9, an e-signature binds a person only if it was their act, provable by security procedure. An OTP sent to a number given by an interested party proves nothing about who signed. The design checks how many record owners there are, not that each one independently authenticated. A forged co-owner signature voids the lien and exposes the seller to fraud claims.
- Defense: The scenario breaks an existing rule: "no Needle tool can create outbound contacts (build check)" (R74). The caller cannot speak a co-owner's number into the system. R88 builds the signer list from "record-owner lookup on validated address" plus "lender stipulated signers". The lender runs its own identity check on each borrower. The OTP goes to the dialed number (R22), not to a dictated one. The attacker is right on one point: the design never says where a non-dialed co-owner's contact comes from, or how that person proves identity.
- Verdict: R74 does stop the caller from dictating the co-owner's number. But R22 sends the signing link and gates the envelope with an OTP "to dialed number", while R88 puts every record owner on the signer list. As written, then, every owner's OTP goes to the phone of the person on the call, and one spouse can sign for both. Lender KYC covers only borrowers, not a non-borrowing co-owner on title.
- Fix: Each signer needs independently sourced contact data (their own consent or lead record, or a phone verified by lender KYC), and no two signers may share a phone or email. Any envelope that creates a lien or UCC filing requires per-signer vendor ID verification, with a notary or in-person consultant as the fallback. No lien filing or install proceeds until every owner has been independently verified.


### Round 124. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Shadow-mode evaluation cannot measure Needle's whole-call harm: after its first divergent label, the real call follows the big model's path
- Attack: Build phase: big model drives a live call, Needle runs in shadow. Turn 4: big model labels "objection_price", Needle labels "acknowledge". The real call continues down the big model's branch; Needle's branch (skip rebuttal, move to QUOTE, different read-back timing) never happens. Turns 5-20 are scored for Needle only on inputs produced by the other policy. The team reports Needle's "whole-trace harm" from these shadow logs and certifies states for live use. The design certifies on "whole audited call traces", but shadow traces are off-policy after the first disagreement: callers react to what was actually said, not what Needle would have said. Compounding-error results (Ross et al., DAgger, 2011) show per-step agreement understates on-policy error. The only true trace measurement is putting the uncertified Needle on real callers first.
- Defense: Shadow logs are never the release gate. The gate is on-policy: R20 says certification comes from "the disclosed agent's own build-phase calls", and R36 retires the big model per state only after an "audited error bound over N consecutive calls + fallback rate < X% + A/B handoff lift", which requires Needle actually driving those calls. Shadow agreement only nominates a state for that live trial. Compounding is also capped structurally: both policies walk the same compiled graph, every qualification-critical slot is read back (R2, "a single-turn Needle error becomes a caught correction"), and commit needs a lexical gate independent of Needle (R87). The point is fair, though, that "whole audited call traces" must mean Needle-driven traces only, so I am stating that explicitly below.
- Verdict: The existing certification text (R2) says outright that Needle "runs in shadow on live build-phase calls" and is compared to the big model and a gold sample, and rule-of-three sizes are "computed on those live samples per state". After the first divergence those traces are off-policy, so the whole-trace bound in R66 does not hold for Needle as the driver. R20's "on-policy" means the disclosed agent's calls with the big model as classifier, not Needle driving. R36's A/B handoff lift only suggests some Needle-driven traffic and does not say the bound is computed on it. The Designer's key remedy, "only Needle-driven traces enter the gate", plus a staged ramp, is new.
- Fix: Treat shadow agreement only as evidence for nominating a state. Whole-trace Tier A/B bounds and rule-of-three counts come only from calls where Needle actually drove the transitions. Promotion runs as a staged ramp: shadow, then a Needle-driven canary cohort with 100% human audit, then a wider cohort. The bound is recomputed at each stage, and any breach sends the state back to deterministic fallback.

### Round 125. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Constrained decoding renormalizes probability over allowed events, so the near-tie margin hides how much mass the model put outside the schema
- Attack: In COMMIT, the caller says something truly novel. Unconstrained, Needle would spread mass over junk tokens; 85% of its first-token mass is off-schema. Constrained decoding masks those, leaving "affirm" 0.12 vs "unclear" 0.03 among allowed tokens. After renormalizing, affirm is 0.80, unclear 0.20: a wide margin, no clarify. Multi-token event names make it worse: the margin is computed on a shared first token ("stop"/"stop_dnc", "request_human"/"request_caller_identity") before the meaningful token is chosen. Grammar-constrained decoding distorts the model's distribution and gives outputs whose likelihood does not reflect the model (Park et al., "Grammar-Aligned Decoding", NeurIPS 2024). The design's near-tie gate reads confidence after masking, so out-of-distribution inputs look most confident exactly where the schema is narrowest: the commit state.
- Defense: The attack assumes a renormalized post-mask margin is the only confidence check. Three other layers make that confidence irrelevant at COMMIT. First, the commit gate is "lexical over final ASR tokens (independent of Needle output)" (R87), and it accepts only a short, direct affirm from a narrow set aimed at the read-back question id (R77, R81). A truly novel utterance contains no such affirm, so nothing commits whatever Needle outputs. Second, the GLOBAL and LOCAL passes are separate (R34), so stop and request_human never compete with local tokens. Third, R19's max-softmax OOD backstop exists. The real gap is where that score is read, and I fix it below.
- Verdict: The scenario's harm, a confident wrong affirm at COMMIT, is blocked by existing parts that do not depend on Needle. R87 makes the commit gate lexical over the final ASR tokens. R77 and R81 require a short, direct, narrow-set affirm aimed at the read-back question id. A truly novel utterance therefore cannot commit, whatever label Needle picks. The shared-first-token and post-mask margin problems are real calibration flaws, but in other states they lead to Tier B mislabels that real-traffic certification measures. They do not cause an irreversible failure.

### Round 126. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Multiple comparisons and winner's curse: hundreds of per-state, per-group, per-tier 95% bounds let bad states certify by chance
- Attack: The graph has ~300 states, ~6 dialect strata and 3 severity tiers: ~5,000 separate 95% upper-bound tests per release. The team tunes thresholds and fragments, reruns, and promotes whichever states pass; failing states go to fallback and are retried next release with fresh luck. A state whose true Tier A rate is 3e-5 passes once by sampling luck and is promoted; the release-level claim "every live state is under 1e-5" is published. A 95% bound is wrong 5% of the time per test. Across thousands of tests and repeated retries with selection on passing, false certifications are expected, not rare. With no family-wise or false-discovery correction and no fresh holdout after tuning, the certificate's confidence level is invalid by construction.
- Defense: The per-state bounds are allocations of one per-call budget (R2: "per-state budgets derived from it"), and that budget is checked on whole audited call traces (R66). That single release-level test is the one the published claim rests on, not 5,000 separate ones. Tier A states also replay frozen human-audited gold sets plus sealed holdouts and a post-release shadow window (R3), and continuous audited sampling with drift alarms (R33) catches a lucky pass in production. Still, the attack correctly points out that per-state tests with retry-until-pass and no correction would inflate false certifications. Below I make the statistics valid by construction.
- Verdict: The release claim rests on per-call, per-severity budgets over whole audited traces (R66), not on thousands of separate per-state claims. Per-state and per-group bounds are release blockers, so every one of them must pass, which makes the release more conservative. Tier A also has sealed holdouts and a post-release shadow window (R3), and continuous audited sampling with rollback (R33, R66) re-tests any state that passed by luck. Retry-until-pass at the per-state level is a genuine weakness, but existing fresh-data and monitoring layers bound it.

### Round 127. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Shortcut learning on generator artifacts: big-model synthetic examples carry label-correlated style tells that Needle learns instead of intent
- Attack: The big model writes 200k "stop/DNC" paraphrases. They are polite, complete, often start with "Please" and contain "list" or "call". Its "objection_price" items mention money words. Needle hits 99.9% on held-out synthetic data. A real caller mutters "ugh, don't bother me again, got it?" with none of those tells: labeled "acknowledge". Another says "I'd rather put the money in my 401k, please stop": money word wins, labeled objection_price. Crowd-written NLI data has annotation artifacts so strong a hypothesis-only classifier gets ~67% on SNLI without seeing the premise (Gururangan et al., NAACL 2018). Generator-written data has stronger, more uniform artifacts. Held-out synthetic accuracy measures how well Needle reads the generator's style, not callers.
- Defense: Held-out synthetic accuracy certifies nothing: "Synthetic data trains Needle and finds bugs; it never certifies" (R2). Only real, human-audited on-policy calls count (R20). Both examples also fail for structural reasons. "please stop" sits in a sentence about money, but stop is decided in the GLOBAL pass, whose tool list has no objection_price (R34), so a money word cannot outvote it there. It is also unioned with the deterministic lexicon (R66), which flags "please stop" outright. "don't bother me again" matches the R93 dialect/negation patterns, and any miss is caught by the post-call DNC sweep before any redial. Real data is never replaced (R33), per-source caps (R51) limit any one generator, and the red team is model-diverse with human-written seeds (R97).
- Verdict: The attack targets held-out synthetic accuracy, which the design explicitly never uses to certify (R2: "Synthetic data trains Needle and finds bugs; it never certifies"). Shortcut learning would show up in the real, audio-labeled gate. The money-word example also fails on structure: stop is decided in the separate GLOBAL pass, which has no objection_price (R34), and is unioned with the deterministic lexicon (R66, R24). Lexicon coverage of "don't bother me again" is not certain, but such a miss is a measured stop-recall error on real calls, not hidden by synthetic scores.

### Round 128. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Train/serve skew on prosody tokens: synthetic training text carries "final-rise" tokens invented by the big model, not produced by the prosody detector
- Attack: The commit gate excludes rising-pitch answers using a final-rise token from the prosody front end. Synthetic transcripts have no audio, so the big model inserts final-rise tokens where it thinks questions are. In production, the detector fires on list intonation, regional uptalk and line noise, and misses flat-pitch questions. Needle learned "final-rise implies question" from clean invented placements; it now ignores the token on uptalk "yes" or over-trusts it on noise, flipping commit gating. A feature that exists only in serving, simulated by guesswork in training, is classic training/serving skew. The token gates the most consequential decision (COMMIT), yet its training distribution was written by the big model, not measured from the pinned detector on real audio. No amount of synthetic volume fixes a mismatched feature distribution.
- Defense: The rise token gates COMMIT through deterministic code, not through what Needle learned: "affirm with <rise> excluded from commit set" (R80) sits inside the lexical commit gate (R87), which is independent of Needle. Both failure modes stay safe. Detector false rises on uptalk "yes" block the commit and force a closed re-ask (R77), costing only a turn. A missed flat question still has to be a short, direct, lexical affirm to the read-back question id (R81), which questions like "and that's the total?" aren't. Certification is on real live calls with human audio labels (R26), so any skew shows up in the gate numbers.
- Verdict: The rise token's safety role is deterministic. R80 excludes affirm with <rise> from the commit set inside the lexical gate that is independent of Needle (R87). A false rise only forces a closed re-ask (R77). A missed rise still needs a short, direct lexical affirm to the read-back question id (R81). Whatever Needle mislearned about the token, commit gating stays safe, and other effects are measured on real audio-labeled traffic (R26, R68).

### Round 129. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Train/test contamination: "finetune on the union of all data each release" absorbs red-team items, regression suites and golden canaries
- Attack: Release 7's red-team finds 400 failing attacks; they are labeled and added to the union. The golden-audio canary transcripts, reviewed for a breach last month, were also labeled and added. Release 8 passes 100% of past red-team attacks and the daily canary. The team reads this as robustness. Near-duplicate paraphrases of the canary set also sit in the synthetic pool from the same generator prompts. Once evaluation items or their near-duplicates enter training, those sets measure memorization, not generalization. A 26M model easily memorizes a few thousand items. The design states no frozen, never-trained-on holdout or deduplication between training and test pools, so its regression and canary gates become self-fulfilling and detect nothing new.
- Defense: Contamination can't inflate the gate, because the gate is fresh real traffic: certification needs "held-out + fresh calls" on live traffic (R2), sealed holdouts for Tier A (R3), and continuous post-release audited sampling (R33). None of these can be in a training set that was frozen before they were collected. The golden-audio canary (R68) is a bit-identical determinism check on the pinned front end. It asks whether output changed, not whether it is right, so memorizing it proves nothing and fools nothing. Past red-team pass rates are regression checks, not robustness evidence. The attack still has a point, though: the design states no dedup rule between pools. I add one below.
- Verdict: The gate is fresh, human-audited live traffic plus sealed Tier A holdouts (R2, R3, R33), and none of it can be in a training union frozen before it was collected. The golden-audio canary (R68) is a "diff -> block" determinism check, so memorizing it fools nothing. Red-team pass rates are not the certification gate. One real gap remains: R3 replays "frozen human-audited gold sets" while training on "the union of all data", so Tier B gold-set replay may measure memorization. That does not defeat the live-traffic gate.

### Round 130. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Rare n-gram quarantine (anti-poisoning) systematically removes dialect, code-switched and rare-name data, starving the groups the per-group bounds protect
- Attack: The anti-poisoning filter quarantines rare n-grams. Real calls containing "finna", "ion want it" (I don't want it), Spanglish "no, gracias, ya tengo", Gullah or Cajun forms, and uncommon surnames are rare by definition and get quarantined with the true attacks. Each release, minority-dialect real data shrinks relative to synthetic Standard English. Needle's stop recall for those speakers stalls; their states never pass the per-group bound. Rarity is the shared signature of both triggers and minority speech; a frequency filter cannot tell them apart. NLP tools already misread African-American English as non-English (Blodgett et al., EMNLP 2016). The quarantine turns a security control into a disparate-performance generator, and per-group blockers then keep those callers on fallback or exclude them.
- Defense: R51 quarantines rare *repeated* n-grams, meaning a rare string recurring across many items with a skewed label, which is the signature of a planted trigger. Natural dialect forms like "finna" or "ion" appear across many independent callers, sources and labels. Quarantine is review, not deletion, and minority speech is protected from several sides: per-dialect error bounds are release blockers (R93), gold sets are stratified by dialect with per-group bounds (R26), Multi-VALUE dialect transforms run on all synthetic data (R93), and stop is unioned with a deterministic lexicon that includes dialect patterns (R66, R93). Starvation would show up as a failing per-group bound, which blocks release rather than hiding the problem.
- Verdict: R51 quarantines rare *repeated* n-grams, not every rare form. R93 applies Multi-VALUE dialect transforms to all synthetic data, which keeps dialect features frequent in the training union. The stop lexicon has dialect patterns (R93, R66). Certification gold sets are real audited data, separate from the quarantine path, and per-dialect bounds are release blockers. So starvation would surface as a visible failing bound, not a hidden harm. The attack correctly notes that the filter's criteria are thin, but the existing design does not turn it into a silent disparity.

### Round 131. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Dialect/accent stratification needs a group label for every audited call, which is itself an unreliable and sensitive inference
- Attack: To enforce per-group bounds, each audited call must be assigned a dialect/accent group. Callers don't self-report it. Auditors guess from audio, or an accent classifier does. A Black caller with a Midwestern General American accent is binned "majority"; a Nigerian-English speaker is binned with Caribbean English. Failures land in the wrong strata, diluting the true minority-group error rate below its alarm threshold, while the group label is stored per customer. Group-wise guarantees are only as good as group labels; misclassified membership biases per-group estimates toward the pooled rate and hides disparities. ASR error gaps are large (WER 0.35 vs 0.19 for Black vs white speakers; Koenecke et al., PNAS 2020), so the metric matters, yet inferring ethnicity-linked dialect from voice is itself sensitive data.
- Defense: Stratification applies only to the audit gold set, never to the runtime or the customer record. Gold sets live in deletable stores with PII surrogates (R7). Prohibited-basis fields carry tags and a build invariant that bars their use (R46). The strata are dialect/accent, which are linguistic features, not ethnicity. Mis-binning dilutes per-group rates, but the per-call bound across all calls still holds (R66), and matched-pair tests (R46) probe disparity without any per-caller label. The attack is right that the binning method is unspecified and that storing a label is sensitive, so I specify both below.
- Verdict: R26 requires per-group bounds on dialect/accent strata but does not say how a call gets its group label. Label noise biases each per-group estimate toward the pooled rate and can hide exactly the disparity the blocker exists to catch. The Designer's existing points (the per-call bound still holds overall, deletable gold sets in R7, and R46 matched-pair tests, which are about the lending menu and not ASR or labeling error) do not restore the per-group guarantee. Feature-based strata, inter-rater correction and label-free worst-cluster checks are all new.
- Fix: Define strata by observable linguistic and accent features (a Multi-VALUE feature checklist and L1-accent family), annotated by two trained auditors. Measure inter-rater agreement and use the confusion matrix to correct per-group estimates. Keep labels only on audit records under surrogate IDs, never in CRM, runtime or model inputs, and delete them with the gold item. Add a label-free worst-group check: unsupervised clusters over acoustic and ASR-confidence embeddings, with the worst cluster held to the same bound.

### Round 132. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Drift alarms at 1e-5 rates have detection delays of weeks to months, so harm accrues long before audited sampling can notice
- Attack: Fleet: 100,000 calls/day; 1% audited (1,000/day). A news story about "solar scam calls" changes how callers refuse; Tier A misses jump tenfold from 1e-5 to 1e-4. Expected audited Tier A events: one every 10 days. A per-state alarm split over many states sees almost nothing. Meanwhile the fleet produces ~10 Tier A harms every day. Detection needs several events per state: months. The detection delay of any sampled-rate monitor scales with 1/(rate x sample size); at 1e-5 rates statistical power is near zero. The design monitors only labeled audited outcomes, with no unlabeled input-distribution drift detection (embedding or event-mix shift) to trigger early. Delayed labels make it slower. Thousands of harms can occur before rollback.
- Defense: The scenario's premise that monitoring is "only labeled audited outcomes" on 1% of calls is false for stop, the main Tier A harm. The post-call DNC sweep re-runs the lexicon and GLOBAL pass over every transcript before any redial (R66), giving 100% coverage, and the lexicon is unioned with Needle mid-call. Several 100%-coverage operational signals already exist: fallback and clarify rates (R36), per-fragment repair rates (R48), cancellation rate per branch (R50), and complaint joins (R71). A budget breach triggers rollback to the previous certified artifact (R66). The attack does show that leading, label-free alarms need to be explicit, so I add them below.
- Verdict: The existing drift alarms (R33) run on audited samples, and at 1e-5-scale rates with roughly 1% audit they need weeks to months to fire. The post-call DNC sweep (R66) covers 100% of calls but re-runs the same Needle GLOBAL pass plus the lexicon. It prevents redials, but it does not detect a Needle drift that the lexicon also misses, and the in-call harm has already happened (the design's own open item says "missed voice stop caught post-call, not instantly"). Fallback, repair and cancellation rates (R36, R48, R50) exist as metrics but are not wired as Tier A drift triggers. The label-free leading alarms are new.
- Fix: Run label-free drift monitors on 100% of traffic: per-state event-mix shift, clarify and near-tie rate, pre-mask off-schema mass, lexicon-vs-Needle disagreement rate, out_of_scope cluster growth, and hang-up-after-turn rate, all with CUSUM or sequential tests. Any alarm triggers an immediate 100% audit surge on the affected states. Affected Tier A states revert automatically to deterministic fallback until the surge clears.

### Round 133. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Label-prior shift: upsampled rare safety events and per-source caps change Needle's class priors each release, silently moving stop and commit error rates
- Attack: Real calls contain stop/DNC in ~2% of turns; the training union is rebalanced to 15% to teach it, and per-source caps change the mix again in each release. Needle's implicit prior swings: release 9 over-fires "stop" on "not right now" (false exits), release 10 under-fires after synthetic stop items are capped. The clarify margin, fixed in config, now sits at a different point on each release's score scale. Classifier posteriors depend on training class priors; when they differ from deployment priors, outputs and thresholds are miscalibrated (label-shift literature, e.g., Lipton et al., ICML 2018). The design re-mixes data every release but states no prior correction or per-release threshold recalibration on real traffic, so certified error rates do not carry over.
- Defense: Certified error rates aren't carried over between releases. The certificate is keyed to the weights hash (R3, R53), so each release's rates are re-measured on real human-audited traffic (R2). If release 9 over-fires stop, its own false-stop rate (a Tier A metric, R24) is measured and blocks it, and the same goes for under-firing. Stop is also the union of Needle and the deterministic lexicon (R66), so a Needle prior swing toward under-firing stops can't drop lexicon hits. Over-firing produces safe exits, and R24's resume check handles ambiguous ones. The real gap is that a fixed clarify margin sits on a shifting score scale, which I fix below.
- Verdict: Error rates are not carried between releases. The certificate is keyed to the weights hash (R3, R53), and each release is re-certified on real, human-audited traffic (R2). False-stop and false-continue are Tier A metrics (R24), so a prior swing in either direction is measured and blocks that release. The lexicon union (R66) keeps under-firing from dropping lexical stops. A fixed clarify margin on a shifting score scale costs accuracy and throughput, and is a tuning gap rather than a hidden safety failure.


### Round 134. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Model-checked graph is not the binary that runs: StateSmith codegen and C toolchain supply chain
- Attack: Invariants (every commit path passes READ_BACK, no price writer from Needle) are proven on the exported graph. The C that actually runs is produced by StateSmith (an open-source generator pulled from a package registry) plus a compiler and build image. A compromised or buggy generator release emits a transition from QUOTE straight to CONTRACT_SENT under one rare guard combination. The graph still model-checks clean; the binary skips READ_BACK and the bundle hash faithfully certifies the wrong artifact. Verification targets the model, not the compiled artifact; nothing in the design proves generated C refines the graph (no translation validation, no reproducible build, no diverse double-compile). Build-chain backdoors are real: xz-utils CVE-2024-3094 hid in release tarballs, not source. A sampled 1e-5 audit cannot find a guard-gated path.
- Defense: The skipped READ_BACK would still have to get past checks that are separate from the transition table. confirm_order/accept_offer commits "only the quote hash read back immediately before the yes" (R49), and "Read-back bound to config hash" (R23). Disclosure flags are "written only by playback engine on uninterrupted completion" (R39), and the playback engine is a separate component from the generated C. The lexical commit gate (R87) is aimed at "this read-back question id". So a backdoored QUOTE->CONTRACT_SENT edge reaches the outbox with no read-back hash and no playout mark. Property-based random walks (R49) and fault injection run against the built artifact, not just the graph. That said, the attack is right that nothing formally proves the binary refines the graph.
- Verdict: The design's core safety claims (every commit path passes READ_BACK, no Needle price writer) are proven only on the exported graph by our own checker (R69); nothing in design.md or final-plan.md checks that the StateSmith-generated, compiled C matches that graph. The existing checks the defense cites (R49 quote-hash commit, R23 config-hash binding, R87 lexical gate) are themselves logic inside the same generated machine, and no outbox-side check requires a READ_BACK playout mark before CONTRACT_SENT. The defense admits the gap, and its real answers (translation validation, independent trace monitor, reproducible build) are all NEW.
- Fix: Vendor StateSmith and the compiler by hash with reproducible builds; at release, enumerate the compiled binary's (state, event, abstract-guard) transition relation by harness and diff it against the exported graph (any extra edge fails the release); and add an independently written runtime trace monitor at the outbox that refuses CONTRACT_SENT or any commit effect unless the committed trace contains READ_BACK with a playback-engine completion mark for the same quote hash.

### Round 135. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Security patching vs a certificate keyed on runtime build and hardware class
- Attack: A critical CVE lands in the inference runtime, glibc, OpenSSL or the GPU/CPU driver on the pinned Needle fleet. Patching changes the runtime build hash, so every certificate on every state expires at once. Options: keep serving calls on a known-vulnerable fleet that holds live customer PII and payment-adjacent data, or drop the entire fleet to deterministic fallback while recertifying on fresh human-audited real calls. Rule-of-three at 1e-5 needs ~300,000 clean audited calls per bound; recertification after every patch means weeks to months dark. The design forces a standing choice between being insecure and being offline, and patch cadence (monthly at minimum) outruns certification cadence.
- Defense: Recertification does not mean 300,000 new live calls. A certificate's empirical content is the input-to-decision behavior of (weights, tool list, graph). The design already has the tools to show a patched runtime preserves that behavior: "cross-node bit-identical replay before release" (R53), replay of "all states' frozen human-audited gold sets" (R3), the daily golden-audio canary, and "immutable retained bundles". If the patched runtime produces bit-identical outputs on every audited call ever retained, the 1e-5 evidence carries over unchanged. Most CVEs (OpenSSL, glibc networking, drivers) sit outside the inference math. States that do diverge drop to the existing deterministic fallback, so the fleet never goes dark.
- Verdict: The existing design already has the mechanism for admitting a new runtime build: R3 makes every candidate build replay all states' frozen human-audited gold sets, and R53 requires cross-node bit-identical replay before release, so a patched runtime that reproduces every audited real call bit-for-bit carries the same evidence rather than needing 300,000 fresh calls. Most of the CVEs named (OpenSSL, glibc networking) sit outside the "runtime/kernel build" the R53 key names, and states that diverge fall to the existing per-state deterministic fallback rather than the fleet staying on a vulnerable build.

### Round 136. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Forged or replayed e-sign webhooks advancing calls to SALE_PENDING
- Attack: "Status only from vendor webhooks." The webhook endpoint is a public URL. An attacker (or a rogue lead vendor wanting commissions) replays a captured "envelope-completed" payload, or posts a forged one with another envelope id. The state machine moves WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE to SALE_PENDING, starts the cancellation clock and schedules install against a contract nobody signed; conversely a forged "declined/voided" kills real sales. DocuSign Connect HMAC signing is an opt-in feature the integrator must enable and verify (docusign.com/blog/developers/dsdev-connect-feature-hmac); replay protection and envelope-to-call binding are not in the design. The graph treats vendor events as ground truth with no authentication or idempotency spec.
- Defense: A forged "completed" event does not install anything. "Install waits out the longest cancellation window" (point 11), the success label is "joined later from CRM/install records" (R50), and "No CRM sale/crew before signature" (R17). The envelope goes to all record owners and lender signers, "gated by SMS OTP to dialed number" (R22/R88), so real signatures exist on the vendor side to check against. Vendor events enter as a "versioned status enum" (R60) through WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE substates with "holds + idempotency keys" (R55). Duplicate replays are already deduplicated. The real gap is authentication and envelope binding, which the design does not spell out.
- Verdict: R60 makes vendor webhooks/APIs the only source of signature status, but nothing in the design authenticates those events, binds an envelope id to the session, or blocks out-of-order transitions; R55 idempotency keys stop exact replays only, not forged events with other ids. A forged "completed" starts the cancellation clock and schedules install, and a forged "declined/voided" kills a real sale. The defense concedes this, and its fixes are all NEW.
- Fix: Treat any inbound webhook as a wake-up hint only: on receipt, pull envelope status from the vendor API with the system's own credentials and make only that pulled status a StateSmith event; also require HMAC verification (with a build check that it is enabled), timestamp windows, envelope id stored in the session's outbox record with unknown or mismatched ids rejected, and monotonic status (void after completed goes to human review).

### Round 137. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Carrier failover changing codec and sample rate outside the certified bundle
- Attack: The primary SIP trunk degrades; traffic fails over to a second carrier. Audio now arrives as AMR-NB transcoded to G.711 at 8 kHz with heavier packet-loss concealment instead of the wideband path ASR was certified on. ASR version and ITN flags are unchanged, so the bundle hash still matches, but word error rate on numbers and negations jumps. Needle gets cleaner-looking but wrong text all afternoon. The certificate pins ASR software, not the acoustic channel (codec, sample rate, jitter buffer, transcoding hops, carrier noise suppression). Narrowband and transcoded audio measurably raise ASR WER. Per-dialect bounds were measured on one channel; failover silently invalidates them while every hash check passes.
- Defense: Channel-induced WER errors still hit the design's ASR-agnostic checks. "Tier A numbers confirmed digit-wise" (R26) and "critical-slot read-back" (R2) let the caller catch the error. The "argument grounding gate" (R61) and the "ASR-confidence/no-speech pre-gate" route low-confidence numbers and negations to clarify. The commit gate is "lexical" and "closed re-ask" on anything ambiguous. "Continuous audited sampling with per-state drift alarms" (R33) reverts a state to fallback when clarify or error rates move. The attack is right that the certificate pins ASR software, not the acoustic channel, so an afternoon of degraded quality could pass before drift statistics accumulate.
- Verdict: Wrong ASR text on a degraded channel still has to pass the design's ASR-independent checks: digit-wise confirmation of Tier A numbers (R26), critical-slot read-back that turns a single error into a caller correction (R2), the grounding gate (R61), the ambiguous-polarity closed re-ask (R77), and a commit gate that needs a short lexical affirm to the read-back question id (R81/R87). So the realistic result is more clarifies and fallbacks, which the R33 drift alarms then catch, not committed harm. US PSTN audio is mostly narrowband G.711 already, so "certified on wideband" overstates the shift. The certification-validity gap is real but its consequences fail safe.

### Round 138. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Distributed dialer race on frequency caps and DNC across workers and regions
- Attack: Ten dialer workers in two regions share caps and suppression through a replicated store. A number gets a call at 10:00 that ends in an opt-out; the post-call DNC sweep runs after transcript processing. Meanwhile workers in region B, reading a lagged replica, place two more calls on the same campaign, and a fourth from a retry queue. Florida's three-calls-in-24-hours cap and the opt-out are both breached. The pre-dial gate is specified as logic, not as a linearizable check-and-reserve. Fla. Stat. 501.059(8)(a) caps calls at three per 24 hours on the same subject; each extra call is a per-call statutory violation. Eventually consistent counters plus asynchronous DNC sweeps make over-dial a property of the architecture, not a bug.
- Defense: The design already makes a redial wait on prior outcomes: "post-call DNC sweep of every transcript ... before any redial" (R66) is a barrier, not a background job. Mid-call opt-outs are handled at once: "Stop = union of deterministic lexicon and Needle GLOBAL" ends the call, and DTMF opt-out does a "DNC write" at the root (R75). Caps are evaluated by the deterministic pre-dial gate "for the most restrictive candidate jurisdiction" (R41, R70), and Florida's three-per-24h is a regulation-profile row. The attack correctly notes the gate is specified as logic, not as a concurrency primitive.
- Verdict: R66 makes the post-call DNC sweep a precondition "before any redial," so the scenario's region-B and retry-queue calls (all redials placed before the first call's sweep committed) are already forbidden by the design; a replica-lag implementation that allows them breaks a stated invariant rather than showing a design gap. Mid-call opt-outs end the call and write DNC at once (R24, R75), and caps are evaluated for the most restrictive jurisdiction (R41, R70). The attack fairly notes that the concurrency primitive is unspecified, and the defense's permit only makes the existing ordering concrete.

### Round 139. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Clock, NTP and tzdata outside the content-hashed bundle drive calling-hours compliance
- Attack: Calling-hours guards depend on the host clock and the OS time-zone database. A container image ships stale tzdata, an NTP source fails and the clock drifts, or a DST transition is mishandled in one region. On the November fall-back day the dialer computes local time an hour off and places a wave of calls at 7:15 a.m. or 9:20 p.m. local across a whole time zone. The bundle pins graph, weights, ASR and policy table, but not time source or tzdata, which every hours guard reads. TSR 16 CFR 310.4(c) bans calls before 8 a.m./after 9 p.m.; several states are stricter. One bad clock is fleet-wide and simultaneous, so harm arrives as thousands of violations at once.
- Defense: Hours are already conservative by design: "calling hours and frequency caps for the most restrictive candidate jurisdiction" (point 1), using "address tz (stricter overlap with area code)" (R41), with hour rules as versioned regulation-profile facts rows. The fleet-wide failure needs a wrong clock or tzdata on the dialer. The attack is right that time source and tzdata are outside the content-hashed bundle.
- Verdict: Every calling-hours guard reads the host clock and the OS tz database, yet neither is in the content-hashed bundle and the design has no clock-integrity check, guard band or dial-time canary; R68 pinned ASR for exactly this kind of silent dependency drift, but time was left out. A missing or broken tz database (e.g. falling back to UTC) or a bad time source is fleet-wide and simultaneous, so one fault yields thousands of 16 CFR 310.4(c) violations. The existing most-restrictive-candidate logic only helps when address and area code differ. The defense concedes this, and every mitigation it gives is NEW.
- Fix: Pin tzdata in the certified bundle and make the dial gate use only that copy; take time from at least three authenticated sources and fail closed when offset or uncertainty exceeds a small bound; shrink the lawful window by a guard band (wider on DST-transition days); and property-test the gate over every zone across DST boundaries, plus a daily per-zone test-dial canary.

### Round 140. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Blue/green rollback resurrecting superseded prices, warranties and legal rows
- Attack: A new bundle ships a price increase, a recalled inverter removed from the warranty table and a new state disclosure effective today. The golden-audio canary trips on an unrelated latency regression; auto-rollback restores yesterday's bundle. The old policy table is now live again: lower prices, the recalled inverter's warranty, and no new disclosure, all quoted under a still-valid certificate for that old bundle. "Certificates expire when a cited policy row changes," but rollback makes the old row current again, and its certificate was never revoked. Rollback is treated as safe by construction, yet legal and commercial facts are time-dependent; nothing checks a restored bundle against today's effective-dated obligations.
- Defense: The design states that facts carry effective dates: "versioned, owned facts table with effective dates" (design runtime), and "certificate = (state, weights hash, facts-table version)" (R3). A restored bundle's certificate is tied to a policy-table version that is no longer current, so under the design's own keying it is not valid for today's facts. "Certificates expire when a cited policy row changes" applies at expiry time, not at rollback time. Derived values are "computed from versioned inputs on read" (R23), so a quote checks row validity at render time. The attack still exposes that rollback is not specified as policy-aware.
- Verdict: The final plan puts the policy table inside the content-hashed bundle, and R66/R67 roll back to "the previous certified artifact," so a rollback brings back yesterday's prices, the recalled inverter's warranty and the missing disclosure, together with a certificate that was valid for that bundle. Effective dates protect nothing here, because yesterday's table did not know its rows would be superseded. "Certificates expire when a cited policy row changes" is never triggered by a rollback, and the design never tracks the current effective table separately from the bundle. The defense admits rollback is not policy-aware, and all its fixes are NEW.
- Fix: Take the policy table out of rollback: it is forward-only, and recalled or withdrawn rows become tombstones no bundle can cite. Rollback restores graph, weights, fragments and ASR only, always runs against the current effective table, and may target only a retained bundle that is continuously re-checked (lints, fact-key coverage, gold replay) as compatible with that table; otherwise QUOTE/READ_BACK states drop to lead-gen or callback fallback. The runtime refuses any row outside its effective_from/until window.

### Round 141. Winner: Designer
- Topic: SIM-swap or port-out hijack defeats challenge-response identity and SMS-OTP e-sign
- Attack: A fraudster SIM-swaps a homeowner's number. The outbound call (or a callback) rings the fraudster, who passes the identity challenge using breached address and household data matching the lead record. They qualify, accept the read-back, then receive the e-sign link and OTP by SMS on the hijacked number. A solar contract, a lender application and a UCC/lien filing now sit on the victim's home. Both factors ride the same phone number, and knowledge-based challenges use data sold in breach dumps. FBI IC3 logged 1,611 SIM-swap complaints and over $68M losses in 2021 alone (ic3.gov/PSA/2022/PSA220208). The design has no number-port/SIM-change check or out-of-band verification.
- Defense: A SIM-swapper on this design still faces checks that do not ride the phone. The lender "runs application and decisions" (its own KYC/CIP). Signers are "all record owners + lender stipulated signers" from "record-owner lookup on validated address" (R88). "Capacity flags ... -> human review path." Install "waits out the longest cancellation window". The voice channel never collects SSN or bank data (R45, R89), so breached-data KBA unlocks nothing financial on its own. RND blocks reassigned numbers. The attack is right that the identity challenge and OTP share one factor (the number), and the design has no port or SIM-change signal.
- Verdict: The SIM-swap fraudster gains nothing and cannot get the contract to lien or install. The design schedules a site survey as the next step after signature (whole-call scope) and ROOF_FIRST inspection, both needing physical access at the victim's home; install waits out the longest cancellation window (R17); and signers come from record-owner lookup on the validated address, with capacity flags going to human review (R88). The lender runs its own application and KYC, and the voice channel never collects SSN, bank or card data (R45, R89), so the call leaves the fraudster nothing to monetize. The shared-factor weakness the attack names is real, but its harm is caught before anything irreversible happens.

### Round 142. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Predictive-dialer pacing outrunning Needle fleet capacity causes abandoned calls
- Attack: The dialer paces outbound connects on expected Needle throughput. A host failure or latency spike halves fleet capacity. The dialer keeps connecting live humans; turns queue, the PREAMBLE starts late or not at all, and callees hear seconds of silence and hang up. Fallback "callback or human" piles up booked callbacks that human staff cannot absorb. TSR 16 CFR 310.4(b)(4) and 47 CFR 64.1200(a)(7) require connection within two seconds of the greeting and abandonment at or below 3% per campaign per 30 days. Nothing couples dial pacing to live model capacity; an infrastructure outage becomes a regulatory abandonment breach at full fleet scale.
- Defense: The first agent audio does not need Needle. OPENING "waits for callee" and then plays the "compiled, unskippable" PREAMBLE, a pre-rendered certified fragment that the deterministic machine can start on the callee's greeting with no inference. So the two-second connection rule is met by deterministic playback even when the fleet is degraded. Mid-call, "state failing its live error bound -> deterministic fallback" and "call budgets -> WRAP_UP" end calls gracefully. The dial list is limited to "prior-express-written-consent and inbound/warm leads" (R59), which is not mass predictive overdial. The attack is right that pacing is not coupled to fleet capacity, and callback bookings can exceed staff.
- Verdict: The two-second rule concerns what the callee hears after their greeting, and in this design that is the compiled, unskippable PREAMBLE (R31), started deterministically by OPENING once the callee answers (R35), with no Needle inference. Later slowness on a degraded fleet causes delays, which the wall-clock budget counters turn into WRAP_UP (R69), and failing states drop to fallback; neither is an "abandoned call" in the regulatory sense. The dial list is limited to PEWC and warm leads (R59), and callback bookings already have CONFLICT handling (R55). The attack assumes predictive overdial pacing that the design never specifies.

### Round 143. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Human-audit and replay pipeline forces retention of full raw audio with third-party labelers
- Attack: Certification and drift alarms require humans listening to real call audio, all disagreements plus random samples, and bit-identical replay needs the inputs kept. So raw audio with identity answers, addresses, finances and household details is retained and shipped to an outsourced labeling vendor. That vendor is breached, or a labeler exfiltrates recordings, exposing thousands of homeowners' voices and verified personal data. "No-retain mode" and "PII surrogates" cannot apply to audio that the release gate needs humans to hear. The safety case structurally depends on the largest, least-controlled copy of customer data. Third-party file-transfer breaches (MOVEit, 2023) show vendor tiers are the usual leak point; voiceprints also enable cloning.
- Defense: The audio is less rich than claimed. Data minimization means the agent "collects only coarse facts ... no account/income/credit" (R7). The "payment-data redactor on ASR stream (text + audio mute)" (R45) and lint bans on requesting "SSN, DOB, bank/card data" (R89) keep the most dangerous items out of recordings. "NO_RETAIN mode" applies without affirmative retention consent, and the "build pipeline rejects transcripts lacking retention-consent flag" (R40). Real utterances live "only in deletable gold sets" (R7). Certification needs audited samples from consented calls, not all calls. The attack is right that outsourced listening is still a large exposure surface.
- Verdict: The existing design already limits what labelers can hear and who may keep it: data minimization with no account, income or credit data (R7), real-time audio mute of payment data (R45), lint bans on requesting SSN, DOB, bank or card data (R89), NO_RETAIN without affirmative retention consent, a pipeline that rejects transcripts lacking the consent flag, processors barred from training (R40), and deletable gold sets (R7). Certification needs only audited samples from consented calls, not every call. The "outsourced labeling vendor" is the attacker's assumption, and the remaining exposure is the ordinary QA-recording risk of any call center, not a structural flaw specific to this safety case.


### Round 144. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Culturally deferential "yes" (face-saving acquiescence) passing the lexical COMMIT gate
- Attack: A first-generation Vietnamese-American homeowner, fluent in English, treats the caller as an authority. Throughout READ_BACK she says "yes" after each item to mean "I am listening, I respect you", the norm she grew up with. At the commit question id she says a flat, falling, unhedged "Yes." No condition, no rising pitch, no overlap. The gate sees a short, direct lexical affirm to the right question id and fires CONTRACT_SENT. She believed she had agreed to receive information. Every COMMIT exclusion is acoustic or syntactic (conditional, reported, rising, overlapping). Acquiescent "yes" is pragmatic, culturally patterned, and textually identical to consent. Needle labels text, so no feature exists to separate it. Per-dialect release strata measure ASR error, not pragmatic meaning, so the audit labels this call "correct commit".
- Defense: A bare "Yes" cannot close on its own. R15 requires a "Teach-back before close (customer states payment+term; code compares)": she has to say the payment and the term in her own words. A listening-"yes" can't produce those. Even after COMMIT, CONTRACT_SENT only sends a written state-specific contract with a cancellation notice (R17). Signature is a separate OTP-gated e-sign act (R22), and install waits out "Longest statutory cancellation window ... for all" (R15). A contract signed under that misunderstanding gets rescinded, and that fails the success label (R71). That's the point where this pragmatic failure would show up and get counted.
- Verdict: The attack treats the lexical affirm as the last gate, but the design already has R15 teach-back: before close the customer states payment and term, and code compares them. After that come a written state-specific contract with a cancellation notice (R17), a separate OTP-gated e-sign act (R22) and a cancellation window applied to everyone (R15), all checked in the design file. A listening-"yes" alone cannot reach a signed contract. The residual risk is parroting, which the design already lists as an open item.

### Round 145. Winner: Breaker
