- Topic: High-context indirect refusals ("I'll consider it", "that's a little difficult") routed into booked callbacks
- Attack: A Japanese-American retiree, asked to book a follow-up, answers "Hmm, that may be a little difficult... I will consider it." In his norms this is a firm no; saying "no" outright is rude. Needle labels it a soft objection/defer, not stop. The graph books a callback inside frequency caps. On the callback he repeats the same polite formula; another callback is booked. He never says a stop-lexicon word, so the post-call DNC sweep finds nothing. Stop is a lexicon plus a GLOBAL event trained on explicit refusals. Culturally indirect refusal is a known pragmatic pattern, not a hedge on the words "don't call". The design converts a polite no into a recurring sales pipeline, which under TSR 310.4(b)(1)(i) and state harassment rules is repeated calling of a consumer who declined.
- Defense: "I will consider it" books nothing. Under R41, the agent "proposes concrete times and books only the confirmed one". With no confirmed time there's no callback. R21 sets a refusal budget ("max one approved acknowledge-and-ask") and an invariant of "no pitch after decline". R15's defers_decision(to) feeds the vulnerability counter, and NO_SALE_SAFE_EXIT suppresses redial. The real gap is that indirect refusal isn't labeled as decline, so I'm closing it.
- Verdict: R41 does stop the specific "booked callback" mechanism, since only a confirmed time is booked. But the deeper failure stands in the existing design. The no-pitch-after-decline invariant and the refusal budget (R21) fire only on a recognized decline. So a high-context refusal labeled as a soft objection or defer still gets rebuttals on the call, and the lead stays redialable within the frequency caps. The Designer concedes that indirect refusal is not labeled as decline, and the remedy is entirely new.
- Fix: Add an indirect_decline event class, trained from a high-context-refusal red-team stratum and bound by the decline invariants. Answer it with one fixed closed question ("Would you prefer we don't call again?"). Anything except an explicit time request suppresses the lead. A second non-committal defer on a later contact closes the lead and writes internal DNC. Extend the post-call sweep with the same lexicon.

### Round 146. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Non-Western name structures and gendered honorifics breaking the identity challenge and the greeting slot holes
- Attack: Lead record stores "Maria Garcia Lopez" as first "Maria", last "Lopez"; county title says "Garcia". Challenge-response against the lead record mismatches when she confirms "Garcia", so she lands in WRONG_PARTY and is dropped. Another lead, a Korean name stored family-name-first, is greeted by given name as surname. A third, a trans homeowner whose purchased lead still says "Mr.", is greeted "Mr." in every certified fragment. Identity is a deterministic string match against a vendor record, so false WRONG_PARTY rates track ethnicity, a disparate-impact pattern the per-accent audit never stratifies. Honorific and name-order slots are filled from lead data the caller never controls, and fragments are certified by id, not by how the filled name sounds.
- Defense: Speech already avoids names and honorifics. R62 makes "name-free fragment variants by default", so nobody gets greeted "Mr." or has given and family names swapped. Name changes are "confirmed in writing". Identity isn't checked on the name alone: R88 looks up record owners "on validated address", and R73 challenge-response takes name and address together. R46's matched-pair tests already track "path-divergence" as a Tier A metric. The real gap is exact-string matching on name tokens.
- Verdict: The honorific and name-order prongs fail against R62, which already makes name-free fragment variants the default. The identity prong holds. R73's challenge-response is a "deterministic match" against the lead record with no name-normalization spec, and a false WRONG_PARTY result DNCs the real owner's number and flags the lead, so compound surnames and family-name-first records cause permanent exclusion. R46's matched-pair tests cover fair-lending offer paths, not identity. The Designer concedes the exact-string gap.
- Fix: Use a token-set name matcher across the lead and county records that ignores order and normalizes compound surnames, particles, hyphens and romanization. An address match plus any surname-token match counts as VERIFIED. A partial match goes to clarify or a human, never to a WRONG_PARTY DNC. Add a release-blocking false-WRONG_PARTY stratum over synthetic name structures.

### Round 147. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Speech disabilities (dysarthria, aphasia, stammer, electrolarynx) filtered out by the ASR pre-gate with no disability stratum in certification
- Attack: A homeowner with post-stroke dysarthria answers every QUALIFY question correctly but slowly and slurred. ASR confidence falls under threshold: unintelligible re-ask, then "clarify once, then fallback" to a callback. The callback repeats the same path. A laryngectomee with an electrolarynx is classified by the media/far-field detector as device audio and ignored. Neither ever reaches a human or a quote. The release blockers stratify by dialect/accent only; speech disability is not a group with its own bound, so systematic exclusion never trips a gate. A business that serves everyone except people with speech impairments risks disability-discrimination claims (e.g. California Unruh Act, Civ. Code 51) and loses legitimate buyers.
- Defense: Some of this is already covered. The R25 incomplete() label and the dangling-word rule stretch endpointing to 2-3s, and Tier A states wait longer. R56 request_human is available in every state. Fallback is "callback or human", and R42 RELAY_MODE already has a Tier A accessibility metric. R98's media detector looks for far-field speech (low direct-to-reverberant ratio, a music bed), and a near-field electrolarynx doesn't fit that. Still, the attacker is right that there's no disability stratum and a callback can loop, so both get fixed.
- Verdict: R26's per-group bounds are by accent/dialect only, so systematic failure on dysarthric or electrolarynx speech trips no release gate. The fallback is "callback or human" with no rule forcing a human after an unintelligible fallback, so the callback can repeat the same failing path. The Designer admits both gaps. R25 endpointing and R56 request_human help only a caller who can make themselves understood.
- Fix: Add speech-disability strata (dysarthria, stammer, aphasia, electrolarynx) with per-group release-blocking bounds. A number that hit an unintelligible fallback is routed only to a human on its next contact. Switch to an extended-timing profile (no speculation) after the first unintelligible turn. Add electrolarynx audio as near-field negatives for the media detector.

### Round 148. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Coerced owner: a relative, caregiver or abusive partner in the room scripting the owner's answers
- Attack: An adult son, deep in debt, wants the solar loan so he can later refinance against the house. He holds the phone on speaker and whispers each answer; his mother, the verified record owner, repeats them clearly: "Yes, I own it." "No, not moving." At commit she says "Yes." Whispers fall under the no-speech gate; side talk is never transcribed. Identity, suitability and COMMIT all pass on her own clean voice. Every safeguard keys on the verified speaker's words. Coercion is invisible in a transcript of compliant answers, and the vulnerability counter detects confusion, not duress. FinCEN's April 2024 elder-exploitation analysis singles out theft by people the victim knows, family and caregivers (fincen.gov). The design certifies the exact pattern exploiters use.
- Defense: A solar loan gives the son no cash. The lender runs the application and decides, and the money goes to the installer, so this pattern's payoff is small. Existing layers still apply. R9 speaker_change resets identity, and side_talk is a labeled event. R88 sends capacity flags (POA, conservatorship) to human review. R15 has a delayed teach-back. Everyone gets the senior 5-day cancel window before install, and anti-phishing lines are in the preamble. I accept that a transcript can't show whispered duress.
- Verdict: Whispered coaching of a lucid owner who speaks for herself cannot be detected from audio by any telesales process, human or AI. The existing design already has the reasonable layers: speaker_change and side_talk events (R9, R63), capacity flags to human review (R88), teach-back and a cancellation window for all (R15), an anti-phishing line (R89), and a lender-run application where the money goes to the installer. The attack's motive is also weak, since a solar lien hinders rather than helps a later cash-out refinance.

### Round 149. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: E-sign SMS to "all record owners" exposing a domestic-violence survivor to an estranged co-owner
- Attack: A woman who fled an abusive husband still shares title with him pending divorce. She agrees to solar. CONTRACT_SENT texts envelopes to all record owners, using the husband's number from a data vendor. He receives an SMS naming the company, the property, financing terms and that she is active and making decisions. He also never consented to texts from this seller. The design treats co-owners as signers, not as people whose contact may be dangerous, and has no "do not contact co-owner" household state. It sends a commercial text to a cell number without that person's prior express consent (TCPA risk) and broadcasts a survivor's activity and location-linked data to her abuser. Needle never hears anything that would block it.
- Defense: Signing by every record owner (R88) is legally necessary and protects owners. Whose contact gets used is a separate decision, and the design already treats outbound contact as gated: R74 says "no Needle tool can create outbound contacts" and has a per-number consent gate. R88's "co-owner notice before read-back" is the moment she'd raise the problem, and sensitive_event(distress) exits with no pitch. Still, sending an SMS envelope to a vendor-sourced co-owner number is a real gap.
- Verdict: The final plan sends the e-sign envelope by SMS "to all record owners", while R74's per-number consent gate sits in the dialer and R22 speaks only of the dialed number. Nothing in the design governs where a co-owner's number comes from or bars a vendor-sourced number. Nor is there any household-safety state that suppresses co-owner contact. The "co-owner notice" (R88) gives her a moment to speak but no path that acts on it short of a generic distress exit. The Designer concedes the gap.
- Fix: Extend the consent gate to every outbound SMS and email, and never send a co-owner envelope to vendor-sourced contact data. The verified customer gets a "co-signer required" packet and chooses the channel, or the co-owner self-opts in via a link. Add a sensitive_event(household_safety) subtype that means no co-owner contact, no close, and a confidential human-review path.

### Round 150. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Financial distress and affordability absent from suitability: desperate homeowners pushed into liens they cannot carry
- Attack: Caller: "We're two months behind on the mortgage; anything that cuts the power bill, I'll do it." Ownership, roof, shade, move horizon all pass. "Behind on the mortgage" is not a suitability column, and the vulnerability counter tracks confusion, not hardship. QUOTE offers financing with a UCC lien; the lender, not the agent, decides credit. The customer signs out of panic; savings do not cover the payment. CFPB's Aug 2024 solar-financing spotlight documents dealer fees and payments exceeding savings; its PACE ability-to-repay rule took effect March 1, 2026 (consumerfinance.gov). A suitability table blind to stated hardship lets the best-converting segment be the most financially vulnerable, and "not rescinded, installed" counts it a success.
- Defense: "Two months behind on the mortgage" is a sensitive_event (R19): "type ∈ ... financial_hardship" with a deterministic lexicon pre-emption and a "no pitch on hardship" scripted exit. It doesn't need a suitability column because it's GLOBAL in every state. Savings come only from verified usage (R61). The dealer fee is in the finance charge (R32), the lender decides credit, and unsuitable closes are Tier A harm (R71). Affordability itself isn't yet a table row, though.
- Verdict: R19 already defines sensitive_event(financial_hardship) as a GLOBAL event in every state, with deterministic lexicon pre-emption and "no pitch on hardship". "Two months behind on the mortgage" is exactly that case, so no suitability column is needed to stop this call. Savings come only from verified usage (R61), and an unsuitable close is Tier A harm (R71).

### Round 151. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Yes-ladder conditioning inside DISCOVERY/QUALIFY (the lint bans yes-eliciting openers only)
- Attack: After the preamble, approved discovery fragments ask: "You'd like lower bills, right?" "You care about your family's future?" "Makes sense to lock in a rate?" Each is a certified question with an expected "yes". By READ_BACK the customer has said "yes" eleven times. The commit question gets a habituated "yes" that the gate accepts as short, direct and lexical. Foot-in-the-door and commitment-consistency effects are well documented (Freedman & Fraser 1966). The design guards against yes-eliciting language only at OPENING; mid-call leading questions pass every lint and are exactly what success-tuned wording selection will favour. The COMMIT gate checks form of the yes, not whether earlier fragments manufactured it.
- Defense: Early yeses get no commit credit. R27 says "Commit tools exist only in post-read-back states; early affirmatives map to permission_to_continue." Every fragment is human-approved, compliance-signed and linted (R18), and the close needs R15's teach-back, where the customer produces the payment and term, which a habituated "yes" can't do. Wording variants are "chosen deterministically" (R59), not by a conversion-maximizing bandit. Success includes a suitability audit and survival of the cancellation window. Still, the opener-only ban is too narrow.
- Verdict: The harm path needs habituated yeses to produce a commit. But R27 maps all pre-read-back affirmatives to permission_to_continue, R15's teach-back requires the customer to produce the payment and term, and signature is a separate OTP act. Wording is chosen deterministically (R59) from compliance-signed templates (R18), not by conversion optimization, which removes the premise that selection drifts toward leading questions. The opener-only lint is narrow, but the existing design does not fail the attack.

### Round 152. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Hypomanic or manic buyers: high enthusiasm reads as the ideal close, not as vulnerability
- Attack: A homeowner in a manic episode talks fast, interrupts to say "Yes, yes, I want it, biggest system, sign me up," answers every slot crisply, and needs no clarify. No confusion markers, no repeated questions, so the vulnerability counter stays at zero. READ_BACK plays; he affirms instantly at the commit id. Two weeks later his family finds a $60,000 lien. The vulnerability counter is built on confusion signals (repetition, contradictions, clarify loops). Mania shows the opposite: fluency, certainty, eagerness, which every sales-derived signal scores as a strong buyer. Contracts by persons of unsound mind are voidable in many states (e.g. Cal. Civ. Code 39), and the design has no detector for this profile.
- Defense: "Biggest system" is impossible. Sizing comes only from calculator_output on verified usage (R61, R72), so stated wants can't enlarge the contract. Barge-in during mandatory read-back segments replays the segment from the start (R39), so interrupting can't skip READ_BACK. R15's teach-back requires stating payment and term. Install waits out the cancellation window for everyone, and the lender runs the application. Enthusiasm isn't a vulnerability signal yet, though.
- Verdict: "Biggest system" is blocked because sizing comes only from calculator_output on verified usage (R61, R72). Barge-in during mandatory segments replays them (R39), and every commit path passes READ_BACK (R85). Teach-back, the cancellation window for all, and lender underwriting apply as well. No telesales process reliably detects mania, and a pressured-speech detector risks penalizing fast or enthusiastic speakers, so the existing safeguards meet a reasonable standard.

### Round 153. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Lonely, isolated but lucid customers buying to keep the conversation going
- Attack: A widowed 74-year-old living alone enjoys the call. Small talk about weather and grandchildren is an internal transition, so the agent keeps returning warmly via approved icebreaker variants. She is lucid, passes every suitability row, and answers precisely. She says yes because the friendly caller "has been so kind" and she wants a reason for the installer to visit. Social isolation is a top fraud-risk factor for older adults, independent of cognition. The design only exits on confusion or sensitive events; rapport is treated as a free sales asset and small_talk as an unbudgeted loop. A deterministic warm voice that never tires is the ideal companion for an isolated person, and the audit labels the sale suitable.
- Defense: The agent can't be a companion. The AI designation is in the unskippable preamble (R31). R94's persona-truth lint bans first-person life claims ("family", "I live"), so it can't share grandchildren stories. R69's call budget counters (total turns, per-state visits, wall-clock) force WRAP_UP. Suitability rows, verified-usage savings and the cancellation window all apply. Rapport as a buying motive is a real gap.
- Verdict: The AI designation is unskippable (R31), and the persona-truth lint (R94) bans first-person life claims, so the agent cannot act as a companion sharing grandchildren stories. The R69 budget counters (total turns, wall-clock) bound any small_talk loop. The customer in the scenario is lucid and passes the suitability table with verified-usage savings and a cancellation window, so the residual harm is a suitable sale with a social motive, not an unsuitable one.


### Round 154. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Lender dealer fees buried in the "total cost" the policy table and calculator quote, so the cash and financed prices silently diverge
- Attack: Price table lists a system at $30,000. Customer picks the 25-year loan. The lender's program charges the installer a 25% dealer fee, so the installer's financed contract price is $40,000 while the quote stays at $30,000. Calculator reads the price row, not the lender's fee schedule. READ_BACK recites the table's "total cost" and an APR that passes the Reg Z lint. The contract the customer signs by SMS shows the $40,000 price. The cash buyer next door would have paid $30,000. Policy rows are seller-side facts; dealer fees are set by the lender and per program, outside the table. "Same menu for all" doesn't stop financed buyers paying a hidden markup. CFPB's 2024 solar spotlight found dealer fees often above 30%, hidden in the price and leaving a low-looking APR. The trigger-term lint can't see this. https://files.consumerfinance.gov/f/documents/cfpb_solar-financing-issue-spotlight_2024-08.pdf
- Defense: The design already treats the dealer fee as part of financing, not price: R32 puts "dealer fee in finance charge" and requires a "representative fully-disclosed example from versioned lender rate sheet". The R85 READ_BACK must state total cost and financing terms (Reg Z), and the R23 read-back is "bound to config hash", so a financed quote with a different amount is a different quote. R60 covers the lender side: "conditional approval at new amount invalidates quote hash". The R46 product-policy table ("all options to all") plus matched-pair tests make a financed-vs-cash divergence measurable. The real gap is that nothing yet forces the e-sign contract amount to equal the amount read back.
- Verdict: The CFPB scenario rests on a dealer fee hidden in the price so the APR looks low. R32 in design.md already puts the dealer fee in the finance charge and takes the representative example from a versioned lender rate sheet. R23 and R60 bind consent to the config/quote hash, and a new lender amount invalidates it. The Designer admits one residual gap: no invariant yet forces the e-sign contract amount to equal the read-back amount. That is hardening on a mechanism that already targets the attack.

### Round 155. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Net price quoted after a capped, first-come incentive that is exhausted or needs a reservation by the time of install
- Attack: Policy table row: "state battery rebate, $X per kWh," substantiated by the program's published step. QUOTE fragment presents the net price after the rebate. The customer signs on that net figure. During the cancellation-window wait, the program's funding step fills, or the application is rejected because the installer had to reserve before signing. The rebate drops to the next lower step or zero. The contract obligates the gross price. Certificates only expire when a cited row changes, and nobody edited the row. The substantiation id proves the rebate existed, not that this customer will get it. Capped programs are queue states held outside the bundle. The design deliberately delays install, which puts customers at the back of the queue. A deterministic, certified net-price claim becomes a misrepresentation (FTC s5) with no runtime check on reservation status.
- Defense: The design never lets an unverifiable external fact gate a number. R29's calculator gives "insufficient-inputs -> no figure", R78 "bans ... unverifiable external-fact fragments", and R18's banned-claims lint (e.g. "guaranteed credits") already covers promising money the seller can't guarantee. Facts rows have "effective dates" and certificates "expire when a cited policy row changes". The R85 READ_BACK must state "total cost", meaning the contract obligation, not a net figure. The attack is right that a capped, first-come rebate is queue state. It shouldn't be modelled as a static row, and the delay from the cancellation window does make that worse.
- Verdict: Nothing in the design stops a policy-table rebate row from reducing the spoken or read-back price. A substantiation id proves the program exists, not that this customer gets the money. R78's "unverifiable external fact" lint is about urgency and weather pretexts, not queue-state incentives. Certificates expire only on row edits, so a filled funding step leaves a certified net-price claim that is now false, made worse by the install delay the design itself imposes.
- Fix: Type incentive rows (guaranteed / capped / reservation_required). Capped and reservation rows can never reduce the quoted or read-back price. READ_BACK states the gross contract price and the incentive only as "if approved, not guaranteed". A net price may be spoken only with a program_reserved provenance slot set by a program or installer API event. A daily program-status feed expires capped rows. The contract carries an incentive-failure no-penalty cancel clause, and the reservation is filed inside the cancellation window.

### Round 156. Winner: Designer
- Topic: "Verified usage" can't be obtained on a cold call: utility interval data requires the account holder's separate authorization
- Attack: QUOTE needs a verified_usage slot. Utilities release interval data only through an authorization flow (utility login, Green Button Connect, signed release) by the account holder. On the call, the account is in a tenant's or late spouse's name, or the customer won't hand over utility credentials. Writer allowlists bar spoken usage from becoming verified_usage. Every call ends with no savings figure, or the team adds a "spoken bill amount" writer to rescue conversion, which is exactly the provenance the design forbids. The design assumes a data feed it never specifies. Utility data access is consent-gated by state utility privacy rules and depends on the account holder, not the record owner the IDENTITY step verified. The outcomes are either no savings pitch, and unit economics collapse, or an allowlist exception that reopens customer-asserted numbers into savings claims.
- Defense: The attack describes the designed behavior, not a failure. R61 says savings figures come "only from verified bill or usage data, never spoken estimates". R72 says "spoken amounts only fill coarse routing bands", and the writer allowlists plus "slot-source allowlist enforced at build time" make a "spoken bill amount" writer a failed build. A team can't quietly add one. R29 handles missing data: "insufficient-inputs -> no figure, offer survey". The call still covers product, price, warranties and financing without a savings claim, and R28 tracks "time-on-fallback ... as a throughput metric", so the economics are visible. Unit economics are never a reason to reopen provenance. What's missing is the path by which verified usage legitimately arrives.
- Verdict: The existing design fails safe exactly as intended. R61 allows savings only from verified bill or usage data, R72 enforces slot-source allowlists at build time (so a "spoken bill" writer fails the build), and R29 gives "insufficient inputs -> no figure, offer survey". R28 already tracks time-on-fallback as a throughput metric. Weaker conversion is a cost, not a safety or compliance break, and the design cannot be quietly loosened.

### Round 157. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Savings depend on a future utility tariff and rate-escalation assumption, not on usage; NEM changes make the certified savings fragment a forecast stated as fact
- Attack: Calculator takes verified 12-month usage times a policy-table "utility escalation 4%/yr" and the current export rate. A California customer is on the Net Billing Tariff, where export credit is far below retail and varies by hour. The calculator's single export rate overstates savings. Next year, the utility restructures fixed charges and the escalation row is never touched. Customers sign 25-year escalator leases priced against projected rates that don't arrive. "Savings only from verified usage" checks inputs, not the model. Export value and future rates are assumptions, and a policy-table key gives them a substantiation id they don't deserve. NEM 3.0 cut average export compensation about 75%, and a court upheld it in March 2026. https://www.pv-tech.org/new-california-net-metering-proposal-would-slash-export-rates-critics-warn ; https://pv-magazine-usa.com/2026/03/10/california-court-upholds-nem-3-0-dealing-blow-to-rooftop-solar
- Defense: The calculator isn't a single-number guess. R29 specifies "calculator with versioned tariff tables", and R79 makes calculator inputs explicit. R18 bans "zero bill" and "guaranteed credits", and the facts table carries "effective dates". So a tariff change is a row change, and certificates "expire when a cited policy row changes". The attack does land on two points: a flat export rate can't represent Net Billing Tariff hourly credits, and an escalation percentage with a substantiation id dresses up an assumption as a fact. Projections need a different provenance and different wording than verified facts.
- Verdict: R29's "versioned tariff tables" and R72's calculator_output provenance check where the inputs came from, not whether the model is right. Nothing requires hourly Net Billing Tariff export values, and nothing marks rate-escalation or future-tariff rows as assumptions. A single export rate or a 4%/yr escalator carrying a substantiation id is a forecast stated as fact. The Designer concedes both points.
- Fix: Add an "assumption" provenance type for escalation and future-rate rows. Any fragment filled from one must carry a lint-enforced "estimate, assumes X, not guaranteed" qualifier. The default savings figure uses today's rates only, and any projection is paired with a zero-escalation case. The calculator must use the customer's exact tariff schedule (e.g. NBT hourly export) on verified interval data, else no figure. A tariff-filing monitor expires rows. Escalator-lease READ_BACK states the year-1 and final-year payments.

### Round 158. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Interconnection and permission-to-operate gap: loan payments and the "installed" success label arrive before the utility lets the system run
- Attack: Contract signed, cancellation window waited out, panels installed. The utility's interconnection review requires a transformer or service upgrade, or the queue runs months. Loan payments start on the installation milestone. The customer pays the loan plus a full utility bill with a dead system. CRM marks "installed, not rescinded, no complaint yet." The success label joins, and this call trains and certifies as a win. A frustrated customer complains to the AG in month five, after the label is frozen. The design's success definition ends at install, not PTO or energization, and READ_BACK never discloses utility-side upgrade costs, approval risk or payment start timing. Interconnection is a third-party decision with costs the price table can't hold. The release gate is measured on labels that count non-operating systems as successes.
- Defense: R71 defines success as "installed AND not rescinded AND no complaint ... AND passes sampled suitability audit". R50 joins labels from install records, and R83 already requires "material-limitation disclosures" in every read-back. But the release gate isn't measured on those labels. R2 and R66 make it a "per-call harm bound measured only on real calls labeled by humans listening to audio". A label error affects training reward and metrics, not certification. The attack is right that success should mean energized, and that READ_BACK doesn't list interconnection risk or when payments start.
- Verdict: The claim that certification uses these labels is wrong: R2 and R66 gate release on audio-audited per-call harm. But the design's own success label (R71: installed, not rescinded, no complaint, which feeds training reward, the pass criterion and R50 branch metrics) counts systems that are installed but not operating. READ_BACK (R85) has no required disclosure of utility approval risk, upgrade cost or when payments start relative to PTO. An audio audit cannot see harm that happens months after the call.
- Fix: Redefine success as PTO granted plus N months of monitored production plus no complaint across the whole window, with labels provisional until then. Add policy-table rows required in READ_BACK and the contract: utility interconnection approval required, possible utility-side upgrade cost and who pays it, and the exact loan first-payment event. Make an install-to-PTO lag over threshold, and any "paying, not operating" state, Tier A harm signals per branch.

### Round 159. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Post-signature site survey change orders (main panel upgrade, reroof, trenching, structural) changing the price after the read-back the commit was gated on
- Attack: The phone quote has no site visit. After signing, the installer's surveyor finds a 100A panel, rafters needing reinforcement and a roof with five years left. They issue a change order: +$4,800 MPU, +$2,100 structural. The customer is told the system can't go in otherwise, and the cancellation window has passed. The lender re-papers the loan amount by e-sign. The AI's certified READ_BACK, the legal anchor of consent, no longer describes the deal. Every commit path passes READ_BACK, but the real price is set later by a subcontractor's survey the graph never sees. Suitability checks roof age and shade from customer answers, not electrical service size. Waiting out the cancellation window before install means change orders always land after cancellation rights have expired. That is the bait-and-switch pattern regulators target.
- Defense: The design already makes price changes after read-back void consent: R23 says "any change after read-back forces a new read-back", R49 says "accept_offer commits only the quote hash read back", and R60 says "conditional approval at new amount invalidates quote hash". R71's ROOF_FIRST ("inspection before any quote") handles roof age, and the original close definition schedules the "site survey ... as the next step". A change order is therefore a new quote that the design already refuses to treat as consented. What's missing is enforcing that downstream of the call, and the timing relative to the cancellation window.
- Verdict: R23, R49 and R60 bind consent to the hash read back on the call, but nothing in the design governs an installer's post-signature change order. Suitability (R71) has no electrical-service slot. Because install waits out the cancellation window (R17), every change order lands after cancellation rights expire. The Designer concedes that enforcement downstream of the call and the timing are missing.
- Fix: Schedule the site survey inside the cancellation window and make the contract contingent on a survey-confirmed price. Every change order becomes a new quote hash with written re-disclosure, a fresh e-sign by all signers, a no-penalty exit and a restart of the cancellation window. Install is blocked until then. Add main-panel amperage and roof-structure slots. When they are unknown, READ_BACK must state "price subject to survey" with policy-table cost ranges. An unconsented price increase is Tier A harm.

### Round 160. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Equipment substitution after signing (tariffs, supply shortages, discontinued SKUs) making the named product and warranties in the read-back false
- Attack: READ_BACK names panel model P and inverter I with their manufacturers' warranties from the policy table. Between signing and install (delayed by the cancellation window), new tariffs or a supply gap make P unavailable. The installer subcontractor swaps in a different brand with a shorter product warranty and a different degradation curve. The customer isn't told until the crew arrives. The policy row and certificate still cite P. Contract, install and the AI's certified statement now disagree. Fragments certify words, not fulfilment. The product facts the commit was gated on are controlled by a downstream supply chain and a subcontractor. The design has no post-sale reconciliation that re-discloses or re-consents when the installed BOM differs from the read-back, so the deterministic "truthful claim" is true only on the day of the call.
- Defense: R82 requires READ_BACK to state the "explicit product type/size/price from quote object", and R23 binds consent to a "config hash". The named model and warranties are therefore part of what the customer agreed to, not loose wording. A different SKU is a different config, which the design already says can't ride on the old yes. R91 separates product_warranty_years from workmanship_warranty_years as distinct table keys. The attack correctly notes there's no reconciliation step between the signed config and the installed bill of materials.
- Verdict: R82 and R23 make the named product part of the consented config, but no step compares what is installed to what was signed. The design controls install release (an outbox effect that waits for the cancellation window) yet never gates it on a bill-of-materials match. So the certified read-back can be silently falsified by substitution, which the Designer admits.
- Fix: Gate the install effect on WAITING_FOR_BOM_MATCH, comparing the installer's planned SKUs (then installed serials) to the signed config hash. On a mismatch, block install and require a written re-disclosure of the new product, warranty and degradation terms plus a fresh e-sign with a no-penalty cancel. Add a supply-status field to product rows that expires discontinued or tariff-hit SKUs. Count an un-reconsented substitution as Tier A harm.

### Round 161. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Installer or partner insolvency leaving the named workmanship and production warranties with no one behind them
- Attack: The policy table substantiates "25-year workmanship warranty" with the installer partner's contract. The partner, paid by the lender at install milestones, goes bankrupt mid-season. Customers signed last month have loans funded and roofs half-done. Earlier customers hold warranties nobody honors. The certified fragment keeps saying "25-year warranty" because the row's text didn't change. Only the counterparty died. Substantiation ids point at documents, not at a solvent obligor. Milestone disbursement pays dealers before completion. SunPower's August 2024 Chapter 11 and other installer collapses left homeowners with unfinished work and disputed warranties. Certificates expire on row edits, not on counterparty credit events. https://pv-magazine.com/2024/08/07/sunpower-goes-bankrupt ; https://news.bloombergtax.com/california-brief/solar-panel-bankruptcies-leave-frustrated-homeowners-in-limbo
- Defense: Warranty claims already cite substantiation ids in one owned, versioned policy table (R91, R18), and certificates "expire when a cited policy row changes". The mechanism for pulling a claim exists. Lender decisions and disbursement stay with the lender ("lender runs application and decisions"). The attack shows the trigger is too narrow, though. Substantiation points at a document, not at a solvent obligor, and nothing watches counterparty health.
- Verdict: A warranty term in a signed contract is a true statement when made. The design already has the mechanism to pull it everywhere: one owned policy table, separate warranty keys (R91), and certificate expiry on row change. A sales-call agent cannot make a counterparty solvent, and milestone disbursement is the lender's domain. The real weakness is that pulling a row depends on someone editing it, which is a trigger gap rather than a failure of the agent's design.

### Round 162. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Dealer or lead-partner fraud through the lead record: fabricated identity answers and partner-controlled phone numbers receiving the e-sign OTP
- Attack: A dealer partner paid per signed contract uploads leads. For a real elderly homeowner's address, it creates a record with an accomplice's mobile number and "challenge" answers it wrote. The AI calls the accomplice, the challenge-response "verifies" against the fraudster's own record, and the accomplice affirms the read-back. The e-sign SMS and OTP go to the same phone. The lender funds and a UCC/lien lands on the real owner's home. IDENTITY verifies against the lead record, which is partner-supplied, and SMS OTP proves only phone possession. Record-owner names come from public records, so nothing ties the signer's phone to the titleholder. Forged-signature and unauthorized installs are a documented solar fraud pattern. https://pv-magazine-usa.com/2024/08/14/what-happens-when-solar-is-installed-without-homeowners-permission ; https://www.npr.org/2024/08/14/1244330369/solar-rooftop-panels-environment-fraud-deception
- Defense: Several existing layers already work against this. R74 needs a "PEWC artifact from that subscriber", and R73 checks the Reassigned Numbers Database. R88 looks up record owners "on validated address" from public records, not the partner's lead, and the envelope goes to "all record owners + lender stipulated signers". The lender runs the application and its own identity checks. R15's vulnerability counter and R71's sampled suitability audit catch some elder cases. The attack is right that the challenge-response in R73 checks against a partner-supplied record, and that an SMS OTP proves only possession of a phone. Nothing ties the phone to the titleholder.
- Verdict: R73's challenge-response checks answers against the partner-supplied lead record, and R74's consent artifact can come from the same fraudulent partner. R22 sends the e-sign OTP to the dialed number. R88 sets the signer names from public records, but delivery and OTP still go to the fraudster's phone, so nothing ties the signer to the titleholder. The design leaves this open, as its own "lead-vendor consent audit" open item shows.
- Fix: Draw identity-challenge answers only from independent sources (public records, KYC vendor), never from partner lead fields. Run a pre-dial phone-to-record-owner match (carrier or identity graph); with no match, the call is lead-gen only. Require lender-grade identity verification (document plus selfie or KBA) for each record owner at e-sign. Mail a contract summary with cancel instructions to the property address of record and hold install for a set time after it. Track per-partner fraud metrics with auto-suspension, and use an independent consent-capture provider.

### Round 163. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Success labels from the CRM, controlled by installers and ops staff who are paid on them, gamed to certify bad branches
- Attack: Success = installed, not rescinded, no complaint. Install subcontractors and the ops team enter install dates and complaint tickets in the CRM, and their bonuses depend on low complaint and cancellation rates. They log angry calls as "service inquiry," offer small credits to drop complaints, and backdate installs. Suitability audits sample from what the CRM shows. A branch that closes vulnerable customers into change orders shows a clean success label, and the release gate certifies it. Joining labels from a CRM that interested parties write puts the training and certification signal in the hands of people with incentives to suppress harm. The design's integrity controls (hashes, replay, invariants) cover the call. None cover the label pipeline. Goodhart: the per-call harm bound becomes whatever the installer chose to record.
- Defense: The release gate doesn't read the CRM. R2 and R66 measure the "per-call harm bound measured only on real calls labeled by humans listening to audio ... on whole audited call traces". Gaming CRM fields can't certify a branch that the audio audit flags. R51 already says "labels never from self-reported outcomes". R71's "sampled suitability audit" is a separate check, and "unsuitable close = Tier A harm". The attack still hits training reward and the success metric, because R50 joins outcomes through a CRM that interested parties write. Those inputs need their own integrity controls.
- Verdict: The release gate is audio-based (R2/R66), so the "certifies it" claim is overstated. But post-call harm is invisible on audio. The only signals for it are the R71 success label, R50 cancellation-rate-per-branch tracking and the sampled suitability audit, and all of them flow through a CRM written by people paid on those numbers. R51's "no self-reported labels" targets customer poisoning, not operator edits, and nothing specifies where audit samples come from.
- Fix: Source outcome labels first from independent feeds (outbox post-install customer survey, AG/CFPB/BBB complaint feeds, lender disputes and delinquency, cancellation API logs, utility PTO records), which override CRM fields. Make CRM outcome fields append-only with signed, attributed edits and two-person approval for reclassifications or backdates. Sample suitability audits from the e-sign webhook list of all signed contracts. Raise a Tier A alarm on divergence between CRM and independent complaint rates per installer, and decouple ops and installer bonuses from training labels.


### Round 164. Winner: Designer
- Topic: No leave-taking event: "okay, thanks, bye" is neither stop/DNC nor anything else, so it is labeled acknowledge and the pitch continues
- Attack: In QUALIFY the agent asks about roof age. Caller: "Okay, I think I've heard enough, thanks, bye now." They stay on the line a beat, waiting for a polite goodbye, as people do. No GLOBAL event covers leave-taking; "bye" isn't in the stop lexicon (it isn't a DNC request). Needle must pick one event: acknowledge wins. The agent plays the next approved fragment: "Great! And roughly how old is your roof?" Caller: "I said bye." Again acknowledge plus the same question. The caller hangs up angry. Closing is a distinct conversational act (pre-closing then terminal exchange, Schegloff and Sacks 1973). The GLOBAL list has stop, cancel, sensitive, human, unlawful, identity and DNC policy, but nothing for "I'm ending this call." Talking through a goodbye reads as a pushy robocall and feeds complaints and carrier spam labels. The closed schema forces it into acknowledge, and the call carries on.
- Defense: The premise is wrong. R24 Tier 1 is a deterministic lexicon of "unambiguous, caller-directed" endings that explicitly includes "goodbye" and "not interested" and ends the call immediately. Stop = "union of deterministic lexicon ... and Needle GLOBAL", and the lexicon pre-empts Needle, so acknowledge never gets a chance. "I think I've heard enough" is also covered by R145 indirect_decline (lead suppressed unless the caller asks for a time). The post-call sweep re-runs the same lexicon. The real gap is narrower: the variant forms ("bye now", "gotta go", "I'm done") and the fact that Tier 1 hangs up without a polite closing.
- Verdict: R24 Tier 1 really does list "goodbye" (and "not interested") as caller-directed endings that end the call at once, and the stop path is the union of that lexicon and Needle GLOBAL. So the attack's premise, that no event covers a goodbye and the pitch keeps going, is false. R145 indirect_decline also exists. The remaining gap is narrower: lexicon variants ("bye now", "gotta go", "I'm done") and the lack of a polite closing line before hang-up.

### Round 165. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The unskippable seven-segment preamble plus "counts only on uninterrupted playback" turns a normal "Hello? Hello?" pickup into a restart loop
- Attack: Callee answers in a car, hears 600 ms of delay, says "Hello? ... Hello?" over "This is SunCo calling about—". That is a barge-in, so the segment is not counted and replays. Callee: "Who IS this?" Replay again. Even without interruptions the monologue covers company, sales purpose, AI designation, recording notice, opt-out, ADMT notice and anti-phishing line, which is 30+ seconds before the caller gets a turn. Talkative callers never let a segment finish. The call either loops or hits a budget counter and wraps up with the disclosures never "delivered". A fix for logged-but-cut disclosures creates a new failure. Most people who answer an unknown number decide within seconds. The design has no rule for a caller who keeps interrupting: no shortened form, no cap on replays, and no rule for which segments must come first. That leaves only two outcomes: annoying loops or calls that end without the required disclosures.
- Defense: The loop case is already handled. R108's yield rule means any near-end burst stops playout and enters PAUSE ("Sorry, go ahead"), so the caller gets the turn at once. R107 handles a callee who keeps saying "hello?": that enters MEDIA_FAULT, which either replays the full PREAMBLE once the path is confirmed or books a lawful callback. R109 starts the first segment within ~1.5 s, so the 600 ms gap is bounded. R69 budget counters cap the call and go to WRAP_UP. Disclosures are never "skipped": the invariant "every path DIAL->QUALIFY/SALES passes DISCLOSED" means a call that runs out of budget never reaches a pitch. That fails safe and lawful, not silent. What's missing is a replay cap and a required segment order.
- Verdict: R108's yield rule hands the turn to the caller at once, and R107 sends a repeated "hello?" to MEDIA_FAULT. R69 per-state visit and turn counters put a hard bound on any replay loop. The R31 invariant (no path to QUALIFY or SALES without DISCLOSED) means the worst case is a short call that ends with no pitch, which is safe, not an undisclosed pitch. The 30-second monologue costs conversion, but it is not a safety or legal failure.

### Round 166. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: "How did you get my number?" / "I never signed up for anything" have no event, no fragment and no consequence, though consent is the call's legal basis
- Attack: Right after the preamble, the caller asks: "Where'd you get my number? I never gave it to anyone." Needle has to choose. request_caller_identity gives the company name again, which doesn't answer the question. out_of_scope gives a deflection, and the next turn moves to IDENTITY. The honest answer means reading out the lead vendor's site, date and form. That is vendor-controlled text, and the fragments can't hold it. The caller's flat denial of consent never reaches the consent record or the dialer, so the next campaign redials them. Under FCC 24-17 and TCPA case law, the whole call stands on that consent. A consumer who disputes it is the start of a TCPA claim. Here the system dodges the question and keeps no record of the denial. "Lead-vendor consent audit" is still an open item, so nothing true can be said in reply.
- Defense: The legal basis is solid. The dialer requires a per-number PEWC artifact ("timestamp, seller, disclosure version", R74) captured by an independent consent-capture provider (R162). There is no vendor free text, so a fixed fragment can state system-sourced fields from that artifact: date and capture channel. A flat denial is exactly the kind of signal R115's revocation intake bus and R162's per-partner fraud metrics are built to consume. What's missing is an event that routes the question and denial to them. Today it falls into out_of_scope, which is safe (no false claim) but loses the record.
- Verdict: The consent infrastructure does exist (R74 PEWC artifact, R162 independent capture, R115 revocation bus). But nothing routes a caller's "where'd you get my number?" or "I never signed up" into any of it. The Designer concedes the turn falls into out_of_scope, the call moves on to IDENTITY, and the denial is never recorded, so the next campaign can redial. The defense rests on two new events.
- Fix: Add a GLOBAL consent_source_question event. Its fixed fragment states the seller, capture date and capture channel from the R74 artifact, and the outbox texts a copy of the consent record. Add a GLOBAL consent_dispute event (lexicon plus Needle): no further pitch, a DNC or suppression write through the R115 bus, legal_hold on the session (R117), an increment to the R162 partner-fraud counter, and a human audit of that lead's consent. Add a red-team stratum of consent-denial phrasings and a Tier A metric for a denial followed by a redial.

### Round 167. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Icebreaker replies carry no valence: small_talk/acknowledge have no sentiment slot, so the canned acknowledgment can cheer bad news that falls short of a sensitive_event
- Attack: Approved icebreaker: "How's your week been going?" Caller: "Honestly awful, the kid's had the flu, the basement flooded, I haven't slept." This is not bereavement or an emergency, so not sensitive_event. Needle emits small_talk. The deterministic picker plays variant 2: "Great to hear! So, I'd love to learn a bit about your home." With a neutral variant instead ("Thanks for sharing that"), it still pivots straight to the pitch. Both readings are robotic, or mocking. Rapport moves only work when they are contingent on what the caller said. Here the icebreaker is the one place the design invites open, free answers, and then answers them with a fixed fragment. The event schema has no sentiment slot, so there is no way to match the answer. The first unscripted exchange shows the caller that nobody is listening, which defeats the reason the icebreaker exists.
- Defense: The attack misreads the schema. R63 defines the event as small_talk(valence), so a valence slot exists. The "Great to hear!" pick can only happen if a fragment ignores that slot. R153 already lints out flattery and rapport fragments, caps small_talk at ≤2, and forbids re-entering icebreakers. R151 requires discovery fragments to be "open or neutral". Real distress ("haven't slept", flood, sick kid) is a red-team target for R19 sensitive_event(distress / financial_hardship), and R150 adds hardship rows. What's missing is a build check that ties acknowledgment wording to valence.
- Verdict: The attack's premise is false: R63 defines small_talk(valence), so the design already carries a sentiment slot. R153's lint bans rapport and flattery fragments and caps small_talk, R151 requires neutral discovery wording, and R19 sensitive_event(distress) plus R150 hardship rows cover serious bad news. "Great to hear!" would have to pass human approval despite those lints. What's missing is only an explicit build check that ties acknowledgment wording to the valence value.

### Round 168. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Unsafe-context replies ("I'm driving, but go ahead") aren't events, so the agent runs READ_BACK and pushes an SMS e-sign with OTP to a driver
- Attack: Caller at pickup: "I'm on the freeway, but go ahead, I've got a few minutes." Needle hears acknowledge or small_talk; there is no unsafe-context event. The call proceeds: QUALIFY, QUOTE, a dense READ_BACK of lien, escalator and financing terms, then the commit "yes". CONTRACT_SENT texts a link and OTP: "You'll get a text now, just tap the link and enter the code." The caller picks up the phone at 65 mph to sign a 25-year lien. Most states ban handheld phone use while driving (GHSA distracted-driving law chart: https://www.ghsa.org/state-laws-issues/distracted-driving). The agent prompts that use, at the exact step where comprehension is the legal point (the read-back and the signature). Suitability and the vulnerability counter only look at the caller as a person, not at their situation, so a distracted driver passes through to the close.
- Defense: Several layers already slow this case. R153 plays "There's no need to decide today". R120 asks before CONTRACT_SENT whether the caller wants electronic or paper/in-person signing. R144 requires an action-production commit. R65 provides HOLD and callback_request, and R41 turns those into concrete lawful times. Still, no state flags the driving context, so this is a real gap and I harden it.
- Verdict: No existing state or event detects that the caller is driving. R65 HOLD and callback only fire if the caller asks for them, and R120 and R153 don't stop the agent from reading a dense READ_BACK and then telling the driver to open an SMS and enter an OTP. The Designer concedes the gap, and the whole defense is new.
- Fix: Add a GLOBAL unsafe_context(driving | operating_machinery | working) event, fired by a lexicon ("driving", "on the freeway", "behind the wheel") or by Needle. It plays a fixed fragment ("Please don't use your phone while driving. When's a better time?") and routes to callback_request or a polite end. Add a model-checked invariant: once unsafe_context is set, READ_BACK, COMMIT and CONTRACT_SENT/OTP are unreachable on that call, and no fragment may tell the caller to act on the handset.

### Round 169. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Bad lines (car Bluetooth, wind, dropouts): "clarify once, then fallback" turns noisy calls into booked callbacks or warm transfers nobody agreed to, and a garbled stop goes nowhere
- Attack: Caller on a windy street: "[wind] ...nt... call... ag[dropout]..." That gets an unintelligible re-ask, then the same noise, then fallback. The fallback is a callback or a human. A callback needs a time slot the caller can't give, so the system books a default or a fuzzy one. The garbled turn was "Don't call again." The post-call DNC sweep runs on the same garbled transcript and finds nothing. Tomorrow the dialer calls back under a "requested callback" label. The fallback is meant as the safe exit, but it takes the worst-understood calls and books more sales contact on them. Noise isn't treated as a reason not to engage; it gets a booked callback. Under TSR 310.4(b)(1)(iii)(A), any opt-out the noise hid is still binding, and both the live call and the post-call check miss it.
- Defense: The fallback does not book unknown slots. R41 says the agent "books only the confirmed one", so a caller who can't state a time gets no booking. R111/R121 route repeated unintelligible turns with clear speech energy to an ACCESS_PATH or a human, and "never suppresses redial" covers only disability access. R108 already handles a garbled stop: overlap residue with stop-adjacent tokens ("calling", "off") triggers the R24 check at once, and the post-call sweep marks such calls suppress-pending until a human reviews them. R113 suppresses redial on any unresolved unclear in the closing turns. What's missing is extending that review to every unintelligible turn, not only overlapped ones.
- Verdict: The booking half is refuted, since R41 books only a confirmed time. The hidden opt-out half stands. R108's stop-adjacent residue check covers only overlap during agent playback. R113's "unresolved unclear in closing turns suppresses redial" applies only to the NO_RETAIN stratum, not to every call as the Designer implies. R111/R121's ACCESS_PATH explicitly "never suppresses redial". So a garbled "don't call again" on a windy line can end in fallback with redial allowed, and a lexicon sweep over the garbled ASR finds nothing.
- Fix: Any call that ends on unintelligible fallback books nothing and ends with "I'm having trouble hearing you; I'll let you go." The number stays under the R138 pending_sweep lock until a human has listened to every low-confidence or unintelligible span for an opt-out. A stop-adjacent token in any such span means DNC. The ACCESS_PATH no-suppression exception applies only after that human review clears the call.

### Round 170. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Call outcomes never write back to the pre-dial gate: WRONG_PARTY, NOT_SUITABLE, tenants and preamble hang-ups get redialed
- Attack: Call 1 reaches a tenant who says "I don't own this place." The outcome is NOT_SUITABLE, counted as success. Call 2 is a number the lead vendor mistyped; the callee says "wrong person." The outcome is WRONG_PARTY. Call 3 hangs up four seconds into the preamble. The pre-dial gate checks consent, DNC, the Reassigned Numbers Database, hours and caps, emergencies, deceased and open tickets. None of these outcomes are on that list. Next week's campaign redials all three, and calls each one up to the frequency cap. Consent belongs to the current subscriber. Once a caller learns a number is wrong, more calls on the old consent are TCPA violations (FCC 2015 Order; RND safe harbor covers only database-reliant first calls: https://www.fcc.gov/reassigned-numbers-database). Repeated calls after immediate hang-ups risk TSR 310.4(b)(1)(i) harassment. The only post-call write-back is the stop-phrase sweep.
- Defense: The main cases already write back. R73's WRONG_PARTY state is defined as "apologize, DNC number, flag lead", so a mistyped number is not redialed. R15 NO_SALE_SAFE_EXIT means "suppress redial". R145 suppresses leads on indirect declines. R109/R142 count abandonment and attempts. R9 and R71 handle non-owners and NOT_SUITABLE as honest exits. The real gap: there is no general invariant that every terminal outcome writes to the dial gate, so NOT_SUITABLE and early hang-ups depend on campaign hygiene.
- Verdict: The legally decisive case is handled. R73's WRONG_PARTY state really does "DNC number, flag lead", and R73's identity challenge comes before QUALIFY, so a caller who isn't the consenting lead reaches WRONG_PARTY, not NOT_SUITABLE. R15 and R145 suppress redial on vulnerability exits and indirect declines, and R41/R142 frequency caps bound early hang-up redials. What remains (redialing a NOT_SUITABLE owner-tenant or a preamble hang-up within the caps) is campaign-hygiene waste, not the TCPA wrong-number exposure the attack claims.

### Round 171. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The IDENTITY challenge-response is a phishing script: an unsolicited caller asks you to confirm personal details, the exact pattern the preamble's anti-phishing line and FTC advice tell people to refuse
- Attack: The preamble ends: "...we'll never ask for sensitive information." The next state asks: "To make sure I'm speaking with the right person, can you confirm your street address and last name?" A careful caller says "I don't give that out to people who call me." There is no event for a principled refusal. That gives clarify, then a re-ask, then fallback or end. A trusting caller confirms, which builds exactly the habit scammers (and the cloned-audio fraudsters already flagged) rely on. FTC guidance is not to give personal information to unexpected callers (https://consumer.ftc.gov/articles/phone-scams). The design's opening asks for it seconds after promising not to. The outcome is backwards: careful people get filtered out of the funnel, and the people most open to phishing go through to the close.
- Defense: The challenge does not phish. R73 has the caller state their name and address, and the agent checks them against records it already holds. R89 bans any request for SSN, DOB, bank, card or credit data. R89 also gives verify_agent, which sends an outbox text "from registered short code to verified number" so the caller can check us out-of-band. R146 accepts an address plus a surname token, so the ask is small. The real gap is that a principled refusal has no event, and the anti-phishing wording is vague enough to look contradicted.
- Verdict: R73's challenge-response asks only for name and address, which the system already holds. R89 lints out any request for SSN, DOB, bank, card or credit data, and R89's verify_agent short-code text gives the caller an out-of-band way to check the caller. FTC guidance targets sensitive data, not a name and address. A careful caller who refuses gets clarify, then fallback or an end with no pitch, which costs the funnel but harms no one.

### Round 172. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The success-path goodbye after a verbal yes ("You're all set, welcome aboard") misstates the deal's status before any signature, chilling cancellation and making people ignore the e-sign text
- Attack: The COMMIT gate passes. The agent plays the closing fragment: "Congratulations, you're all set! Welcome to the SunCo family." No contract exists yet. Customer A thinks they're done and ignores the SMS link; follow-ups then look like "I already bought it, why are you texting?" Customer B later wants out and thinks "I agreed on a recorded line" binds them, so they never use the cooling-off window that starts at signing. Lints ban numbers, "pre-qualified" and banned claims, but not status claims. Telling someone the sale is done before a contract exists misrepresents a material term, and it chills a cancellation right (TSR 310.3(a)(2)(iv); Florida 501.615 written-contract rule). One canned fragment, played at the moment a call ends in a sale, misstates where the deal stands on every sale.
- Defense: Status is already deterministic. Nothing is "sold" until the vendor webhook moves the call to SALE_SIGNED (R17, R60). The CRM records no sale before signature. The R141 mailed notice gives cancel instructions, R76 runs a CANCEL path with no persuasion, and R153 plays "no need to decide today". Every closing fragment is a human-approved, lint-checked fragment, so "welcome aboard" exists only if a reviewer passed it. I accept that the lint has no status rule, and I add one.
- Verdict: Every template needs compliance sign-off and must pass R18's TSR-mapped banned-claims lint and claims audit. Sale status is set only by the webhook (R17/R60). The e-sign packet carries the cancellation notice (R17), and R141 mails cancel instructions to every record owner, so a misleading goodbye cannot remove or hide the cancellation right. The attack depends on a reviewer approving a misleading fragment, not on a structural gap. Still, a state-keyed status lint is a cheap, sensible addition.

### Round 173. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Hang-up race at the end: the caller's last words during or right after the closing fragment land after the terminal state and are lost
- Attack: The agent plays its goodbye; WRAP_UP schedules BYE when the audio ends. Two seconds before that, the caller says "wait, actually, cancel it, I changed my mind", or "what was the monthly again?" The turn is still being transcribed when the terminal state fires. Barge-in only throws away speculation; a terminal state has no handlers. The ASR text arrives after teardown with no state to receive it. "Cancel" is not a stop phrase, so the DNC-only post-call sweep ignores it, and the contract goes out. GLOBAL cancel_or_rescind is meant to work from any state, but the last seconds of a call fall in a gap where no state is listening. The close is the part of the call where last-second doubts and questions cluster. The design treats the goodbye as a closed one-way segment, not a turn the caller can still use. The post-call sweep looks only for DNC, not cancel or rescind.
- Defense: The goodbye is not one-way. R108's yield rule stops any playout, the closing included, on near-end energy and enters PAUSE, so "wait, actually" takes the turn. R66's post-call sweep is "lexicon + GLOBAL re-run", and GLOBAL includes cancel_or_rescind, not only DNC. R113 makes the teardown sweep mandatory before purge and fails closed. Even a missed cancel happens before signature: CONTRACT_SENT is only an envelope, and R17 plus the cancellation window protect the caller. The real gap is that the sweep's non-DNC hits have no routed action.
- Verdict: In the scenario the caller speaks two seconds before BYE, which means during the goodbye audio. R108's yield rule stops any playout on near-end energy and enters PAUSE, so the turn is taken and GLOBAL cancel_or_rescind can fire. R66's sweep re-runs GLOBAL (not only DNC), and R113 makes it mandatory and fail-closed. A missed cancel at that point leaves only an unsigned envelope (R17), backed by the cancellation window and R141's mailed notice. The real but small gap is speech after the audio ends and the routing of non-DNC sweep hits.


### Round 174. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Active-duty military buyers: Military Lending Act terms and PCS-order move horizons the uniform "same menu for all" financing cannot handle
- Attack: A Navy petty officer in Norfolk answers. QUALIFY asks move horizon; caller says "we're here till my orders come, probably two years, maybe sooner." Needle labels move_horizon with grounded "two years", which passes the table. QUOTE reads the standard loan menu (dealer fee inside the price, arbitration clause, standard APR) in READ_BACK. Caller says "yes." Contract goes out by SMS. Nothing in the graph asks about military status, and nothing changes the terms read back. MLA (32 CFR 232.6, 232.8) requires covered-borrower checks, an oral MAPR and payment disclosure, a 36% all-in MAPR cap, and no mandatory arbitration. A loan breaking these rules is void from the start. The design's fair-lending rule ("same menu for all") hard-codes the terms a covered borrower cannot get. And the vague "till my orders come" answer passes a numeric move-horizon guard. Sources: ecfr.gov 32 CFR 232.6; FDIC Compliance Manual V-13.1.
- Defense: The agent never sets credit terms. "Financing (lender runs application and decisions)" means the creditor makes the covered-borrower determination at prequal, which is where the MLA puts it. Terms come back as "lender_verified" status, and "conditional approval at new amount invalidates quote hash" (R60). The read-back is bound to that hash (R23/R49), so the agent cannot read a non-MLA menu once the lender flags the borrower. "Same menu for all" covers offers only. R46 keys the product policy on non-protected facts, and a legally required term is one of those. The hedged horizon is weak: "two years, maybe sooner" is conditional speech. Under R81 that wording stays out of commits, and R43 range checks send it to clarify.
- Verdict: In this design the creditor is the lender ("lender runs application and decisions"). The MLA's covered-borrower check, MAPR cap, oral disclosure and arbitration ban are the creditor's duties, and the agent only reads back lender_verified terms bound to a quote hash that a new lender status invalidates (R23, R60). "Same menu for all" (R46) covers offers keyed on non-protected facts, and it does not override lender terms. The hedged move horizon is a minor slot-grounding issue, not an MLA failure.

### Round 175. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Homeowner in an active Chapter 13 bankruptcy, legally barred from taking on new debt or liens without trustee or court approval
- Attack: The caller says calmly, "We're in a Chapter 13, payments are fine, so money's not an issue." Needle has no bankruptcy event. It picks acknowledge or small_talk (the closest allowed labels), and the payments-are-fine framing never triggers the vulnerability counter. Ownership, roof and shade all pass. The lender's soft pull is ordered. The caller affirms the read-back. A UCC fixture filing is recorded against a house that belongs to the bankruptcy estate. Under 11 U.S.C. 1306, the house and the debtor's post-petition income belong to the bankruptcy estate. New credit needs trustee or court approval (11 U.S.C. 1305(c), 364, and local rules). Unapproved debt can cause plan default or dismissal. The suitability table checks physical and financial facts, not legal capacity. The closed event schema has no label for a court-supervised debtor, so the fact is lost.
- Defense: The bankruptcy does not stay hidden. Financed deals need a lender pull ("lender runs application and decisions"), and an open Chapter 13 shows on any credit file. The lender then returns declined or conditional, a "versioned status enum" event that the agent cannot override (R60). R88 already sends "capacity flags (conservatorship/trust/POA) from records and lender checks -> human review path", and no lien filing proceeds until R120 verification is done. The R147 hardship lexicon covers "foreclosure, collections". Saying "payments are fine" does not stop a deterministic lexicon hit on "Chapter 13" if that row exists. The real gap is narrower: cash deals, and the fact that the spoken words are lost.
- Verdict: The harmful outcome named, new debt plus a UCC filing, needs the lender's application. An open Chapter 13 shows on any credit pull, and the agent cannot override a lender decline or condition (R60). A cash deal takes on no new debt and files no lien. The fact being lost in speech is a real but secondary gap: the hardship lexicon (R150) lists foreclosure and collections, not bankruptcy.

### Round 176. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Probate and estate-owned homes: an heir passes the identity challenge and commits a house titled to a dead person
- Attack: The lead record names Dorothy. Dorothy died eight months ago, and the deceased list has not caught up. Her son, who lives in the house, answers her landline. The challenge-response (address, name on the account, year bought) is family knowledge, so he passes as VERIFIED. "Do you own the home?" "Yeah, it's ours." Grief is long past, so no sensitive_event fires. The e-sign goes to "all record owners," which is the contact on file: the son's mobile. Title sits with the estate. A personal representative can encumber estate property only with court authority or the statutory notice procedure (e.g., California Probate Code 10514 notice of proposed action under independent administration). An heir has no authority at all before distribution. Knowledge-based identity checks fail by design against family members. Ownership is a spoken yes/no slot, not a check of title or probate status.
- Defense: The son cannot complete the contract. The signer list is "all record owners" from "record-owner lookup on validated address" (R88), and that is Dorothy, not whoever answered the phone. R159 requires "lender-grade identity verification (document plus selfie or KBA) for each record owner at e-sign", and R120 requires "per-signer vendor ID verification" with no shared phones. Family knowledge can pass the call's challenge, but it cannot produce Dorothy's ID with a matching selfie. R138 mails the contract to every record owner, and install is blocked until then. The deceased list is a pre-dial gate. I accept that R143's "address plus any surname-token match counts as VERIFIED" lets a son pass on the call. That is a gap, and the commit gates catch it.
- Verdict: Passing the call's challenge does not get the son a contract. The signer list comes from record-owner lookup on the validated address (R88), so it names Dorothy. Each signer needs independently sourced contact data and vendor ID verification for any lien envelope (R123), plus document-and-selfie or KBA identity proofing (R162). So the son would have to impersonate his dead mother at e-sign, which the existing gates are built to stop. The surname-only VERIFIED match (R146) is a real weakness, but the commit gates downstream catch it.

### Round 177. Winner: Designer
- Topic: HOA architectural approval and state-specific solar-access law: approval arrives after the cancellation window and moves or shrinks the system
- Attack: A Texas homeowner in a deed-restricted subdivision affirms a 9 kW south-and-street-facing design. QUALIFY never asks about an HOA. Install waits out the 3-day window, then the HOA application goes in. Under its rules the HOA moves the panels to the rear, west-facing roof. If the customer instead asks "Can my HOA stop this?", the policy table holds one answer, worded as true for every state. Tex. Prop. Code 202.010 lets an HOA require approval and dictate panel location, as long as estimated production drops no more than 10%. California Civil Code 714 gives HOAs a 45-day review. Either way, the size, production and savings read back can legally change after the commit, once cancellation has expired. HOA law differs by state, and no suitability row or read-back item covers it.
- Defense: Install does not happen on the read-back's terms if the design changes. R156 schedules the survey "inside the cancellation window" and makes the contract "contingent on a survey-confirmed price". It also says "every change order becomes a new quote hash with written re-disclosure, a fresh e-sign by all signers, a no-penalty exit and a restart of the cancellation window". An HOA-forced relocation that changes size or production is that kind of change. R157's WAITING_FOR_BOM_MATCH blocks install on any config mismatch. On the legal question, policy rows are jurisdiction-keyed ("jurisdiction profiles", "most restrictive wins"). A row worded as if it held in every state violates R91's rule that each fact maps to one table key.
- Verdict: R159 already makes every change order a new quote hash, with written re-disclosure, a fresh e-sign by all signers, a no-penalty exit and a restart of the cancellation window. R160 blocks install on any BOM mismatch, so an HOA-forced cut in size is caught. What gets through is a pure re-orientation with the same BOM. The cited statutes cap its production loss (about 10%), and savings are already "estimate, not guaranteed" figures (R157). There is no HOA concept anywhere in the design, but the existing change-order machinery covers the material harm.

### Round 178. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Contractor licence status changing daily at state licensing boards (suspensions, bond or workers' comp lapses) outside the certified policy table
- Attack: The policy table names partner installer "CSLB #XXXXXX, licensed, bonded and insured," and the PREAMBLE/READ_BACK fragments cite it. On Tuesday the CSLB automatically suspends the licence for a workers' compensation lapse. The certificate expires only when a cited policy row changes, and nobody changed the row. For two weeks the agent reads "licensed" to every Californian, closes 300 deals, and the installer performs them. Under B&P 7031, unlicensed work can't be compensated, and the customer can recover everything paid. Suspension for a workers' comp lapse is automatic (B&P 7125.2). B&P 7159 requires accurate licence details in the contract. Arizona ROC, Florida DBPR and others act on their own schedules. Licence status is an external, live legal fact. The pre-dial gate checks the consumer side only, never the seller's legal right to perform.
- Defense: The design already treats seller-side legal status as a live, expiring guard. R119 does this for telemarketer registration ("no DIAL and no SMS effect without valid registration", with an expiry alert). R158's "counterparty-health monitor (bankruptcy filings, covenant breach, lender watch-lists, missed-milestone rates)" watches the installer. R137 makes the policy table forward-only, with tombstones no bundle can cite, and "the runtime refuses any row outside its effective_from/until window". So a licence row with a short validity window simply stops being citable, and QUOTE/READ_BACK fall to lead-gen or callback. I accept that today the licence row has no live feed behind it. That part is NEW.
- Verdict: Contractor licence, bond and workers'-comp status are live legal facts set by licensing boards, and nothing in the design feeds them. Certificates expire only when someone edits a row, and the counterparty-health monitor (R161) watches bankruptcy, covenants and milestones, not licence status. R122 covers telemarketer registration, not the installer's licence. The Defender concedes the licence row has no live feed, so the agent can keep saying "licensed" and closing deals for an installer whose licence is suspended.
- Fix: Add installer_license rows per jurisdiction (CSLB, AZ ROC, FL DBPR and others) fed by an automated daily pull of board status plus bond and workers'-comp feeds. Each row's effective_until is at most 24 hours after its last successful check, and a stale feed fails closed. Model-checked invariant: no QUOTE, READ_BACK, CONTRACT_SENT or install effect unless the installer's licence row is valid for the property's jurisdiction. Re-check at install. A lapse after signing holds install, sends written notice and offers a no-penalty cancel.

### Round 179. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Post-hurricane ROOF_FIRST outcomes colliding with Florida's ban on contractors soliciting insurance-claim roof work
- Attack: The county emergency suppression lifts after a hurricane. A Tampa homeowner: "Roof's damaged, but insurance should cover a new one. Can you guys handle that with the claim?" Suitability gives ROOF_FIRST. The approved fragment offers the partner roofer and a consult. The roofer-referral consult, "we'll come assess the storm damage," and any certified answer about coverage or deductibles now count as solicitation tied to a property-insurance claim. Fla. Stat. 489.147 (SB 76, 2021) bars contractors and their agents from soliciting homeowners to make roof claims, from offering to interpret policies or help with claims, and from waiving deductibles or offering rebates, with fines per violation. Texas Insurance Code 707 bars deductible waivers. The design treats ROOF_FIRST as a safe honest exit. In storm states it is a regulated insurance solicitation path that no profile row governs. Source: flsenate.gov Statutes 489.147.
- Defense: The agent cannot improvise any of the 489.147 violations. "Caller free text never spoken". Every utterance is "a human-approved fragment", and the banned-claims lint plus "lint bans fake urgency and unverifiable external-fact fragments (e.g. weather)" (R78) mean no fragment can mention storms, coverage or deductibles. A coverage question has no tool in QUOTE/QUALIFY, so it becomes out_of_scope -> clarify, then fallback.
