R78 also suppresses emergency counties for "declaration + cool-down" and audits emergency-area closes as Tier A. ROOF_FIRST says "inspection before any quote". It is a suitability exit, not a claims offer. Still, a roofer referral in a storm state needs a profile row.
- Verdict: 489.147 bars encouraging roof insurance claims, interpreting policies and offering deductible waivers or rebates. The agent can do none of these, because every utterance is a certified fragment, no fact row on coverage or deductibles exists (R91), and R78's lint bans weather-based and fake-urgency pretexts. A coverage question has no in-state tool, so it goes to out_of_scope, then clarify, then fallback. ROOF_FIRST is an inspection-before-quote suitability exit, not a claims offer, and emergency counties are suppressed through a cool-down (R78).

### Round 180. Winner: Designer
- Topic: PACE (property-tax-assessment) financing: a tax lien, not a loan, with its own solicitation bans, recorded confirmation call and ability-to-pay rules
- Attack: The caller says, "My neighbor paid through his property taxes, no credit check. Can I do that? And it's tax-deductible, right?" The lender menu includes a PACE administrator. Needle labels this financing_question. The certified fragments for "pay through taxes" come from the generic loan branch. Any yes/no fragment on deductibility, or "it's a government program," is spoken. The read-back gives a monthly payment, not the annual assessment added to the tax bill ahead of the mortgage. California PACE law (SB 242, AB 1284; Streets & Highways Code 5913 onward, DFPI oversight) bars solicitors from claiming tax deductibility or government sponsorship. It also requires a recorded oral confirmation of key terms and an ability-to-pay determination. The CFPB's 2024 PACE rule brings PACE under TILA ability-to-repay. The Reg Z trigger-term lint assumes a lender loan, not a senior tax lien.
- Defense: R147 already decides this: "PACE products are excluded or follow ability-to-repay per the regulation profile." The default is exclusion. A PACE administrator can be offered only with its own profile, so it cannot ride on generic loan fragments. "Pay through my taxes" names a product the menu doesn't hold, so it maps to out_of_scope(product_request), "honest no-offer fragment and referral" (R82). "It's a government program" is barred twice: by the banned-claims lint ("government affiliation", R18) and by "lint bans utility/government pretexts" (R31). A deductibility answer would need a policy-table row with substantiation (R91), and none is authored. So the agent cannot say yes or no.
- Verdict: The design already says "PACE products are excluded or follow ability-to-repay per the regulation profile" (R150), so PACE cannot ride on generic loan fragments by default. "Pay through my taxes" names a product that is not on the menu and maps to out_of_scope(product_request) with an honest no-offer (R82). "Government program" is banned twice (R18, R31). A deductibility answer would need a substantiated table row (R91), and none exists.

### Round 181. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Homes on tribal trust land: leasehold title, BIA approval for encumbrances, and tribal (not state) consumer, contractor and utility law
- Attack: A Navajo Nation homeowner near Shiprock answers. The address resolves to New Mexico, and the most-restrictive state profile loads. "Do you own your home?" "Yes, my family's homesite." The read-back covers a lien/UCC filing with New Mexico. The utility is NTUA, not a state-regulated IOU, so the savings row is wrong. The installer holds a state licence. The e-sign goes out and SALE_PENDING follows. Homesites on trust land are usually leaseholds. Encumbering them needs BIA approval under 25 CFR Part 162, so a state UCC fixture filing may be unenforceable. Tribal sovereignty means tribal law and courts govern consumer, contractor and lien rules, which no state profile captures. The jurisdiction model treats geography as states and counties, with no tribal row at all.
- Defense: Each of these failures is stopped by an existing fail-closed input. R88's record-owner lookup on a trust homesite returns no fee owner, or the United States in trust, and that is a capacity case routed to human review, not a signer list. R154 says "the calculator must use the customer's exact tariff schedule ... else no figure". NTUA has no tariff row, so no savings are spoken. R153 makes verified usage come only from a utility API or bill extractor. The lender makes the lending decision and will not secure on a leasehold without BIA approval. R120 says "no lien filing or install proceeds until every owner has been independently verified". The missing tribal jurisdiction layer is a real gap.
- Verdict: Some harms fail closed: there is no NTUA tariff row, so no savings figure (R157), and a lender won't secure a trust leasehold. But the jurisdiction model has no tribal layer at all. The call runs under the New Mexico profile, with state contractor, consumer and lien rules that may not govern on trust land, and nothing makes record-owner lookup fail closed when it finds no fee owner. A cash deal under the wrong legal regime remains reachable, and the Defender concedes the missing layer.
- Fix: Add a tribal jurisdiction layer (BIA land-area and Census AIANNH boundaries checked on the validated address). An address on trust or reservation land loads a tribal profile, and with no certified profile the call is lead-gen only, goes to a human consultant, and offers no lien or UCC product. Record-owner lookup that returns no fee owner, or a trust owner, fails closed to human review. Utilities missing from the tariff table force no figure.

### Round 182. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Texas homestead constitutional lien rules: SMS e-sign cannot meet the required signing location, waiting period, spousal consent and rescission steps
- Attack: A Houston couple's homestead. One spouse affirms the read-back for a financed rooftop system with a lien. CONTRACT_SENT sends SMS OTP envelopes, and both sign on their phones the same evening. Install waits out the federal 3-day window. The lien is filed. Nothing in the graph knows that Texas treats a lien on a homestead improvement as a constitutional matter. Texas Constitution art. XVI §50(a)(5) and Property Code 53.254 to 53.255 require, for a lien on existing homestead improvements: a written contract signed before work starts; consent of both spouses given "in the manner of a conveyance"; a 5-day wait after application; signing at a lender's, attorney's or title company's office; and a 3-day rescission. A same-night phone e-sign voids the lien. Source: statutes.capitol.texas.gov CN.16.50; 7 TAC 152.15.
- Defense: The design already holds lien envelopes to more than an SMS OTP. R120: "Any envelope that creates a lien or UCC filing requires per-signer vendor ID verification, with a notary or in-person consultant as the fallback". R117 offers paper or in-person signing. Jurisdiction profiles already move whole states to lead-gen with asynchronous contracting (R47), so a Texas homestead row fits the existing machinery. I accept that the profile lacks the art. XVI §50(a)(5) row: the 5-day wait, the closing venue, and the consent of a spouse who is not on title.
- Verdict: A lien on a Texas homestead for work on existing improvements needs both spouses' consent, a 5-day wait after the credit application, signing at a lender's, attorney's or title company's office, and a 3-day rescission right (Tex. Const. art. XVI §50(a)(5)). The design's lien-envelope gates (R123: per-signer ID verification, with a notary or in-person consultant fallback; R120: paper option) meet none of the venue or waiting requirements. The design reads back lien/UCC terms and files them, so it actively produces a defective homestead lien. The Defender concedes the missing row.
- Fix: Add a TX homestead profile row keyed on the county homestead exemption. Any lien or UCC product on such a home makes the call lead-gen only. Closing happens only at a title company, lender or attorney office, signalled by a closing_venue event. A 5-day timer runs from the application timestamp, both spouses sign (including a spouse not on title), and there is a 3-day rescission. Model-checked invariant: no lien-filing effect without the closing_venue event. Cash and no-lien products may still e-sign.

### Round 183. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Travelling or roaming callers: calling-hours rules apply at the called party's physical location, which neither the address nor the area code gives
- Attack: A Phoenix snowbird with a 602 number is in Lisbon. The pre-dial gate checks Arizona and the area code: 2 p.m., cleared. In Lisbon it is 9 p.m. or later; on a second campaign wave it is 2 a.m. The groggy caller says "I'm in Portugal, it's the middle of the night." Needle labels small_talk. Later the e-sign arrives while the caller is abroad, the cancellation window runs out unnoticed, and the empty house gets an install. 47 CFR 64.1200(c)(1) and TSR 310.4(c) measure calling hours by local time at the called party's location, not their billing address. A roaming mobile's location is unknowable before dialing, and when the caller says it out loud no event changes anything. The "most restrictive candidate jurisdiction" set only covers address and number geography, so the per-call compliance invariant fails for travellers, military deployments and snowbirds.
- Defense: Before dialing, the gate uses the best available knowledge: "calling hours ... for the most restrictive candidate jurisdiction". It pins tzdata and adds guard bands (R136). Once the caller states a location, R70 says jurisdiction_changed "fired on any stated residence change, validated or not; unvalidated -> treat as added candidate" and "re-evaluates hour ... guards -> WRAP_UP + lawful callback". The install fear is answered by R156 (survey inside the cancellation window, needing the owner), R138 (mailed notice, install blocked), and R117 (install waits for verified delivery). The gap: "I'm in Portugal" is a current location, not a residence, and it can be labeled small_talk.
- Verdict: A roaming phone's location cannot be known before dialing, and the address/area-code candidate set is a reasonable best effort. But once the caller says "I'm in Portugal, it's the middle of the night", nothing acts on it. jurisdiction_changed (R70) fires only on a stated residence change, the Defender concedes the line can be labeled small_talk, and nothing stops a later campaign wave from dialing the same number at 2 a.m.
- Fix: Add a GLOBAL current_location event (lexicon plus Needle, with a certified small_talk confusion pair) for "I'm in/abroad/travelling", "middle of the night" and "you woke me". It adds the stated place's time zone to the candidate set. Outside the window, the agent apologizes and ends with a callback in the caller's stated local time. It also writes a number-level away_until hold into the dial permit so later waves skip the number. A stated absence from home means no same-call close.


### Round 184. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Batch-variant inference on the pinned Needle fleet makes certified near-tie decisions depend on how many other calls are in flight
- Attack: Caller says "yeah, I guess that's fine" at COMMIT. Replay at certification ran Needle alone (batch size 1): margin 0.031, just above the near-tie threshold, so affirm. In production at 6 pm the server batches this turn with 40 other calls; padding and different kernel tiling change reduction order, logits shift in the third decimal, the margin falls to 0.029, so clarify (or flips the other way and commits). Same weights hash, same build, same hardware class, so the certificate still claims it applies. The certificate keys on weights, build, hardware and tool list, not batch composition. Thinking Machines (Sept 2025) showed common inference kernels are not batch-invariant: identical inputs give different outputs depending on concurrent load. "Bit-identical replay" and every threshold-sized margin are then unreproducible. Source: thinkingmachines.ai "Defeating Nondeterminism in LLM Inference"; simonwillison.net/2025/Sep/11/defeating-nondeterminism.
- Defense: The scenario's commit never rests on Needle's margin. R87 makes the commit gate "lexical over final ASR tokens (independent of Needle output)", and R141 replaces yes/no with an action-production prompt ("please say 'send the contract'"). "Yeah, I guess that's fine" is a hedged, non-lexicon answer, so R77 sends it to a closed re-ask whatever the logits are. For non-commit near-ties, both sides of the threshold are safe: clarify is a re-ask. Still, batch variance does undermine "bit-identical replay" (R53), so we close it.
- Verdict: The commit example is blocked by existing parts: R87 makes the commit gate lexical, R144 asks for the action phrase "send the contract", and R77 sends hedged answers to a re-ask. But the attack is about the certificate and replay claims. R53 keys the certificate on weights, kernel build and hardware, and promises cross-node bit-identical replay. R135 re-keys runtimes on bitwise-identical margin decisions. Nothing in the design pins batch composition or uses batch-invariant kernels, so those reproducibility claims, the daily canary and the equivalence re-keying are not guaranteed under production co-batching. The Designer concedes this, and the remedy is entirely new.
- Fix: Run certified inference with batch-invariant kernels (fixed reduction order and split sizes), or at batch size 1 per pass. Put the batching policy in the certificate key. Add a release CI test that replays every gold set under random co-batched load at sizes 1..N and requires bitwise-identical tokens and margin decisions.

### Round 185. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The argument grounding gate is a presence check, so partial spans and truncated values pass ("150" is found inside "1500")
- Attack: Caller: "our bill runs about fifteen hundred in summer, one-fifty in winter." ITN gives "1500" and "150". Needle emits monthly_bill=150 for the summer question. Grounded. Another caller: "the roof is fifteen years old"; Needle emits roof_age=5 after a max-token cut or a dropped digit; "5" occurs inside "15", so grounded. A street "1234 Oakmont Ridge" truncated by the argument token limit to "1234 Oak" is also a substring. Every one passes the gate and lands in the slot store as spoken. "Found in the final ASR text" checks that a string exists, not that it is the whole span the caller meant. Prefixes, digit fragments and truncations of a real value are always "found". The gate catches invented values but blesses exactly the corrupted-real-value errors a 26M copier makes most.
- Defense: R61 does not use a substring search. It requires the value to be "found by deterministic extractor in the turn's final ASR span", and the R43 number normalizer yields whole numeric entities with unit and period tags, not character runs. Spoken bills "only fill coarse routing bands" (R72). Savings and sizing use only verified_usage (R153), never speech. Roof age is a qualification-critical slot, so it is read back and confirmed (R2). Street addresses are postal-validated, with the house number confirmed digit by digit (R22), and identity runs by challenge-response against the record (R73). So "1234 Oak" or a dropped digit is caught before anything depends on it.
- Verdict: R61 does not describe a substring search. It requires the value to be "found by deterministic extractor in the turn's final ASR span", and R43's normalizer produces whole numeric entities with unit and period tags. A fragment such as "5" from "15", or "150" applied to the summer period, therefore does not match. Existing layers also limit the damage: spoken bills only set coarse bands (R72), sizing and savings use only verified usage (R61/R72), roof age goes through critical-slot read-back (R2), and addresses are postal-validated with the house number confirmed digit by digit (R22).

### Round 186. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Grounding accepts the right string bound to the wrong role or under negation (argument swap and scope errors)
- Attack: QUALIFY asks roof age and move horizon. Caller: "Roof's not twenty, it's maybe eight, and we're here another twenty easy." Needle emits roof_age=20, move_horizon_years=8. Both numbers appear in the transcript, both pass grounding, suitability table says roof under 25 and horizon over 7: SUITABLE. The real answer (horizon 20, roof 8) also passes, but a negated "not twenty" and a swap between two numeric slots are invisible to a gate that only checks membership. Multi-number turns are common in discovery, and small seq2seq models are weakest at role binding and negation scope. Provenance says "spoken", so downstream guards treat a swapped value as caller fact. Suitability, sizing and the read-back then rest on a mis-bound number that no deterministic check can detect.
- Defense: The swap is exactly what R2's "deterministic critical-slot read-back" exists for. Roof age and move horizon are qualification slots (R71), so each is read back ("roof about twenty years, staying about eight more?") and confirmed before suitability uses it. Two independent errors are needed for harm. R44 adds a "summary read-back after multi-fact turns", and R43 cross-slot consistency checks route conflicts to clarify. A caller who just said "not twenty" will correct "twenty" on the read-back. The correction then goes through correct_fact (R52) and triggers a backtrack.
- Verdict: R2's critical-slot read-back names roof age, and R71 makes move horizon a qualification slot. A swapped pair is read back ("roof about twenty, staying about eight") before suitability uses it. R44 adds a summary read-back after multi-fact turns, and R52's correct_fact sends a correction back through the flow. Harm needs both a binding error and a caller who confirms the wrong read-back, which is the two-error structure R2 was built for.

### Round 187. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Constrained decoding masks are written in characters but Needle emits subword tokens, so some valid argument values are unreachable or forced into tokenizations Needle never saw
- Attack: The LOCAL grammar for monthly_bill allows digits only, emitted digit by digit. Needle's tokenizer normally writes "250" as one token, which the grammar masks to fit its per-character automaton, forcing "2","5","0", a non-canonical sequence absent from training. Probability mass collapses onto whichever single digit was most likely after "2"; the emitted value becomes "25" or "200". Similar boundary clashes hit enum names sharing prefixes ("battery" vs "battery_backup"). Token/grammar misalignment is a known constrained-decoding failure: masking at token boundaries forces non-canonical tokenizations and distorts outputs (Beurer-Kellner et al., "Guiding LLMs the Right Way", arXiv 2403.06988; "Lost in Space", arXiv 2502.14969). Certification on unconstrained eval data never sees this distortion.
- Defense: The premise that "certification on unconstrained eval data" is false. Under R2 and R121, certification counts only real calls in which Needle drove the transitions, so it measures the deployed constrained decoder, distortion included. R122 already made "each event name a single reserved vocabulary token", so names can't share prefixes. Numbers never become spoken facts: R43's normalizer and R61's grounding check every value. A forced "25" for "250" is not the extracted entity, so the turn goes to clarify. Bills only set coarse bands (R72).
- Verdict: Certification counts only real calls (R2), so it measures the constrained decoder as deployed, distortions included. The premise that it is certified on unconstrained data is wrong. R125 already makes each event name a single reserved token. A forced "25" for "250" is not an extracted entity, so R61 sends the turn to clarify, and bills only set bands (R72). The remaining effect is a higher clarify rate, which costs availability but causes no harm.

### Round 188. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Encoder context overflow: state tool schemas plus agent context plus a long caller turn exceed Needle's input window, and truncation silently cuts the caller's last words or the tail of the tool list
- Attack: At READ_BACK the LOCAL tool list (affirm, deny, clarify-per-term, dispute_prior_statement, out_of_scope, incomplete...) with JSON schemas takes most of the encoder window. Caller gives a 70-word answer ending "...so yes on the size, but not the loan, I'm paying cash." Tokenizer left-to-right truncation drops "but not the loan, I'm paying cash." Needle sees "...so yes on the size" and emits affirm with high confidence. No error is raised; the input simply fits after truncation. A 26M on-device model has a short fixed context; state-dependent tool lists vary input length by state. Nothing in the design measures or blocks truncation, and per-state certification on typical-length turns never exercises the overflow. The lost words are the ones that change meaning.
- Defense: A 70-word answer never reaches a commit. R81: "affirm must be a short direct answer turn (length/structure check in deterministic commit gate)". R86/R87: "conditional affirms ('yes, but ...') excluded from commit" by a lexical gate over the full final ASR tokens, which never see Needle's truncated input. Long turns also go through R44's "deterministic segmenter -> per-segment Needle -> merger", so "but not the loan, I'm paying cash" is its own segment. Long turns are a separate Tier A stratum, so overflow-length inputs are certified.
- Verdict: The example commit fails existing gates that never see Needle's input. R81 requires a short direct answer turn, R86 excludes conditional affirms, and R87 checks the full final ASR tokens lexically. Long turns already go through R44's segmenter, are handled per segment, and form their own Tier A stratum. GLOBAL's fixed ~8-tool list (R34) keeps the safety pass short. Nowhere does the design state an explicit window-fit check, but the harmful paths in the attack are already gated.

### Round 189. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: The "exactly one event" constraint collapses multi-intent turns, so logits decide which of two safety intents survives
- Attack: Caller: "Put me on your do-not-call list and let me speak to your supervisor about how you got my number." GLOBAL must output exactly one event. request_human edges out stop/DNC; the lexicon misses because the caller said "do-not-call list" inside a longer clause the dialect patterns don't anchor. The call warm-transfers a DNC requester to a sales supervisor. Another caller: "Cancel it, and who am I even talking to?" yields request_caller_identity; the cancel is never recorded. Real callers routinely bundle intents. A single-label schema has no way to say "both", so the lower-scored legal obligation is dropped by construction. TCPA/TSR DNC requests must be honored whatever else is asked; a forced single choice turns compliance into an argmax.
- Defense: DNC never depends on a GLOBAL argmax. R66: "mid-call stop = union of Needle GLOBAL + lexicon". The lexicon is a pattern match, not anchored to the whole utterance, and it includes "don't call / take me off / remove my number". R105 fires the stop check on stop-adjacent tokens ("list", "calling"). R44 splits bundled turns into segments, each classified separately. As a backstop, R66's "post-call DNC sweep of every transcript (lexicon + GLOBAL re-run) before any redial" catches any miss. R6 already canonicalizes sets by precedence, "safety/opt-out" first.
- Verdict: R63 constrains decoding to exactly one call, and the final plan repeats that for GLOBAL. So a bundled turn can only keep one safety intent. DNC is partly covered by the lexicon union (R66) and the post-call sweep before any redial. However, R24's Tier 1 list ("don't call", "take me off"...) does not clearly match "put me on your do-not-call list". R108's stop-adjacent check applies only to overlap residue during agent playback, and R44's segmenter covers long turns only. The cancel case has no backstop at all: cancel_or_rescind is a Needle-only GLOBAL event with no lexicon (R76), and the post-call sweep checks only DNC. "Cancel it, and who am I even talking to?" therefore loses the cancel by construction.
- Fix: Make GLOBAL multi-label: each safety event gets its own calibrated threshold and every event above it fires, handled in a fixed order (DNC write, then cancel, then identity/human answers; any transfer carries the DNC flag and is service-only). Add a cancel/rescind lexicon to the deterministic union, and extend the post-call sweep to cancel intents. Add "do-not-call" and "do not call list" to Tier 1, plus a bundled-intent Tier A stratum.

### Round 190. Winner: Designer
- Topic: GLOBAL and LOCAL are scored by separate softmaxes over different label sets, so a strong-but-sub-threshold safety signal is discarded while LOCAL commits
- Attack: At COMMIT: "Okay, yes, but honestly I want to run it past someone at your company first." GLOBAL: none 0.52, request_human 0.44; no near-tie rule fires across "none" vs a safety event, so none. LOCAL, seeing a short lexical "yes": affirm 0.91. Arbitration sees GLOBAL=none, LOCAL=affirm, and commits. The design compares top-1 vs top-2 within a pass but has no rule combining a 0.44 safety probability with a LOCAL commit. The two passes have no common probability scale and no joint decision rule. A commit should be blocked by substantial safety mass, yet the gate only reads each pass's argmax. Calibration of each pass separately says nothing about the joint error rate of "commit while a safety event was nearly chosen".
- Defense: This caller fails the commit gate on its own: "yes, but ..." is a conditional affirm, and R86 excludes it from commit. R141 requires the action phrase "send the contract", not "yes", and R87's gate is lexical, so LOCAL affirm=0.91 is never enough to commit. R130 calibrates both passes with temperature and prior correction, so their probabilities are on a meaningful common scale. R149's overcommitment and vulnerability signals also block a same-call close.
- Verdict: The example caller cannot commit under existing rules: "yes, but..." is excluded by R86, R144 requires the phrase "send the contract" rather than "yes", and R87's gate is lexical and short-answer only (R81). R133 calibrates both passes to target false-commit rates, and R152's overcommitment signals block a same-call close. A caller who says only "send the contract" is unlikely to carry heavy request_human mass. The lack of a formal joint rule is a refinement, not an open path to harm.

### Round 191. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Fragment wording variants change Needle's input distribution, but certification is keyed by fragment id, not by wording
- Attack: The read-back question has three approved wordings, chosen deterministically (e.g., by call hash). Needle's input includes the agent's last utterance to interpret "yes". Wording 1 ends "Shall we go ahead?"; wording 3 ends "Any questions before we go ahead?" The caller says "No, go ahead." Under wording 3 "no" answers the questions clause; Needle learned "no"-first strongly as deny. Wording 3's traffic shows a different commit/deny confusion matrix, but the per-fragment-id certificate pools all three. If Needle doesn't see the question, a bare "yes" is ungroundable; if it does, every wording is a distinct input distribution. Rule-of-three per state on pooled traffic hides a variant with several times the error rate, and adding a new wording silently changes classifier behaviour without touching weights.
- Defense: "No, go ahead" cannot commit. R141 made the commit question an action-production prompt, and R87's lexical gate needs "send the contract". A polarity-mixed answer is R77's ambiguous_polarity, which leads to a closed binary re-ask. R38: "confirmation prompts contain no answer words." R148's leading-question lint covers every state. The R113 render log already records the wording id per call, so per-wording error rates are measurable today.
- Verdict: The premise fails on the design itself. Needle maps "one utterance + the current state's tool schema" (Runtime section), and affirm/deny carry the target question id deterministically (R58), so the agent's wording is not part of Needle's input. "No, go ahead" cannot commit anyway: R144's action phrase, R87's lexical gate, R77's ambiguous_polarity re-ask and R38's no-answer-words rule all stop it. R59 chooses wordings deterministically and logs them, so production traffic has the same wording mix as the certified traffic. R116's render log records the wording id, so per-wording rates can already be measured.

### Round 192. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Hierarchical statechart precedence: a substate that handles the same event shadows the root GLOBAL exit
- Attack: In WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE, designers add a local handler for cancel_or_rescind meaning "cancel and resend the envelope" (caller: "cancel that text, send it to my other phone"). The caller later says "cancel the whole thing." StateSmith, following UML semantics, gives the innermost state first claim on the event; the local handler consumes it, resends the envelope, and the root cancel_or_rescind exit never runs. The model check passes because the root transition exists and is reachable from other states. In hierarchical machines an event handled by a child stops propagating to parents (UML run-to-completion, child-first). "GLOBAL exits from any state" is only true where no substate reuses the event name. The invariant checks existence of a root edge, not that every state actually reaches it for every GLOBAL event.
- Defense: The checker already works on the right artifact. R131 enumerates "the compiled binary's (state, event, abstract-guard) transition relation by harness" and diffs it against the graph. The relation includes child-first resolution, so (WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE, cancel_or_rescind) mapping to resend shows up there. R76's cancel_or_rescind exits to CANCEL with no persuasion. R60 already gives envelope help its own events ("help_with_screen offers resend/wait/callback only"), so "cancel that text" was never meant to be cancel_or_rescind.
- Verdict: The final plan states "GLOBAL exits from any state" as a model-checked invariant over the exported graph. The attack's claim that this checks only whether a root edge exists is an assumption, not a fact stated in the design. R134 already enumerates the compiled binary's effective (state, event) relation, which follows child-first resolution, so a shadowed pair would appear in the artifact the checker uses. R60 already gives envelope help its own events (help_with_screen: resend/wait/callback), so the design never needed to reuse cancel_or_rescind locally. The risk is real enough that the check should be spelled out.

### Round 193. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: StateSmith machines are single-threaded run-to-completion code with no event queue, so concurrent event sources are serialized by hand-written glue outside the model check
- Attack: A Needle turn dispatches affirm into COMMIT; during its entry action (outbox write, fragment selection) the e-sign vendor webhook, a WAITING_FOR timeout tick and a telephony hangup arrive on other threads. Glue code calls dispatch() from the webhook thread while the first dispatch is mid-transition. Current-state variable and slot store interleave: the machine records CONTRACT_SENT, then the hangup handler exits to WRAP_UP, then the stale webhook moves it to SALE_PENDING after the call ended. StateSmith generates plain C with a dispatch function and no built-in queue, locking or re-entrancy protection; concurrency, ordering and timers are the integrator's job. The model checker verifies the graph assuming atomic, ordered events, so every race lives in uncertified code the invariants never covered.
- Defense: The existing design already serializes effects. Actions only append intents to the outbox, which drains only on committed turns (R13). Batches are transactions on a staged store (R6). Webhooks are only "a wake-up hint" with pulled status, and with "monotonic status" (R133). Hangup is a root telephony event (R35). The R131 runtime trace monitor refuses commit effects without READ_BACK on the trace. A post-hangup move to SALE_PENDING driven by a genuinely pulled "completed" status is also correct: callers may sign after the call. The real gap is glue re-entrancy.
- Verdict: The existing parts serialize effects and harden webhooks. R13 only lets effects leave through the outbox on committed turns. R136 treats a webhook as a wake-up hint with pulled, monotonic status. R134's trace monitor guards commit effects. None of these says who calls dispatch() or from which thread. StateSmith generates plain dispatch code with no queue or re-entrancy protection, so Needle results, webhooks, WAITING_FOR timers (R55) and telephony events (R35) can interleave on the current-state variable and the slot store, outside anything the graph checker verifies. The Designer concedes the "glue re-entrancy" gap, and the fix is new.
- Fix: Use a single-writer actor per session. All event sources enqueue into one FIFO mailbox, drained by one thread that calls dispatch() run-to-completion. dispatch asserts non-reentrancy and fails closed. Events carry a session epoch, and stale ones are dropped. Model-check the mailbox glue over all interleavings (e.g. TLA+), and add random-interleaving fault injection that must yield only legal traces.


### Round 194. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Competitor sabotage by spoofed inbound calls that rescind signed contracts and opt out live leads
- Attack: A rival installer buys the same aged lead list. Inside the cancellation window, it spoofs each signed customer's caller ID into INBOUND_OPEN. It passes the identity challenge with name, address and roof facts from data brokers, then says "cancel my contract, I changed my mind." cancel_or_rescind runs with no persuasion and no friction. Unsigned leads get "put me on your do-not-call list." Then the rival calls the same homeowners, who have now been "rescinded". Inbound ANI can be spoofed. STIR/SHAKEN attestation is often B or C on inbound, or missing. The challenge-response only checks facts from the lead record, and those are public or brokered. The design treats a cancel or DNC request as irreversible and frictionless by design. That gives no confirmation step on the e-sign channel, and it leaves no way to undo a forged rescission.
- Defense: A forged DNC fails safe. It only suppresses our own calls and gives the rival nothing, because our DNC ledger is never shared. A forged rescission is not silent either. R76's CANCEL path "sends written confirmation and right-to-cancel reminder" to the contract's channels, and R141 mails a notice "to the validated property address ... for every record owner". So the real customer learns of the forgery. Inbound is never trusted on ANI: R30 says "never caller ID alone", R162 draws challenge answers "only from independent sources ... never from partner lead fields", and R141 runs a SIM-swap/port-out check before IDENTITY. The real gap: brokered facts can pass the challenge, and a cancel has no undo. Adding friction to a genuine cancel is not an acceptable fix.
- Verdict: A forged DNC only stops our own calls, and the DNC ledger is never shared, so it fails safe for the consumer. A forged rescission is not silent: R76's CANCEL state sends written confirmation and a right-to-cancel reminder to the contract's channels, and R141 mails a notice to every record owner at the property address, so the real customer learns of it and can re-sign through the normal flow. Keeping cancels frictionless on a knowledge-only check is the correct legal posture, and the remaining harm is a business loss that the design already surfaces.

### Round 195. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Identity challenge-response as a PII enumeration oracle for fraudsters and data brokers
- Attack: An attacker scripts thousands of inbound calls, rotating spoofed numbers. Each call answers the IDENTITY challenge with a guessed name and address pair or a guessed co-owner. VERIFIED goes on to QUALIFY, where the agent asks about "your home" and its roof age. WRONG_PARTY branches differently. The deterministic timing and fragment ids tell the two outcomes apart. The attacker learns who owns which home, which homeowners are leads, and who is mid-purchase, then sells that as "solar-intent, verified owner" data or uses it for targeted scams. A deterministic verifier gives a distinguishable answer on every guess. The design has no rate limit by lead, no attempt counter across numbers, no constant-shape failure path and no lockout. Confirming that a record matches is itself a disclosure of lead data. This turns topic #125 (passive leakage) into an active oracle the attacker can query again and again.
- Defense: The design already denies the oracle its inputs and caps its rate. R162 sources challenge answers from "public records, KYC vendor", and R146 says "a partial match goes to clarify or a human, never to a WRONG_PARTY". So failure is not a crisp binary. R87 gives "inbound per-caller rate limits", R141 runs a number-verification check before IDENTITY, and R132's 100% label-free monitors ("per-state event-mix shift", CUSUM) would flag a surge of IDENTITY failures. R73's VERIFIED guard sits on "all personalized fragments". The attack is right that a fresh inbound caller who passes the challenge then hears QUALIFY wording. That difference in what happens next is a membership disclosure, and the rate limits are keyed by caller, not by lead.
- Verdict: Inbound IDENTITY gives an observable difference: a match moves on to VERIFIED and QUALIFY wording, while a mismatch goes to clarify or WRONG_PARTY. R87's rate limits are keyed by caller, so rotating spoofed ANIs gets round them. R132's CUSUM can only detect a surge after it starts, not stop a slow one. The Designer concedes the membership disclosure and the missing per-lead keying. Every element that closes the oracle (constant-shape response, OTP unlock, per-lead counters) is new.
- Fix: Inbound IDENTITY must answer match and mismatch with the same fixed fragment and timing ("we'll text a code to the number on file"). Only an OTP entered from the number of record unlocks VERIFIED or any personalized or QUALIFY fragment. Add attempt counters keyed by lead and address across all ANIs, with lockout to human-only plus a mailed notice, and a red-team test that timing and fragment ids can't be told apart.

### Round 196. Winner: Designer
- Topic: SMS pumping and toll fraud through the e-sign OTP, envelope resends and "outbox copy" messages
- Attack: A fraud ring plants leads, or reaches INBOUND_OPEN, using number ranges it shares revenue on. Bots run each call through to CONTRACT_SENT, then ask "resend the link" and "text me your DNC policy" again and again. Every request triggers a vendor SMS plus an OTP. Across 50,000 bot calls, the company pays carrier termination fees that the ring splits. The e-sign vendor also flags the account and suspends it, which stalls real signings. OTP flows are the main channel for SMS pumping (artificially inflated traffic). Twitter put its losses at about $60M a year. The outbox drains on every committed turn, and the design has no cap per number, cap per call or check on number reputation. WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE models webhooks, not cost or abuse. Sources: https://www.group-ib.com/blog/sms-pumping/ , https://cybelangel.com/blog/sms-pumping-fraud-telecom-attack/
- Defense: SMS here never goes to arbitrary numbers. R74 says "no Needle tool can create outbound contacts", and R22 sends the signing link and OTP only "to dialed number". R149 extends "the consent gate to every outbound SMS and email", and R122 allows "no SMS effect without valid registration". A planted lead on a revenue-share range fails R162's "pre-dial phone-to-record-owner match ... no match, the call is lead-gen only", and lead-gen calls never reach CONTRACT_SENT. Reaching CONTRACT_SENT at scale also means passing identity, suitability, the R144 action-production commit and record-owner lookup (R88). R60 limits help to "resend/wait/callback". R55 idempotency keys and R162's "per-partner fraud metrics with auto-suspension" are already in place. The real gap: the outbox executor has no explicit cap on SMS volume or spend.
- Verdict: Under the existing design, SMS reaches only consented numbers that are gated and owner-matched. R22 sends to the dialed number only, R149 extends the consent gate to every SMS, R122 adds the registration guard, R74 blocks Needle-created contacts, and R162's phone-to-record-owner match sends a planted revenue-share number to lead-gen only, so it never gets to CONTRACT_SENT. Getting 50,000 bots through identity, suitability, READ_BACK and the R144 action-production commit is not plausible, and R55 idempotency keys plus R69 call budgets limit repeats within a call. The missing spend cap is defense in depth, not a hole the attack can drive through.

### Round 197. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Consent bombing: third parties file "prior express written consent" forms for numbers they don't own
- Attack: An extortionist, or a plaintiff's firm's recruiter, fills the company's own web lead form 20,000 times. Each form uses a victim's real phone number and ticks the box naming the seller and the AI voice. The pre-dial gate sees valid per-number consent, Reassigned Numbers Database "no change" and no DNC, so the dialer calls them all. The victims never consented. A class action follows, along with a demand letter offering to settle quietly. Under the TCPA, consent must come from the called party (the subscriber or customary user). A form record is not proof of that. The gate checks whether consent exists, not who gave it. It has no double opt-in by SMS reply from that number and no link between form device and number. Damages run $500–1,500 per call, with no cap on the class.
- Defense: The gate is already meant to check who gave consent, not just whether a record exists. R74 requires a "PEWC artifact from that subscriber", and R162 requires "an independent consent-capture provider" plus a "pre-dial phone-to-record-owner match (carrier or identity graph)". A victim's number put on a stranger's address fails that match. Per-partner fraud metrics with auto-suspension (R162) and R51's "per-source/per-number" caps cover bulk injection. R8 lists "audit lead-vendor consent records" as an open item. A wrong-subscriber call ends in R73's WRONG_PARTY ("DNC number, flag lead"). The real gap: the company's own form, filled with the victim's real name and address, can pass every check without the subscriber ever confirming.
- Verdict: The Designer concedes the core gap: a form filled with the victim's real name, address and phone passes R74's PEWC record, R162's consent-capture provider and the phone-to-record-owner match, because the identity graph matches a real person's real data. Nothing in the existing design proves the subscriber controls the number. The caller carries the burden of proving consent, so 20,000 dials is class-action exposure. Number-possession proof is entirely new.
- Fix: A PEWC artifact is valid only if the form-filler entered a one-time code sent at submission to that number (double opt-in), and the verification record is stored in the consent artifact. Add form velocity controls per IP, device and ASN, with burst quarantine (never dialed). Add a consent_dispute event ("I never signed up") that writes DNC and quarantines the whole submission cluster, with a per-source dispute-rate Tier A metric and auto-suspension.

### Round 198. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: "AI-verified" tickets and warm transfers launder an impostor's requests to human ops
- Attack: The attacker passes the inbound identity check with brokered facts, as in A1. They raise service_issue: "my installer needs my new number and email for the install, and change my loan autopay account." The agent opens a ticket and sends a warm transfer tagged VERIFIED. Ops staff trust the AI's verification and update contact and payment details. The next e-sign OTPs, installer messages and refund checks go to the attacker. The design's verification is a weak knowledge check, but its outputs carry authority downstream. That is the business email compromise pattern, with the agent vouching for the caller. Nothing in the slot provenance marks "verified by challenge" as too weak for changes to account, contact or payee. A human acting on an agent-made ticket is outside every certified invariant.
- Defense: The agent has no channel through which an impostor can change contact or payee details. R5 writer allowlists let "customer-sourced calls write only customer-fact slots". R7 collects "no account/income/credit". R45 says "voice channel never takes payment", and R89 bans requesting "bank/card data". R62 requires "name changes confirmed in writing (SMS/e-sign)". R22 says "identifiers confirmed not transcribed". R123 requires "independently sourced contact data" per signer, and R141 runs a SIM-swap check. The R99 service_issue path creates a "service ticket via outbox/contractor API with callback". The attack is right about one thing. The ticket or transfer can carry a bare VERIFIED tag, and a human may read that as strong authority.
- Verdict: The agent itself can't write contact or payee data (R5, R45, R62). But the R99 tickets and R56 warm transfers have no defined payload. The Designer concedes they can carry a bare VERIFIED that humans will read as strong authority, even though it only reflects a knowledge-based check that brokered facts can pass. In the existing design, nothing stops the impostor's request from reaching ops with the agent's vouching attached. That is the BEC pattern the attack describes, and every control that closes it is new.
- Fix: Use a typed handoff contract carrying verification_strength (knowledge_only / otp_number_of_record / lender_kyc), never a bare VERIFIED. Ticket and transfer schemas get no fields for a new phone, email, address or payee, and callbacks go only to the contact of record. Add a CRM-enforced rule that no contact, payee or autopay change comes from an agent-originated ticket or transfer without an out-of-band OTP to the old contact of record, a mailed notice and a cooling delay.

### Round 199. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Insider logic bomb hidden among a fragment's approved alternative wordings
- Attack: A disgruntled script approver adds wording #7 to the savings fragment. It passes every lexical lint: no literal numbers, no banned claims, Reg Z co-occurrence satisfied. It says "most of our customers end up paying nothing out of pocket." The deterministic selector picks #7 only when the hash of certain slots (for example age band plus a financing selection) falls in a narrow bucket, about 0.3% of calls, and skewed toward seniors. Random audit samples almost never hit it. "Human-approved" means one person can be the single point of failure. Lints are syntactic, while deception is semantic. Deterministic wording selection keyed on slot values gives an insider a precise trigger. Audits stratified by dialect and state do not stratify by wording id crossed with slot conditions. The design has no two-person rule and no requirement that every wording is audited on live calls.
- Defense: Wording #7 should not pass existing checks. "Most of our customers ... paying nothing out of pocket" is an aggregate claim plus a free/no-cost claim. R92 allows social proof "only as audited aggregate policy-table facts ... with substantiation id". R91 says "every fact token is a policy-table key". R18's banned-claims lint covers "free/no-cost, zero bill", and every template needs "compliance sign-off". Every wording is "logged (certification keyed to fragment id)" (R59) and archived as a "unique message" in WORM with its wording id (R116), so it can be traced after the fact. R151 says "variant selection is never optimized on conversion", and R46 runs matched-pair tests. The attack is right about two things: the selector's inputs are not restricted, and audits are not stratified by wording id.
- Verdict: R59 says wording selection is "deterministic" but never restricts what feeds it, and the Designer concedes the selector's inputs are unrestricted and that audits aren't stratified by wording id. That is exactly what makes a 0.3% senior-skewed trigger possible and keeps random audits from finding it. R18's lexical lint and single compliance sign-off may miss a paraphrase like "nothing out of pocket", and R18 lists whole-path net-impression review as still open. R116's WORM log only finds the trigger after the harm. The closing controls (selector input restriction, two-person signed commits, per-wording audit quotas) are new.
- Fix: Add a build check that the wording selector's only input is hash(session_id, fragment_id), so no slot or customer attribute reaches it. Every wording needs two-person approval (author plus an independent compliance reviewer) with signed commits. Add a quantifier and aggregate lint ("most", "nothing out of pocket") that requires a table key. Each new wording gets a 100%-audited canary and a minimum live audit quota per release, plus a matched-pair test of the wording-id distribution across age bands.

### Round 200. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Corrupt or infiltrated human audit labelers poison the ground truth that gates certification
- Attack: A labeling vendor's staff, or one bribed lead labeler, mark harmful closes as "no harm" and correct stops as "missed buying signal" in one or two target states. Disagreement adjudication sends those calls to the same small pool. The per-state 1e-5 bound certifies the bad state. Drift alarms stay quiet because they read the same labels. Retraining on the "union of all data" then teaches Needle the corrupted labels. The whole safety case rests on "humans listening to audio," but the design never threat-models the labelers. It has no blind gold calls planted to catch dishonest labelers, no rotation, no limit on labeler concentration per state, no conflict-of-interest controls and no independent re-audit. Topic #212 covers CRM success labels. This attack hits the harm labels behind certification itself.
- Defense: Certification does not rest on labels alone. The worst harms are prevented structurally, independent of labels. Model-checked invariants (every commit "passes READ_BACK", R85) are enforced, and R134's independent outbox monitor refuses commit effects without a READ_BACK playback mark. The "drift alarms read the same labels" premise is false: R132's monitors are "label-free ... on 100% of traffic". R163 makes independent feeds (AG/CFPB/BBB complaints, lender disputes, cancellations) override internal labels, with divergence alarms. R113 compares outcome proxies across strata. R143 confines labeling to "in-house or vetted staff" with "access logging and anomaly alerts". R131 uses two auditors with an inter-rater confusion matrix. The real gap: no gold calls are planted to test labelers, and no limit stops one pool from labeling a whole state.
- Verdict: The attack's key premise is wrong: R132's drift monitors are label-free and run on 100% of traffic. The worst harms are also blocked structurally, regardless of labels, by the R85 model-checked invariant and R134's independent outbox monitor refusing commits without READ_BACK. Missed stops are also caught by the label-independent lexicon sweep (R66). R131's two auditors with a confusion matrix, R143's in-house or vetted enclave with anomaly alerts, and R163/R113's independent outcome feeds give several cross-checks, so a single corrupt labeler can't quietly certify a state. A whole corrupt vendor is still a residual risk, which the suggested addition closes.

### Round 201. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Threats of violence, swatting and doxxing aimed at installers and reps hit a closed scripted exit
- Attack: A caller says "send your installer to my street and he won't leave," or "I know where your crew lives, I'll burn down the next house you put panels on." Needle has no threat event. The nearest options are sensitive_event (scripted sympathetic exit) or out_of_scope. The call ends politely. The call ran in no-retain mode, so nothing is preserved, and no human safety team is told. The crew is sent on schedule. GLOBAL has about 8 events and none is "threat to others." Closed-world forcing makes a credible threat look like distress or noise. The design has no escalation duty, no evidence hold that overrides no-retain or automated deletion, and no hold on install or visit dispatch. A competitor or harasser can also use the same gap to threaten without leaving a trace.
- Defense: "Nothing is preserved" is false. R116 keeps the TSR record set "in WORM storage, whatever the privacy mode": CDR, real number, render log and agent-side audio. R113 makes the teardown sweep "mandatory before purge" even on NO_RETAIN, so a threat lexicon in that sweep sees the transcript before deletion. R117 already established the pattern this needs: a GLOBAL event that "freezes every deletion ... keeps the original audio and transcript, and alerts" a human team. R149 adds a confidential human-review path for household safety. R36 promotes out_of_scope clusters to new events. The attack is right that threats to others have no event of their own, and that nothing holds a dispatch.
- Verdict: No GLOBAL event covers threats to others. R117's legal_threat hold covers litigation, and R149's household_safety covers the caller's own household, so a credible threat to crews falls into sensitive_event or out_of_scope and ends politely. R116's WORM keeps the CDR and agent-side audio, not the caller's words. R113's pre-purge sweep is a DNC sweep with no threat lexicon. Nothing pages a human or holds a dispatch. The Designer concedes both gaps.
- Fix: Add a threat_to_others GLOBAL event (lexicon plus Needle, also re-run in the post-call sweep). It plays a fixed neutral fragment, ends the call and sets a safety_hold. The hold reuses legal_hold to freeze deletion and keeps caller audio where the profile allows, or otherwise an incident record. It pages a staffed safety team and blocks every consult, survey and install dispatch to that address and number until a human clears it. Add a red-team stratum with a Tier A false-negative bound.

### Round 202. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Flooding request_human and booked in-home consults to exhaust closers and lure reps to an address
- Attack: A rival's bot farm calls in, or answers outbound calls, and says "I want to talk to a real person" or "book me an in-home consult." Warm transfers tie up the few human closers, so real buyers wait or abandon. Booked consults pull reps across the county to empty lots. In one case, a fake owner passes the brokered-fact identity check and books a 7 pm consult at a house the attacker controls to rob the rep. request_human is a GLOBAL exit that is always honored, and the design treats it as a safe fallback with no limit on abuse. Booked consults are outbox effects whose WAITING_FOR substates model only booked, conflict, timeout and error. They do not check address ownership against record owners, flag one address booked many times, or send bot-scored callers to a callback queue. Door-to-door reps already face real safety risk.
- Defense: request_human is honored, but not as an unlimited live transfer. R56 says "warm transfer to staffed queue if open, else book consultant call + send written proposal", so a full queue degrades to bookings, not to closer exhaustion. R87 gives "inbound per-caller rate limits", R142 adds admission control, and R132's event-mix CUSUM flags a request_human surge. In-home bookings are guarded by "identity && owner && adult" (R9), use R88's "record-owner lookup on validated address" and R22's postal validation, and depend on R162's phone-to-record-owner match (no match = lead-gen only). The attack is right that knowledge-only identity can still book a real-looking address, and that rep safety and booking density aren't modeled.
- Verdict: The flooding half is largely contained: R56 falls back from a full queue to a booking, and R87, R142 and R132 limit and detect surges. The lure half is not. An inbound caller who passes the knowledge-only challenge with brokered facts satisfies R9's identity && owner && adult guard and R88's record-owner lookup. R162's phone-to-record-owner match is a pre-dial check and doesn't apply to inbound. The Designer concedes that rep safety and repeat bookings at one address aren't modeled, and the closing controls are all new.
- Fix: An in-home consult needs an OTP to the independently sourced number of record; otherwise the booking is a phone or video consult. Cap active consults at one per address and N per ANI or range per week, with a repeat-address alarm. Give OTP-verified callers transfer priority, and give unverified request_human a guaranteed callback to the number of record. Add a rep safety protocol: daylight slots by default, check-in and check-out with an escalation timer, a second person on flagged bookings, and a pre-visit confirmation call to the number of record.

### Round 203. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Insider poaching at the human handoff: closers divert transferred customers to side deals or rivals
- Attack: A human closer receives warm transfers carrying the full slot store: verified usage, the quoted price, financing appetite, record owners and the vulnerability counter. Off the recorded line, by personal cell, they offer the "same system, $3k cheaper through my other company." Or they sell the warm-transfer feed to a competitor. Customers sign elsewhere, and the high-vulnerability flags tell the insider who is easiest to pressure. Certification ends where request_human starts. The design gives humans everything and constrains nothing: it has no data minimization in the handoff, no masking of vulnerability flags, no monitoring of contact outside the recorded channel and no alarm on transfer-to-close leakage. Passing a vulnerability flag to a commissioned human turns a safety signal into a targeting list.
- Defense: The design minimizes data before the handoff, not after. R7 collects "only coarse facts ... no account/income/credit". R119 persists "only a type-less sensitive_exit label" for health events. Vulnerability above threshold yields NO_SALE_SAFE_EXIT with "decision-maker-only follow-up" (R15), so high-vulnerability callers are not warm-transferred to close. Humans are bound into the process too. R148 adds an owner-only human confirmation call for 65+ owners, R141 mails every record owner a contract summary with cancel instructions, and R163 requires "two-person approval" and decouples bonuses from labels. The attack is right that the handoff payload has no defined schema, and that leakage of deals to side channels isn't measured.
- Verdict: The attack assumes the closer gets "the full slot store", but the design never says that. R7 keeps the agent to coarse facts with no account, income or credit data. R119 persists only a type-less sensitive_exit label. R15 sends high-vulnerability callers to NO_SALE_SAFE_EXIT with decision-maker-only follow-up, so they are not warm-transferred to a closer. Off-channel poaching by commissioned staff is a generic sales-org insider risk that the agent doesn't make worse, and R141's mailed notice plus R148's owner-only confirmation call put a human check on any side deal.


### Round 204. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Condo/HOA owner "owns" the home but the roof is association common area needing board approval, insurance and roof-removal duties
- Attack: Agent calls a townhouse owner in an HOA. QUALIFY asks "Do you own your home?" ("Yes"), roof age ("about 10"), shade ("none"). The suitability table passes, QUOTE runs, READ_BACK covers price, warranties, lien and cancellation, and the owner says "yes" and signs. The roof is a common element. The board takes months, attaches removal-for-reroof and liability-insurance conditions, or turns down the requested placement. The signed contract, financing and lien sit on a system that may never be installed as sold. Ownership and roof age are the only title-type inputs. The design has no HOA, CC&R or common-area slot and no approval-contingency fragment. Cal. Civ. Code 714.1 and 4746 let associations require approval, insurance, maintenance and removal for repairs on shared roofs. Those are material limitations the READ_BACK never states. Sources: california.public.law/codes/civil_code_section_4746, codes.findlaw.com/ca/civil-code/civ-sect-714-1
- Defense: An HOA townhouse cannot reach install on a roof the board controls. R156 schedules "the site survey inside the cancellation window", makes "the contract contingent on a survey-confirmed price" and blocks install until then, with "roof-structure slots" and a no-penalty exit on any change order. R83 requires every material limitation from the product-policy table in each read-back, and R71's suitability table already supports pre-quote holds (ROOF_FIRST). R88's record-owner lookup on the validated address returns the parcel record, which for a common-interest development names the association parcel. The real gap: nothing asks about governance before the quote, so association approval and its conditions are never read back.
- Verdict: Nothing in the design asks about HOA, CC&R or common-element governance, and the Designer concedes this. R159's survey contingency covers price changes found by a physical survey; it does not cover a board's approval, insurance or removal-for-reroof conditions. R83's material limitations are product limits, not title or governance limits. The claim that R88's record-owner lookup returns the association parcel is wrong for a townhouse unit, whose parcel names the unit owner. The contract, financing and lien can therefore be signed on a roof the buyer cannot authorize.
- Fix: Add a governance slot sourced from county parcel data (common-interest development or recorded CC&R flag), plus one neutral QUALIFY question. An HOA or common-element roof goes to an HOA_APPROVAL_FIRST suitability row: no price and no close on the call, lead-gen plus a written proposal. Where a close is allowed, a policy-table row "association approval required; may impose insurance, maintenance and removal duties" is mandatory in READ_BACK and the contract, with a no-penalty cancel on denial or conditions. Install is blocked until written approval is on file.

### Round 205. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Manufactured/mobile home on a leased park lot: chattel title, no land ownership, wrong lien instrument
- Attack: The lead record shows a residential address. The caller says "Yes, I own it." They own a manufactured home in a land-lease park. QUALIFY records ownership, roof age and shade, and the suitability table passes. READ_BACK recites a "lien/UCC" on the property. The home is titled as personal property (in California through HCD, not the county), the park owner controls roof additions and the lot, and the lender's real-property lien or fixture filing does not attach. "Ownership" is one boolean. Nothing separates the structure from the land or real property from a chattel-titled home, and the park landlord's consent is not modeled. The read-back's lien/UCC disclosure is certified as one fragment, so it describes a security interest that is legally wrong for this collateral. Roof-load limits on HUD-code homes are not in the suitability table either.
- Defense: The chattel home fails the design's ownership checks before any lien is written. R159 draws identity answers "only from independent sources (public records, KYC vendor)" and runs "a pre-dial phone-to-record-owner match"; for a land-lease park the county record names the park owner, so the call is "lead-gen only". R88 runs the "record-owner lookup on validated address", and the R88 capacity flags from records and lender checks go to a human review path. The lender runs the application and decisions (R60), so a lender that will not take a real-property lien or fixture filing on HCD-titled collateral declines it, and nothing closes. R156 survey contingency covers roof structure. The real gap: the lien/UCC fragment is one wording, not keyed to collateral type.
- Verdict: Ownership is still a single boolean. The Designer's defense depends on the county parcel naming the park owner so that the R162 phone-to-record-owner match fails, but many manufactured homes appear on assessor or HCD records under the homeowner. In that case the match can succeed and the call closes. The certified lien/UCC READ_BACK fragment would then describe a real-property or fixture security interest that is legally wrong for chattel collateral. Park-landlord consent and HUD-code roof load are also unmodeled, and the Designer concedes the lien fragment is not keyed to collateral type.
- Fix: Add a structure_type slot from the parcel use code and the state manufactured-housing title registry (HCD in CA), never from speech. Manufactured, chattel-titled or land-lease homes go to NOT_SUITABLE for the residential product row (honest exit plus referral) or to human review. Make the lien/UCC READ_BACK fragment a policy-table key per collateral type, chosen only by the lender's stated security instrument. Add a HUD-code roof-load row.

### Round 206. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Home held in a revocable living trust: record owner is a trustee capacity, not a person with a phone
- Attack: Title reads "Jane Doe and John Doe, Trustees of the Doe Family Trust." Jane answers, passes the identity challenge and says "We own it." John died last year, and their adult son is now successor co-trustee. The "all record owners" step fans out e-sign envelopes to the people on the lead record, Jane and the late John, by SMS. Jane signs as an individual, not as trustee, and the son is never contacted. "Record owners + lender signers" assumes owners are natural persons with phone numbers. The design has no trust, trustee-capacity or successor-trustee slot and no check of who currently holds signing power. A contract or lien signed in the wrong capacity can be unenforceable or a cloud on title. The deceased-list check covers the dialed lead, not a dead co-trustee on title.
- Defense: Trusts are named in the design. R88: "capacity flags (conservatorship/trust/POA) from records and lender checks -> human review path", so "Trustees of the Doe Family Trust" on title sends the deal to a human, not to an automatic envelope. Nor does the envelope go to the late John: R146 says "never send a co-owner envelope to vendor-sourced contact data", R120 requires each signer to have "independently sourced contact data" and per-signer vendor ID verification for any lien, and R159 requires "lender-grade identity verification (document plus selfie or KBA) for each record owner". A dead co-trustee cannot pass that, so no lien files. The gap: the deceased check covers only the dialed lead, and nothing records the capacity a signer signs in.
- Verdict: R88 explicitly routes "capacity flags (conservatorship/trust/POA) from records and lender checks -> human review path", so a trust-titled property never gets an automatic envelope. R123 blocks any lien or install until every owner is independently verified with per-signer ID. A deceased co-trustee cannot pass that check, so a lien signed in the wrong capacity cannot file.

### Round 207. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Executor or heir in a probate estate encumbering a decedent's house without court or beneficiary authority
- Attack: The adult child living in the late parent's house answers. They say, matter-of-factly, "Mom passed last year, I'm the executor, it's basically mine." Nothing distressing is said, so sensitive_event never fires. The challenge-response matches the household, and QUALIFY records "owner." QUOTE, READ_BACK and COMMIT proceed, and an e-sign envelope with a financed lien goes to the executor. The design has no estate or probate state. Under California's independent administration, encumbering estate real property needs Notice of Proposed Action to the heirs (Prob. Code 10510 ff.), and without such authority it needs court approval. Heirs can void or contest the lien, and the "installed, no complaint" success label fires anyway. Source: law.justia.com/codes/california/2009/prob/10510-10520.html
- Defense: This caller is never VERIFIED as the owner. R159 draws identity answers from public records, and title still names Mom, so the adult child fails the R73 challenge-response as record owner; R143 sends a partial match "to clarify or a human". R88's record-owner lookup feeds the signer list, and R120 and R159 require ID verification for each record owner, which a decedent can't pass, so no lien files. "Mom passed last year" is also a bereavement phrase: R19 puts sensitive_event(bereavement) in every state with "deterministic lexicon pre-emption", which fires on wording, not on distress, giving a condolence exit with no pitch. The pre-dial deceased suppression list covers Mom as owner. The gap: no explicit estate route.
- Verdict: Title still names the deceased parent. Under R162 the caller fails the independent-source identity and phone-to-record-owner match, so the call becomes lead-gen only. R123 blocks any lien until every record owner is independently verified, which a decedent cannot do. The lexicon pre-emption for sensitive_event(bereavement) is a likely extra stop, but the design does not depend on it.

### Round 208. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Pending divorce: automatic restraining orders forbid encumbering the home, and the "move horizon" answer hides a court-ordered sale
- Attack: One spouse on title answers mid-divorce. The other spouse lives elsewhere. The caller wants the house "improved" before the property split and says "I'm staying put." They pass identity, and the e-sign SMS goes to both record owners. The estranged spouse's phone number on the lead record is stale, so a number the caller controls receives it. The caller signs both envelopes, and a financed lien is recorded. California Family Code 2040 automatic temporary restraining orders (ATROs) bar either party from "encumbering" any property without the other's written consent or a court order once a petition is served. The design has no marital-status or pending-dissolution slot. Move horizon is self-reported, and a forced sale makes the honest answer different from the one given.
- Defense: One spouse cannot sign both envelopes. R146 says "never send a co-owner envelope to vendor-sourced contact data"; the stale lead-record number gets nothing. The co-owner either self-opts in or gets a packet through a channel the verified customer chooses. R120 requires "independently sourced contact data" per signer, forbids two signers sharing a phone or email, and requires per-signer vendor ID verification for any lien envelope. R159 adds document-plus-selfie or KBA for each record owner. So the estranged spouse must independently sign, which is the written consent Fam. Code 2040 requires. R146's sensitive_event(household_safety) handles conflict cues. The gap: a pending dissolution isn't asked about, and the self-reported move horizon hides a court-ordered sale.
- Verdict: The core harm, one spouse encumbering without the other's consent, is blocked by existing rules. R149 bars envelopes to vendor-sourced contact data, and R123 requires independently sourced contact data, no shared phone between signers, and per-signer ID verification for any lien. R162 adds lender-grade ID for each record owner. A lien therefore needs the estranged spouse's own verified signature, which is the written consent Fam. Code 2040 asks for. The hidden forced sale is a caller-honesty issue no qualifying script can fully close.

### Round 209. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Non-owner-occupied rental: tenant pays the electric bill, so certified savings go to someone else, and the loan stops being consumer credit
- Attack: A landlord is called about a single-family rental listed in the lead record. They own it, the roof is new, and there is no shade, so the suitability table passes. The savings fragment uses "verified usage" from the tenant's utility account. READ_BACK recites Reg Z financing terms and senior/home-solicitation cancellation rights. The landlord signs. The design never asks who pays the utility bill or whether the owner lives there. The savings claim is substantiated against an account the buyer doesn't pay, which is deceptive as to that buyer. Credit to improve non-owner-occupied rental property counts as business purpose (Reg Z 12 CFR 1026.3(a), Comment 3(a)-5). The certified TILA disclosures and cancellation statements then misstate the buyer's rights.
- Defense: The savings claim can't fill from the tenant's account. R153 makes verified_usage writable only by the utility data API or bill extractor, and "authorization must come from the utility account holder. A mismatch with the record owner routes to a written aut[horization path]", so the tenant's account is not the landlord's to authorize. R61 permits savings "only from verified bill or usage data", and R72 requires a calculator_output with a substantiation id. On Reg Z, R60 has the lender run the application and decisions, and the lender classifies loan purpose; R17's packet carries the lender's own disclosures. The real gap: no owner-occupancy slot, so certified consumer fragments (home-solicitation cancel, TILA) can be spoken to a business buyer.
- Verdict: The design has no owner-occupancy or bill-payer slot, and the Designer concedes this. R156 routes an account-holder vs record-owner mismatch to a written-authorization path, so a tenant-authorized usage record can still become "verified_usage". The savings it produces would be told to a landlord who does not pay that bill. The certified consumer framing (home-solicitation cancel, TILA-style financing read-back) is also spoken to a business-purpose buyer, even though the lender decides the credit purpose.
- Fix: Add an owner_occupied slot: one QUALIFY question cross-checked against homestead-exemption and mailing-address records, with disagreement going to clarify and then a human. Non-owner-occupied goes to NOT_SUITABLE for the consumer product, with referral to a human commercial desk. Any savings figure requires bill_payer equal to a signing record owner, else no figure. Add an invariant that consumer-credit and home-solicitation fragments are reachable only when owner_occupied is verified.

### Round 210. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Duplex/triplex with separate meters: system sized and priced on one unit's usage, interconnected to one meter
- Attack: An owner-occupant of a duplex qualifies. "Verified usage" pulls the owner's own meter. The calculator sizes the system to the whole roof, and the savings fragment states savings for that system. The roof covers both units, which have separate meters and a house meter. Net metering credits only the meter the system ties into. The quoted savings assume a usage profile that one meter doesn't have, and splitting the credit needs a different tariff and interconnection. The slot store has one usage value and one meter. The calculator, the policy table and the certified savings fragment assume one dwelling with one service. No QUALIFY question asks unit count or meter count. Duplexes look like single-family homes in lead data, so the calculator output is certified and substantiated yet wrong for the building.
- Defense: A whole-roof system priced on one unit's usage is blocked twice. R154: "the calculator must use the customer's exact tariff schedule ... on verified interval data, else no figure", and R29's calculator returns "insufficient-inputs -> no figure, offer survey". R43 adds per-slot ranges and cross-slot consistency checks that route to clarify. R156 makes the contract contingent on a survey-confirmed price, adds main-panel slots, and makes any change (a second service, a different interconnection) a new quote hash with re-disclosure, fresh e-sign and no-penalty exit. The gap: nothing compares the system size to the authorized meter, and unit and meter counts aren't slots.
- Verdict: The attack assumes the system is sized to the whole roof, but R61 already says "savings/sizing figures only from verified bill or usage data". R157 requires the exact tariff on verified interval data from the authorized account, else no figure. Sizing, tariff and savings therefore all key to the one meter that was authorized, and they stay consistent with that meter's interconnection. R159's survey contingency turns any second-service or interconnection change into a re-disclosed change order with a no-penalty exit.

### Round 211. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Owner of several properties answers qualifying questions about the house they're in while the contract binds the lead-record address
- Attack: The lead record is a lake house. The owner answers on their mobile from their primary home. "Roof age?" "Replaced 2019" (the primary home). "Shade?" "None" (the primary home). "Planning to move?" "No." Every answer passes the argument grounding gate because the owner really said it. The quote, read-back address and lien all point to the lake house, which has a 25-year-old roof under heavy pines. Slots carry provenance (spoken) but not referent: which property the speaker means. The read-back never states the property address before COMMIT, and no fragment ties QUALIFY answers to an address. Suitability is satisfied by true statements about the wrong house. This defeats the success metric's own suitability audit only after installation.
- Defense: The design already ties the call to a specific address. The R73 challenge-response has the caller state name and address, matched against the record; R22 validates the address with the postal service and confirms the house number digit by digit. R79 makes the "roof/shade data lookup" an explicit calculator input, so "none" for a lake house under pines conflicts with aerial shade data, and R43 cross-slot consistency checks route the conflict to clarify. R156 makes the contract contingent on a survey-confirmed price with roof-structure slots, and blocks install, inside the cancellation window. The gap is real, though: QUALIFY questions and READ_BACK don't name the property, so the answers have no referent.
- Verdict: The R73 challenge-response matches the stated address against the record, and R146 sends partial matches to clarify or a human. If wrong-house answers still get through, R79's aerial shade lookup is a calculator input. R159 schedules the physical survey inside the cancellation window, with roof-structure slots, a no-penalty exit and install blocked until it finishes. A 25-year roof under pines is therefore caught before install, and the Breaker's stated harm (install on an unsuitable roof) does not occur. The missing property referent in QUALIFY and READ_BACK is a real quality gap, but it is not an unguarded harm.

### Round 212. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Farmhouse on a shared agricultural meter: "verified usage" includes irrigation pumps and barns on a farm tariff
- Attack: A farm family's residence and barns share one agricultural service. Utility data authorizes fine, and usage comes back huge in summer. The calculator sizes a large residential system and the certified savings fragment quotes big savings at residential rates. The account is on an ag time-of-use tariff, the property may be owned by the family's farm LLC, and the system may need a ground mount on cropland.
