Verified usage is trusted because of its provenance, not checked for what it measures. The policy table has no tariff-class key, so residential rate assumptions apply to an ag tariff. Entity ownership (LLC or partnership), ground-mount siting and the meter's mixed business load fall outside the residential suitability table, yet the substantiation id still validates the claim.
- Defense: The residential figure never renders on an ag account. R154 requires "the customer's exact tariff schedule ... on verified interval data, else no figure", and the policy table carries only residential schedules, so an ag time-of-use tariff yields no figure. R43's per-slot ranges send out-of-range usage to clarify. R88's record-owner lookup returns a farm LLC, and R153 routes an account-holder mismatch with the record owner to the written-authorization path. R72 lets savings fill only with calculator_output plus a substantiation id, and the calculator can't produce one without a tariff row. R156 makes a ground mount a survey change order. The gap: the policy table has no explicit tariff-class key.
- Verdict: The core harm is residential-rate savings on an agricultural account. R157 blocks it: the calculator must use "the customer's exact tariff schedule ... on verified interval data, else no figure", and R72 requires a calculator_output with a substantiation id. An LLC record owner fails the R162 record-owner match, which makes the call lead-gen only. A ground mount becomes an R159 survey change order.

### Round 213. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Contract-for-deed (land installment) buyer: occupant believes they own it, while legal title and the e-sign recipient are a third-party seller
- Attack: A family bought their house on a land contract eight years ago. They say "We own it, we pay the mortgage." The suitability table's "record owners" lookup returns the seller-vendor. The design sends e-sign envelopes to "all record owners," so the seller gets an SMS about the buyers' solar deal and loan. Or the seller ignores it, the deal stalls, and the occupants have already made commitments. The design assumes speaker ownership plus record owners equals the buyers. It has no equitable-versus-legal title state, so it either discloses the occupants' financial dealings to a stranger or encumbers property the occupants can't encumber. Many land contracts make new liens or alterations a default, which risks forfeiture of years of equity.
- Defense: No stranger gets an SMS. R146: "never send a co-owner envelope to vendor-sourced contact data"; the verified customer gets a "co-signer required" packet and chooses the channel, or the co-owner self-opts in. So nothing is disclosed to the vendor-seller without the occupants' own choice. Nor is anything encumbered: R159 draws identity answers from public records and runs a phone-to-record-owner match, and the occupants aren't record owners, so the call is "lead-gen only". R120 blocks lien filing until every record owner is independently verified, and R88 sends capacity issues to human review. The gap: the system doesn't explain equitable vs. legal title, so occupants may act on the call.
- Verdict: Both harms are blocked by existing rules. R149 forbids any co-owner envelope to vendor-sourced contact data, so the seller-vendor is never sent an SMS without the occupants' own choice. Because the occupants are not record owners, the R162 pre-dial phone-to-record-owner match fails and the call becomes lead-gen only. R123 also blocks any lien until every record owner is independently verified.


### Round 214. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Follow-up and upsell calls to borrowers who are behind on their solar loan become debt-collection communications
- Attack: The pre-dial gate suppresses open tickets and complaints but never checks whether the loan is current. A battery-upsell campaign dials a customer who is 75 days late. Her husband answers. He is not a co-borrower. The challenge-response asks about "the Elm St solar account", and a READ_BACK-style fragment names the lender and the monthly payment. He asks, "Is this about the missed payments?" Needle labels it out_of_scope(service_issue), so the agent apologises and logs a ticket that says "the account". She gets three more campaign calls that week. California's Rosenthal Act covers creditors collecting their own debts (Civ. Code 1788.2(c)). It limits contact with third parties about the debt (1788.12) and bars unreasonably frequent calls (1788.11(e)). The design has no delinquency status, no collection mode, and no fragments that keep the debt from a non-borrower. Its "existing customer" handling is built for complaints, not for debts.
- Defense: The scenario relies on two things the design forbids. R73 identity is challenge-response: the *caller* states name and address and code matches them, so the agent never says "the Elm St solar account". The VERIFIED guard sits on every personalized fragment, so a non-borrower husband gets WRONG_PARTY or not_the_person(relation) before any lender or payment fragment can play. R92's lint bans fragments that reference any specific payment, and R7 says the agent holds no account data. "Missed payments" is on R150's hardship lexicon (collections, behind on payments), so it gives sensitive_event(financial_hardship): scripted exit, no pitch, suppression. It is not service_issue. Frequency caps (R41) bound repeat calls. The real gap is that delinquency is not yet a pre-dial input, although R163 already ingests the lender delinquency feed.
- Verdict: The scenario depends on the agent saying "the Elm St solar account" and on campaign calls going on after the husband raises the issue. R73's challenge-response has the caller state the name and address, so the agent never says either. R99 already suppresses all sales campaigns while a service ticket is open, so the out_of_scope(service_issue) label the Breaker assumes would itself stop the three follow-up calls. The Rosenthal theory is also weak: a marketing call that never mentions the debt is not a communication "in connection with the collection" of it. One caveat: R146's address-plus-surname match could mark a spouse VERIFIED. That is why the new lint on lender and payment fragments is worth having.

### Round 215. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Bankruptcy automatic stay and discharge injunction ignored by follow-up and upgrade campaigns
- Attack: A customer files Chapter 7 eight months after installation, and the solar loan is listed. The pre-dial gate scrubs deceased lists, DNC lists and complaints, but runs no bankruptcy (PACER) scrub. A "system check-in plus battery upgrade" call goes out. The deterministic quote fragment offers an upgrade "for existing customers in good standing". The customer says "I'm in bankruptcy". Needle forces this onto the nearest event, sensitive_event or service_issue. The ticket is closed and the campaign continues after the discharge. 11 U.S.C. 362(a)(6) stays any act to collect a pre-petition claim. Section 524 permanently bars collection of discharged debts. An offer that depends on being "in good standing" on a discharged loan pressures the customer to pay a debt that was wiped out. Nothing in the slot store, the suppression lists or the graph represents bankruptcy status.
- Defense: "I'm in bankruptcy" is a hardship phrase. R150 adds explicit rows for foreclosure, collections and shutoff, and the event gives sensitive_event(financial_hardship): a scripted exit with no pitch and campaign suppression. It is not a service ticket that closes. The "good standing" upgrade fragment could not pass the build. R46 says the product-policy table offers all options to everyone, keyed only on non-protected keys. R86 says a promise fragment may reference only policy items, and R37 says concessions come only from verifiable facts. The agent never collects: R45 takes no voice payment, and R89 bans any fragment asking for bank data. Even so, the agent should not market to a customer under a stay without a positive bankruptcy check.
- Verdict: No existing path collects a debt. There is no voice payment (R45) and no fragment asks for bank data (R89). Under R46 every option goes to everyone, so an offer cannot hinge on whether a loan is in good standing. "I'm in bankruptcy" lands on R150/R19 hardship (scripted exit, no pitch) or on service_issue, which brings R99 campaign suppression. Marketing a new product with no collection content is not an act to collect a pre-petition or discharged debt. The real gap is that the design never checks bankruptcy status, so the conversation is the only way it finds out. That is worth closing, but the harm in the scenario rests on a "good standing" fragment the design would not produce.

### Round 216. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: A spoken cancellation to the bot neither meets the written-notice requirement nor reaches the separate lender contract, the UCC filing and the install schedule
- Attack: On day 3 of the 5-day window, during a follow-up call, a senior says "cancel it". The GLOBAL event cancel_or_rescind fires and the agent plays its fixed acknowledgment: "Your cancellation request has been recorded." The outbox sends one CRM effect. The customer believes it is done and mails nothing. The lender's loan agreement, which is a separate e-sign envelope, stays active, the UCC-1 is recorded, and the installer keeps the slot. On day 9 the window is closed and the loan funds. Statutory cancellation rights are exercised by signed written notice, using the cancellation form the design itself sends (for example Cal. Civ. Code 1689.7). A spoken "recorded" from the agent's own voice sounds final but is not. The design models cancellation as a single outbox effect with no fan-out across the seller, lender, UCC and installer and no confirmation that each party processed it. Nothing joins all of those into the success label.
- Defense: R76 does more than record the request. The CANCEL state "calls contractor cancellation API, sends written confirmation and right-to-cancel reminder", so the customer is sent the written path in the same call. Its fragments are limited to acknowledge, confirm and process, and R55 gates every confirmation template on outbox success. Nothing irreversible happens inside the window. R17 makes install wait for the longest cancellation window, R123 allows no lien or UCC filing until every owner is verified, R141 blocks install until the mailed notice, and R154 ties the lender amount to the contract. What the design lacks is a fan-out across parties and a spoken wording that does not overstate.
- Verdict: R76 does call the contractor cancellation API and send written confirmation, and R55 gates the spoken confirmation on that effect succeeding. So the seller side is real. But cancellation is one effect aimed at one party. The design has no step that voids the separate lender envelope, terminates or holds the UCC filing, or releases the installer. It also has no invariant that blocks loan funding while a cancellation is open. The Designer's fan-out saga is entirely new.
- Fix: Turn CANCEL into a saga. One cancel intent fans out to the seller contract API, the lender envelope void or loan cancellation, a UCC hold or termination, and the installer slot release. Each party gets its own WAITING_FOR substate and idempotency key, and the customer is told cancellation is complete only after every party acknowledges. Add an invariant: no loan funding, UCC filing or install while any cancel is open. Treat oral cancellation to the agent as valid notice under the contract, and also send the pre-filled statutory form by e-sign and mail. The spoken fragment says "started", not "recorded/done".

### Round 217. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Lender milestone funding and deposits that exceed home-improvement payment limits, and a read-back with no start date or progress-payment schedule
- Attack: The certified READ_BACK covers product, size, cost, escalator, warranties, cancellation, lien and financing. It has no install start date and no completion date, because permitting is unknown. The loan the customer signs lets the lender pay the dealer at notice-to-proceed, before any work on the roof. A deposit is also taken at the close. Permitting stalls for five months while most of the price has already been paid for work not yet done. Cal. Bus. & Prof. Code 7159 requires a home-improvement contract to state the approximate start date and estimated completion date and to include a schedule of progress payments. Section 7159.5 caps the down payment at $1,000 or 10%, whichever is less, and bars payment for work not yet performed. The design's model-checked READ_BACK is complete by its own checklist but not by this statute.
- Defense: In California this read-back never runs on the call. R47 says where CA HIS salesperson registration applies, the call is in lead-gen mode with no price or terms. A registered salesperson of record issues the contract, and an invariant blocks on-call price states. That contract is R17's "state-specific written contract" from the facts table, so 7159 content belongs to the template that compliance owns. No deposit is taken by voice: R45 uses a processor-hosted page. R159 makes the contract contingent on a survey-confirmed price, and R158 states the first-payment event. Still, the regulation profile should enforce the 7159 fields and caps rather than trust template authors.
- Verdict: R47's lead-gen mode keeps the on-call read-back out of California, but the deposit (R45) and the lender's disbursement still happen. Nothing in the design caps the deposit (Cal. B&P 7159.5: $1,000 or 10%, whichever is less) or stops payment ahead of work performed. The contract template's 7159 fields (start date, completion date, progress-payment schedule) are left to template authors, with no required keys. The Designer admits enforcement is missing.
- Fix: Add a home_improvement_contract row in each regulation profile (CA 7159 and analogues) with required keyed fields: estimated start and completion dates (ranges allowed when tied to permits) and a progress-payment schedule. A build and runtime check fails any contract or read-back that lacks them. Compute the deposit effect as at most min(cap amount, cap %) from the profile, and have the outbox refuse anything larger. Admit a lender program into a profile only if its draw milestones never pay ahead of work performed. Make paying for work not yet done a Tier A harm signal.

### Round 218. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Lease or PPA "escalator" contracts read back without the home-sale transfer, buyer qualification and buyout terms that decide what happens when the customer sells
- Attack: The read-back says "escalator 2.9% per year, 25 years" and stops there. Four years later the customer sells. The buyer's lender requires the lease or PPA to meet agency property-eligibility rules, and the solar provider requires the buyer to pass its own credit check. The buyer fails, so the seller must pay the buyout from a formula that was never spoken, or the sale falls through. The success label recorded this customer as "installed, no complaint" years earlier. For a 25-year contract on a home, the transfer-on-sale and buyout terms are the material limitation. Mortgage agencies impose specific conditions on homes with leased solar or a PPA (e.g. Freddie Mac Guide 5601.4). A buyout value is a formula over future years, so the slot-hole fragments with no literal numbers cannot express it. The certified sale is silent on the scenario most likely to hurt the customer.
- Defense: The design already treats resale as material. R71 makes move/sale horizon a qualification slot that leads to NOT_SUITABLE with no rebuttal. It bans the claim "transfers to the buyer" and requires a "written lien/resale disclosure in contract". R83 requires material-limitation rows in every read-back, R85 lists "material restrictions" and lien/UCC, and R157 makes escalator-lease read-backs state year-1 and final-year payments. Fragments with no literal numbers can still express a buyout: the value is calculator_output (R72) over a policy-table formula, read through table keys (R91). Labels are provisional (R158) and come from independent complaint feeds over the term (R163), so a later sale dispute does count.
- Verdict: Resale is already treated as material in the existing design. R71 makes move/sale horizon a suitability slot leading to NOT_SUITABLE, bans the claim "transfers to the buyer" and requires a written resale disclosure in the contract. R83 and R85 require material-limitation rows in every read-back. R157 has escalator leases state the year-1 and final-year payments. A buyout figure fits as a calculator_output over a table formula (R72, R91), so the no-literal-numbers rule does not block it. Adding a specific transfer_on_sale row is content inside those mechanisms, not a new mechanism.

### Round 219. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Ownership changes between signature and installation (sale, move, death, divorce) with no re-check of record owners before work starts or the lien is recorded
- Attack: The contract is signed in March. The install waits out the longest cancellation window, then permitting and interconnection queues, and the crew arrives in August. By then the customer has moved and the house is in escrow with a buyer, or the house was quitclaimed in a divorce. The pre-dial gate and IDENTITY check ownership only at the sales call. The UCC-1 and roof penetrations land on a property now owned by someone else, and CRM records "installed". The design's record-owner and capacity checks (QUALIFY, the e-sign to "all record owners") are point-in-time and done only during the sale. Nothing after signing re-verifies title before an irreversible physical act and a lien recording. Its months-long gap between signature and install makes stale ownership routine rather than rare, and the success label rewards the install anyway.
- Defense: Install is not blind to time. R123 allows no lien filing or install "until every owner has been independently verified". R141 blocks install until the mailed notice to the property address. R148 adds a human owner-only call before install for owners 65+. R159 schedules the survey inside the window, and R160 gates install on WAITING_FOR_BOM_MATCH. R158 sets success at PTO plus N months of production with no complaint, so an install on a house someone else now owns produces a complaint and a harm label, not a success. The gap is that ownership is verified at signing and not again right before the irreversible acts.
- Verdict: Every ownership check the Designer cites runs at or around signing. R88 looks up record owners, R123 verifies signer identity, and R141/R159 happen early in or near the cancellation window. None of them re-checks title just before the install or the lien. R158's success label would eventually record the harm, but only after roof penetrations and a lien on property someone else now owns. The Designer admits the gap and the WAITING_FOR_TITLE_RECHECK gate is new.
- Fix: Add a WAITING_FOR_TITLE_RECHECK gate within N days before install and again immediately before any UCC filing. It re-runs the record-owner lookup and checks recorded deeds and transfers, listing and escrow feeds, the deceased list and capacity flags, and every signer reconfirms through their verified channel. Any change blocks install and filing and goes to human review. The current owners must re-sign, or the contract is cancelled with no penalty. A stale-title install is Tier A harm.

### Round 220. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Dual-purpose follow-up calls: the same compiled agent making install-scheduling or survey calls turns them into telemarketing, or announces a false purpose
- Attack: Ops reuses the certified bundle for "install confirmation" calls, because it is the only certified voice. The PREAMBLE is compiled and cannot be skipped, so it says "sales call" on a call that is not one. If ops builds a scheduling preamble instead, the existing DISCOVERY and QUOTE edges still let the agent mention battery add-ons or referral bonuses. Calls go to existing customers who are on the national DNC list, relying on their status as existing customers. Under the FCC's 2003 dual-purpose ruling, a call with any marketing component is telemarketing. Since the 2012 TCPA order, an existing business relationship no longer exempts artificial or prerecorded-voice telemarketing to residential lines from prior express written consent. The design has a single purpose statement and a single consent model. It has no certified non-sales call type and no lint stopping marketing edges from appearing in service calls.
- Defense: Existing-customer status was never a consent basis here. R74 puts a per-number PEWC gate in the deterministic dialer (the subscriber's own artifact naming the seller and the AI voice). R59 limits the dial list to PEWC and inbound or warm leads, and R114 scrubs state DNC lists. The TCPA concern therefore holds for any call the bundle makes. Ops also cannot "build a scheduling preamble". R31's PREAMBLE is compiled, R67 content-hashes bundles, and R134 diffs the compiled binary against the certified graph, so any new preamble means a new certified bundle. R99 also blocks sales calls to customers with open service tickets. What is missing is a certified non-sales call type.
- Verdict: The attack's legal harm requires calls that rely on the existing-customer relationship instead of consent. Every dial in this design passes R74's per-number PEWC gate, which names the seller and the AI voice, plus federal and state DNC scrubs (R59, R114). So a call with marketing content is lawful telemarketing under the design's own consent model. Ops cannot quietly swap in a scheduling preamble: R31's preamble is compiled, R67 hashes the bundle, and R134 diffs the binary against the certified graph. A "sales call" disclosure on a call that also schedules is over-disclosure, not a false purpose.

### Round 221. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The agent's own follow-up calls are the complaint intake, so its label taxonomy decides whether the success label ever sees a complaint
- Attack: The success label requires "no complaint". Every grievance raised on the agent's follow-up or upsell calls goes to out_of_scope(service_issue): apology, ticket, campaign suppression. A ticket is not a complaint. A customer says "your salesman lied about the savings, I want this reported". If Needle labels it service_issue instead of dispute_prior_statement or request_human, the call becomes a service ticket and the branch that made the claim stays certified as successful. The design is grading its own work. The classifier being certified also decides which outcomes count as failures in its training and release metric. Each release lowers measured harm by moving complaints into the "service" bucket. Nothing defines "complaint" independently of Needle's labels, and complaints filed with regulators never enter the CRM join.
- Defense: Needle does not grade itself. The R163 success label draws first on independent feeds that override CRM: AG/CFPB/BBB complaint feeds, lender disputes, cancellation logs and the post-install survey. Regulator complaints therefore enter. Certification uses humans listening to audio, auditing all disagreements plus random agreements (R2, R26). R51 says labels are never self-reported outcomes, and R144 adds a human post-sign check of whether consent was understood. "I want this reported" also hits dispute_prior_statement (R91) and legal_threat (R117). R132 watches out_of_scope cluster growth on 100% of traffic. The remaining gap is that "complaint" has no definition independent of the routing label.
- Verdict: Release certification does not rest on Needle's labels. It uses humans listening to the audio, covering all disagreements plus random agreements (R2, R26, R66). The R163 success label takes independent feeds first, including AG/CFPB/BBB complaint feeds, lender disputes and cancellation logs, and these override the CRM. So the claim that regulator complaints never enter the join is false. The leftover risk is mislabeling a complaint as service_issue on follow-up calls. Audits catch that, and the new links between follow-up tickets and the original sales branch tighten it.

### Round 222. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Warranty rows in the policy table that carry illegal tie-in or void-on-repair conditions, read back as certified fact
- Attack: The warranty rows in the policy table are copied from the seller's own warranty document, for example: "Workmanship and production warranties void if the system is serviced, moved or monitored by anyone other than [Seller]." The READ_BACK states this as a "material limitation". Every lint passes: no banned claim, no literal number, a valid substantiation id. Years later the seller has stopped trading, an independent technician fixes the inverter, and the remaining claims are denied on that clause. Magnuson-Moss bars conditioning a warranty on using a particular service provider (15 U.S.C. 2302(c)). In July 2024 the FTC sent warning letters about "warranty void if" terms ([FTC](https://search.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2024/07/ftc-warns-companies-stop-warranty-practices-harm-consumers-right-repair)). The design's substantiation id proves that a row exists, not that the row is lawful. The certified voice repeats the unlawful term to every buyer.
- Defense: R161 already changes what a warranty row means. A row is substantiated only by a backing instrument that survives the installer (third-party warranty insurance, manufacturer-backed coverage or escrow), and the READ_BACK names who backs each warranty. In the "seller stopped trading" scenario the claim goes to that backer. R18 requires compliance sign-off and a claims audit on every cited row, and R10's facts table is versioned and owned. Under R140, a withdrawn row becomes a tombstone that no bundle can cite. It is true that a substantiation id proves provenance and not lawfulness. The lint runs over fragments, not over row text or contract terms.
- Verdict: The agent's read-back accurately states the seller's own warranty term. Whether that term is lawful is a legal review, and the design already assigns it to compliance sign-off and the claims audit on every row a template cites (R18), on a versioned, owned table (R10). Withdrawn rows are tombstoned (R140). No lint could judge lawfulness, so the attack is really about human legal review, which exists. The Designer is right that the banned-claims lint covers fragments, not row text. The FTC's 2024 "warranty void if" warnings make that extension cheap and worthwhile.

### Round 223. Winner: Designer
- Topic: ACH disputes and chargebacks on deposits or first payments taken by bank debit, where the redactor and no-retain mode destroy the "express verifiable authorization" the TSR requires
- Attack: At the close the customer authorizes a bank-account debit for the deposit. The payment-data redactor removes the account details from the audio and transcript, and the no-retain profile deletes the recording. Six weeks later the customer, or a spouse on a joint account, disputes the debit as unauthorized. The bank asks for the authorization. The seller has a fragment id and a redacted log with no amount, date, account identification or customer-service number spoken back. For payment methods other than credit or debit cards, TSR 310.3(a)(3) requires written authorization or a recorded oral authorization that states the amount, date, number of debits, account details and a customer-service number. That recording must be available to the customer and the bank. The design's PCI-style redaction and privacy modes remove exactly that evidence. It either loses every dispute or violates the TSR.
- Defense: The scenario assumes an oral bank authorization, and the design does not take one. R45: "voice channel never takes payment; deposit via processor-hosted page". R89 bans any fragment that asks for bank data. The authorization is therefore a written electronic authorization signed on that page, and E-SIGN-valid signatures satisfy TSR 310.3(a)(3)(i). R120 adds ESIGN consent, and R123 binds each signer to independently verified identity. The redactor is defense in depth against a caller who reads out digits anyway. It does not destroy authorization evidence, because none is spoken. R116 keeps the TSR record set in WORM for 5+ years "whatever the privacy mode". The gap is that the processor page's capture fields and retention are not yet specified.
- Verdict: The scenario assumes an oral bank-debit authorization, and the design never takes one. R45 says the voice channel takes no payment and the deposit goes through a processor-hosted page, and R89 bans asking for bank data. The authorization is therefore a written electronic one, which TSR 310.3(a)(3)(i) accepts. Redaction and NO_RETAIN destroy no evidence because none is spoken, and R116 keeps the TSR record set in WORM storage in every privacy mode.


### Round 224. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Tax-credit "re-amortization" loans: the quoted monthly payment is a teaser that jumps when the customer doesn't prepay the assumed credit
- Attack: Lender product in the policy table is a 25-year solar loan built around a voluntary prepayment of ~30% at month 18. The calculator quotes the pre-re-amortization payment ($189). READ_BACK speaks "financing terms" from table keys: rate, term, $189/month. Customer, a retiree with no tax liability (and no 25D credit at all for 2026 installs), never prepays. At month 19 the payment re-amortizes to ~$270. The commit was gated on a number that was never the real payment. CFPB's 2024 solar financing spotlight flags exactly this structure: payments rise sharply if the assumed tax-credit prepayment isn't made. The design's read-back lists what to say, not that the spoken payment must be the payment the customer will actually owe. Lints check Reg Z trigger terms, not payment-shock structure. Source: files.consumerfinance.gov/f/documents/cfpb_solar-financing-issue-spotlight_2024-08.pdf
- Defense: The payment spoken at READ_BACK isn't free calculator output. R154 makes "the lender program's fee schedule ... a versioned policy-table input", and the financed total is "the lender contract price". R32 requires a "representative fully-disclosed example from versioned lender rate sheet". R85 requires READ_BACK to include "financing terms (Reg Z)". The tax credit can't prop up the payment either: R155 types incentive rows, and "capped and reservation rows can never reduce the quoted or read-back price". An expired 25D row would be refused as outside its effective_from/until window (R140). R150's suitability row compares the financed payment with verified savings. The gap is that no rule says which payment in a multi-step schedule gets spoken.
- Verdict: R154, R32 and R85 make the spoken financing terms come from the lender rate sheet and require "financing terms (Reg Z)" in READ_BACK, but nothing says which payment in a multi-step schedule gets spoken. R157's year-1/final-year rule covers escalator leases only, and R150's affordability check doesn't say which step it tests. The Designer concedes this gap, and the defense rests on new hardening. R155/R140 stop an expired 25D row from cutting the price, but a re-amortizing loan still quotes the pre-step payment.
- Fix: Every lender product row carries its full Reg Z payment schedule. Where a product assumes a voluntary prepayment or re-amortizes, READ_BACK speaks every scheduled step ("<pay_1> until month <n>, then <pay_2>") with the no-prepayment payment as the payment owed, and the R150 affordability row is tested against the highest step. Products whose payment depends on a tax credit the customer can't be shown to receive are excluded from on-call close.

### Round 225. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Financing terms read back before the lender's credit decision, so the commit is gated on an assumed credit tier
- Attack: The lender runs the application after the call (the design keeps underwriting with the lender). To read back "financing terms" on the call, the calculator has to assume a tier, in practice the best one ("as low as"). Customer says "yes" to $162/month at 3.99%. The lender approves them at tier C, 9.99%, $231/month, in the e-sign envelope. Either the read-back carried hypothetical terms, or the agent pulls credit mid-call, which is a hard inquiry on a cold call. The COMMIT gate certifies agreement to read-back values. When the lender owns the decision, those values can't be lender_verified at commit time. "Same menu for all" makes this worse: everyone hears the top-tier price, and lower-credit buyers, who are disproportionately protected groups, get the surprise.
- Defense: The design never lets an assumed tier reach a commit. Credit facts are system-owned: "credit band slot accepted only with verified (lender prequal) provenance, never from speech" (R90), and provenance includes lender_verified. No Needle tool sets a credit fact (R5 writer allowlists). The lint bans requesting credit consent by voice and the word "pre-qualified" (R89, R96), so no mid-call hard pull happens. If the lender's decision changes the terms, R60 applies: "conditional approval at new amount invalidates quote hash". The R134 outbox monitor refuses CONTRACT_SENT unless READ_BACK played for the same quote hash. Same menu for all (R46) means the same products are offered, not the same rate. Matched-pair tests check path divergence.
- Verdict: The design already blocks an assumed tier. Credit band is accepted only with lender-prequal provenance and never from speech (R90). The calculator takes lender prequal as an input (R79), and missing inputs mean no figure (R29). The lints ban voice credit consent and "pre-qualified" (R89/R96). If the decision later moves the terms, R60, R23 and R154(3) invalidate the quote hash and force a new read-back, so a mismatched envelope can't go out.

### Round 226. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Interconnecting forces a rate-plan switch, so savings are computed against a tariff the customer won't be on after install
- Attack: Verified usage comes from a California customer on a tiered or legacy TOU plan. The calculator prices avoided cost at that plan's rates. Under the Net Billing Tariff, solar customers must take service on an eligible TOU rate, and utilities put them on high-differential electrification rates with low midday value and an expensive 4-9pm peak. The savings fragment's baseline (current plan) and post-solar (new mandatory plan) are different tariffs. The quoted savings compare apples to a rate the customer will lose. The design says "savings only from verified usage". Usage is verified, but the rate it is multiplied by is chosen by the utility because of the sale itself. That's not a forecast (A206). It's a known, mandatory, present-day change the calculator ignores. Source: CPUC NBT decision D.22-12-056; cpuc.ca.gov/nem
- Defense: R157 already rejects a single-tariff shortcut: "The calculator must use the customer's exact tariff schedule (e.g. NBT hourly export) on verified interval data, else no figure." A tariff-filing monitor expires rows. R212 puts "a tariff_class key on every calculator row, read from the utility authorization API". Under NBT the eligible post-solar rate is a published, present-day rule, so it can be a versioned policy-table row like any other tariff. The attack is right that "exact tariff" should name both sides of the comparison, and the files don't say so.
- Verdict: R157's "customer's exact tariff schedule (e.g. NBT hourly export)" values exports under NBT. It still reads as the customer's current import schedule, but CPUC's NBT decision requires solar customers to take service on an eligible high-differential/electrification TOU rate. The design never names a separate post-solar tariff, and the Designer admits this. So the baseline and the post-solar bill can be priced on a tariff the customer will lose.
- Fix: The calculator computes two bills on verified interval data: the baseline on the current schedule, and the post-solar bill on a policy-table interconnection_tariff row (the mandated rate per utility and program). Savings = baseline minus post-solar. READ_BACK must state the rate change. If several eligible rates are possible, use the least favorable one or give no figure.

### Round 227. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Community Choice Aggregator customers: generation rates and export credits are set by the CCA, not the utility tariff the calculator uses
- Attack: Customer in Marin or San Diego gets generation service from a CCA (MCE, SDCP) and delivery from the IOU. The policy table holds IOU tariffs keyed by utility territory. The calculator values offset and exports at IOU generation rates and IOU NEM rules. The CCA has different generation rates, its own export-credit adders and an annual cash-out policy, and can change them by board vote without CPUC rate cases. Utility territory isn't the rate-setter for about a quarter of California load. Nothing in the slot store records the generation provider, and verified usage (kWh) doesn't reveal it. Every savings number for CCA customers is computed against the wrong tariff while being "certified" as calculator_output with a substantiation id.
- Defense: Savings figures already need the customer's exact tariff on verified data, else no figure (R157). The only writer for verified_usage is the utility data API or the deterministic bill extractor (R156). Bills and Green Button data for bundled-delivery CCA customers identify the generation provider and its charges. So the provider can be read from those sources and doesn't have to be guessed from territory. The real gap is that no slot stores the provider, so the calculator can default to IOU generation rates.
- Verdict: Nothing in the design records the generation provider. Calculator rows are keyed by utility and tariff_class (R212), so a CCA customer's generation rate, export adders and cash-out policy can silently default to IOU rows. "Exact tariff, else no figure" (R157) can't fire when the system doesn't know a different rate-setter exists. The Designer concedes the missing slot.
- Fix: Add a generation_provider slot, written only by the utility API or bill extractor. Key calculator rows by (delivery utility, generation provider, schedule), and add CCA rate/export/cash-out rows watched by the tariff-filing monitor. An unknown provider or missing row means no figure and a written proposal.

### Round 228. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Discount-rate households (CARE/FERA, medical baseline) get savings computed at full price, overstating benefit for the poorest customers
- Attack: Customer is on CARE, which is roughly a 30-35% discount on the electric bill, plus a lower income-graduated fixed charge. Verified usage gives kWh, and the calculator multiplies it by the standard residential rate. It reads back "$140/month savings" against a bill that really costs them about $90 at discounted rates. The loan payment is real dollars, and the "savings" are against prices the customer never paid. Medical-baseline customers get the same distortion. The rate discount isn't a usage fact, so "verified usage" doesn't fix it. The error falls entirely on low-income and medically vulnerable households, the group that A199's affordability gap and the fair-lending matched-pair tests are meant to protect. A matched-pair test passes because both pairs get the same wrong rate.
- Defense: "Savings only from verified usage" never meant usage alone. R157 requires the customer's exact tariff schedule, else no figure. R212 reads tariff_class from the utility authorization API. CARE, FERA and medical baseline are billing attributes in the same utility data, not things the caller has to tell us. R150 limits the downside: the financed payment must be ≤ the calculator-verified savings band, so an inflated savings number is exactly what that check is meant to catch. A matched-pair test passing on a shared wrong rate is a real blind spot, and I add a check that doesn't depend on pairs.
- Verdict: CARE/FERA and medical baseline are part of the customer's billed tariff schedule. At PG&E, for example, CARE customers are on distinct schedules, and the utility API reports them. R157 requires that exact schedule, else no figure, and R156 restricts the data source to the utility API or bill extractor. A calculator that multiplies by the standard residential rate already breaks an existing rule. Matched-pair tests were never the control for calculation correctness.

### Round 229. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The savings baseline includes charges solar can't offset (fixed charges, minimum bills, non-bypassable charges)
- Attack: Customer's verified bill is $210. The calculator computes offset as 100% of annual kWh, so the fragment says "your bill drops to near zero". In California, the income-graduated fixed charge adopted May 2024 (about $24/month for most customers) plus non-bypassable charges on all imports keep being billed regardless of production. Other utilities have minimum bills or grid-access charges. Year one, the customer pays the loan plus about $40-60/month in electric bills they were told would disappear. The design checks where each number comes from, not what it means. A "savings" fact assembled as bill minus production value is unsubstantiated if parts of the bill aren't volumetric. FTC s5 and the TSR forbid misrepresenting material costs. Sources: utilitydive.com/news/california-puc-approves-income-based-fixed-charge/715748/; sce.com fixed-charge FAQ
- Defense: The scenario's key line, "your bill drops to near zero", is already banned: R18's build-time banned-claims lint covers "zero bill", and the runtime lint runs on rendered replies (R29). R157 requires the exact tariff schedule, and a tariff schedule includes its fixed, minimum and non-bypassable components. R72 requires savings wording to carry calculator_output with a substantiation id, and R161's principle (a substantiation id must be backed by something real) applies here as well. Still, the files don't say outright that the post-solar bill includes non-volumetric charges.
- Verdict: The scenario's key line, "near zero bill", is already a banned claim (R18) that the runtime lint checks on rendered replies (R29). A calculator bound to the exact tariff schedule on interval data (R157) must bill fixed and non-bypassable components, which carry through both bills and can't be offset away. The design doesn't spell out the post-solar bill composition, but the existing rule already forbids the "100% offset of the bill" shortcut the attack relies on.

### Round 230. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Production is self-reported: savings need modeled kWh from roof azimuth, tilt and shading that a phone call can't verify
- Attack: QUALIFY asks "Any shade on your roof?" and the customer says "Not really". The calculator assumes standard south-facing production for the size. The roof is actually east-west split, 14° tilt, with a neighbor's oak shading it after 2pm. Real production is 30% below the estimate, so the savings and payback in the read-back are off by a third, even though usage was verified to the kWh. The design pins verified provenance on usage only. Production, the other half of every savings number, gets no slot provenance class (no "verified_design"). So a self-reported spoken "not really" feeds a certified calculator_output. Garbage in, certified fact out.
- Defense: Production isn't taken from speech. R79: "calculator inputs include roof/shade data lookup". R211(3) cross-checks shade "against aerial" data. R61: savings and sizing figures come "never from spoken estimates". R71 makes shade a qualification slot, so "not really" only routes suitability and never feeds a figure. R159 schedules the site survey inside the cancellation window and makes the contract "contingent on a survey-confirmed price", with re-disclosure, fresh e-sign and a no-penalty exit on any change. What's missing is a named provenance class for production.
- Verdict: Production doesn't come from speech. R79 puts the roof/shade data lookup into calculator inputs, R211(3) cross-checks shade against aerial data, and R61 forbids savings or sizing from spoken estimates. Shade is only a qualification slot that routes suitability (R71). R159 makes the contract contingent on a survey-confirmed price, with re-disclosure and a no-penalty exit. A spoken "not really" can't become a certified production figure.

### Round 231. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Lifetime savings and payback omit known future costs: inverter replacement, remove-and-reinstall for a reroof, insurance and property tax
- Attack: The policy table's "25-year savings" sums avoided bills minus loan payments. String inverters are typically warranted for 10-12 years, so a replacement around year 12 costs about $2-4k. QUALIFY recorded a 15-year-old roof that passed suitability, but it will need reroofing during the term, which means about $3-6k for detach-and-reset. Homeowner's insurance riders and, in some states, property tax on added value also go unmentioned. The read-back states the lifetime number as certified fact. Every cost the system has on record (roof age, equipment type, term) predicts these expenses, and the deterministic calculator ignores them. A substantiation id proves the formula ran, not that the claim is complete. An omitted material cost is deceptive under FTC s5 even when each number shown is true.
- Defense: Several of these costs are already gated. The roof case: "roof age ... qualification slots" feed the suitability table and route to ROOF_FIRST (R71). That is the row for a roof that won't outlast the term. Future-value figures must carry R157's "estimate, assumes X, not guaranteed" qualifier, paired with a zero-escalation case. R161 says who backs each warranty, and R83 requires material limitations in READ_BACK. But a lifetime savings sum that leaves out predictable costs is a completeness gap, and provenance checks can't catch it.
- Verdict: The design lets multi-year projections be spoken with R157's "estimate, assumes X" qualifier, but no rule requires a lifetime or payback figure to subtract predictable costs the system already knows about, such as inverter replacement within the term or detach-and-reset for a roof that passed suitability but won't outlast the term. A qualifier on assumptions doesn't cure a material omission. The Designer calls it a completeness gap and relies on new bans and expected-cost rows.
- Fix: Ban lifetime-savings and payback keys from phone fragments: only year-one savings on the call, multi-year figures only in the written proposal. Any multi-year figure must subtract policy-table expected_cost rows keyed on equipment type versus term (inverter replacement), remaining roof life versus term (detach-and-reset) and jurisdiction (insurance and property tax). A suitability row sends any roof whose remaining life is shorter than the term to ROOF_FIRST.

### Round 232. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Quote validity across WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE: the price table changes between the spoken read-back and a signature days later
- Attack: Tuesday: READ_BACK speaks $3.10/W, policy table v41, and the envelope is sent. Wednesday: equipment cost update ships v42 at $3.35/W, and certificates citing v41 expire. Saturday: customer signs. Which price is the contract? If the envelope froze v41, the company sells at a retired price with an expired certificate. If it regenerates, the signed price differs from the one the commit gate verified. Either way, there's a re-send question the graph never models. The design tracks status "only from vendor webhooks" and expires certificates when rows change. It has no quote-expiry date in the read-back, no binding of envelope to table version, and no re-read-back path when they diverge. The verbal-commit-to-paper link breaks exactly at a version bump.
- Defense: The contract is bound to what was read back, not to the live table. R23 says derived values are "computed from versioned inputs" and "read-back bound to config hash". R49 says accept_offer "commits only the quote hash read back". R134's outbox monitor refuses CONTRACT_SENT unless READ_BACK played for the same quote hash, so the envelope carries v41. Certificate expiry (R140, "certificates expire when a cited policy row changes") stops future calls from speaking retired rows. It doesn't void offers already made. Any price change after the read-back follows R159: new quote hash, written re-disclosure, fresh e-sign, no-penalty exit. What's missing is an explicit validity period.
- Verdict: The envelope is bound to the read-back. R49 commits only the quote hash read back, R23 ties the read-back to a config hash over versioned inputs, and the R134 outbox monitor refuses CONTRACT_SENT without READ_BACK for that same hash. So the envelope carries v41, and a later table bump doesn't rewrite it. Certificate expiry stops new calls from speaking retired rows. It isn't contract validity. Any later price change goes through R159's re-disclosure path.

### Round 233. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The verified usage history isn't the household's future load: new owners, prior occupants, planned EVs and heat pumps
- Attack: Customer bought the house 7 months ago. The utility's 12-month history is half the previous owner's (two people, no AC) and half theirs (five people, AC). Or the customer mentions "we're getting an EV next month". The calculator sizes and prices savings on the verified 12 months anyway, because only verified_usage may feed savings and the EV remark is ungrounded speech. The system ends up undersized, and the savings fragment is wrong in either direction. "Verified" means the utility confirms past kWh, not that it represents this household's next 25 years. The design forbids using spoken load changes (no provenance), so it must either quote known-wrong savings or none. Sizing rules (often 100-120% of historical usage) lock the error in at interconnection.
- Defense: The design picked the honest failure mode on purpose. Figures come from verified history (R156, R61). Sizing is "capped to the authorized meter's verified usage" (R210), so the system can't be oversized on a spoken EV plan. A smaller system is a conservative error. The claim stays true: it's savings against the household's verified past usage. The EV remark isn't lost either: customer-mentioned facts become provenance slots in the discourse register (R4), and R5's claim_prior_promise logs customer_asserted. R157's assumption-provenance type is the lawful way to talk about future load. What's missing is detecting that part of the history belongs to someone else.
- Verdict: The design deliberately chooses the conservative, truthful path. Figures come only from verified usage (R61/R156), sizing is capped to verified meter usage (R210), and spoken future loads can't upsize anything. They are only recorded as customer-mentioned provenance slots (R4), and any projection must use R157's assumption-typed qualifier. The mixed-occupant scenario is also weak, because account-holder usage authorization (R156(2)) normally returns only the current account holder's data, not the prior occupant's.


### Round 234. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Interpreters speak in the first person, so an interpreted "yes, I agree" passes the COMMIT gate while the owner never heard the read-back
- Attack: Mrs. Tran (Vietnamese, record owner) puts her bilingual grandson on speaker. He renders consecutively in the first person, as interpreters are trained to: "I understand, I'm 82, I agree." The agent's read-back is heard in English by him; what reaches Mrs. Tran is his summary ("it's cheap, sign"). At COMMIT he says "Yes." One voice, first person, no "she says": the reported-speech filter, speaker-change detector and lexical gate all pass. IDENTITY already verified the owner. The COMMIT gate's exclusions key on surface reporting ("she says") and voice change. First-person interpreting removes both markers, so the agent's evidence that the owner heard READ_BACK is a hearsay rendering from an unvetted, possibly interested, possibly minor interpreter. Nothing tells the agent that interpretation is happening.
- Defense: The grandson cannot be both the identity speaker and invisible. If Mrs. Tran answered IDENTITY, the commit "Yes" comes from a different voice, and R112 requires "the affirming speaker's diarization id to match the VERIFIED speaker at the commit gate." If he answered IDENTITY for her, then her Vietnamese on speaker is a second voice, and R148 says "any side_talk or second-voice detection during QUALIFY/READ_BACK/COMMIT means no close on this call." Her Vietnamese also trips R16's "deterministic lexicon/script detector" and mixed counter, which hand off to a per-language build or human, and confirm_order is guarded by negotiation_language. R144's action-production commit, R15's teach-back and R148's owner-only call for older owners each have to be passed too. Where audio language-ID is missing, that is a real gap.
- Verdict: The existing design blocks the interpreted close at several points that are really in design.md. R112 requires the committing voice to match the VERIFIED speaker's diarization id. R148 means no close on any second voice during QUALIFY/READ_BACK/COMMIT, so Mrs. Tran's Vietnamese on speaker blocks the close. R144 requires an action phrase plus a teach-back. For an 82-year-old, R148's human owner-only call before install would expose that the owner never understood the deal. The residual case is a grandson who answers IDENTITY himself and interprets off-mic. That is impersonation more than interpretation, and R123's per-signer ID verification and the human call still stand in front of install.

### Round 235. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Cross-lingual false friends in one-word polar answers (Polish/Czech "no" = yeah, Greek "ne" = yes, Spanish "ya" = enough)
- Attack: Polish-born owner, fluent English, answers the commit question by reflex: "No, no, dobrze" = "yeah, yeah, fine". English ASR emits "No, no, the best." The stop lexicon fires or the gate re-asks, and he leaves. Reverse case: a Greek speaker says "Ochi... ne, ne" ("no... well, yes, yes"); or a Spanish speaker's annoyed "Ya, ya, ya" ("enough!") is transcribed "Yeah yeah yeah" and passes as a short lexical affirm on a closed re-ask. The COMMIT gate and stop lexicon are English lexical tests. Code-switched discourse particles are the commonest switch and are exactly one short token, the shape the gate trusts. English ASR maps them onto English homophones with high confidence, so the confidence pre-gate and grounding gate see clean text.
- Defense: The commit no longer turns on a polar word. R144 made the commit "an action-production prompt ('To have the contract sent, please say send the contract'), not a yes/no." Neither "Ya ya ya" nor "Ne, ne" produces that phrase, so a misheard particle can't commit anything. A Polish "No, no" that hits the stop lexicon goes through R24's Tier 2 path for a bare ambiguous "no", which asks "Should I keep going, or would you rather I let you go?" That is a false stop, which costs a sale but never harms the caller. R77 has the audio-informed ambiguous_polarity class and certifies "discourse-marker idioms per dialect". R130 keeps a "dialect/code-switch allowlist", and R131 uses L1-accent strata with release-blocking per-group bounds. R16's mixed counter flags code-switching.
- Verdict: R144 turned the commit into an action-production prompt ("say 'send the contract'"), so a misheard "ya ya ya" or "ne, ne" can't commit anything. R24 treats a bare "no" as Tier 2 at most: one check question, then a polite end. A false stop costs a sale but doesn't harm the caller. R77's per-dialect discourse-marker certification and R16's mixed counter already exist.

### Round 236. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Opt-out spoken in the caller's other language ("ya no me llamen", "huwag na kayong tumawag") missed by an English lexicon and English-trained Needle
- Attack: A Spanish-dominant caller sits through the English preamble, then says "No, no, ya no me llamen, por favor." English ASR: "No no Jan oh may yeah men por favor." No lexicon match; Needle labels unclear or acknowledge, the agent clarifies once and falls back to a booked callback. The post-call DNC sweep runs the same English lexicon and Needle over the same garbled transcript, so it misses it too. The number is redialed. Since April 11, 2025 the FCC rule lets consumers revoke consent "by any reasonable means" (47 CFR 64.1200(a)(10); FCC 24-24). A plain request in the caller's own language is reasonable. Both stop paths and the backstop sweep share one English blind spot. Sources: govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2024-10-11/pdf/2024-23605.pdf; kelleydrye.com (one-year waiver note).
- Defense: Partly covered. R16's script and lexicon detector raises the mixed counter on "por favor", and a scripted bilingual handoff follows. R41 books a callback only for "the confirmed one", so a garbled turn can't confirm a time and no callback gets booked. R145's closed question, "Would you prefer we don't call again?", suppresses on anything except an explicit time request. R147 routes "a number that hit an unintelligible fallback… only to a human on its next contact". R113 suppresses on any unresolved unclear in the closing turns. The real gap is that the lexicon and sweep are English-only, so I harden both.
- Verdict: The live stop lexicon, Needle GLOBAL and the R66 post-call sweep all run on the same English ASR text. A garbled "ya no me llamen" is missed in both places, and R16 itself lists "English-only ASR anglicizing speech" as open. The defender overstates the existing backstops. R113's "unresolved unclear suppresses" applies only to the NO_RETAIN stratum. R147 sends the number to a human on next contact, and that is still a redial after a company-specific DNC request. The defense rests mainly on its NEW multilingual ASR and lexicon.
- Fix: Run an audio spoken-language-ID on every near-end segment. Any non-English or low-confidence turn gets a multilingual ASR second pass checked against a stop/revocation lexicon in the service area's top languages, both live and in the post-call sweep. Any non-English turn the system can't resolve sets suppress-pending (no redial of any kind) until a human reviews the audio. Add a Tier A foreign-language revocation red-team stratum.

### Round 237. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Captioned-telephone (IP CTS) users answer seconds-late, error-filled captions in their own voice, so no relay is detected
- Attack: A hard-of-hearing 77-year-old uses a CapTel phone: she speaks with her own voice and reads captions of the agent. Captions lag 3-5 seconds and mis-render "$31,400 total, 2.9% escalator" as "$13,400 total." She answers "yes" to the price question while the agent has already moved to the cancellation question, so her yes lands on the cancellation fragment's question id. Or she affirms a number she read wrongly. Her voice is natural, so RELAY_MODE never triggers. The design detects relays only through operator voice and GA. IP CTS is voice carry-over with machine or human captions whose delay and errors the FCC and MITRE track as known quality gaps. The COMMIT gate binds an answer to "this read-back question id" by timing. Lag rebinds the answer to the wrong question, and the read-back she "heard" is a mis-captioned one. Source: mitre.org Caption Quality Working Group report (2024).
- Defense: Caption lag already counts as path delay. R106 extends the overlap windows by the measured one-way delay, and "answers inside that guard band count as overlap and get a closed re-ask." R58 binds answers to a target question id against the start-of-turn state, and R14 enters AWAIT only after the question finishes. A late "yes" can't commit either, because R144's commit needs the spoken phrase "send the contract". A misread price is caught by R15's teach-back, where the customer states the payment and term and code compares them, and by R144's delayed teach-back. R120 offers paper or consultant-assisted signing "on any stated access need". R141 mails a summary, and R148 adds a human confirmation call.
- Verdict: One of the defender's claims is wrong: R106 measures network audio delay from RTCP and echo lag, not caption-service lag, so it does not see CapTel delay. The decisive layers do exist and hold. R144 means a late "yes" on the wrong question id can't commit, because the commit needs the spoken phrase "send the contract". R15's teach-back has the customer state the payment and term while code compares them, so a mis-captioned figure she believes produces a mismatch rather than a signed wrong deal. R148's human call and R141's mailed summary sit before install.

### Round 238. Winner: Designer
- Topic: "Speak up / slow down / say that again" has no event, and pre-rendered fixed-rate audio can't comply, so hard-of-hearing callers bluff
- Attack: An 80-year-old with presbycusis says, "Honey, you talk too fast, slow down." There is no accommodation event: Needle picks small_talk or unclear. The agent repeats the same certified audio at the same rate, pitch and level, or a "variant" that is just as fast. After the second failure he does what hard-of-hearing people commonly do, nods along and says "yes" to keep it moving. The read-back passes on clean "yeah"s. Rate, loudness and pausing aren't slots. Fragments are certified fixed audio, and "clarify once, then fallback" counts repairs, not comprehension. Clinical guidance says to rephrase and slow down for hearing loss; identical replay is the least effective repair. The vulnerability counter keys on confusion events. A bluffer gives none, so the clean yes is just what the commit gate accepts.
- Defense: A bluffer's clean "yes" does not reach a contract. R144 makes the commit an action phrase, and "a run of 3+ consecutive bare-affirm turns forces a delayed teach-back after an intervening topic." Someone nodding along can't state the payment and term that R15's teach-back compares. "Slow down" is a repair: R48's repair_request(target, kind) gives "each fragment … repeat and clarify variants", with the per-fragment repair rate tracked. R121/R111 route repeated access failures to a human with extra time or a DTMF/SMS path. R153 says "There's no need to decide today", and R148 adds the human confirmation call. The gap is real but small: the repair variants are not slower or louder.
- Verdict: The stated break, that a bluffer's clean "yeah"s pass the gate, is false against the existing design. R144 requires an action phrase and forces a delayed teach-back after 3+ consecutive bare affirms. R15's teach-back makes the caller state payment and term, which someone who didn't hear can't do. R148 adds a human call for this 80-year-old before install. R48's repair_request does exist. The defender rightly concedes that its repeat variants are not slower or louder, but that is an accessibility-quality gap, not a path to a harmful contract.

### Round 239. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Response-order (recency) effects and echolalia make the deterministic closed binary re-ask hand the last-spoken option to cognitively loaded callers
- Attack: At COMMIT an ambiguous answer triggers the certified closed re-ask: "Just so I'm clear: is that a no, or a yes?" A caller with mild cognitive impairment, or post-stroke echolalia, repeats the last word: "Yes." It is short, direct, lexical, bound to the right question id, not conditional, not rising. The gate passes. Because the fragment is deterministic, every impaired caller gets the same order, so the bias is built into the certified path. Telephone surveys reliably show recency effects in aural closed questions (Krosnick and Alwin), strongest among older and less-educated respondents. Echolalia in dementia and aphasia repeats the final word. The design's fallback for ambiguity is the very format that converts ambiguity into the last-named option, and per-branch certification will measure that as a clean commit.
- Defense: The binary re-ask no longer decides the commit. R144 made the commit an action-production prompt, so a recency-biased "Yes" to a closed re-ask is not the commit phrase. Ambiguity also adds to R15's vulnerability counter ("unclear()/contradictions -> NO_SALE_SAFE_EXIT"). A commit still has to survive R15's teach-back, where the caller produces payment and term unprompted, which echolalia can't do. R144 delays the teach-back after an intervening topic. R152's overcommitment signals and R148's owner-only call follow. Per-branch certification counts unsuitable closes as Tier A harm (R71) through the human-audited "consent understood" label (R144), so this would show up as harm, not a clean commit.
- Verdict: Under R144 the binary re-ask no longer decides the commit. A recency-biased "yes" is not the action phrase, and the close still needs R15's teach-back, where a number has to be produced, which echolalia can't supply. R15's counter also takes unclear answers and contradictions toward NO_SALE_SAFE_EXIT, and R144's "consent understood" audit label would count such closes as harm. The Breaker missed the sharper variant: R144's prompt ends with the commit phrase itself, which an echolalic caller would repeat. The teach-back still stands behind it.

### Round 240. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: The owner is under a court conservatorship or guardianship (or has a POA agent), which is invisible to the lead record, the identity challenge and the call
- Attack: A 79-year-old owner in early dementia is under a probate conservatorship of the estate. Title still shows her name, she answers the identity challenge correctly, and she is lucid for 20 minutes. She commits; the e-sign SMS goes to her phone. Or her daughter says, "I'm her power of attorney, talk to me." There is no POA event: it maps to out_of_scope or wrong-party, or the agent keeps selling to the mother. A conservatee generally can't contract for the estate. The contract is void or voidable and the lien unenforceable, yet READ_BACK and COMMIT see a valid buyer. The pre-dial gate checks deceased, DNC and complaints, not court capacity orders. An agent under a valid POA has no path, and requests from them have no event or authority model.
- Defense: This was attacked in R88: "capacity flags (conservatorship/trust/POA) from records and lender checks -> human review path." R206 and R207 add trust and estate handling with no same-call close. The daughter's "I'm her power of attorney" is a new voice, so R112's NEW_PARTY and R148's second-voice rule mean no close and a human consult. "Talk to me" is also R15's defers_decision(to), which feeds NO_SALE_SAFE_EXIT with "decision-maker-only follow-up". For a lucid conservatee alone on the line, R123 requires lender-grade ID per signer, the lender's title pull runs before the lien (R208), R141 mails a notice, and R148 adds the owner-only human call before install. Court orders missing from title data are a real residual gap.
- Verdict: R88 does name "capacity flags (conservatorship/trust/POA) from records and lender checks". But the records the design actually sources are title/record-owner and lender data, which normally don't carry probate conservatorship or guardianship orders, and the defender concedes this residual gap. So a lucid conservatee alone on the line passes IDENTITY, READ_BACK, COMMIT and R148's "anyone else asking you?" call, and signs a void or voidable contract. The POA daughter is stopped from closing by R112/R148, but an authority claim has no event, so the agent has no defined path for it. The defense for the main scenario rests on the NEW probate-index search.
- Fix: Before the envelope, run a court-records (probate conservatorship/guardianship index) search through a vendor for every record owner; a hit suppresses sales and routes to human review. Add an authority_claim(POA|conservator|guardian) GLOBAL subtype with a lexicon: no pitch and no close, routed to an AUTHORITY_REVIEW human path that verifies the instrument. The agent never resumes selling to the principal on that call.

### Round 241. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Age-dependent cancellation rights (California: 5 business days for buyers 65+) need the caller's age, which the design has no lawful slot for
- Attack: The CA profile carries "senior 5-day cancel," but which window does READ_BACK state? The agent doesn't know the age. Asking "are you over 65?" creates an age record on a credit-linked sale, and age is an ECOA prohibited basis under the "same menu for all" and matched-pair regime. Not asking means a 68-year-old hears "three business days," believes it, and misses the right she actually has. Waiting out the longest window doesn't fix a misstated disclosure. AB 2471 (2020) amended Civil Code 1689.6/1689.7 and B&P 7159 to give buyers 65+ five business days and a matching notice. The read-back must state the right that applies. The design can either misstate cancellation rights to seniors or collect age as a slot in a fair-lending-audited flow. Sources: legiscan.com/CA/text/AB2471/2019; cslb.ca.gov Warnings and Exceptions.
- Defense: No age slot is needed, and none is collected. R15 already sets the "Longest statutory cancellation window (CA 65+ 5 business days) for all", and the final plan says "install waits out the longest cancellation window." So every CA buyer gets five business days as a contractual right, and READ_BACK states five. That is true for a 68-year-old and generous to a 40-year-old, so no disclosure is misstated. R46's "prohibited-basis field tags + build invariant" bar age as an input. The one honest wrinkle is R148, which keys a human call on "owners 65+"; I remove that age dependency below.
- Verdict: R15 already sets the "longest statutory cancellation window (CA 65+ 5 business days) for all", and the final plan has install wait out the longest window. READ_BACK can therefore state five business days to every CA buyer without knowing anyone's age, and R46's prohibited-basis tags bar age as an input. The one real inconsistency is R148's "owners 65+" trigger, which implies an age source. The defender removes it.

### Round 242. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Low-literacy signers: terms that exist only in the written e-sign document (arbitration, class waiver, assignment, credit-pull authorization) never appear in READ_BACK
- Attack: A customer who reads at a 4th-grade level relies entirely on the spoken read-back, the only form of the deal he can understand. It covers product, size, cost, escalator, warranties, cancellation, lien and financing. The 40-page SMS envelope also has mandatory arbitration, a class-action waiver, assignment to an unnamed financier, a hard-pull authorization and data-sharing consents. He taps through the OTP as the agent said and signs all of it. For low-literacy buyers (about half of US adults read below a 6th-grade level, per PIAAC-based estimates), READ_BACK is the contract in practice. Its fixed field list omits the clauses that most limit the buyer's remedies. The "every commit passes READ_BACK" invariant certifies the spoken summary, not the binding text, and the gap falls on those who can't read the difference.
- Defense: Several layers already protect this buyer. R120 asks before CONTRACT_SENT whether the buyer wants "electronic documents, or paper or an in-person signing", and any stated access need goes to a consultant-assisted signing. R89 lints out credit-consent requests, and R45/R90 keep credit with the lender, so no hard-pull authorization is collected on the call. R141 mails a summary with cancel instructions, R148 adds the human confirmation call, and R153 says "no need to decide today". The attack is right that READ_BACK's field list (R85) leaves out remedy-limiting clauses. The R85 list was meant to cover every material term, so I close the gap with a coverage invariant.
- Verdict: R85's READ_BACK list (total cost, escalator, material restrictions, cancellation, lien/UCC, financing) has no invariant tying it to the actual e-sign template. Arbitration, class-action waiver, assignment and data-sharing clauses can sit in the binding document without ever being spoken. R120's paper or consultant signing triggers only on a stated access need, which low-literacy buyers rarely state. The defender concedes the gap and closes it with a NEW clause-coverage invariant.
- Fix: Give every clause of the e-sign template a policy-table key tagged material or not. A build invariant requires every material key (arbitration and opt-out, class waiver, assignment plus the FTC Holder Rule notice, credit-pull authorization, data sharing) to have a plain-language READ_BACK fragment. A template with an untagged clause, or a mismatch between the template version and the READ_BACK version, fails the release. The mailed summary repeats these clauses.

### Round 243. Winner: Designer
- Topic: "I want my son to hear this first" (a request to involve a trusted support person) has no event, so the agent proceeds alone or books a consult without them
- Attack: An 84-year-old widow says, "My son handles these things. Can you call when he's here?" That isn't stop, request_human, sensitive_event or a callback slot. Needle picks out_of_scope, or acknowledge followed by "That's great. Now, how old is your roof?" If clarification succeeds it books a callback to her number at a time with no son attached. The agent can't add a third party, can't send him materials, and can't hold the deal until he is present. Elder-financial-exploitation guidance (FINRA Rule 4512 trusted contacts, CFPB) treats a request to involve a trusted person as a protective signal to honor. The design reads it as an objection or noise, and its only "human" edges are the seller's closers. The one safeguard the elderly caller asks for herself is the one the graph can't represent.
- Defense: The design has this event. R15 defines "defers_decision(to)" as a Needle signal that feeds the counter to NO_SALE_SAFE_EXIT: "no close reachable, suppress redial, decision-maker-only follow-up." "My son handles these things" is the textbook case. Nothing in the graph can follow it with "how old is your roof". R153's "no need to decide today", R148's owner-only call before install with elder-exploitation resources, and R56's booked human consult plus written proposal give the protective path. R74 rules out any Needle tool creating an outbound contact, and R213 allows contact with others only "unless the verified caller chooses it in writing", so the son is never cold-contacted.
- Verdict: R15 really does define defers_decision(to) as a Needle signal leading to NO_SALE_SAFE_EXIT: "no close reachable, suppress redial, decision-maker-only follow-up". "My son handles these things" is the core case, so the graph can't go on to ask about the roof. R56 gives a booked human consult with a written proposal. R74 and R213 keep the son from being cold-contacted without the owner's written choice.


### Round 244. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Privacy-rights requests spoken mid-call ("delete my info", "don't sell my data", "what do you have on me?") have no event, so they get filed as DNC or out_of_scope and never start the 45-day clock
- Attack: 1) Caller: "Before anything, delete everything you have on me and don't sell my number." 2) The GLOBAL pass has no privacy_request event. It picks stop/DNC, the nearest match. 3) The DNC effect adds the number to the suppression list, which keeps the number, name and call record. 4) No deletion or opt-out-of-sale ticket is opened and no confirmation goes out. 5) Day 46: the consumer files a complaint with CalPrivacy. Every artifact from the call is still held, including the transcript, labels, augmentations and replay copies. CCPA gives 45 days to answer deletion and right-to-know requests and 15 business days for an opt-out of sale or sharing (Cal. Civ. Code 1798.130, 1798.120; 11 CCR 7022, 7026). The regulations require a business to handle a request sent by a method it did not designate, or to explain how to submit one (7020). DNC and deletion pull in opposite directions: suppression keeps the number. The event schema cannot express the request, so it is lost.
- Defense: The request is not "lost". GLOBAL is multi-label (R189): every safety event over its threshold fires, in a fixed order. So "delete my info and don't sell my number" fires stop/DNC, and that already writes the ledger. Anything unmatched falls to the root UNEXPECTED_EVENT handler and is logged (R52). Unmatched phrasings go to human review through the revocation intake bus (R115). R7 already makes deletion "a data operation, no retrain". Keeping the suppression record does not conflict with deletion. The TCPA and TSR require it, and CCPA exempts data kept for a legal obligation. The real gap: no event opens the CCPA clock.
- Verdict: The design has no event for a spoken privacy request. Multi-label GLOBAL (R189) and the root UNEXPECTED_EVENT log (R52) only make sure a DNC write happens and the turn is logged. Nothing opens a deletion or opt-out-of-sale request, starts the 45-day or 15-business-day clock, or tells the caller how to submit one, as 11 CCR 7020 requires for a request sent by a method the business did not designate. The Designer concedes that "no event opens the CCPA clock", so the defense rests on the new privacy_request event.
- Fix: Add a GLOBAL privacy_request(kind: delete, know, correct, opt_out_sale_share, limit_sensitive) event, detected by lexicon plus Needle, that co-fires with DNC and never replaces it. It plays a fixed acknowledgement fragment. Its outbox effect opens a DSAR ticket stamped with the statutory due date and sends written confirmation with submission instructions. Deletion then spares only the TSR WORM set, legal_hold and a hashed-number suppression entry. Extend the post-call sweep to privacy phrasings and add a Tier A red-team stratum.

### Round 245. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Right-to-know/access requests: the design cannot assemble the "specific pieces" and inferences it holds on a person, and cannot verify the requester to the required standard
- Attack: 1) The customer emails: "Send me every piece of personal information you hold, including inferences." 2) Their data is spread across lead records, slot stores and provenance, GLOBAL labels (sensitive_event, vulnerability counter), dialect strata, suitability outcomes, PII-surrogate transcripts, big-model paraphrases of their call, human audit labels and golden-canary audio. 3) No record-level index links a person to these artifacts. 4) Verification relies on the same lead-record facts the IDENTITY challenge uses, which a data broker could also supply. CCPA covers "inferences drawn" to build a consumer profile (1798.140(v)(1)(K)), and the right to know covers specific pieces (1798.110). A request for specific pieces needs a "reasonably high degree of certainty": three data points plus a signed declaration (11 CCR 7062). Leaving out labels such as "vulnerable" or "sensitive_event" is an incomplete response. Disclosing them to the wrong requester is itself a data leak.
- Defense: The design keeps very little about a person. Agents collect only coarse facts, never account, income or credit data (R7). There are no voiceprints (R118). Health spans are redacted, and only a type-less sensitive_exit label survives (R119). Dialect labels live only on audit records under surrogate IDs, never in CRM, and are deleted with the gold item (R131). Labeling audio is cut off from name and number and has a TTL (R143). Adverse outcomes already produce plain-language reason codes (R90 ADMT), so the inferences are enumerable.
