Verification also does not rely only on lead fields, because identity answers come from independent sources (R162). What is missing is the person-to-artifact index and a verification standard for requests.
- Verdict: The cited elements (R7, R118, R119, R131, R143, R90) do shrink what is held, but labels, outcomes, reason codes, vulnerability or sensitive_exit flags and transcripts still exist. No element maps a person to those artifacts. R162 covers identity answers in the call, not a 7062-grade verification standard for a request for specific pieces. The Designer concedes both gaps, and its answer (registry, OTP plus mailed code plus declaration) is new.
- Fix: Build a subject-artifact registry. Every written artifact carries a session id, and only the DSAR service can resolve number/lead → sessions → manifest (lead record, slots with provenance, outcome, reason codes, flags, audit labels, transcripts, derived paraphrases) through a keyed surrogate map. A request for specific pieces needs OTP to the verified number, a code mailed to the property of record and a signed declaration, and falls back to a categories response if verification fails. A release check fails any artifact type that is not registered.

### Round 246. Winner: Designer
- Topic: California Delete Act DROP: lead vendors (registered data brokers) must purge consumers every 45 days, which erases the consent proof the pre-dial gate relies on and keeps feeding DROP-deleted people into purchased lead files
- Attack: 1) The pre-dial gate passes a number because the lead vendor (a registered data broker) certified prior express written consent. 2) The consumer submits a DROP request. 3) Within 45 days the broker deletes their record, along with the consent-form capture, IP address and timestamp. 4) Another affiliate resells a stale copy, and the seller dials again. 5) The consumer sues under the TCPA. The seller has to prove consent, and the only evidence has been destroyed under state law. Starting Aug 1, 2026, registered data brokers must check DROP at least every 45 days, delete matching consumers' data and tell their service providers to delete it as well (Cal. Civ. Code 1798.99.86; privacy.ca.gov/drop-for-data-brokers/process-drop-requests). Under the TCPA the burden of proving consent falls on the caller. The design has no DROP-aware lead intake, no seller-side escrow of consent evidence and no re-suppression feed.
- Defense: The consent proof never lives only at the broker. The pre-dial gate requires a per-number PEWC artifact (timestamp, seller, disclosure version) in the seller's own ledger (R74), captured by an independent consent-capture provider (R162). The R116 TSR record set keeps "the consent artifact" for 5+ years in WORM storage. That is a legal-obligation retention, which a broker's DROP purge does not reach. A stale resold copy fails the gate if its artifact is not already in the seller's ledger. The Reassigned Numbers Database and internal DNC checks run at every dial (R73, R138). The one real gap is that a DROP request should mean "stop contacting me", and the design never hears about it.
- Verdict: The attack assumes the consent proof exists only at the broker, and the design already says otherwise. R74 requires a per-number PEWC artifact (timestamp, seller, disclosure version) at the seller's own dial gate. R162 requires an independent consent-capture provider. R116 keeps the consent artifact in the seller's WORM record set for 5+ years under a legal obligation the broker's DROP purge does not reach. A DROP deletion at a broker is not a TCPA revocation of consent given to this seller, and stale resold copies still have to pass the seller-side artifact gate.

### Round 247. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Algorithmic disgorgement: one tainted data source forces the FTC-ordered destruction of every Needle release, because each release is trained on "the union of all data" with no per-source unlearning
- Attack: 1) Seed transcripts come from earlier human-seller calls whose recording notice said "for quality and training", meaning staff training. 2) Each release finetunes Needle on the union of all data, and real data is never replaced. 3) A regulator finds that the seed audio was collected or repurposed unlawfully. 4) The remedy is to delete the data and every model and algorithm derived from it. 5) Every certified weight hash since v1 descends from it, so certificates, golden canaries and branch coverage all fail together. The FTC has ordered models deleted, not only the data: Everalbum (2021), WW/Kurbo (2022), Rite Aid (2023), Amazon Alexa (2023, voice recordings). Its Feb 2024 guidance warns that quietly widening data use to AI training can be unfair or deceptive. Union training with no data lineage means one bad source taints every release. There is no rollback target and no retraining path that has been certified.
- Defense: Disgorgement here would remove a labeler, not the product. Everything that speaks, prices, commits or complies is deterministic code. Needle only labels turns. Every state already has a deterministic fallback (callback or human, R2/R25), so destroying every weight sends calls to fallback and they keep running. Seed data is also narrow: historical calls "only seed the first graph" (R20), "teach caller intents only" (R18), and are PII-scrubbed (R7). Statistics and certification come from the disclosed agent's own calls. The pipeline already rejects transcripts that lack a retention-consent flag (R40). The real gap is per-source lineage: there is no certified model that avoids the seed data.
- Verdict: The deterministic fallback keeps calls running, but only as callbacks or humans, so the AI product itself is gone. Needle is finetuned on "the union of all data" and seed transcripts do feed intent training (R18). The design has no per-source lineage and no certified model that excludes the seed data. R7's "deletion = data operation, no retrain" removes data, not what the weights learned, which is exactly what FTC disgorgement orders target. The Designer concedes there is no clean target, so the defense rests on the new lineage manifest and clean-room variant.
- Fix: Give each release a source-lineage manifest (per-source content hashes plus a legal-basis/purpose record), and have the build reject any source without one. Train and certify a standing "clean-room" Needle on synthetic plus consented on-policy data only, as the disgorgement fallback. Require written legal review before any legacy seed source is used for AI training.

### Round 248. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Indefinite retention by design: "real data never replaced", union retraining, bit-identical replay and golden canaries make every call's personal data permanent, which violates CCPA's purpose and retention limits
- Attack: 1) A 2026 call that ended NOT_SUITABLE in 40 seconds produces audio, a transcript, labels, augmentations and audit labels. 2) Policy keeps real data in the training union forever, because deleting it would break bit-identical replay and re-certification of old bundles. 3) Ten years later, data about a non-customer is still held to retrain an AI. 4) The privacy notice has to state a retention period for each category, and "forever, for model training" is the only true answer. CCPA bars keeping personal information longer than reasonably necessary for each disclosed purpose. It also requires the notice at collection to state a retention period per category (1798.100(a)(3), (c)). The 11 CCR 7002 data-minimization rules measure processing against consumers' reasonable expectations. The certification design makes erasure impossible: removing a record breaks replay determinism, so retention is set by engineering, not by purpose.
- Defense: The attack misreads two phrases. "Real data never replaced" (R33) means synthetic data never displaces real data. It is not a promise to keep real data forever. R7 says outright that "real utterances only in deletable gold sets; deletion = data operation, no retrain". Bit-identical replay is about inference determinism on the retained set, and R135 re-keys against whatever gold set is retained, so deleting an item does not break replay. Raw audio already has a retention TTL (R143). NO_RETAIN keeps nothing (R40, R113). The TSR set has its own defined 5-year retention period (R116). What is missing is a published per-category retention schedule.
- Verdict: The attack misreads the design. R33 means real data is never replaced by synthetic data, not that it is kept forever. R7 explicitly puts real utterances in deletable gold sets, with deletion as a data operation. R135 re-keys equivalence against whatever gold set is retained, so deleting an item does not break replay. R143 sets a raw-audio TTL, R40 and R113 provide NO_RETAIN, and R116 has a defined 5-year period. Erasure is possible by design. The missing published schedule is a notice item, covered under A10.

### Round 249. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Subprocessor chain: the big-model API (live stand-in classifier during build), the ASR vendor, labeling crowd, e-sign and SMS vendors that reserve rights to use call data become "third parties", turning each data flow into an undisclosed sale or sharing and a third-party wiretap
- Attack: 1) During the build phase, live disclosed calls stream to a hosted LLM API that acts as the stand-in classifier. 2) The ASR and LLM vendors' standard terms allow use of customer data to improve their services. 3) Audit audio goes to a labeling platform, which uses subcontractors. 4) Consumers were told about recording, not about outside AI vendors listening. 5) A plaintiff sues under CIPA 631, arguing that each vendor's capability to use the audio makes it an eavesdropper. CCPA treats a vendor as a service provider only under a contract that bars use for its own purposes (1798.140(ag); 11 CCR 7050-7051). Otherwise the disclosure is a sale or sharing that needs notice and an opt-out. Ambriz v. Google (N.D. Cal. 2025) let CIPA claims against an AI contact-center vendor proceed on the vendor's capability to use the data. The certificate pins ASR versions but not vendor terms or subprocessors.
- Defense: Much of this is already closed. Transcripts get surrogates before the big model sees them (R7). "Processors [are] barred from training on data" (R40). Labeling runs in an in-house or vetted enclave with VDI, no download and watermarking (R143), not on an open crowd platform. ASR is "prefer self-hosted" (R68), and vendor embedding settings are disabled (R118). Vendor contracts already carry forwarding duties (R115). Needle runs sandboxed with no network (R135). Still, the attack is right that live build-phase audio streams to vendors, and that the certificate pins ASR versions but not vendor terms.
- Verdict: The existing design contradicts the scenario's key steps. R40 already bars processors from training on call data, the core term that makes a vendor a CCPA service provider rather than a third party. R7 applies surrogates before the big model sees transcripts. R143 confines labeling to an in-house or vetted enclave with no download, not an open subcontracted crowd. R68 pins ASR and prefers self-hosting. Ambriz v. Google is still pending on a capability theory, but it turned on vendor terms that allowed the vendor's own use, which R40 forbids here. The leftover risk is that vendor terms are not pinned with the bundle, which is a polish item.

### Round 250. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Cross-border data: offshore labeling, red-team crowd work and model vendors give "countries of concern" access to bulk U.S. voice and financial data under the DOJ Data Security Program
- Attack: 1) The audit pipeline sends thousands of call recordings and lender_verified slot data to a labeling vendor. 2) The vendor subcontracts dialect-stratum labeling to a firm that a Chinese company owns, or that has staff in a country of concern. 3) The same happens with a red-team crowd platform. 4) No one screens vendor ownership, employee locations or downstream access. 5) Within months the bulk thresholds are passed: about 1,000 people's voice biometrics and 10,000 people's financial data. 28 CFR Part 202 (effective April 8, 2025; full compliance Oct 6, 2025) restricts vendor, employment and investment agreements that give covered persons access to bulk U.S. sensitive personal data. That data includes biometric identifiers such as voice prints and patterns, at 1,000 or more U.S. persons, and personal financial data, at 10,000 or more. It requires CISA security controls, due diligence, audits and recordkeeping. Data brokerage to those parties is prohibited (federalregister.gov 2024-31486).
- Defense: Existing controls already cap exposure. Labeling is in-house or done by vetted staff in a VDI enclave, with no download, watermarking and access logs (R143). IDENTITY answers and address segments are muted, and audio is decoupled from name and number. No voiceprints or speaker embeddings are ever persisted (R118). The agent collects no account, income or credit data (R7). Credit facts come only from lender prequal (R90). Red-team work runs on generated scenarios. What is missing is formal Part 202 screening: ownership, employee location and subcontracting are not checked.
- Verdict: Under 28 CFR 202.204, "biometric identifiers" are voice prints and patterns "enrolled in a biometric system and the templates created by the system". R118 bans persisting any speaker embedding or template, so raw call audio does not count toward the 1,000-person voice threshold. The agent collects no account, income or credit data (R7). Credit facts are never spoken (R73) and exist only as lender-provenance slots (R90), so labelers, who get clipped and muted audio in a no-download enclave (R143), do not receive bulk financial data. The scenario's offshore-subcontractor route is excluded by R143's in-house or vetted labeling.

### Round 251. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Arranging solar loans makes the seller a GLBA "financial institution", which brings privacy-notice, opt-out, Safeguards Rule and 30-day FTC breach-notice duties that the compliance list never mentions
- Attack: 1) The agent quotes financed terms, sends lender signers the envelope and stores lender_verified slots. 2) In lead-gen mode it hands qualified borrowers' data to partner installers. 3) No GLBA initial privacy notice is given and no opt-out from sharing with nonaffiliated parties is offered. 4) Later, the replay store holding lender_verified data on 4,000 borrowers is exposed. 5) The company follows only state breach laws and does not notify the FTC. Businesses that arrange or broker consumer loans are "financial institutions" under the FTC's GLBA rules. That brings Reg P/16 CFR 313 notices and opt-outs before nonpublic personal information is shared with nonaffiliated parties, and the amended Safeguards Rule (16 CFR 314): a qualified individual, MFA, encryption and risk assessment. Since May 13, 2024, a notification event affecting 500 or more consumers must be reported to the FTC within 30 days of discovery. The design's financing gate covers Reg Z and ECOA only.
- Defense: The design already does much of the Safeguards work. The lender runs the application and decisions. The voice channel never takes payment (R45). Lints ban SSN, DOB and bank requests (R89). Credit facts exist only as lender-verified provenance (R90). Labeling is enclave-only with access logs (R143). Needle has no PII store access (R135). Lead-gen mode already exists (R47). But the attack is right: arranging financing likely makes the seller a GLBA financial institution, and the compliance list names only Reg Z and ECOA.
- Verdict: Arranging or brokering consumer financing plausibly makes the seller a GLBA financial institution under the FTC's rules. Information a consumer gives in order to obtain financing is NPI even without an SSN. The design's financing gate covers only Reg Z and ECOA: there is no initial privacy notice, no opt-out before NPI goes to nonaffiliated partners, no Safeguards program (Qualified Individual, MFA, encryption, risk assessment) and no 30-day FTC notification path for 500 or more consumers. The cited elements (R45, R89, R90, R143) cut down sensitive fields but do not meet these duties, and the Designer concedes the gap.
- Fix: Add a GLBA row to the regulation profile. The initial privacy notice goes in the e-sign packet and the outbox. A build invariant on the lead-gen/partner handoff effect blocks NPI sharing with nonaffiliated parties unless there is an opt-out or a 313.13 service-provider or joint-marketing contract. Stand up a 16 CFR 314 Safeguards program, keep only lender reference tokens in replay stores, and add an incident runbook with FTC notice within 30 days for 500 or more consumers.

### Round 252. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Breach scoping is impossible: no person-to-artifact data map across transcripts, PII surrogates, synthetic derivatives, audit labels, replay stores and weights, so the company cannot name the affected people or data elements within statutory deadlines
- Attack: 1) Attackers exfiltrate the training bucket: real transcripts with PII surrogates, the surrogate-to-real mapping used for audits, big-model paraphrases and the golden-canary audio. 2) State laws require notice listing the affected residents and the data elements exposed. 3) The design content-hashes bundles but has no record-level lineage. Paraphrases keep addresses and loan details that the surrogates missed. 4) Counsel cannot say whose data was exposed, which states are involved or whether biometric or financial elements are included before the deadlines run out. Several breach statutes set fixed deadlines: Colorado (CRS 6-1-716) and Washington (RCW 19.255.010) require 30 days, and Florida (501.171) 30 days. Many states, including IL, NY, WA, CO and TX, count biometric data as breach-triggering personal information. Pseudonymized data plus its key is personal information. The design's rigor covers model artifacts, not people, so the company cannot determine who to notify or by when.
- Defense: The training bucket is designed to hold little that can be notified. Surrogates go in before training (R7). Health spans are redacted (R119). No biometric templates are persisted (R118), so the voice-template element of the IL/TX/WA statutes does not apply. Audit audio is decoupled from name and number and has a TTL (R143). Dialect labels sit under surrogate IDs (R131). The TSR WORM set holds real numbers in separate storage (R116). But the attack is right that there is no record-level lineage, that paraphrases can leak details the scrubber missed, and that the surrogate map location is unspecified.
- Verdict: Minimization (R7, R118, R119, R131, R143, R116) limits what a breach exposes, but nothing maps people and data elements to artifacts. Big-model paraphrases can carry details the scrubber missed, and the design never says where the surrogate-to-real map lives or that it is kept apart from the training bucket. Counsel could not reliably list affected residents per state, or which elements were exposed, inside 30-day deadlines. The Designer concedes the lineage gap.
- Fix: Reuse the A2 subject-artifact registry, tagged with data-element classes and jurisdiction from the validated address, so a breach query returns affected residents per state. Keep the surrogate-to-real map in a separate KMS-protected vault, never co-located with training data. Re-scrub every big-model paraphrase and reject outputs with address or loan patterns. Run a yearly tabletop drill against the 30-day statutes.

### Round 253. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: No CCPA notice at collection in the PREAMBLE, while lead-gen mode silently sells the call's personal data and inferences to partner installers
- Attack: 1) The PREAMBLE says: company, sales purpose, AI, recording, opt-out, ADMT notice, anti-phishing. 2) It does not cover the categories collected (voice, usage, ownership, suitability inferences), the purposes (including AI training), whether data is sold or shared, retention, or a pointer to the privacy policy. 3) In a salesperson-registration state the agent runs lead-gen mode and passes roof, usage and qualify answers to a licensed partner for value. 4) The consumer was never told about the sale and was never offered a Do Not Sell or Share opt-out. CCPA requires notice at or before the point of collection, including for offline and phone collection, where it may be given orally (1798.100(a)-(b); 11 CCR 7012(c)(4), 7013). Selling without a notice of the right to opt out bars the sale (7013(c)-(d)). A lead handed over for referral fees is a sale. The "unskippable, model-checked" preamble certifies an incomplete disclosure set.
- Defense: The PREAMBLE is the right mechanism, and adding an element is one profile row. It is "compiled, unskippable" (R31). Its mandatory segments count only on uninterrupted playback (R39). It is built per regulation profile (R75) and already carries the recording notice, opt-out and ADMT notice (R90). Lead-gen mode is not an uncontrolled sale. The contract comes from the registered salesperson of record (R47). Outbound contacts and referrals happen only through self-opt-in (R74). The real gap: the CCPA notice-at-collection elements and a sale/share opt-out are not in the profile.
- Verdict: The PREAMBLE is the right mechanism, being compiled, unskippable, playout-marked and profile-driven (R31, R39, R75), but its certified segment set has no CCPA notice at collection: categories, purposes including AI training, sold/shared status, retention, and a policy link. CCPA requires that notice at or before collection, including orally on the phone. The weaker half of the attack is that R47 does not show a third-party lead sale (the salesperson of record may be in-house), but the missing notice alone is a gap, and the Designer concedes it.
- Fix: Add a CCPA profile row: a short oral notice-at-collection segment (categories, purposes including AI training, sold/shared yes or no, retention, link) with a lint against the 7012 element list, plus an outbox SMS with the full notice. Add a GLOBAL opt_out_sale_share event. A build invariant blocks any partner handoff unless the notice segment is playout-marked and no opt-out is set, or the partner is contracted as a service provider.


### Round 254. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Release cadence vs sample size: every content-hashed release needs ~300,000 fresh harm-free audited real calls before it can certify, so fixes cannot ship
- Attack: Week 3, auditors find a stop phrase Needle misses. The fix means a finetune on the union of all data, so a new weights hash, a new bundle and a new certificate. Tier A <= 1e-5 at 95% needs ~300,000 consecutive harm-free audited real calls on the new bundle. At 4,000 calls a day that is 75 days of real calls on an uncertified bundle, or 75 more days on the known-bad bundle. Next week a policy-table fix triggers another reset. Rule of three: zero events in n trials gives a 95% upper bound of about 3/n, so 1e-5 needs n of about 300,000 per release. Whole-call audits at ~8 minutes each come to ~40,000 listening hours per release. Certification only on real calls means each fix is first exposed, uncertified, to the public it is meant to protect.
- Defense: Certificates are per state, not per bundle: "certificate = (state, weights hash, facts-table version)" (R3), and under R135 "a patched runtime inherits a state's certificate if, on that state's full retained gold set plus canary corpus, outputs are bitwise identical"; only diverging states recertify and "run on fallback meanwhile". So nothing uncertified ever faces the public. A missed stop phrase is fixed the same day without a finetune: "Stop = union of deterministic lexicon (incl. dialect patterns) and Needle GLOBAL", and the "post-call DNC sweep of every transcript before any redial" catches what slips through. GLOBAL is "versioned separately" (R34), so a stop fix never touches LOCAL certificates. Adding to a union can only add stops, never remove one. That is a one-way change for harm.
- Verdict: The design already fails safe on slow certification: "states that can't reach the bound stay on deterministic fallback", R124's ramp audits 100% of the canary cohort, and R66 rollback plus R132 alarms pull a known-bad state back to fallback instead of leaving it live. The scenario's own trigger, a missed stop phrase, has a same-day fix that needs no finetune: stop = lexicon ∪ GLOBAL (R66, R93), plus the post-call sweep. The certification cost is real, but it shows up as fallback time, not as uncertified exposure or a stuck known-bad bundle.

### Round 255. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Per-group release blockers at low volume: small dialect/accent groups can never reach the bound, so the release stalls or those groups get quietly dropped from the dial list
- Attack: Gullah-Geechee, Vietnamese-accented and Navajo-English speakers are each under 1% of dialed calls. Each per-group Tier A bound needs ~300,000 audited calls from that group, which means 30M+ total calls per release. The release can never ship. Ops "fixes" it by excluding those ZIP codes and surname-matched leads from campaigns until volume allows. The certified agent now serves only well-represented accents. The blocker is unreachable by arithmetic (3/n per group). The workaround is marketing-avoidance redlining by national-origin proxy, which DOJ/CFPB redlining settlements have treated as illegal. Here a credit product is offered, so ECOA/Reg B applies. Statistical power, not the model, decides who gets offered solar.
- Defense: Exclusion by ZIP or surname is already illegal inside this design. R46's "product-policy table (all options to all, non-protected keys only); prohibited-basis field tags + build invariant" fails any selector keyed on a proxy. A group that cannot yet reach its bound is not dropped: "states that can't reach the bound stay on deterministic fallback (callback or human)", and R121 and R147 send repeated-unintelligible calls to a human, never out of the campaign. R131 already admits that group labels are not runtime inputs. It uses a "label-free worst-group check" over acoustic and ASR-confidence clusters, which has power because clusters are large. Small groups also feed R147's consented strata.
- Verdict: The design makes per-dialect bounds (R93) and per-disability bounds (R147) release blockers, and R131 holds the worst cluster "to the same bound". At 1e-5 per call, small groups cannot reach that bound, so the release is blocked by arithmetic. The runtime cannot route by group because R131 keeps group labels out of runtime. R46's prohibited-basis invariant covers the product-policy table, not dial-list or campaign selectors, so the ZIP or surname exclusion workaround is not blocked by anything existing. The defense's working parts (blocker redefinition, selector invariant) are new.
- Fix: Redefine per-group release blockers as the pooled absolute call-level bound plus per-group non-inferiority tests on turn-level Tier A rates, with a stated power analysis. A group with too little evidence gets acoustic-condition routing (low ASR confidence or worst cluster goes to a human), never exclusion. Extend the prohibited-basis build invariant to every campaign and dial-list selector (only consent, DNC, registration and suitability keys may filter), and add a reach-parity report comparing dialed share with the consent pool by census tract.

### Round 256. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Fair-lending matched-pair tests have no statistical power at campaign volume, and paired scripts cannot see outcome gaps that real-call error rates create
- Attack: Matched-pair tests send identical scripted profiles through the graph. Same menu, pass. On real calls, accented callers hit clarify-then-fallback more often, get booked callbacks instead of quotes, and close at 3% vs 5%. Detecting a 2-point gap per protected basis, per state, with outcomes joined weeks later, needs thousands of real outcomes per cell. Most cells get dozens a quarter. Nothing ever alarms. Disparate-impact liability turns on outcomes, not menus (ECOA effects test, CFPB exam practice). A two-proportion test at 5% vs 3% with 80% power needs roughly 1,500+ per arm per cell. The design's fairness evidence (same menu plus paired tests) is structurally blind to its largest disparity source, ASR/clarify routing.
- Defense: The mechanism the attack names, ASR and clarify routing, is already measured at high base rates, where power exists. R111 makes "per-group false-unintelligible, false-vulnerability and fallback rates release blockers". R121 tracks "Reached quote or human" parity, and R131 holds the worst acoustic cluster to the same bound. Fallback is not a dead end. It is a "warm transfer or booked consult" (R56) in which a human quotes from the same R46 product-policy table. So "callback instead of quote" is a delay, not a different menu. Matched pairs test the menu, and process rates test the routing. Outcome-level fair-lending tests belong to the lender, who "runs application and decisions".
- Verdict: The disparity source the attack names, ASR and clarify routing, is already measured where the base rates give power: R111 makes per-group false-unintelligible and fallback rates release blockers, R121 tracks "reached quote or human" parity, and R131 holds the worst acoustic cluster to the bound. Fallback leads to a human quoting from the same R46 menu (R56), so routing changes timing, not the offer. Matched pairs test the menu and process rates test the routing, which together cover what close-rate tests would detect too late.

### Round 257. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Human labelers' own miss rate puts a floor on the measurable harm rate; certifying 1e-5 needs a known labeler recall, meaning seeded gold harms and repeated independent listens that multiply audit cost
- Attack: Labelers listen to full 10-minute calls, hundreds a week. Vigilance fades. A quiet "please don't call again" at minute 7 under crosstalk is missed 1 time in 20. The audited bound says 0 harms in 300,000. The true rate is unknown, because labeled harm equals true harm times recall, and recall was never measured. Measuring recall means injecting thousands of seeded harm calls and having 2-3 independent labelers per call. A 95% bound on labeled harm is not a bound on true harm unless recall is bounded too. Human inspection miss rates for rare defects are commonly 10-30% (vigilance-decrement literature). Fixing that means redundant listening, which at least doubles the already huge per-release audit bill.
- Defense: Labelers are not doing a vigil over 10-minute calls. R143 gives them "only the clipped turns under audit", and the queue is "all disagreements + random agreements" (R2), so attention goes where harm is likely. Human labels are also not the only detector. The "post-call DNC sweep (lexicon + GLOBAL re-run)" runs on 100% of calls, R132's label-free monitors run on 100% of traffic, and R113 bounds harm "with outcome proxies (later complaints, repeat DNC requests, rescissions)". R131 already measures inter-rater agreement with "two trained auditors" and corrects with the confusion matrix. A 1-in-20 human miss must also coincide with misses by the lexicon, GLOBAL and the downstream channels.
- Verdict: The attack says labeler recall "was never measured", but it is: R200 mixes planted known-answer calls, including correct stops, blind into every labeler's queue and drops labels from labelers who fail; R281 double-labels a random share of Tier A blind with adjudication; R131 corrects estimates with a two-auditor confusion matrix. Labelers hear clipped turns, not 10-minute vigils (R143), and the 100% post-call sweep (R66) and outcome proxies (R113) are independent detectors. The defense cited weaker items and missed R200/R281, but the design holds.

### Round 258. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Certificate-expiry storms: lender rates and tariffs change weekly, every citing state expires at once, and fixed audit throughput can never re-certify financing states before the next change
- Attack: The lender reprices APRs every Monday with Treasury yields. Each Monday's new financing rows expire every certificate that cites them: QUOTE, READ_BACK, COMMIT and the financing branches. All drop to deterministic fallback (callback/human) at once. Recertifying needs hundreds of thousands of audited calls. The next repricing lands in 7 days. Financing states run uncertified on fallback permanently, flooding the human queue. "Certificates expire when a cited policy row changes" makes certificate lifetime equal row-change frequency. Audit throughput is fixed by labeler hours. When rows change faster than audits finish (rates, utility tariffs, capped incentives), the revenue path is never certified, and fallback cost scales with every financed call.
- Defense: A rate change is a value change, not a decision change. "Fragments hold no literal numbers; every fact token is a table key" (R91). Needle's input is the caller turn plus a tool schema, and no tool sets price or credit facts (writer allowlists). A new APR row therefore leaves every Needle output and every transition bitwise identical, and R135 re-keying lets states inherit when "outputs are bitwise identical" on gold replay. What does depend on the value is deterministic: Reg Z lints, the calculator, and the R105 "pre-render every fact-bearing clip... pinned-ASR round-trip check". All of these run in minutes. Lender terms come from the "versioned lender rate sheet" (R32), and the lender decides.
- Verdict: The design says plainly that "changing a row decertifies every branch that cites it" and "certificates expire and must be re-earned". It has no exemption for a value-only change. R135's bitwise-identical inheritance is written for patched runtimes, and R140/R155 do not change expiry. A weekly lender repricing therefore expires QUOTE, READ_BACK and COMMIT every week. The defense's working mechanism, typed value-only rows that re-validate without audit, is new.
- Fix: Type every policy-row change. A value-only change (same key, unit and authored spoken form, value inside the row's declared certified range, no new Reg Z trigger term) re-validates automatically through lints, calculator property tests, the R105 audio round-trip and bitwise gold replay, with no new audited calls. A structural change (new key, new wording, a value outside the range, a new trigger term) expires the certificate and needs audited recertification.

### Round 259. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The mandatory preamble produces mass short-duration hang-ups, and carrier analytics read those as spam, so "Spam Likely" labels crash answer rates and number rotation makes it worse
- Attack: The callee answers, hears "This is an AI calling on behalf of..." and hangs up at 8-15 seconds. That happens 60% of the time. Hiya, TNS and First Orion score numbers on volume, low answer rate and short average duration. Within days the numbers show "Spam Likely" on T-Mobile and AT&T. Answer rate halves, so dials per sale double, which worsens the same signals. Rotating fresh numbers ("snowshoeing") is itself flagged and resets branded-caller-ID vetting. Short call duration and low answer rate are documented reputation signals (Telnyx number-reputation guide; Hiya Connect). A-level STIR/SHAKEN attestation does not prevent analytics labeling. The compliant, unskippable disclosure is exactly the pattern that kills reach, which breaks the unit economics.
- Defense: The 60% hang-up premise belongs to cold lists. This dial list is "limited to prior-express-written-consent and inbound/warm leads" (R59), and the consent names "seller + AI voice", so the callee agreed to exactly this call. They see "branded caller ID + A-level STIR/SHAKEN + Free Caller Registry" before answering. R142 enforces "pacing 1:1 by default, with no overdial", and frequency caps keep per-number volume low, which is what analytics engines score. R59 already runs "answer-rate/spam-label monitoring per number". The preamble opens with the company and purpose, so the expected caller identifies itself first. Disclosure is a legal floor, and a design that skipped it to save reach would be the real failure.
- Verdict: The 60% preamble hang-up premise fits cold lists. This list is consent-only, the consent names the seller and an AI voice (R59), and the call shows branded caller ID with A-level attestation. R142 keeps pacing at 1:1 with frequency caps, which keeps per-number volume low, and R59 already monitors answer rate and spam labels per number. The design never rotates numbers, and R170 suppresses a lead after two preamble hang-ups, which cuts repeat short calls. The disclosure itself is a legal floor.

### Round 260. Winner: Designer
- Topic: A2P 10DLC daily caps and per-second throughput throttle the e-sign OTP SMS at the evening peak, so closes die while the customer waits on the line
- Attack: The new solar brand has a low 10DLC trust score. T-Mobile caps it at 2,000 messages a day. Each close sends an envelope plus OTP to every record owner and lender signer, plus outbox copies (DNC policy, callbacks). By 7pm the cap is spent. OTPs are rejected or queued past expiry. Customers say "nothing came," and WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE times out. Carriers also filter link-bearing financing texts. T-Mobile enforces brand-level daily caps tied to trust score, as low as 2,000/day (Twilio support; Infobip 10DLC reference), and per-campaign MPS limits. Peak closes cluster in the legal evening window. The certified commit path depends on a throttled third-party channel whose capacity the graph never models.
- Defense: The design does not send these messages over a low-trust 10DLC brand. R89 sends "outbox text from registered short code", and R115 names "SMS short codes" as the channel, which are not subject to T-Mobile's 10DLC brand daily caps. Volume is small: only commits send envelopes, a few percent of calls. A miss is not a lost sale. WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE has "timer and handlers for BOOKED/CONFLICT/TIMEOUT/ERROR" (R55), "help_with_screen offers resend/wait/callback" (R60), the state is SALE_PENDING, and R120 offers "mailed or consultant-assisted signing". DNC-policy copies already have email and "print-and-mail API" routes (R84).
- Verdict: The design sends its outbox texts from a registered short code (R89; short codes named as a channel in R115). Short codes are not under T-Mobile's 10DLC brand daily caps, and only commits send envelopes, a small share of calls. A delivery failure is a delay with modeled exits, not a lost or harmful close: WAITING_FOR substates with TIMEOUT handlers (R55), resend/wait/callback (R60), mailed or consultant signing (R120) and per-number SMS budgets (R196).

### Round 261. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Vendor-outage recovery flush: when the SMS/e-sign vendor comes back, the outbox drains hours of queued sends at midnight, outside texting hours and long after the calls
- Attack: The SMS aggregator is down 6:30-11:45pm Eastern during peak. Committed turns keep queuing effects in the outbox: envelopes, OTPs, DNC-policy copies, callback confirmations. At 11:45 the vendor recovers. The outbox drains thousands of texts at midnight, some duplicated by retries. Florida and Oklahoma recipients get solicitation texts at 12:05am, and OTP envelopes for calls they ended hours earlier. TCPA calling-hour limits apply to texts. Florida's FTSA limits commercial texts to 8am-8pm. The calling-hours gate sits on the dialer, not on outbox drains. "Drained only on committed turns" says when effects are released, not when they may lawfully arrive. Outage size scales the violation count.
- Defense: Most of the flush is already blocked. R55 "holds + idempotency keys" stop duplicate retries. R122 makes registration "a pre-dial and outbox guard, with texts counted as solicitation". R149 "extend[s] the consent gate to every outbound SMS and email". R139 pins tzdata and fails closed on clock uncertainty. OTPs have a TTL bound to the session, and the session's WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE has already timed out (R55), so a stale OTP has no live state to serve. The gap is real in one respect: the calling-hours check sits on the dial gate, not at outbox send time.
- Verdict: No existing element checks texting hours when the outbox drains. The defense itself says "the calling-hours check sits on the dial gate, not at outbox send time". R122 and R149 put registration and consent on outbound texts, R41 clamps callback booking times, and R55 stops duplicates, but none of them stops a midnight flush of solicitation texts to Florida (FTSA 8am-8pm, texts included) after a vendor outage. The working fix is new.
- Fix: Give every outbox SMS and email effect a [not_before, not_after] window, computed at enqueue by the pinned-tz calling-hours gate for the most restrictive candidate jurisdiction, and re-check it in the executor at send time. Add a model-checked invariant that no message effect is released outside its window. Never send expired session-bound items (OTP, envelope) late; re-plan them as one next-day, in-hours notice or a human callback. Rate-limit the drain after an outage and deduplicate it by idempotency key.

### Round 262. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Reassigned Numbers Database at scale: per-query cost on every dial attempt, "no data" responses that freeze whole lead segments, and RND downtime halting the campaign
- Attack: Low answer rates mean ~15 attempts per contact, and each attempt must re-query the RND for safe harbor. Query volume (and subscription tier) multiplies by 15. Numbers whose carriers have not reported, or whose consent date falls before RND coverage, return "no data," so they are never dialed and the paid leads are wasted. During an RND maintenance window or outage at 6pm, every query fails. "No data = no dial" stops the fleet in prime time. The FCC says a "no data" result gives no safe harbor (2021 supplemental guidance). RND data starts January 27, 2021, and access is a paid subscription tiered by query volume. A single-administrator dependency on the pre-dial gate becomes a hard availability and cost ceiling that grows with dial volume.
- Defense: "'No data' = no dial" is deliberate fail-closed design, consistent with R114 ("do not dial when a list is stale or unavailable"). The volume math assumes 15 blind attempts. R142's 1:1 pacing, frequency caps and consent-only warm leads (R59) keep attempts per contact low. Pre-2021 consent dates are not a cost of this design: leads with consent that old fail the consent ledger's freshness requirement anyway. RND data changes only when carriers report, so an answer is valid for one data release, not for one dial. The dial permit (R138) needs a current answer, not a live query.
- Verdict: "No data = no dial" is a deliberate fail-closed choice, matching R114. Its cost is availability and spend, not consumer harm. Nothing in the design requires a live query on every attempt. Safe harbor needs a check against the most recent RND data, so an answer stays valid for the current data release, and R142 pacing, frequency caps and the consent-only list (R59) keep attempts per contact low. Leads with no data can still reach the agent through INBOUND_OPEN (R30).

### Round 263. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Correlated fallback surges at peak overwhelm human warm-transfer capacity: every uncertain path ends at a human, and the human floor must be staffed for the worst evening
- Attack: Clarify-once-then-fallback, uncertified states, request_human and booked consults all end at a human closer. At 7pm Central, a codec change or storm noise raises ASR errors fleet-wide. Fallbacks jump from 8% to 35% of calls within minutes. Warm transfers queue. Callers who just asked for a person hear hold audio, and many hang up. To prevent this, ops must staff closers for peak in each time zone's narrow window. Queueing (Erlang C) makes wait times blow up nonlinearly as occupancy nears 100%. Fallback demand is bursty and correlated, not independent. Staffing for peak recreates a human call center's labor cost, which erases the bot's savings, and understaffing breaks the promised warm transfer exactly when the agent is least reliable.
- Defense: Outbound means the operator controls arrivals, which is unlike the inbound call centers Erlang C usually models. R142 already ties every "dial permit" to "a reserved Needle capacity token from live fleet health", so new dials stop first and in-flight calls keep priority. Fallback is "callback or human", and R56 transfers warm only "if open, else book consultant call + send written proposal". A fleet-wide ASR shift is caught by R137 (channel class in the certificate key, uncertified class goes to callback fallback), by R132's 100% drift monitors, and by the daily golden-audio canary. The surge becomes bookings, not hold queues.
- Verdict: Most fallback in a noise storm never reaches a live queue. R169 ends unintelligible calls with no booking, R137 sends uncertified channel classes to callback fallback, and R56 transfers warm only to an open queue and otherwise books a consult. Because the operator controls outbound arrivals, R142 admission control and R132 drift alarms can stop new dials. The real gap is that the dial permit reserves Needle capacity but not human capacity. That is a valid optimization, but nothing breaks without it.


### Round 264. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Industry traceback: claiming A-level STIR/SHAKEN makes the seller an originating provider with 24-hour traceback, KYC and Robocall Mitigation Database duties the design never models
- Attack: A recipient reports one AI call as illegal. The Industry Traceback Group (ITG) traces it hop by hop to the originating carrier that signed it A-level and asks within 24 hours: who is the customer, what was the consent, show the call. The design keeps the consent in a lead record, the audio under a surrogate ID, and the bundle hash. Nothing links a SIP Call-ID or SHAKEN origination ID to those. The deadline passes. The carrier sends a cease-and-desist and cuts all of the seller's traffic, service lines included. Under 47 CFR 64.1200(n)(1), providers must answer traceback within 24 hours. The FCC has removed providers from the Robocall Mitigation Database for missing ITG requests (SK Teleco, tcpaworld.com, May 2026), and downstream carriers must then block them. A-level attestation means a provider vouches for the caller. The design certifies each call but has no call-ID-to-evidence index, no traceback responder and no RMD/KYC obligations.
- Defense: The seller is not the originating provider. Its carrier signs, and the design already keeps what a traceback asks for. R116 keeps a "TSR record set kept for 5 years or more in WORM storage" holding the CDR (real number, time, duration, disposition, caller ID), the consent artifact, the per-call render log and the rendered agent-side audio. An ITG request gives number + time + caller ID, which index straight into that CDR. R74's PEWC artifact (timestamp, seller, disclosure version) answers "what consent". R59 already runs branded caller ID, A-level STIR/SHAKEN and Free Caller Registry. What is missing is speed and a direct ID join, not the evidence.
- Verdict: The attack's premise is weak: a seller placing calls through a carrier is that carrier's customer, not the originating provider, and the 24-hour duty under 64.1200(n)(1) falls on the provider, which then asks the seller. An ITG request arrives as number + time + caller ID, and the existing R116 WORM record set (CDR with real number, time, caller ID, plus consent artifact, render log and agent audio) already answers it from that key, with R74's PEWC artifact answering "what consent". What is missing is an on-call process and a Call-ID join, which is operational speed, not missing evidence.

### Round 265. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Determinism hands plaintiffs class certification: one compiled script and bit-identical replay turn any single defect into a common question for every call
- Attack: A TCPA/TSR plaintiff finds one flaw: a preamble wording omits the opt-out mechanism, or the compiled graph re-pitches after "I'm good". Discovery gets the content-hashed bundle and the dialer logs. The plaintiff's expert replays the certified graph and shows bit for bit that every call on that bundle in that state behaved the same. Commonality and predominance under Rule 23 are now proved by the defendant's own CI. There are 2 million calls at $500 to $1,500 each. Human reps' variability is the usual defense against predominance: individual issues defeat a class. This design removes that defense on purpose. "Same menu for all", one fragment table and replay that the design itself guarantees mean one error is a classwide fact. TCPA damages are statutory per call (47 USC 227(b)(3)), so exposure grows with fleet size, not with harm.
- Defense: Determinism cuts both ways. The plaintiff can bound the class, and so can the defense. R67 stamps every session with its bundle content hash, so a defect is limited to calls on that bundle and that state path, not the whole fleet. R124's staged ramp (shadow, then a 100%-audited canary cohort) keeps new bundles small until proven. Consent, the usual TCPA class battleground, is individual and documented per number (R74). Re-pitch after "I'm good" depends on how each caller's own words were classified, which is individual. Human variability is not a defense worth having: it is where violations come from. One fixed, counsel-signed script gives one defensible answer.
- Verdict: Scripted and prerecorded telemarketing is already routinely treated as a common question, so determinism does not create the class exposure; it bounds it. R67 stamps every session with its bundle hash, so any defect is confined to the calls on that bundle and path, and R124's audited canary ramp, R21's model-checked "no pitch after decline" invariant and R18's compliance sign-off attack the defect itself. Consent, the usual TCPA battleground, stays individual and documented per number (R74).

### Round 266. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The written harm budget is discoverable proof of knowing violations: 1e-5 Tier A "accepted harm", open items and red-team logs make violations willful
- Attack: An AG Civil Investigative Demand (CID) or class-action discovery gets the certification dossier. It holds "Tier A harm <= 1e-5 per call", per-state rule-of-three bounds, the open-items list ("missed voice stop caught post-call, not instantly") and the red-team corpus that lists known failure modes. Counsel adds it up: at 3M calls a year, management approved about 30 Tier A harms a year in writing. No lawyer wrote any of it, so none of it is privileged. TCPA damages triple for "willful or knowing" violations (227(b)(3)). FTC civil penalties under s5(m)(1)(A) require actual or implied knowledge, and the design writes that knowledge down per release. An engineering harm budget reads in court as a priced decision to break the law at a set rate, and it removes any good-faith or isolated-error defense.
- Defense: The budget is not a quota of approved harm. It is a 95% upper confidence bound on undetected harm, with "breach -> rollback to previous certified artifact" (R66). States that can't show it stay on deterministic fallback (R2). Every detected Tier A event gets remediated: the post-call DNC sweep before any redial (R66), the R132 audit surge and fallback. Written procedures, monitoring and correction are what the TCPA DNC safe harbor (64.1200(c)(2)) and FTC good-faith analysis reward. A company with no measurement has the same knowledge that errors happen, minus the proof that it fought them. R126 publishes only the corrected claim, and R113 states the coverage gaps openly, which reads as candour, not concealment.
- Verdict: Every real compliance process has a residual error rate; the design's figure is a 95% upper bound on undetected harm paired with rollback on breach (R66), fallback for states that can't meet it (R2), the post-call DNC sweep before any redial and R132 audit surges, which is the written-procedures-plus-correction posture the DNC safe harbor and good-faith analysis reward. The documents show active detection and remediation, not a priced decision to violate.

### Round 267. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Algorithmic disgorgement: a regulator orders destruction of every model trained on unlawfully obtained call data, and "union of all data, never replaced" leaves nothing that can be saved
- Attack: An investigation finds that part of the seed transcripts came from calls with defective consent or recording notice (a lead vendor, a CIPA state). The FTC or an AG demands deletion of the data and of "any models or algorithms derived" from it. Needle is finetuned each release on the union of all data. Big-model paraphrases and red-team items were generated from those seeds. Every certified release, its certificates and the shadow labels inherit the taint. There is no lineage to cut out. The FTC has ordered model deletion in Everalbum (2021), WW/Kurbo (2022) and Rite Aid (2023). The design never removes real data, mixes everything into one set of shared weights and builds its synthetic data from the seeds, so no data-lineage ledger can prove that a release is clean. One tainted source can force deletion of the whole runtime, and with it the certification record that took years of audited calls to build.
- Defense: The design already makes deletion a data operation and keeps behaviour out of the weights. R79: "policy is authored, only perception learned". Graph, fragments, policy table and calculator are authored code, not derived from seeds, so a deletion order reaches Needle's weights only. R7: "deletion = data operation, no retrain" for gold sets. R40: the build pipeline rejects transcripts lacking a retention-consent flag. R20: historical calls only seed the first graph. Certification evidence comes from the agent's own consented, gate-checked calls (R74, R124), not vendor seeds. "Real data never replaced" means not swapped for synthetic, not undeletable. R129 already tracks generator-prompt lineage, and the fallback path (R2) keeps calls lawful while Needle is retrained.
- Verdict: R79 does keep the graph, fragments and policy table out of the blast radius, but Needle's weights are trained on the union of seeds, big-model paraphrases derived from them and real calls, and nothing in the existing design records which training items descend from which source. R7's "deletion = data operation" covers gold sets, not weights; R129's lineage check guards eval contamination, not provenance; R40's consent flag does not catch consent that was defective at the source. Certificates are keyed on the weights hash (R53), so a forced deletion wipes the certified runtime with no way to prove a retrained release is clean; the defense's lineage ledger, taint drill and clean-room model are all new.
- Fix: Add a training-data lineage ledger (every item, real or synthetic, carries source id, consent-artifact id and parent seed hashes; every release manifest lists its item-hash set), a per-release taint drill that drops one source plus its transitive closure, retrains and replays gold sets within a set time, and a standby clean-room Needle trained only on first-party consented on-policy calls, with certification evidence kept only from those calls.

### Round 268. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Party-specific consent orders, AVCs and telemarketing bans bind the seller, its lead vendors and anyone "in active concert", but the regulation profile is keyed only by jurisdiction
- Attack: The seller settles an AG investigation with an Assurance of Voluntary Compliance (AVC). Its terms: verbatim disclosures, no calls to people 75 or older, a third-party monitor who pre-approves every script change, and recordings kept for every call. A lead vendor is under its own FTC telemarketing ban. The "most restrictive jurisdiction wins" profile has no row type for "this seller, by order". Weekly fragment releases and Needle retrains go out without the monitor's sign-off, and leads from the banned vendor still pass the pre-dial gate. Consent orders bind successors and "persons in active concert or participation" with notice. Contempt and order-violation penalties are much larger than first-violation exposure. The design's compliance model is geographic, and its release pipeline (certify, then ship) has no outside approval step and no check of vendors or principals against orders.
- Defense: The regulation profile is not purely geographic. Its rows are facts-table entries, and R122 already adds a party-scoped seller_registration row used "as a pre-dial and outbox guard" with a model-checked invariant. R162 tracks "per-partner fraud metrics with auto-suspension" and uses an independent consent-capture provider, so lead sources are already individually gated. R18 makes fragments cite "compliance sign-off" with "template changes decertify", so an approval step exists in the pipeline. An order's terms (verbatim disclosures, retention) map onto existing mechanisms: fixed compiled fragments (R31) and WORM records (R116). The gap is a row type for orders and an outside signer.
- Verdict: The existing hooks are adjacent but do not cover this: R122 is a per-state telemarketer registration row, R162 suspends partners on fraud metrics rather than legal orders, and R18's sign-off is internal with no outside approver. Nothing in the design can express "this seller, by order" terms such as an age ban (which the data-minimised lead record can't even evaluate), monitor pre-approval of releases, or screening of vendors under their own bans; the defense itself names the order row type and outside signer as the gap and supplies them only as new hardening.
- Fix: Add party-scoped obligation_order rows (seller, vendor, principal, successor) whose terms compile into pre-dial predicates, lints and retention rules under most-restrictive-wins; make the release pipeline require a monitor's signing key whenever an active order demands pre-approval; screen vendors and principals against FTC/AG orders at onboarding and weekly, check each lead's source id at dial, and fail closed on age-limited orders when record-sourced DOB is missing.

### Round 269. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Consent the gate checks today cannot be proved in court years later: the burden is on the caller, limitation periods outlast vendor-held consent evidence
- Attack: The pre-dial gate checks a flag: "prior express written consent naming seller + AI voice". The proof (form page, disclosure text, timestamp, IP, session replay certificate) sits with the lead vendor or a certificate service under its retention rules. Three years later a TCPA class action is filed within the 4-year federal limitations period. The vendor is gone or the certificates have expired. The seller has the flag and nothing else. The FCC puts the burden of proving consent on the caller. The design gates on consent status but never preserves the evidence under its own control for the limitation period, and it never ties the exact consent text to each dialed call ID. A "lead-vendor consent audit" listed as an open item does nothing for calls already made. Without proof, every call counts as made without consent.
- Defense: The premise is wrong. The seller already holds the consent proof under its own control. R116 places "the consent artifact" in the seller's WORM TSR record set "for 5 years or more", which outlasts the 4-year federal limitations period, joined to each call's CDR. R74 requires a "PEWC artifact from that subscriber: timestamp, seller, disclosure version" before any dial, not just a flag. R162 adds "an independent consent-capture provider". R117's legal hold freezes deletion once a dispute appears. The burden of proof is met by artifacts the seller owns, tied per call.
- Verdict: The premise that the seller holds only a flag is false: R74 requires a per-number PEWC artifact (timestamp, seller, disclosure version) before any dial, and R116 keeps that consent artifact in seller-controlled WORM storage for 5+ years, joined to each call's CDR, which outlasts the 4-year federal limitations period. R117's legal hold freezes deletion once a dispute appears, and R162 adds an independent consent-capture provider.

### Round 270. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Publishing "Tier A harm <= 1e-5" as a performance figure is an AI accuracy claim of the kind state AGs prosecute, and Texas TRAIGA lets the AG demand its basis
- Attack: Sales decks, lender due diligence and regulator responses cite the certification: "99.999% compliant, human-audited". The Texas AG opens a TRAIGA investigation. Its CID asks for the training data used, the metrics, the known limitations and post-deployment monitoring. The figure rests on the seller's own harm taxonomy, sampled audits, inferred dialect groups and labels that may never arrive. The AG treats it as a deceptive representation. In 2024 the Texas AG settled with Pieces Technologies over a claimed hallucination rate below 0.001%, which it called deceptive because the metric's basis was misstated (hklaw.com; orrick.com, Sept 2024). TRAIGA, effective January 2026, gives the AG CID power to demand training-data descriptions, metrics, limitations and safeguards. The design's whole case for safety is one rate it defines and measures itself.
- Defense: Pieces was punished for misstating a metric's basis. This design is built around stating the basis. R126: "The published claim is the corrected one" (family-wise alpha, a fresh confirmatory sample used once). R113 says to "state the coverage gap explicitly in the certification report". R131 corrects per-group estimates with a measured confusion matrix. Facts carry substantiation ids (R92), and the banned-claims lint (R18) keeps any "99.999% compliant" style claim out of what the agent says. Everything a TRAIGA CID asks for (training data, metrics, limitations, monitoring) is already produced per release: the R129 manifests, R66 budgets, open items and R132 drift monitors.
- Verdict: Pieces was penalised for misstating a metric's basis; this design is built to state it, publishing only the family-wise corrected claim (R126), stating coverage gaps in the certification report (R113), correcting group estimates with a measured confusion matrix (R131) and producing manifests, budgets and drift monitors per release that answer a TRAIGA-style CID. The scenario's "99.999% compliant" deck line is a governance misuse the design never authorises, and the banned-claims lint keeps such claims out of the agent's speech.

### Round 271. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The success label is blind to complaints filed with regulators, the place where enforcement starts
- Attack: Customers complain to the FTC (Consumer Sentinel), the FCC (informal complaints), the state AG, the BBB, a carrier's spam report or a robocall-blocking app. None of these reach the CRM. The success label ("no complaint") and the audited harm measure only see complaints made to the company. A branch generates hundreds of Sentinel entries and stays certified as "success". The first sign anything is wrong is a CID that cites 400 complaints. Consumer Sentinel is open only to law enforcement. FCC informal complaints are served to the company with a 30-day response deadline, but they are not tied to call IDs or fed into labels. A certification and drift loop built on internal complaints learns from the wrong signal and reports exactly the branches regulators are already building a case on as successes.
- Defense: The label is not CRM-only. R163 already says outcome labels come "first from independent feeds (outbox post-install customer survey, AG/CFPB/BBB complaint feeds, lender disputes...)", which override CRM fields. R117 makes "inbound complaint and demand channels" set legal hold. R59 runs "answer-rate/spam-label monitoring per number", which is the carrier and blocking-app signal. R132's label-free monitors (hang-up-after-turn rate, event-mix shift) catch a bad branch without any complaint. Sentinel is closed to companies, but an FCC informal complaint is served to the company, and the design's intake model can take it in.
- Verdict: The existing design does not rely on CRM-only labels: R163 already sources outcomes first from independent AG/CFPB/BBB complaint feeds, lender disputes and surveys that override CRM fields, R59 monitors spam labels per number (the carrier and blocking-app signal), and R132's label-free monitors flag a bad branch without any complaint. Sentinel is closed to companies for everyone, and R117 already routes complaint and demand channels into legal hold.

### Round 272. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Carrier SMS enforcement (CTIA, A2P 10DLC) punishes the e-sign and "outbox copy" texts, and suspending the brand cuts off the only path to a signature
- Attack: The e-sign OTP, the envelope, its resends and the DNC-policy/identity "outbox copies" go by SMS to numbers that consented to voice calls. They are sent under one registered 10DLC campaign whose declared use case is not sales contracts. Carrier filters flag links in sales texts that arrived uninvited. T-Mobile charges per-violation fees, and The Campaign Registry suspends the brand. Every pending deal stalls in WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE, and the opt-out confirmations stop going out. Carriers enforce CTIA messaging rules privately. T-Mobile's published non-compliance fees reach $10,000 per violation for the worst tier (support.twilio.com, Carrier Penalties for Non-Compliant Messaging). The design's consent covers "AI voice", not SMS, and nothing in the certified bundle covers campaign registration, matching texts to the registered use case, or the SMS sender's status.
- Defense: These texts are not unsolicited marketing. The OTP and envelope go out after an on-call ESIGN consent step where the caller chooses the channel (R120: "electronic documents, or paper or an in-person signing?"). DNC-policy and identity copies answer the caller's own request (R84, R89, sent "from registered short code"). R149 already extends "the consent gate to every outbound SMS and email". R122's invariant blocks any "SMS effect without valid registration". R115 routes STOP replies on the e-sign SMS into DNC. R120's paper and consultant-assisted signing path means one channel's suspension doesn't strand deals.
- Verdict: The texts are transactional responses to the caller's own on-call choice, not uninvited marketing: R120 asks the channel question before CONTRACT_SENT, R149 puts every outbound SMS through the consent gate, and R89 sends from a registered short code. The scenario's harm (deals stranded, opt-out confirmations stopped) is blunted by R120's paper and consultant-assisted signing path, R55's timeout/error handlers on every effect and R84's email and print-and-mail DNC-policy delivery. The defense's citation of R122 as an SMS registration control is a stretch (that row is state telemarketer registration), but the rest stands.

### Round 273. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Explainability demands meet discarded evidence: per-turn scores, speculative branches, pre-gate drops and redacted audio are exactly what explains a disputed decision, and they are gone
- Attack: An AG or court asks why the agent heard "stop, I said stop" as unintelligible and kept pitching. The answer depends on the ASR confidence pre-gate, both softmaxes, the near-tie margin, the decoding mask and the audio before the payment redactor cut a span. Bit-identical replay needs the exact original inputs. The design keeps committed traces, discards speculative and pre-gate data, and alters audio through redaction and PII surrogates. The company cannot show why, and that looks like spoliation. Once litigation or a CID is reasonably anticipated, there is a duty to preserve relevant electronically stored information (FRCP 37(e) allows adverse-inference sanctions when it is lost). The design's privacy-by-deletion pipeline runs continuously and logs outcomes, not the inputs behind each decision. Replay only proves determinism if the inputs survive, and here they don't.
- Defense: There's no spoliation in a routine, documented, pre-dispute policy. FRCP 37(e) requires reasonable steps and reaches only lost ESI. R117's legal_hold "freezes every deletion, surrogate and scrub job for that session and number, keeps the original audio and transcript", and it fires on mid-call threats, complaint channels and the post-call sweep. R116 keeps agent audio and render logs in WORM. Bundles are immutable (R67), so R53/R68 bit-identical replay reproduces decisions from retained input. Speculative branches can't explain anything because they can't affect committed traces (R64 CI property). The payment redactor (R45) exists because card data must not be retained. That is a legal duty, not concealment.
- Verdict: Routine, documented, pre-dispute deletion is not spoliation; the duty starts when litigation is reasonably anticipated, and R117's legal hold then freezes every deletion and keeps original audio and transcript, firing on complaint channels, mid-call threats and the post-call sweep. Because ASR and weights are pinned and bundles immutable (R53, R67, R68), retained audio regenerates every confidence, softmax and margin bit-identically, and speculative branches provably cannot affect committed traces (R64). The payment redactor (R45) implements a legal duty not to retain card data.


### Round 274. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Label leakage through agent-side context: the state's tool list and the preceding fragment predict the event, so Needle learns "what this state usually hears", not what the caller said
- Attack: The big model generates data state by state. In READ_BACK_CONFIRM, 90% of generated caller turns are affirm, because the generator is prompted "customer responds to the confirmation question". Needle's LOCAL input includes the state's tool list and the agent's last fragment. Training loss drops fastest by keying on that context. Live, a caller answers the confirmation with an off-pattern "hold on, my wife has to see the paperwork first". Needle outputs affirm with a wide margin. No near-tie, no clarify. Per-state generation makes the context a near-perfect label proxy. That is classic target leakage. Held-out synthetic tests share the same leak, so they score high. The margin gate fires only on near-ties, but a leaked prior produces confident errors, not ties. Grounding checks slots, not events, so an affirm with no slots passes it.
- Defense: The leaked prior cannot certify anything: synthetic data "never certifies" (R2), and whole-trace bounds come only from Needle-driven real calls with 100% audit in the canary cohort (R124). There, the real READ_BACK affirm rate is far below 90%, so confident wrong affirms show up as Tier A harm. It also can't reach a commit. "Hold on, my wife has to see the paperwork" holds no lexical affirm. The commit gate is "lexical over final ASR tokens (independent of Needle output)" (R87). It excludes conditional answers (R86). It now requires the caller to say "send the contract" (R144). So the turn becomes a re-ask, not a sale. The R127 artifact audit already probes for shortcut labels per source.
- Verdict: The harm in the scenario can't happen with the existing design. The commit gate is lexical over final ASR tokens and ignores Needle's output (R87). Conditional answers are excluded (R86), and the caller must say "send the contract" (R144), so a leaked confident affirm on "my wife has to see the paperwork first" only causes a re-ask. Synthetic sets never certify (R2), and the Needle-driven canary with 100% audit (R124) measures the real affirm rate, so a leaked prior shows up as real errors instead of passing on a synthetic score.

### Round 275. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The teacher's covert dialect prejudice becomes the label set: the big model, as stand-in classifier and synthetic labeler, scores dialect speakers as hostile, unclear or out of scope
- Attack: In the build phase the big model labels real calls, and Needle is distilled from those labels. A Black homeowner says in AAE "I been told y'all about that roof." The teacher labels it dispute_prior_statement or unclear, where a Standard English paraphrase gets acknowledge. Dialect transforms it generates carry the same skew in their labels. Needle copies the pattern: more clarifies, more fallbacks and hostile-exit routing for AAE speakers. The human audit samples only some agreements. Hofmann et al., Nature 633 (2024), showed LLMs hold covert, dialect-triggered prejudice against AAE even when overt bias is trained away. Distillation inherits the teacher's labels. Per-group bounds measure harm only on audited calls, while the training labels are big-model labels. (news.uchicago.edu/story/ai-biased-against-speakers-african-american-english-study-finds)
- Defense: Dialect transforms are Multi-VALUE-style rule transforms of labeled source items (R93), so the label is inherited, not re-judged by the teacher. The gate never trusts teacher labels. Tier A bounds come from humans listening to audio (R26). Strata are defined by observable features and annotated by two trained auditors (R131), with per-dialect bounds as release blockers (R93) and a label-free worst-cluster check. A skewed teacher therefore blocks release; it can't certify. The cited outcome is also mild: dispute_prior_statement only re-reads a table value (R91), and clarify is not a hostile exit.
- Verdict: Teacher labels never gate a release. Gold labels come from humans listening to audio, with per-group clarification and error bounds (R26). Per-dialect bounds block release (R93), and R131 adds feature-based strata, two auditors and a label-free worst-cluster check, so extra clarifies or misroutes for AAE speakers block release instead of shipping. Dialect transforms are rule-based and keep the source label (R93), and the cited outcomes (dispute re-read, clarify) are not hostile exits.

### Round 276. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: The teacher's own safety alignment sanitizes the safety-critical classes: it refuses or softens realistic suicidal, abusive, threatening and fraud-seeking caller turns
- Attack: The pipeline asks the big model for 5,000 sensitive_event and unlawful_request examples with oblique, realistic wording. It refuses some prompts, and the rest come back textbook-explicit: "I am feeling suicidal." Threats, slurs and loan-fraud asks come out polite and stereotyped. Needle learns that only explicit phrasing is sensitive. A real caller says "doesn't matter, I won't be around for the install anyway." Needle labels it small_talk and the pitch continues. The aligned teacher's refusal and harmlessness filters bias exactly the rare classes that are Tier A. The red team uses the same aligned models, so the sanitized wording also sets the test distribution. Synthetic coverage of these classes looks complete while the oblique real-world tail stays empty.
- Defense: Safety does not rest on synthetic coverage. Sensitive events have "deterministic lexicon pre-emption" (R19) and explicit hardship rows (R150). The red team is "model-diverse ... plus human-written seed attacks" (R97). Certification counts only real audited calls (R2), so a missed oblique ideation turn is counted Tier A harm and blocks the state. Label-free drift monitors on 100% of traffic cover out_of_scope growth and hang-up-after-turn (R132). Euphemisms are a named open item, not a hidden one.
- Verdict: The existing design names euphemisms as an open item and has no rule for training the oblique tail of sensitive_event. Nothing checks whether the aligned generator refuses or sanitizes these classes. R2 lists sensitive misses under neither tier (Tier A is opt-out, confirm_order, consent and critical slots), so they can fall under model-agreement labels. A sanitized teacher and a student that both call "won't be around for the install" small_talk agree, and that turn is seen only through random-agreement audit. The defense's real closure (sanitization audit, non-refusing writers, passive-ideation lexicon rows, recall-biased threshold) is all new.
- Fix: Make missed sensitive_event and unlawful_request turns Tier A, with human-audited gold only. Run a sanitization audit per class each release that compares generator refusal rate and explicitness against a human-written oblique set, and block release if it fails. Source oblique examples from consented, crisis-informed human writers. Add passive-ideation and indirect-threat lexicon rows to the deterministic pre-emption, with the scripted sensitive exit, and give sensitive_event a recall-biased per-event threshold in the R189 multi-label GLOBAL pass.

### Round 277. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Simulated ASR noise does not match the pinned ASR's real confusion profile, so Needle is robust to errors the real ASR never makes
- Attack: The big model writes "ASR errors" as plausible-looking typos, dropped words and homophones. The pinned ASR actually fails differently: it inserts fillers on noise, merges words across a narrowband codec, applies ITN quirks ("for" becomes "4") and has accent-specific substitutions. Needle trains on millions of LLM-imagined corruptions and scores well on synthetic noise sets. Live, the real ASR's patterns fall outside training, and error rates on noisy calls are several times the synthetic estimate. Noise augmentation helps only when it matches the deployed channel's error distribution. An LLM has never seen this ASR's confusion matrix. The certified ASR version is pinned, but the data generator is not conditioned on it. Every robustness number drawn from synthetic noise therefore overstates real performance.
- Defense: No robustness number from synthetic noise certifies anything (R2). Certification runs "on live ASR text" from the pinned ASR (R26, R68), stratified by channel class (R137), so the real confusion profile is what gets measured. The canonicalizer feeds Needle canonical text in training and certification alike (R68), which removes ITN quirks like "4" for "for" before Needle sees them. The design already applies this principle elsewhere. The big model may not write prosody tokens; they come only from the pinned detector run on audio (R128).
- Verdict: Synthetic noise numbers never certify (R2). Certification runs on live text from the pinned ASR (R26, R68), stratified by channel class (R137), so the real confusion profile is what gets measured, and the canonicalizer is applied the same way in training and certification. A mismatch costs capability (more states stay on fallback), not unmeasured harm.

### Round 278. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Event-schema growth leaves historical labels wrong: when an out_of_scope cluster becomes a new event, all older data still teaches the old label
- Attack: Release 14 adds the event leave_taking, split from out_of_scope and acknowledge. 400k historical examples that say "okay thanks bye" stay labeled acknowledge, because real data is never replaced. The new event has 3k fresh examples. Training sees about 130 conflicting acknowledge labels for each leave_taking label. Needle learns acknowledge for most goodbyes, and the new event certifies only on its own small fresh test set. Every schema change creates relabeling debt across the whole union of data, but the design says to add events and to keep real data, with no migration step. Label noise is systematic, concentrated on the very boundary the new event exists to fix. The per-source caps don't help: the conflict lies inside each source.
- Defense: A conflicted boundary can't certify. Bounds are per call, on whole audited real traces (R66), not on the new event's own test set.
