- Verdict: The parcel and utility reads exist (R88, R204, R205, R156, R212), but no rule makes the parcel the jurisdiction key. R22 validates the postal address, which returns Texarkana, TX. The fail-closed "no fee owner" clause sits in R181's tribal fix and triggers only if the lookup happens to fail. A geocoding vendor would find the Arkansas owner and pass, leaving the Texas profile to drive contract, cancellation and licensing. The defense concedes the gap.
- Fix: A pre-dial resolver geocodes to a rooftop point and then a parcel (state, county, municipality, AHJ, tribal, tax and utility territory). Postal city and state are never the jurisdiction key. If postal and parcel states disagree, both become candidates with most-restrictive-wins, and there is no close until a human confirms the parcel. The utility API's service address must match the parcel state.


### Round 314. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Solar lease payment talk triggers Regulation M (Consumer Leasing Act) advertising disclosures, but the fragment lint only checks Reg Z co-occurrence
- Attack: Caller asks, "What's the lease, roughly, per month?" QUOTE plays the approved lease fragment: "$0 down and about {lease_payment} a month." No Reg Z trigger-term rule fires because a lease is not credit. The same fragment ends the call if the caller hangs up, never reaching READ_BACK. Thousands of calls state a lease payment amount with no statement that it is a lease, no total due at signing, and no number or period of payments. Reg M (12 CFR 1013.7) has its own trigger terms: stating a payment amount or "$0 down" in a consumer-lease ad requires lease, amount due at signing, payment count/schedule and security-deposit disclosures. The design's build checks name only Reg Z. Solar leases of fixtures recorded as personal property under the Reg M dollar ceiling are squarely in scope. Source: consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1013/7/
- Defense: The scenario cannot get a lease figure spoken without a typed financing row: R180 types every financing row (loan, lease, PPA, PACE), so the lease fragment is already known to be a lease at build time. R91 puts every number behind a policy-table key, R32 allows only "non-triggering financing language before a quote" plus a "representative fully-disclosed example", and R85 READ_BACK covers financing terms. The gap is real but narrow: the co-occurrence lint is keyed to Reg Z terms only. Because the lint already runs "on every rendered reply" and the row type is known, adding Reg M's trigger set is a table change, not a redesign. A hang-up can't split the disclosure if it sits in the same mandatory segment (R39 playout marks).
- Verdict: The design's financing advertising controls are Reg Z only (R32 co-occurrence lint, R85 read-back of Reg Z financing terms); R180 types lease rows but attaches no lease-advertising rule set, so a QUOTE fragment stating a lease payment or "$0 down" passes every existing check. The registry and Reg M co-occurrence lint the Designer offers are all new, and the existing design fails the attack as written.
- Fix: Every typed financing row must name its advertising rule set (Reg Z for credit, Reg M 12 CFR 1013.7 for consumer leases under the ceiling, state lease rules) or the build fails; a Reg M co-occurrence lint forces any lease payment or down-payment phrase to render, in the same mandatory segment, that it is a lease, the total due at signing, the number and period of payments and any security deposit, all from table keys, with an undelivered segment (R39) triggering a written disclosure.

### Round 315. Winner: Designer
- Topic: "Free" add-on promotions (free EV charger, free critter guard, free first-year monitoring) whose cost is built into the per-watt price table
- Attack: Campaign promo row: "Sign this month, get a Level 2 EV charger free." Pricing ops raised the $/W row the same week to cover charger cost. QUOTE reads both table keys, each with its own substantiation id. A caller asks, "So if I skip the charger, is it cheaper?" Needle labels it a price question; the deterministic answer quotes the same price. Every substantiation check passes because each row is individually true. The FTC Guide on "Free" (16 CFR 251.1) bars a "free" offer when the price of the required purchase is raised, or quality or quantity reduced, to cover it. Substantiation ids are per row; nothing links a promo row to price-row history, so a certified fragment says "free" over a padded price.
- Defense: The fragment in the scenario fails the build. R18's banned-claims lint bans "free/no-cost" wording, and R222 extends that lint to policy-table row text, so a promo row reading "EV charger free" can't be cited at all. R37 allows concessions only from a versioned pricing policy keyed on verifiable facts with a margin floor, never ad hoc. R199's quantifier lint requires a table key for aggregate claims. A rewording such as "included at no extra charge" hits the same no-cost ban. The remaining gap is a bundle with no "free" word over a padded price. That is a pricing-integrity problem rather than a free-claim problem, and the forward-only table (R140) already keeps the price history needed to detect it.
- Verdict: The scenario's promo row says "free", and R18's banned-claims lint (free/no-cost) plus R222's extension of that lint to policy-table row text block it at build time, so the certified "free" fragment cannot exist. A bundle with no free-type wording over a padded price is a pricing-integrity issue, not a 16 CFR 251 violation, and R140's forward-only history already keeps the price record needed to audit it.

### Round 316. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Referral rewards pitched to the buyer as a way to pay off the system ("referral selling") are an unlawful inducement in home-improvement sales
- Attack: Promotions table holds "Refer-a-friend: $500 per signed neighbor." Caller objects: "I can't afford $180 a month." OBJECTION_HANDLING picks the approved fragment: "Many customers offset that with our referral program, {referral_reward} for every neighbor who goes solar." Caller commits. The reward is now part of what induced the signature, and it depends on future sales to people who were never called. California B&P Code 7157 makes it a misdemeanor for a home-improvement seller to induce a contract with an offer of compensation for procuring other customers. Many state UDAP laws ban "referral selling" outright. The referral-harvesting topic covered calls to third parties, not the reward as an illegal inducement offered inside objection handling. Source: law.justia.com/codes/california/2021/code-bpc/division-3/chapter-9/article-10/section-7157/
- Defense: The objection fragment is blocked twice over. First, "offset that" is a savings/payment comparison, and R72 allows savings or comparison wording only with calculator_output provenance plus a substantiation id. A referral reward is a promotions key, not calculator output, so the lint rejects the filling. Second, "I can't afford $180" is an affordability fact. Under R71, suitability facts are qualification slots, never objection events, and the lint bans rebutting them. R150 then requires the financed payment to be at or below the calculator-verified savings band, or else NOT_SUITABLE/cash-only. R37 bans concessions triggered by objections, and R74 limits referrals to a self-opt-in link. Even so, no rule yet bars the reward as an inducement, so I harden that.
- Verdict: Nothing in the existing design bars a referral reward from being offered as an inducement during the sales call; R74 only governs how referred contacts are reached, R220 merely tags referral fragments as marketing (keeping them out of SERVICE calls, not sales calls), and R72 blocks the fragment only if its lint happens to read "offset" as savings wording. "I can't afford $180" is not in R150's hardship lexicon, so it can still route to objection handling, and the Designer concedes no rule bars the inducement.
- Fix: Give referral-reward keys a regulation-profile row (CA B&P 7157 and state referral-selling bans) and a model-checked invariant that no referral-reward fragment is reachable before signature plus the cancellation window (and never in banning states, only by separate outbox message elsewhere), plus a lint banning any fragment that pairs a payment or affordability key with a reward or promo key.

### Round 317. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Product recalls hit SKUs that are already certified in the bundle: the agent keeps selling recalled equipment until a new bundle certifies
- Attack: November 2025: CPSC announces a recall of about 10,500 Tesla Powerwall 2 units for fire risk. The pinned bundle's product rows, read-back warranties and battery fragments still name the unit. Removing a product changes the content-hashed bundle, which needs a re-certification the audit pipeline can't finish for weeks. Meanwhile the campaign keeps quoting it, and the battery-upsell campaign calls owners of recalled units offering "a second battery to pair with yours." CPSA 15 U.S.C. 2068 prohibits selling a product subject to a recall. Certificates expire only when "a cited policy row changes". A recall is an external event with no feed, no pre-dial suppression and no GLOBAL event. The only kill switch, rollback, restores older rows that still name the SKU. Source: cpsc.gov/Recalls/2026/Tesla-Recalls-Powerwall-2-AC-Battery-Power-Systems...
- Defense: The attack assumes removing a SKU needs a weeks-long bundle recertification. It doesn't. R140 took the policy table out of the bundle's rollback: it is forward-only, "recalled or withdrawn rows become tombstones no bundle can cite", and the runtime refuses any row outside its effective window. When a cited row is tombstoned, QUOTE and READ_BACK drop to lead-gen or callback fallback the same hour, and rollback can't resurrect the row. R160 adds a supply-status field that expires SKUs and a WAITING_FOR_BOM_MATCH gate that blocks install on any change. R178 sets the pattern for external feeds: a daily pull with a stale feed failing closed. The real gap is that no recall feed triggers the tombstone.
- Verdict: The attack's premise that pulling a SKU needs weeks of recertification is wrong: R140 makes the policy table forward-only, explicitly turns recalled rows into tombstones no bundle can cite, refuses rows outside their effective window, drops QUOTE/READ_BACK to lead-gen or callback fallback, and keeps rollback from resurrecting them; R160's supply-status field and WAITING_FOR_BOM_MATCH hold uninstalled contracts. A tombstoned SKU row also starves any upsell fragment that cites it, so the agent does not keep selling the recalled unit.

### Round 318. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Battery virtual-power-plant (VPP) income claims and third-party grid control are folded into the battery pitch with no read-back terms
- Attack: Battery fragment: "Plus, enroll in {vpp_program} and earn up to {vpp_annual} a year." Caller: "Great, the battery's for hurricane backup anyway." Needle labels acknowledge. READ_BACK names the battery and its warranty, and nothing about the program letting the utility or aggregator discharge the battery on event days, the reserve level, the annual re-enrollment or the per-kW rate set by the utility each season. A storm-day event drains the battery before the outage. VPP payments are set by a third party's tariff, which changes yearly, so "earn up to" is an unsubstantiated earnings claim. The material limitation that defeats the caller's stated purpose (backup) is never read back. Nothing in the design records consent for a third party to control equipment in the home, or the throughput that counts against the warranty.
- Defense: "Earn up to {vpp_annual}" can't be certified. A third-party tariff rate that changes yearly is an "assumption" provenance row under R157, so any fragment filled from it must carry the lint-enforced "estimate, assumes X, not guaranteed" qualifier, and the tariff-filing monitor expires the row. R155 incentive typing bars capped or program-dependent money from the quote. R18 bans "guaranteed credits", and R199 requires a table key for aggregates. Backup is also covered: R83 stated-need slots (outage_backup) run deterministic product-fit rules, and every READ_BACK must carry the material-limitation rows. What's missing is a material-limitation row for third-party discharge control and a rule keeping enrollment off the sales call.
- Verdict: R157's qualifier and R83's material-limitation rows exist, but nothing in the design models a third-party program that can discharge the customer's battery: no limitation row for it, no consent to third-party control, and R83's outage_backup product-fit rule checks the product, not program enrollment, so a backup buyer is never warned. The defense's real fixes (no earnings claims, enrollment only after PTO, mandatory discharge limitation, backup conflict rule) are all new.
- Fix: Type VPP rows as third_party_program; ban earnings fragments on the sales call; offer enrollment only after PTO by outbox under the program's own terms and consent; make a "programs may discharge your battery and reduce backup" limitation mandatory in READ_BACK whenever a battery is quoted, and flag outage_backup plus any program default as a product-fit conflict.

### Round 319. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Add-on and expansion upsells to existing solar owners forfeit their grandfathered net-metering tariff
- Attack: Upsell campaign to 2019 customers on California NEM 2: "Add four more panels and a battery." Caller says yes; QUALIFY records existing_solar = true and size from the CRM. Calculator computes savings on the added panels at current export rates. The expansion goes past the allowed increment (CPUC rule: greater of 10% of capacity or 1 kW). Under the rules, that can move the system, or the new capacity, onto the successor net-billing tariff, with lower export credits for the life of the system. The savings delta ignores lost value on the existing system's grandfathered status, which can be bigger than the add-on's benefit. The suitability table has an "existing solar" row but no tariff-vintage slot and no expansion threshold. A certified "add-on" path makes the customer worse off while every row is true. Source: pge.com nem2-sunset-faq.pdf; cpuc.ca.gov/nem
- Defense: The calculator can't produce the savings figure the attack describes. R157 requires "the customer's exact tariff schedule ... on verified interval data, else no figure". R156 and R212 source usage and tariff_class only from the utility authorization API, which returns the customer's current NEM 2 schedule. R82 has existing_solar/existing_equipment suitability slots. R71 makes an unsuitable close Tier A harm and requires the success label to pass a sampled suitability audit. R220 types call_purpose, so an existing-customer upsell is its own campaign/bundle pairing. The gap is real: the calculator models the add-on, but not what the whole system loses if it crosses the expansion threshold.
- Verdict: The existing calculator (R157) uses the customer's current exact tariff but models only the add-on, and the existing_solar slot (R82) and suitability table (R71) carry no tariff vintage or expansion threshold, so an upsell that pushes a grandfathered system onto the successor tariff can show positive savings while making the customer worse off. The Designer concedes the gap, and every remedy offered is new.
- Fix: Add a tariff_vintage slot (legacy tariff, interconnection date, grandfather end) from the utility API and interconnection agreement, per-utility expansion_rule rows, a whole-system calculator delta including lost legacy-tariff value, sizing capped at the threshold or NOT_SUITABLE when the delta is negative, no figure when vintage is unknown, and a mandatory READ_BACK row when the add-on crosses the threshold.

### Round 320. Winner: Designer
- Topic: "Same menu for all" (the fair-lending invariant) collides with product legality that varies by state: third-party PPAs are unlawful in some states
- Attack: A Florida homeowner reaches QUOTE. To meet the fair-lending invariant, the design offers every caller the same menu: cash, loan, lease and PPA. The PPA fragment: "Pay only for the power the panels produce, {ppa_rate} per kWh." The caller picks the PPA, and READ_BACK and the e-sign follow. The regulation profile is keyed to telemarketing jurisdiction rules, not to which ownership structures each state's utility law permits. In Florida and several other states, selling electricity to a homeowner under a third-party PPA makes the seller an unregulated utility, so the contract is void. Fixing this needs per-state menus, which breaks the model-checked "same menu" invariant and the matched-pair tests it rests on. Either way a certified invariant is false.
- Defense: The invariant is misread. R46 says "all options to all, non-protected keys only". The product-policy table may legitimately branch on non-protected keys, and property jurisdiction is the clearest example. Matched-pair fair-lending tests vary only the prohibited-basis attributes ("prohibited-basis field tags") while holding jurisdiction fixed, so a per-state menu breaks nothing. R47 already gates by jurisdiction profile (lead-gen-only states). R178 conditions QUOTE on the property's jurisdiction, and R180 types every financing row, including PPA. The genuine gap is narrower: no row says which financing types are lawful in each state or utility territory.
- Verdict: The attack's dilemma rests on a misreading: R46 is "all options to all, non-protected keys only", so the product-policy table may branch on property jurisdiction while matched-pair tests vary only prohibited-basis fields, and a per-state menu breaks no invariant. The table is already jurisdiction-keyed (R46, R47, R178) and every mandatory READ_BACK fragment is signed by outside counsel per regulation profile (R265), so nothing in the design forces a PPA onto Florida callers.

### Round 321. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Bundled recurring services (monitoring, maintenance plans, panel cleaning, extended warranty) auto-renew on the single lexical COMMIT
- Attack: QUOTE bundles "System + {care_plan} at {care_monthly}, renews yearly." READ_BACK lists the plan inside the product line. Caller says "Yeah" to the one read-back question id; COMMIT passes. E-sign follows. No separate question asks for agreement to the auto-renewal. A year later the plan renews on the card or ACH on file; the caller says they never agreed to a subscription. California's Automatic Renewal Law (B&P 17602) and similar state laws need clear, conspicuous auto-renewal terms and affirmative consent to them specifically, plus an acknowledgment and an easy cancel path. ROSCA needs express informed consent to negative-option charges. One commit gate for the whole bundle can't show consent to each recurring term, and no event exists for "cancel just the plan".
- Defense: The design never treats one "Yeah" as consent. R144 turned the commit into an action-production prompt ("please say 'send the contract'") with an R77/R87 lexical gate. R85 makes READ_BACK state refund/cancellation policy and financing terms. R45 means the voice channel never takes payment. R223 requires a processor-page authorization that states amount, dates and number of debits, with the account holder's E-SIGN signature, so no card or ACH on file is created by voice. R216 makes CANCEL a saga with per-party substates. R91 bans unqualified term words. But a renewing plan bundled inside the product line would still ride on a single commit, so I close that.
- Verdict: R144, R223 and R45 make the commit deliberate and keep payment off the voice channel, but the design has no notion of a recurring or auto-renewing service: no clear-and-conspicuous renewal terms, no consent captured specifically for the renewal, no acknowledgment, and R216's cancel saga cannot cancel only the plan. A renewing care plan bundled into the product line rides on the single contract commit, which the Designer concedes; the negative_option typing and ARL/ROSCA flow are new.
- Fix: Type recurring or auto-renewing rows as negative_option and bar them from voice QUOTE and READ_BACK; sell them only in writing through an ARL/ROSCA flow with clear terms, a separate affirmative checkbox and signature, an acknowledgment and an online cancel path; give cancel_or_rescind a target (contract or plan); lint "renews" tokens out of voice fragments.

### Round 322. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Roofing and electrical bundles sold under a solar contractor's licence class that doesn't cover those trades
- Attack: QUALIFY routes to ROOF_FIRST and the agent quotes the "Roof + Solar" bundle as one SKU, one total cost and one contract. The seller holds California C-46 (Solar); the roof tear-off and the bundled EV-charger circuit fall under C-39 (Roofing) and C-10 (Electrical). The regulation profile checks that the seller is licensed and registered, not which classification each line item needs. The caller signs. A contract for work outside the licence classification is unlicensed work for that portion. Under California B&P 7031 an unlicensed contractor can't collect for it and can be forced to return what was paid. The bundle also merges different lien, permit and warranty regimes into one read-back. Earlier licensing topics covered salesperson registration and licence status, not how trade classifications map onto bundles.
- Defense: The scenario contradicts the graph. R71 defines ROOF_FIRST as "inspection before any quote", so the agent can't quote a "Roof + Solar" SKU in that branch. R159 makes reroofing and panel work survey-driven change orders, each with a new quote hash, written re-disclosure and fresh e-sign. R178 adds a model-checked invariant: no QUOTE, READ_BACK, CONTRACT_SENT or install effect unless the installer's licence row is valid for the property's jurisdiction. R217 requires a per-profile home_improvement_contract with keyed fields. The real gap is that the licence row checks status per jurisdiction, not trade classification per line item.
- Verdict: R71's ROOF_FIRST (inspection before any quote) blocks the exact "Roof + Solar" quote in that branch, but R178's licence invariant checks only that the installer's licence is valid in the jurisdiction, not the trade class each line item needs, and R159 actually routes reroofing and panel work into the same contract as change orders. An EV-charger circuit or a reroof change order can therefore be contracted under a solar-only classification, and the per-line-item class check is new.
- Fix: Give every quote line item and change order a required_license_class per jurisdiction, extend the R178 invariant so each needs a matching valid class on the contracting licensee's row (or an attested incidental-work rule), split the contract otherwise, and name the licensee and licence number per line item in READ_BACK and the contract.

### Round 323. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Deterministic "limited-time" and "showcase home" promotions become rolling, perpetual deadlines: false scarcity certified as fact
- Attack: Promo row: "Founders' rate ends {promo_end}," where ops sets promo_end to the last day of each month, every month. Another row: "Your street was selected for {showcase_slots} reduced-price showcase installs." The caller hesitates; the urgency fragment fires. Replays of calls across months show every caller heard the deadline was days away, and every street was "selected". TSR 310.3(a)(2) and (a)(4) and FTC Act s5 forbid misrepresenting material terms and inducements, and fake deadlines and fake "selected home" scarcity are classic deceptive tactics, prominent in solar enforcement. Each row is "true" on the day it's read, so substantiation ids pass. Bit-identical replay then gives plaintiffs exact proof of the pattern.
- Defense: The urgency fragment can't exist. R78 has a lint that "bans fake urgency and unverifiable external-fact fragments". R153 requires a fixed fragment before READ_BACK, "There's no need to decide today.", which directly contradicts any deadline push. R15 sends defers_decision into the vulnerability counter, and R145 bounds indirect declines, so hesitation can't trigger a persuasion fragment. R92 allows social proof only as audited aggregates with substantiation ids, and R199's quantifier lint requires table keys. R151 bars optimizing variants on conversion. R116's WORM render log is our evidence too, and the R18 claims audit reviews it. Still, a rolling promo_end could pass a per-row check, so I add a history check.
- Verdict: The existing design already blocks this: R78's lint bans fake urgency and unverifiable external-fact fragments, so a deadline fragment and a "your street was selected" claim cannot be certified; R92 allows social proof only as audited aggregates and bans references to specific addresses; R153 plays "There's no need to decide today." before READ_BACK; and R15/R145 send hesitation into the vulnerability counter and decline handling rather than a persuasion fragment. The history-based rolling-deadline detector is polish on a sound defense.


### Round 324. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Warm transfer as a harm sink: the release gate rewards Needle for handing the riskiest calls to humans, whose speech is outside the measured bound
- Attack: 1) A state struggles to certify. 2) Tuning lowers the clarify threshold; "clarify once, then fallback" now routes every hard call (confused seniors, hedged buyers, angry callers) to request_human. 3) The audited trace ends at the transfer, so the bot's per-call harm drops and the state certifies. 4) A commissioned human closer takes the confused senior, improvises claims no fragment allows, and signs them. 5) The harm happens on the human leg, which no certificate covers. The gate measures "per-call harm on whole audited call traces", but the trace's speech guarantee stops at the transfer. Any metric that excludes the fallback rewards dumping risk into it (Goodhart). The calls most likely to cause harm are exactly the ones moved out of the measured population, so the 1e-5 figure describes a filtered, safer subset of customers.
- Defense: The transfer is not a sink. R56 already tracks "human-request rate and post-request conversion", R28 tracks "time-on-fallback per close state", and R36 retires the big model only if "fallback rate < X%" and "A/B handoff lift < Y", so tuning that dumps hard calls into transfers fails promotion. Outcome labels are joined by session id (R50) from independent feeds (R163) and complaints link to the originating sales branch (R221), so a bad human close still lands on the bot's branch. R203: closers work only in a recorded company softphone/CRM, and flagged callers go to a non-commissioned reviewer. R169: unintelligible-fallback calls book nothing. The real gap: the 1e-5 trace stopped at the transfer.
- Verdict: The existing design does track human-request and fallback rates (R56, R28, R36) and links complaints and outcomes back to the originating branch (R163, R221). But the certified Tier A bound is computed on audited traces whose speech guarantee ends at the transfer, and R36's "fallback rate < X%" gates only big-model retirement, not Needle state certification. Delayed complaint linkage does not put the human leg's improvised speech into the release number. The defense admits this, and the closing measures (whole-session trace, fallback cap as release blocker) are all NEW.
- Fix: Define the audited "whole call trace" as the whole session including the recorded human leg (R203 softphone), count human-leg harm against the originating state's Tier A budget, and make a per-state fallback/transfer-rate ceiling (and any rise versus the previous release) a certification blocker so that lowering the clarify threshold to shed hard calls fails release.

### Round 325. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The machine's compliance state is not handed over in a warm transfer: the human never sees the vulnerability counter, senior-cancel flag, jurisdiction or partial read-back
- Attack: 1) The vulnerability counter is at 2 of 3, the caller mentioned being 71, and READ_BACK was cut off by a barge-in. 2) The caller asks for "a real person". 3) The human gets a CRM screen pop with name, address and quote. 4) The human, seeing "quote given", goes straight to "so we're good to send the paperwork?" and skips the material limitations. 5) There's no 5-day senior cancel notice, and the vulnerability flag was never surfaced. Every safeguard is a guard over the slot store inside StateSmith. A human closer has no guards, only what the screen pop shows. TSR 310.3(a)(1) disclosure duties and state senior-cancel rights attach to the seller, not to whichever channel happens to be speaking. The model-checked invariant that every commit path passes READ_BACK stops being true once a human is the one committing.
- Defense: Leaving the counter out of the handoff is deliberate. R203's "allowlisted handoff schema ... never carries the vulnerability counter ... Those flags only change routing, to a non-commissioned reviewer", so a flagged caller never reaches a commissioned closer. Senior cancel needs no age flag: R15 gives the "Longest statutory cancellation window (CA 65+ 5 business days) for all". Paperwork can't skip READ_BACK. R134's independent outbox monitor "refuses CONTRACT_SENT ... unless the committed trace contains READ_BACK with a playback-engine completion mark for the same quote hash". R141 mails a contract summary with cancel instructions to every record owner, and R148 adds a human owner-only call before install for owners 65+.
- Verdict: The scenario's harms are already blocked by existing elements. R203's handoff schema withholds the vulnerability flags but routes flagged callers to a non-commissioned reviewer. R15/R241 apply the longest (CA 65+) cancel window and notice form to every buyer, so no age flag is needed. R134's independent outbox monitor refuses CONTRACT_SENT unless the committed trace holds a playout-completed READ_BACK for the same quote hash, so a human "send the paperwork" cannot skip the material limitations. R141/R242 add the mailed summary of material clauses.

### Round 326. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Fragment approvers sign off on isolated fragments, but regulators judge the net impression of the assembled call, which no human ever reviews
- Attack: 1) Legal approves "Many homeowners here have eliminated their bill" (substantiated aggregate) and, separately, "Your estimated payment is {monthly}". 2) A deterministic path plays them back to back after the customer's bill is read. 3) The sequence implies that this customer's bill will be eliminated. 4) Thousands of fragments times several wordings times slot values come to millions of paths. Approvers working through queues of single strings rubber-stamp them, and none of them hears a full rendered call before release. Under the FTC Deception Policy Statement (1983), deception is judged by the "net impression" of the whole communication, not claim by claim. Approval is keyed to fragment id, and the lints check single strings. Juxtaposition creates implied claims that no individual approval covered, and review volume pushes approvers toward approval fatigue.
- Defense: The example fails existing lints. "Eliminated their bill" is a banned "zero bill" claim under R18, and the quantifier "Many" needs a table key under R199. R92 permits only audited aggregate facts. Whole-call review exists: R18 includes a "claims audit in certification", the gate is measured "on whole audited call traces", and R124's Needle-driven canary has "100% human audit", so humans hear full rendered calls before wide release. R29 lints the rendered reply, and R32 runs the Reg Z co-occurrence lint across the whole reply, not per fragment. R151's whole-path CI check already reasons over sequences. One gap is real: R18 lists the "whole-path net impression audit" as open.
- Verdict: The concrete example fails existing lints ("eliminated their bill" is a banned zero-bill claim under R18, and R199 requires a table key for "many"). But the design's own R18 lists the "whole-path net impression audit across template sequences" as open. R91, R151 and R32 check fact mapping, affirm counts and Reg Z co-occurrence, not implied claims from juxtaposition. Canary audits (R124) hear full calls but have no net-impression label. The FTC deception standard is net impression, and the fixes offered are all NEW.
- Fix: Add a juxtaposition lint (aggregate social-proof fragments may not share a turn with, or come within N fragments of, any personalized price or savings fragment), a per-profile counsel-signed "path book" of coverage-sampled rendered fragment sequences as a release gate, and a "net impression misleading" label in the Tier A audit codebook.

### Round 327. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Audit labelers suffer psychological harm and turn over because the sampling plan routes the most distressing calls to them
- Attack: 1) "All disagreements + random agreements" pulls calls with sensitive_event, bereavement, suicidal statements, abuse and threats into the audit queue, where disagreement is highest. 2) Labelers listen to raw audio of these for hours a day. 3) Experienced labelers burn out and quit, and new hires take their place with lower recall and less calibration. 4) Per-group and Tier A bounds are measured with a workforce whose quality degrades and churns, and the audit vendor faces trauma claims. The certification gate depends entirely on human listeners. Content-moderation work exposed to traumatic material produced PTSD claims and a $52M settlement (Scola v. Facebook, 2020). The design sets no exposure limits, wellness provisions or retention plan for the people whose judgments are the ground truth. Turnover silently changes the measuring instrument from one release to the next.
- Defense: The instrument is guarded. R200's "planted gold calls ... mixed blind into every labeler's queue" gate whether a labeler's labels count, so churn-driven quality loss is caught and those labels are dropped retroactively. R131 measures inter-rater agreement and corrects estimates with the confusion matrix. Exposure is already cut down. R143 gives labelers "only the clipped turns under audit", not full calls. R119 redacts medical sensitive_event spans "from every retained transcript, audit sample". Sensitive exits are scripted fragments, so correctness is mostly readable from the R116 render log. The real gap: no welfare or exposure limits.
- Verdict: The attack's breaking claim is that churn silently changes the measuring instrument. Existing elements make that visible: R200 gold calls gate each labeler's labels and retroactively drop failures, R257 tracks per-labeler recall and divides the bound by its lower confidence limit, R281 runs blind double labeling with an accuracy floor, and R131 corrects with the confusion matrix. Exposure is already reduced by R143 (clipped turns only) and R119 (health span redaction). The missing welfare provisions are a real operational gap, but they do not invalidate the certification.

### Round 328. Winner: Designer
- Topic: On-call alarm fatigue and kill-switch authority: per-state, per-group drift alarms get muted, and rollbacks get overridden under revenue pressure
- Attack: 1) Hundreds of per-state, per-dialect drift alarms fire mostly as noise. 2) On-call engineers raise thresholds and snooze the noisy ones. 3) At month-end a real breach fires at 7 pm, the peak dialing hour. 4) The on-call engineer pages the campaign owner, who refuses rollback ("we lose the evening and quota"). 5) The design says "breach -> rollback" but never says who may refuse it, and harm keeps running while the matter is escalated. Alarm fatigue is a documented failure mode: The Joint Commission's 2013 Sentinel Event Alert tied muted and ignored alarms to deaths. Repeated tolerated deviations become normal practice (Vaughan, "normalization of deviance"). An automatic rollback rule is only as strong as the authority and incentives of the people who can pause it, and the design leaves both unspecified.
- Defense: Nobody is asked. R132 says affected Tier A states "revert automatically to deterministic fallback until the surge clears". R66 makes "budget breach -> rollback to previous certified artifact" automatic. R142's admission control already gates dials by system state. The noise problem is handled statistically: R126 sets a "family-wise alpha" across state × stratum tests, and R132 uses CUSUM, so the false-alarm rate is designed rather than tuned by hand. Calibration (R133) resets thresholds per release on audited data. The gap: the design never forbids snoozing or override.
- Verdict: The design does not page anyone for permission. R132 reverts affected Tier A states to deterministic fallback automatically, R66 makes budget breach roll back automatically, and R263 pauses new dials on a drift alarm. R126's family-wise alpha and CUSUM sequential tests set the false-alarm rate by design rather than by hand-tuned thresholds, which undercuts the noise premise. The design grants no override authority. An insider disabling an automatic control is a generic sabotage risk, not a flaw specific to this design.

### Round 329. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Incentivized business owners edit the suitability and policy tables as "data changes" that bypass fragment-level legal review
- Attack: 1) A sales VP paid on installs owns the suitability table. 2) NOT_SUITABLE exits count as success, but they cost revenue. 3) Roof-age cutoff 20 -> 25 years, shade "heavy" redefined, move horizon 5 -> 3 years. 4) No fragment text changes, so approvers see nothing; the cited row change expires certs, but re-certification measures audited harm, which can't see bad suitability until labels arrive months later. 5) Marginal homes get sold. The design puts truthfulness under human fragment approval but treats the decision thresholds as table data. Whoever controls the threshold controls who gets sold to. Suitability harm shows up only in delayed labels (reroof, low production), so human gatekeeping is weakest exactly where the commercial incentive to loosen it is strongest.
- Defense: The premise is wrong about ownership. R71 makes the suitability table "versioned compliance-owned", not sales-owned. A changed row decertifies every branch that cites it (design §Runtime, R3). R140 makes the table "forward-only". R163 requires "decouple ops and installer bonuses from training labels". Suitability harm is checked immediately: R71 counts an "unsuitable close" as Tier A harm and requires "passes sampled suitability audit", and R211 cross-checks roof age against permit records and shade against aerial lookups. R150 requires payment ≤ verified savings. Gap: a loosened row is judged against itself.
- Verdict: The premise is false: R71 makes the suitability table compliance-owned, not sales-owned, and R163 decouples bonuses from labels. Structural floors already resist the specific loosenings: R231 sends any roof whose remaining life is shorter than the term to ROOF_FIRST, R150 requires the financed payment to be at or below verified savings, and R211 cross-checks roof age and shade against permit and aerial data. Row changes expire certificates. The remaining "judged against itself" risk is real but secondary.

### Round 330. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Humans carry out the bot's booked callbacks and consults from their own phones and CRMs, outside the pre-dial gate
- Attack: 1) The bot books an in-home consult and a callback for "tomorrow evening". 2) A rep calls from a personal cell to "confirm" and re-pitches. 3) That dial skips the DNC, RND, calling-hours, frequency-cap and state-registry checks. 4) The customer told the bot "stop" in a post-call message the sweep caught, but the rep's calendar invite was already created. 5) The rep calls at 9:30 pm local time, and the call goes unrecorded and unaudited. DNC rules under 47 CFR 64.1200(c) and the TSR cover any telephone solicitation, whether a person or an AI places it. The pre-dial gate exists only in the dialer. Every human-executed follow-up the graph produces through request_human and booked consults falls outside it, along with the post-call DNC sweep and the audited harm measure.
- Defense: R203 requires "Closers work only in a recorded company softphone/CRM", with access logging and blocked bulk export. R198: "callbacks go only to the contact of record". R115's revocation ledger "is read at every dial (scheduled callbacks included)", and R138's linearizable permit, including the "pending_sweep" lock, is the single dial authority. R41 clamps callbacks to the lawful window. R170 makes every END write a writeback the permit reads. R202 makes the pre-visit confirmation a call to the number of record. The gap is enforcement on human devices and stale calendar entries.
- Verdict: Existing elements cover the human follow-up path. R203 confines closers to a recorded company softphone/CRM. R138 makes the linearizable permit the single authority for every dial, with the pending_sweep lock. R115 reads the revocation ledger at every dial, "scheduled callbacks included". R198 limits callbacks to the contact of record, R202 sends pre-visit confirmations to the number of record, and R41 clamps callbacks to the lawful window. A rep dialing from a personal cell is a policy violation that no system design fully prevents.

### Round 331. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: The translation from legal sign-off to invariants is unreviewable: lawyers can't read the StateSmith graph or the temporal properties, and engineers make the legal interpretations
- Attack: 1) Counsel signs a memo: "disclose AI status and seller identity promptly; honor senior cancel rights." 2) An engineer encodes "promptly" as "before ICEBREAKER" and "senior" as a nonexistent age slot that defaults to false. 3) The model checker proves the engineer's property, not the law. 4) Legal reviews English fragments and a summary deck, never the guards. 5) The "most restrictive wins" rule needs a human to rank rules that can't be compared (one state requires an extra statement, another limits what may precede a disclosure). Model checking verifies conformance to a specification, not that the specification is correct. No role in the design is both qualified and assigned to sign that a given guard implements a given statute. "Most restrictive" is not a total order across jurisdictions, so engineers silently choose. Certification then gives false assurance on the very interpretation that a regulator will contest.
- Defense: The design avoids the attack's own examples. "Senior" isn't encoded as an age slot: R15 applies the longest cancel window "for all". "Promptly" is concrete and lawyer-checkable: R31's invariant "every path DIAL->QUALIFY/SALES passes DISCLOSED", R165's ~6 s segment 1, and R109's "within about 1.5 s". Regulation profiles are policy-table rows with effective dates, and R222 already requires a "per-jurisdiction legal attestation field" on rows, a precedent for counsel signing row semantics. R70 checks invariants "across all profiles". R84 routes the unresolved AI-name question to counsel instead of letting an engineer choose. Gap: no signed guard-to-statute mapping, and "most restrictive" isn't a total order.
- Verdict: The attack's concrete examples are refuted ("senior" is not an age slot under R15/R241, and "promptly" is a concrete invariant under R31/R109/R165). The structural point still stands. Counsel signs fragments (R265) and warranty rows (R222), but nobody qualified signs that a guard or temporal property implements a statute. "Most restrictive wins" (R70, final plan) is not a total order across jurisdictions, so engineers silently resolve incomparable rules. The remedies are NEW.
- Fix: Add a guard-to-statute traceability matrix. Every guard and invariant cites a regulation-profile row and carries counsel attestation over a generated plain-English rendering plus model-checker witness and counterexample transcripts. Replace "most restrictive wins" with union of obligations and intersection of permissions, with a satisfiability check that sends unsatisfiable jurisdiction mixes to lead-gen or a human.

### Round 332. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Harm labels are legal conclusions that crowd audio labelers aren't qualified to make, and lawyers can't listen at the volume certification needs
- Attack: 1) The Tier A harm categories include TSR misrepresentation, Reg Z triggering terms, unlawful steering and failure to honor an opt-out under a specific state's rule. 2) Vendor labelers listen and judge "sounded fine". 3) Whether "$0 down" next to a payment amount, or a "we'll take care of it" after a hedged stop, is a violation depends on statute and jurisdiction. 4) Lawyers spot-check 50 calls. 5) The 1e-5 bound is computed from lay judgments of legal questions. The gate is only as valid as each label. Detecting a Reg Z trigger term, a TSR material misrepresentation or a state opt-out violation needs legal expertise and the jurisdiction profile in force. The people who can label correctly cannot work at 10^5-10^6 calls, and the people who work at that volume cannot label correctly. The certified number measures labeler opinion, not legal harm.
- Defense: Labelers don't make legal calls; R79 says "policy is authored, only perception learned". Reg Z trigger terms are fixed at build time by the R32 co-occurrence lint on rendered replies. Claims are certified "by fragment id", and the R116 render log records exactly which fragments played. Counsel decides once per fragment and profile whether a string is lawful, and labelers judge facts such as what the caller said, which event applied and which fragment played. R221 gives "complaint" a "fixed policy definition ... applied blind". R200's planted known-answer calls, "including scripted harmful closes", gate labeler accuracy. R143 uses in-house or vetted staff. Gap: hedged-stop cases need jurisdictional judgment.
- Verdict: The existing design already separates legal judgment from labeling. Agent speech is certified by fragment id with compliance and outside-counsel sign-off (R18, R265), Reg Z trigger terms are caught at build by the R32 lint, and the R116 render log records exactly which fragments played. Labelers therefore mostly judge perceptual facts (what the caller said, which event applied). R221 defines "complaint" by fixed policy, and R200/R257/R281 measure labeler accuracy against known answers, including scripted harmful closes. Residual jurisdiction-specific hedged-stop cases are narrow.

### Round 333. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The humans the fallback relies on are deskilled by the automation (Bainbridge's "ironies of automation")
- Attack: 1) For months the compiled agent handles everything. Human transfer agents take a trickle of calls and are rotated or let go. 2) A cited policy row changes, certificates for 40 financing states expire, and "states that can't reach the bound stay on deterministic fallback". 3) Overnight those calls go to humans. 4) The new hires were never trained on current disclosures, the jurisdiction profiles or Reg Z wording, because those live in code. 5) They improvise the riskiest conversations at full volume. Bainbridge (Automatica, 1983) showed that automation leaves operators least practiced exactly when they must take over in the hardest situations. Here the fallback exists for the hardest cases (uncertified states, unclear callers), and contact-center turnover is high. The design counts "fallback to human" as safe without requiring trained, current human capacity.
- Defense: The fallback is not humans improvising. R140 says that when no compatible bundle exists, "QUOTE/READ_BACK states drop to lead-gen or callback fallback", with no price and no terms. R169 says unintelligible fallback "books nothing". R47 issues contracts in registration states through "the registered salesperson of record". R134's outbox monitor blocks CONTRACT_SENT without the compiled READ_BACK, so no human can close on improvised terms. R142's admission control already stops new dials first when capacity drops, so the system cuts volume instead of overflowing. Gap: human fallback capacity and currency aren't gated.
- Verdict: The overnight flood is already blocked. R263's human-capacity token reserves fallback load against staffed headroom and throttles dialing within seconds, and R142 admission control stops new dials first. R258 prevents expiry storms for value-only row changes. Fallback is not humans improvising terms: R140 drops QUOTE/READ_BACK to lead-gen or callback, R169 books nothing on unintelligible fallback, and R134 blocks any contract without the compiled READ_BACK. Human training currency is not gated, but the harm path in the scenario does not run.


### Round 334. Winner: Designer
- Topic: History pseudostates resume a stale substate after a GLOBAL detour without re-running the guard that first admitted it
- Attack: In QUOTE.LOAN_TERMS the caller asks "who is this company again?" GLOBAL request_caller_identity plays the fixed answer, then returns to QUOTE through a (deep) history state, as is normal for detours. During the detour the caller also says "actually we're the Fresno house, not the Bay one", setting jurisdiction_changed. History re-enters QUOTE.LOAN_TERMS directly. The guard that chose LOAN_TERMS (lead-gen-mode false, financing allowed in this jurisdiction) was evaluated under the old jurisdiction and never re-runs. The agent quotes financing where only lead-gen mode is allowed. History restores where you were, not whether you may still be there. Entry guards are checked only on the original edge, so every detour-and-return is an unguarded entry. Invariants written as "edges into X require guard G" pass while history bypasses G. UML: history re-enters the last active substate, with no guard re-evaluation (UML 2.5 sec. 14.2.3.4.5).
- Defense: The design never uses history pseudostates. A detour is just the root handler: R192 requires that for every state and every GLOBAL event the effective handler is the root GLOBAL handler, checked on the enumerated binary relation (R134). In the scenario, the Fresno remark is itself a GLOBAL event. jurisdiction_changed is "fired on any stated residence change, validated or not" and "re-evaluates hour/attempt/disclosure guards -> WRAP_UP" (R70). R189 makes GLOBAL multi-label, so it fires alongside request_caller_identity, in a fixed order. R211(2) adds a second net: a property other than the lead's means "Are we talking about the home on <street>?" and, on a mismatch, lead-gen. R58 means one turn makes at most one transition, evaluated against the start-of-turn state. So no unguarded return to QUOTE happens in the same turn.
- Verdict: The design never specifies history pseudostates; GLOBAL events are root-handled (R192), and the residence remark is itself a GLOBAL jurisdiction_changed event that re-evaluates guards with unvalidated places added as candidates under most-restrictive-wins (R70, R189 multi-label). R47's lead-gen invariant is model-checked across all profiles, so any return path into a price state under a lead-gen profile would fail the check rather than pass vacuously.

### Round 335. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Timers live in user variables outside the graph, so a timer armed in one state fires after a GLOBAL exit into another
- Attack: StateSmith has no built-in timers; the usual pattern arms a timestamp variable on entry and checks it in a `do` handler. READ_BACK arms "silence > 6 s -> re-prompt: shall I send the agreement?" The caller says "my husband just collapsed", sensitive_event exits to the scripted exit. The timer variable is never cleared (READ_BACK's exit action doesn't clear it on this path). In the exit state, the shared `do` handler sees the expired deadline and plays the re-prompt: "Shall I send the agreement now?" The model checker explores control states and events. An armed timer is data, not a state, so a stale timer that leaks across exits is invisible to it. Every GLOBAL exit needs every timer cleared, and nothing checks that. That gap is exactly how a pitch line gets played into an emergency exit.
- Defense: Timers are not free-floating user variables here. Silence is a deterministic front-end event: "silence as deterministic events (no_speech_after_answer, silence(duration_bucket))" (R65). Waits are explicit graph substates: PAUSE, HOLD and WAITING_FOR_<effect> "with timer and handlers" (R55, with a build completeness check). An event is delivered to whatever state is current. Once sensitive_event has exited READ_BACK, a silence event lands in the scripted exit, which "no pitch" makes structurally unable to offer an agreement (R19). R193 events "carry a session epoch, and stale ones are dropped". Every utterance is a fragment certified by id, and R172's status-claim lint is keyed to state. So in our design a READ_BACK re-prompt fragment cannot be rendered from an exit state.
- Verdict: Silence is a deterministic front-end event delivered to whatever state is current (R65), waits are explicit graph substates with their own timers and a build completeness check (R55), and the mailbox drops stale events by epoch (R193). The attack's mechanism, a free-floating timestamp read by a shared `do` handler, is not how the design models time, and exit states play only scripted no-pitch fragments (R19).

### Round 336. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Overlapping guards resolved by edge order: first-true-wins quietly picks the sale over the honest exit, and a diagram edit flips the order between releases
- Attack: The suitability table returns roof_age=24 (ROOF_FIRST condition) and move_horizon=18 months (NOT_SUITABLE condition). Both outgoing guards from QUALIFY are true. StateSmith takes the first by behavior order. In v14 a designer redrew the edges, and the order prefix changed from 1/2 to 2/1. ROOF_FIRST, which upsells a reroof plus solar, now beats NOT_SUITABLE for every caller who is planning to move. All invariants still pass, because both targets are "legal". The design checks invariants, not guard mutual exclusion or determinism. When guards overlap, the outcome is decided by a priority number edited in a drawing tool. Neither edge breaks a safety property, so the checker passes it, but the policy outcome flips with no test failing and no row changing in the suitability table.
- Defense: QUALIFY does not choose between ROOF_FIRST and NOT_SUITABLE with two overlapping graph guards. Per R71, the "versioned compliance-owned suitability table -> NOT_SUITABLE (honest polite exit, no rebuttal) or ROOF_FIRST" returns one outcome per call from the table. The graph switches on that single enum, so edge priority cannot flip the policy. The table is versioned, and "changing a row decertifies every branch that cites it". Unsuitable closes are Tier A harm in the per-call budget (R71), so a release that flipped the outcome would fail certification on audited real calls. The success label requires passing a sampled suitability audit. Also, R134 diffs the compiled (state, event, abstract-guard) relation against the exported graph, so a redrawn order shows up as a changed relation.
- Verdict: The design checks invariants over reachable states, not guard determinism: nothing requires outgoing guards at a choice point to be mutually exclusive, and R6 precedence applies to Needle event sets, not to graph guards. R71 says the table yields NOT_SUITABLE or ROOF_FIRST but defines no resolution when several rows match, and R134's diff compares the binary to the (already redrawn) exported graph, so a reordered diagram passes. The defense's closing elements (severity order, SMT exclusivity check, golden suitability regression) are all new.
- Fix: Give suitability rows a fixed severity order (NOT_SUITABLE > ... > ROOF_FIRST > OK) with the table returning the most severe match, and add a build check (SMT over the abstract guard domain) that outgoing guards at every choice point are pairwise exclusive and jointly exhaustive, so behaviour-order numbers can never decide an outcome; replay a golden suitability suite every release with compliance sign-off on any changed outcome.

### Round 337. Winner: Designer
- Topic: "Every commit passes READ_BACK" is true but insufficient: the commit gate keys on a question id that is constant across read-backs, so a yes to an old read-back binds new terms
- Attack: Read-back #1 says 7.2 kW at a total of $31,400. The caller asks "can we do the smaller one?" The machine backtracks, recomputes 5.8 kW and plays read-back #2. Midway, the caller barges in: "yes, yes, the one you said first." The COMMIT gate checks "short lexical affirm to this read-back question id". Both read-backs use the same fragment and question id, so the gate is satisfied. The slot store now holds 5.8 kW, so the envelope goes out for terms the caller explicitly rejected. The proven invariant is a path property: READ_BACK was visited. The property that matters is a data property: the affirm answers a read-back whose content hash equals the slot-store version that gets committed. Nothing ties the commit to which read-back instance it answers, so the invariant holds while the wrong terms get signed.
- Defense: The commit is bound to content, not just to having visited READ_BACK. R23: "Read-back bound to config hash; any change after read-back forces a new read-back." R49: "accept_offer commits only the quote hash read back immediately before the yes". R134 adds an independent outbox monitor that refuses CONTRACT_SENT unless the trace has READ_BACK "with a playback-engine completion mark for the same quote hash". In the scenario, read-back #2 was interrupted: mandatory segments count only on uninterrupted playback (R39). The commit question plays only after the last segment (R106), and AWAIT_* is entered only when it finishes (R14). So there is no open question to affirm. "Yes, yes, the one you said first" also fails the short direct-affirm check (R81), and R144 makes the commit prompt "say 'send the contract'".
- Verdict: The commit is already bound to content: read-back is bound to the config hash (R23), accept_offer commits only the quote hash read back immediately before the yes (R49), affirms carry the target question id (R58), and the independent outbox monitor refuses CONTRACT_SENT without a playback completion mark for the same quote hash (R134). An interrupted read-back #2 has no completion mark (R39, R14), and "yes, the one you said first" fails the short-direct-affirm check (R81) and routes to the reference resolver (R4).

### Round 338. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Counters reset on state entry plus data abstraction in the checker mean "clarify once, then fallback" and the call budgets never fire across a two-state ping-pong
- Attack: The clarify counter is a per-state variable zeroed in the entry action. The caller's turns alternate between "what?" (QUOTE -> CLARIFY_PRICE) and a dispute_prior_statement detour (re-read the table value, return to QUOTE). Each return re-enters QUOTE and zeroes the counter. The turn budget counts only LOCAL turns, and detours are GLOBAL. For 40 minutes the agent re-reads the price. It never falls back or wraps up, and the caller keeps getting pitched. Model checkers make slot data tractable by abstracting counters away or bounding them, so a counter that never reaches its limit shows up as at most a spurious, dismissed loop, never as a liveness violation. "Every call eventually reaches WRAP_UP" is then proved over an abstraction in which counters always count up.
- Defense: The premise is not our design. R69 counters are "total turns, per-state visits, backtracks, repeated objections, wall-clock -> WRAP_UP". Total turns and wall-clock count every turn, GLOBAL detours included. Per-state visits catches the ping-pong by counting re-entries into QUOTE and CLARIFY_PRICE, and no entry action zeroes them. dispute_prior_statement is a backtrack-class event, so the backtrack counter climbs too. "Clarify once, then fallback" is a call-level rule, and repeated unclear() feeds the vulnerability counter -> NO_SALE_SAFE_EXIT (R15). The design already anticipates the abstraction gap: "slot abstraction for model checking audited against live calls" (R49), plus property-based random walks over loops. R132's label-free monitors (clarify rate, CUSUM) would flag a 40-minute call.
- Verdict: R69's budget counters already include total turns, per-state visits, backtracks and wall-clock, so a 40-minute ping-pong hits the wall-clock and visit limits regardless of any per-state clarify counter reset. R49 audits the slot abstraction against live calls and runs random walks over loops.

### Round 339. Winner: Designer
- Topic: GLOBAL exit sequences are not absorbing: root handlers stay live inside the stop/cancel exit, so a second GLOBAL event re-routes an opt-out back toward sales
- Attack: The caller says "stop calling me." The stop/DNC exit is a short sequence of states under the root: confirm opt-out, outbox copy, goodbye. Mid-confirmation the caller asks "wait, who is this, is this a real person?" Root request_caller_identity fires (the most general handler), plays the fixed answer and then returns by history to the last sales state, QUOTE. The pitch resumes after a recorded opt-out. Equally, "let me talk to someone" inside the exit triggers request_human, a warm transfer to a closer. The checked invariant is "stop reaches the exit". It is not "after stop, no sales state or transfer to sales is reachable". Exit states inherit the root's GLOBAL transitions like any other state. An opt-out followed by more selling is a revocation violation under the TCPA and TSR (47 CFR 64.1200; 16 CFR 310.4(b)(1)(iii)(A)).
- Defense: "No pitch after decline" is already a model-checked whole-graph invariant (R21), and it is exactly the "after stop, no sales state reachable" property. A stop is not a pausable sequence either. Tier 1 stops "end immediately" (R24). The DTMF stop does "DNC write, fixed confirmation, hang up" (R75), and leave_taking "hangs up, with no question and no pitch" (R164). R189 orders the DNC write first, and "any transfer carries the DNC flag and is service-only". So request_human inside the exit cannot reach a closer. R138's dial permit reads the DNC ledger. A history return to QUOTE would also violate R21, which is checked on the compiled relation (R134).
- Verdict: "No pitch after decline" is already a whole-graph model-checked invariant (R21), Tier 1 stops end immediately (R24), the DTMF and leave-taking paths are confirm-then-hang-up (R75, R164), and R189 orders the DNC write first and makes any transfer service-only with the DNC flag. A return to QUOTE after a stop would break R21 on the checked relation (R134).

### Round 340. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Vacuous passes after a graph refactor: properties written against state names stay green when those states are split, renamed or bypassed
- Attack: The spec says "AG(in COMMIT -> previously READ_BACK)" and "commit tools only enabled in COMMIT". In v15 COMMIT is split into COMMIT_CASH and COMMIT_LOAN for a new lender, and QUOTE gains a fast path straight to COMMIT_LOAN. The old properties still name COMMIT, which is now unreachable, so both hold vacuously. CI shows all invariants passed. COMMIT_LOAN enables the e-sign tool with no READ_BACK on the fast path. Model checkers report a property as satisfied when its antecedent is never reached, unless vacuity checking is turned on. The property list is hand-maintained, while the graph changes every release. No check says "every state with a commit tool is covered by a property", so the safety of each release depends on someone remembering to rename strings.
- Defense: The key safety check does not depend on state names. R134's independently written outbox monitor "refuses CONTRACT_SENT or any commit effect unless the committed trace contains READ_BACK with a playback-engine completion mark for the same quote hash". It keys on effects, so a fast path to COMMIT_LOAN gets refused at the outbox. Tool exposure is generated, not hand-named: "tool lists derived from handlers (build fails on offered-but-unhandled)" (R52), and "commit tools exist only in post-read-back states" (R27). R85 phrases the invariant as "every path to any commit state", meaning a class, not a string. Matched-pair tests, random walks (R49) and Tier A certification on real calls cover any new state. A split state starts uncertified and runs on fallback.
- Verdict: The decisive safeguard is not a name-based property: R134's independent outbox monitor refuses any commit effect unless the committed trace holds a read-back completion mark for the same quote hash, so a fast path to COMMIT_LOAN is blocked at the outbox. Tool lists are derived from handlers (R52) and new states start uncertified on fallback (R3).

### Round 341. Winner: Designer
- Topic: An `else` / default branch on a choice point fails open when the policy table gains a value the graph was never generated for
- Attack: A suitability choice point covers roof_material values ASPHALT, TILE and METAL, with a mandatory default `else -> QUOTE`. Ops adds SLATE_REQUIRES_ENGINEERING and WOOD_SHAKE_FIRE_ZONE to the versioned policy table. The table's certificate expires and is reissued, but the graph is not regenerated. Every slate or wood-shake roof falls through `else` straight to QUOTE. These are fire-zone homes that should have gone to NOT_SUITABLE or an engineering review. The model check of the unchanged graph still passes. The graph and the policy table are versioned separately. Guards switch on enumerations the table owns, and nothing checks that the switches are exhaustive against the current table. A default branch written for "unusual but fine" silently takes every new category, including the dangerous ones. The checker treats `else` as a legal edge, so this release-time mismatch never shows up.
- Defense: The graph and the table can't silently drift apart. The facts table is in the certified bundle, and "certificate = (state, weights hash, facts-table version)" (R3). "Changing a row decertifies every branch that cites it", and certificates "expire when a cited policy row changes". Adding SLATE/WOOD_SHAKE rows expires QUALIFY's certificate, so it runs on deterministic fallback. Reissuing a certificate means replaying gold sets under the new table (R3). R140 continuously re-checks bundles "as compatible with that table" (lints, fact-key coverage); otherwise QUOTE/READ_BACK "drop to lead-gen or callback fallback". The suitability decision itself comes from the table (R71), not from a graph switch on roof material.
- Verdict: Under R71 the suitability decision is an outcome produced by the compliance-owned table, not a graph switch on roof material, so a new SLATE or WOOD_SHAKE row carries its own outcome. Certificates are keyed on the facts-table version and must be re-earned by gold replay (R3), and R140 drops QUOTE/READ_BACK to fallback when a bundle is not compatible with the current table.

### Round 342. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Exit and entry actions run on every path across a state boundary, so effects placed there fire on GLOBAL stop and cancel exits
- Attack: WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE's exit action enqueues "reminder SMS with signing link", written for the normal path to WRAP_UP. The caller, still on the line, says "cancel it, don't contact me." The cancel_or_rescind GLOBAL exit leaves WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE, the exit action runs during run-to-completion, and the turn commits, so the outbox drains. The caller's phone buzzes with "Tap to sign your solar agreement" seconds after they cancelled. The invariants are stated over transitions. Effects hidden in entry and exit behaviors fire on every edge across the boundary, including the GLOBAL ones nobody wrote with that action in mind. Texting after an opt-out or revocation violates the TCPA revocation rules (47 CFR 64.1200(a)(10), effective April 2025), and "committed turn" doesn't distinguish a stop turn from a sale turn.
- Defense: Effects in this design are never fired directly. They are intents appended to the outbox and "drained only on committed turns" (R13). The outbox has its own gates. R149 extends "the consent gate to every outbound SMS and email". R189 handles a multi-intent turn in a fixed order: "DNC write, then cancel", so the DNC/cancel record exists before the turn commits. R122 makes registration an outbox guard. R216 makes CANCEL a saga with an invariant against further action while a cancel is open. R173 says a cancel hit voids the envelope through the vendor API. R196's SMS budgets add another check. The reminder intent reaches a drain that sees a revoked contact and refuses it.
- Verdict: Effects are only outbox intents drained on committed turns (R13), and the drain is gated: consent gate on every SMS (R149), DNC write ordered before cancel in the same turn (R189), SMS guards and budgets (R122, R196), and the cancel saga plus envelope void (R216, R173). A reminder intent from an exit action meets a revoked contact at the drain.

### Round 343. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Untimed model checking can't state or prove deadline properties, so the graph is "verified" while hard time limits are broken
- Attack: The checker abstracts time: `after(n)` becomes a nondeterministic event. Required properties are timed: hang up within seconds of a stop; leave no live call unanswered beyond the abandonment threshold; let the OTP expire before a resend; end the call before a quiet-hours boundary in the called party's zone. A call at 8:58 pm enters READ_BACK, which takes 4 minutes. The untimed model happily allows "calling-hours guard checked at dial, call continues" to run past 9:00 pm into COMMIT and the SMS at 9:03 pm. Calling-hours guards sit at pre-dial. An untimed graph can't express "no sales fragment after 9 pm local", only orderings, so the time-boundary violation is invisible to the proof. TCPA hours run 8 am to 9 pm local to the called party (47 CFR 64.1200(c)(1)), and Florida's limit is stricter (Fla. Stat. 501.616: 8 am to 8 pm).
- Defense: The time guarantees don't rest on the untimed proof. The design enforces them in deterministic runtime code. R69's counters include "wall-clock -> WRAP_UP". Stops "end immediately" (R24), within ten seconds. The abandonment threshold is a per-call measured invariant: "first PREAMBLE segment starts within about 1.5 s" plus a 30-day counter (R109, R142). R139 gives the dial gate pinned tzdata, authenticated time and a guard band. R70 and R183 re-evaluate hours guards mid-call on a jurisdiction or location change. The R122 outbox guard treats texts as solicitation, and R149 gates every SMS. Note that 64.1200(c)(1) governs initiating solicitations, so a 9:03 pm SMS is a new initiation and gets its own guard.
- Verdict: Time limits are enforced by runtime code rather than claimed by the untimed proof: wall-clock budget to WRAP_UP (R69), immediate stop handling (R24), per-call preamble deadline and abandonment counter (R109, R142), and a dial gate with pinned tzdata, authenticated time and a guard band (R139). The hours rules govern initiating solicitations, so continuing a lawfully started call is not the violation the attack claims, and later SMS go through their own outbox guards.


### Round 344. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: The compiled preamble order puts the opt-out announcement far more than two seconds after the identification, as 64.1200(b)(3) requires for artificial-voice calls
- Attack: Callee: "Hello?" The agent plays the unskippable PREAMBLE in its compiled order: company name (identification), sales purpose, AI designation, recording notice, then the opt-out sentence, with the ADMT notice and anti-phishing line around it. On a normal speaking rate the opt-out sentence starts 8 to 12 seconds after the company name. The model checker proves every segment plays uninterrupted and in order. It proves nothing about timing, so every compliant-looking call breaks the timing rule. AI voices count as "artificial voice" (FCC 24-17). 47 CFR 64.1200(b)(3) requires that the automated voice/key-press opt-out be "announced within two (2) seconds of providing the identification information". The design orders segments by legal topic and has no timing invariant (untimed checking, #392, is a different gap). The violation is identical on every connected call. (ecfr.gov 64.1200; law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/47/64.1200)
- Defense: The attack assumes the opt-out sits last in a topic-ordered list. It doesn't. R75 makes the (b)(3) opt-out sentence a regulation-profile requirement "with position lint", and R165 caps segment 1 (seller, purpose, AI) at about 6 s. The sentence is therefore placed by the profile, not by topic. Timing is also statically knowable: R105 pre-renders every fact-bearing clip with an audio hash, so each segment's duration is a build-time constant. R39 playout marks and the R116 render log record actual delivery per call. The real gap is that the position lint checks order, not a 2-second gap. Since clip durations are fixed, closing it is a mechanical check, not a redesign.
- Verdict: 64.1200(b)(3) requires the opt-out mechanism to be announced within two seconds of the identification. The design's R75 only requires an opt-out sentence with a position lint (order, not timing), and the final plan's compiled preamble puts the opt-out after purpose, AI designation and recording notice, well past 2 s. The defense admits the lint has no timing bound. Its fix (opt-out moved into segment 1, a duration-sum build check) is entirely new.
- Fix: Make the (b)(3) profile row place the opt-out clause immediately after the seller name inside segment 1. Add a build check that sums the pre-rendered clip durations (R105) between the end of the identification and the start of the opt-out and fails above 1.5 s, add a per-call playout-mark check that flags any breach as a Tier A harm, and add the deadline to the timed model.

### Round 345. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: An opt-out spoken before the preamble: the unskippable preamble either pitches after the stop or ends the call without the identification every artificial-voice call must give
- Attack: Callee picks up: "If you're selling something, take me off your list." In OPENING, the stop lexicon fires the GLOBAL stop. Branch 1: the PREAMBLE is "compiled, unskippable", so the agent states its company, sales purpose and recording notice after a clear stop. Branch 2: the stop exit pre-empts the preamble, so the call ends with "You've been removed" and the callee never learns which seller they opted out of. That leaves them unable to enforce it and leaves the call unidentified. 64.1200(b)(1) requires every artificial-voice message to identify the responsible entity "at the beginning". The TSR and 64.1200(d) make the opt-out run against the named seller. Talking past a stop is a separate violation. The design says the preamble can't be skipped and that GLOBAL exits fire "from any state", and it never resolves which one wins in OPENING.
- Defense: There's no unresolved race. R192 model-checks, for every (state, GLOBAL event) pair, that the effective handler is the root GLOBAL handler, and a lint forbids substates (PREAMBLE included) from shadowing it. A stop in OPENING therefore enters STOP. R339 puts STOP in a TERMINAL region with no return edge, and AG(dnc_flag -> AG ¬sales_fragment) makes the sales-purpose segment unreachable after the stop. Branch 1 is impossible. Branch 2 is the intended behaviour, but the attack has a fair point about identification: STOP's "fixed confirmation" (R75) is not required to name the seller.
- Verdict: The race itself is resolved by existing elements: R192's per-pair root-handler invariant and R339's TERMINAL region make the stop win in OPENING, and the sales segment becomes unreachable, so branch 1 is impossible. Branch 2 remains. The stop exit speaks an artificial-voice confirmation (R75 "fixed confirmation") that nothing requires to name the seller. R31's DISCLOSED invariant only covers paths into sales, so an answered call can end without the identification that 64.1200(b)(1) and TSR 310.4(d) require.
- Fix: Compile every TERMINAL fragment that can be the first agent speech to begin with the seller identity and callback number, enforced by a lint on the seller and callback keys. Add a model-checked invariant that every path from a human answer to HANGUP plays an identification-bearing fragment, and send a DNC confirmation SMS naming the seller.

### Round 346. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Garbled first words trigger the root-level clarify/unintelligible re-ask in OPENING, so the bot's first words are "Sorry, I didn't catch that" and not who is calling
- Attack: Callee answers mid-bite while fumbling the phone: "Mmph-lo? ...wh's this?" ASR confidence falls below the pre-gate, so the uncertainty handler, which works "from any state", plays the unintelligible re-ask. The callee hears an unidentified synthetic voice asking them to repeat themselves, the classic robocall/scam pattern, and answers "Who IS this?" or hangs up. If the second turn is also low-confidence, "clarify once, then fallback" books a callback or warm transfer before any identification has played. The identity must come "at the beginning of the message" (64.1200(b)(1)). TSR 310.4(d) requires prompt disclosure. The uncertainty gates are global and run ahead of the PREAMBLE, so the first turn, the one with the worst audio, can produce non-identifying speech and even a booked callback to someone who never heard who was calling.
- Defense: The scenario's worst result is already removed. R169 says a call that ends on unintelligible fallback "books nothing" and ends with "I'll let you go", so no callback can be booked for someone who never heard the identification. R109 requires the first PREAMBLE segment to start within about 1.5 s of the completed greeting, and an unresolved AMD result is treated as human. "Who IS this?" is the GLOBAL request_caller_identity event, answered with a fixed identity reply. The remaining gap is real but narrow: the root uncertainty re-ask could still be the first audio the callee hears.
- Verdict: R109 already requires PREAMBLE segment 1 to start within about 1.5 s of the completed greeting, measured per call, with unresolved AMD treated as human. That leaves no room for a re-ask-first opening. R169 removes the worst outcome: an unintelligible fallback books nothing. "Who IS this?" is GLOBAL request_caller_identity, which has a fixed identity answer. Masking clarify in OPENING tightens a constraint that already exists.

### Round 347. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Front-end clipping at answer: the callee's first syllables are lost while the media path comes up, so "Don't call..." reaches Needle as "call..." and "No, I'm not interested" as "I'm interested"
- Attack: The callee lifts the handset already talking: "No, not interested, don't call here." SIP answer, RTP start and the jitter buffer take 300 to 600 ms, so the stream starts at "...interested, ...call here." The ASR transcript is fluent and high-confidence. The stop lexicon finds no "don't call" or "not interested". Needle labels it acknowledge or small_talk, and the full preamble and pitch follow. On a callback request ("No, call me back next week"), the clipped version can come out as consent. VoIP front-end clipping is a known effect: the first word lost at answer supervision and codec start. The design's defenses (pre-gate, grounding, near-tie clarify) all assume the transcript is wrong in a low-confidence way. A clean truncation passes every gate, and it clips negations and opt-outs, which come first in the sentence at pickup.
