- Defense: A clipped "...I'm interested" in OPENING has no consequence: no OPENING tool writes interest, consent or a callback. Consent exists only as the pre-dial PEWC artifact (R74). Bookings require identity && owner && adult (R9), so nothing can book before IDENTITY. The preamble plays anyway and carries the opt-out sentence (R75), which gives the callee a second chance seconds later. COMMIT needs a lexical affirm to a specific read-back question id. The real gap is the lost opt-out: a clipped "don't call here" becomes "...call here" and escapes Tier 1.
- Verdict: Nothing in OPENING can write interest, consent or a callback. Consent comes only from the R74 PEWC ledger, bookings need identity && owner && adult (R9), and COMMIT needs a lexical affirm to a read-back question id, so a clipped "...I'm interested" does no harm. Onset clipping removes only the first few hundred milliseconds, so in the attack's own example "don't call here" survives and reaches the lexicon. The preamble's opt-out sentence (R75) also gives the callee a fresh chance seconds later.

### Round 348. Winner: Designer
- Topic: A child answers and the non-owner handling asks when a parent will be home, getting and storing "I'm home alone till six"
- Attack: A 9-year-old answers: "Hello?" The PREAMBLE plays, then IDENTITY reaches non-owner handling: "Is [lead name] available? When's a good time to reach them?" Child: "Mom's at work, I'm by myself until six." Needle fills the callback slot ("six") from grounded text. The transcript (child alone, address known from the lead record, empty-house window) goes to the CRM, the booked callback, the warm-transfer context, the audit labelers and, in lead-gen mode, partner installers. The callback fragment asks anyone, child or adult, for exactly the "when they are away from home" safety data that privacy norms treat as sensitive. No event marks a minor speaker, so nothing suppresses the question or redacts the answer. "Real data never replaced" keeps it permanently. The existing "children" topic covered identity, not this disclosure.
- Defense: The design never authorizes the question in the attack. R9 guards bookings with identity && owner && adult. R176 binds VERIFIED to a named record owner, and anyone else takes the non-owner path with no close. A child cannot write a callback that leads anywhere. R7 limits collection to coarse facts, R74 redacts third-party contact details, and R143 mutes IDENTITY-state answers for labelers. R253 blocks partner handoff without the notice. Still, the design doesn't forbid a non-owner fragment from asking about someone's whereabouts, and it has no redaction for "home alone" disclosures. That gap gets closed.
- Verdict: The question in the attack ("when's a good time to reach them?") is not in the design. R9 lets only identity && owner && adult write a booking, and R176 sends any non-record-owner down the no-close non-owner path. R7 limits collection to coarse facts. R74 redacts third-party details, and R143 mutes IDENTITY-state answers for labelers. A volunteered "home alone" remark is a residual retention issue, not an exploit the design invites.

### Round 349. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Wait-for-callee records and analyzes the callee's greeting and room audio before the recording notice plays
- Attack: The callee answers and says to their spouse, before speaking into the phone: "It's another solar spam, honey, I'll tell them off." Then: "Hello?" The OPENING waits for the callee, so ASR, AMD, prosody, speaker-change and far-field features run on all of this. Media is recorded from answer. The recording notice is segment four of the PREAMBLE, about 10 seconds later. The pre-notice household audio is stored with the call, labeled by humans and folded into the training union. California Penal Code 632 and 632.7 (cellular) require consent before recording a confidential communication, and the notice is how consent is inferred. By design, the opening captures speech before any notice, on every call, including things said to others in the home. Earlier CIPA attacks targeted the training loop, not this unavoidable pre-notice window.
- Defense: California is all-party consent, so R40 already puts it in NO_RETAIN mode unless retention consent is given. Pre-notice audio there is processed in memory, never stored, labeled or trained on. "Build pipeline rejects transcripts lacking retention-consent flag." R118 keeps front-end features per-utterance, in memory and discarded. R116 retains only the CDR and agent-side audio. R249 requires self-hosted ASR in all-party-consent states, so no third party listens. R109 starts the preamble within 1.5 s. The honest gap is that retention consent, once given, could be read as covering the earlier pre-notice span in other states too.
- Verdict: The cited law is California's, and R40 already puts all-party-consent jurisdictions in NO_RETAIN (in memory only) until affirmative retention consent is given. The build pipeline rejects transcripts without the consent flag. R118 keeps front-end features per-utterance and discards them, and R249 requires self-hosted ASR in those states. Whether later consent could cover the pre-notice span retroactively is a narrow clarification, not a failure on every call.

### Round 350. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Institutional greetings (hospital, nursing home, care facility or business switchboard) have no event, so the bot runs the sales preamble and asks to be put through to the resident
- Attack: Callee: "Good afternoon, Maple Grove Senior Living, this is Pam." Needle in OPENING: small_talk/acknowledge. The PREAMBLE plays to the receptionist. IDENTITY asks for the lead by name, and Pam transfers the call to the resident's room phone. An 84-year-old resident hears the agent continue at IDENTITY, missing the preamble, and the sale proceeds. The suitability table asks about roof age for a house the resident may no longer live in. 47 U.S.C. 227(b)(1)(A)(ii) separately restricts artificial-voice calls "to the telephone line of any guest room or patient room of a hospital, health care facility, elderly home, or similar establishment". The consent on file named a number, not that room line. The script asks to be transferred, and nothing in OPENING recognizes a facility greeting as a reason to exit.
- Defense: The resident does hear the preamble. A new party after a transfer is a speaker_change, and R112 sends it to NEW_PARTY, which "replays the compiled disclosure block" and reverts to NO_RETAIN. The sale can't proceed either. R176 binds VERIFIED to a record owner. R209 cross-checks owner_occupied against homestead and mailing records, so a resident of senior living fails and gets NOT_SUITABLE. R148 blocks any close after a second voice. Consent is per number (R74), and R73 checks the Reassigned Numbers Database. The real gap is that nothing recognizes an institutional answer, and the IDENTITY ask can lead the receptionist to transfer to a 227(b)(1)(A)(ii) room line.
- Verdict: After a transfer the new voice is a speaker_change. R112's NEW_PARTY replays the full disclosure, so the resident does hear the preamble. R209's owner_occupied cross-check, R176's record-owner binding and R148's no-close-after-second-voice stop the sale. 227(b)(1)(A)(ii) also exempts calls made with the called party's prior express consent, and the resident is the consenting lead. The harms in the attack are already blocked.

### Round 351. Winner: Designer
- Topic: An opt-out spoken by the callee's machine (screening assistant quick reply or the outgoing greeting) at the opening is routed to screener/voicemail handling, outside the stop union
- Attack: The callee's phone answers with its call-screening assistant. The owner reads the live transcript and taps a canned reply, and the phone's synthetic voice says "Please remove me from your mailing list." Variant: a landline greeting says "If you are a telemarketer, place this number on your do-not-call list." The design routes screener audio to IDENTITY screener handling and machine greetings to VOICEMAIL, both at the root. Neither path passes through the stop lexicon or Needle GLOBAL, and the bot leaves its voicemail or waits. The FCC's 2024 revocation order lets consumers revoke consent "by any reasonable means". A request the owner chose and transmitted is theirs, whatever voice speaks it. Pixel Call Screen offers tap-to-speak replies (support.google.com/phoneapp/answer/9118387). The post-call DNC sweep may also skip voicemail and screener legs, so the number gets redialed.
- Defense: The design's stop handling doesn't depend on the voice. R9: "decline detector speaker-agnostic." R192 puts the GLOBAL handler at the root for every state, screener and VOICEMAIL substates included. R295 model-checks that no front-end tag can stop text from reaching the lexicon and the GLOBAL stop pass. R24 Tier 1 includes "take me off" and "remove my number". R66 sweeps every transcript before any redial, R113 suppresses when no transcript exists, and the R138 pending_sweep lock holds the number until then. The gap: the design never says that VOICEMAIL transcribes the outgoing greeting, so "place this number on your do-not-call list" could go unprocessed.
- Verdict: R9 makes the decline detector speaker-agnostic. R192 puts the root GLOBAL handler over screener and VOICEMAIL substates, and R295 model-checks that no front-end tag keeps text from the lexicon and GLOBAL stop pass. OPENING already runs ASR on greetings to expose machine_greeting() (R35). R66 sweeps every transcript before a redial, and R113 fails closed when none exists. A synthetic-voice "remove me" therefore hits the same stop path.

### Round 352. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Whispered answers ("I'm in a meeting / the baby's asleep, not now") fall below the VAD and ASR pre-gate, and the bot answers a whisper at full volume
- Attack: The callee answers in a hospital waiting room and whispers: "Can't talk, don't call this number." Whispered speech has no voicing or pitch, so energy VAD and the no-speech pre-gate treat it as silence or low confidence. Then either the wait-for-callee timeout fires, or the unintelligible re-ask plays at full level. The prosody final-rise token is undefined without F0. The whispered stop never reaches the lexicon. The callee hangs up, and the number stays dialable. Whispered speech is a known failure mode for ASR and VAD: high error rates and no fundamental frequency, so pitch features fail. The design has no whisper stratum in certification, and its silence and garble handling assumes a voiced speaker. A quiet opt-out at the opening is lost and only recoverable by the post-call sweep if the ASR ever transcribed it.
- Defense: Quiet speech is already a certified stratum. R111 lists hypophonia among the release-blocking speech strata, and R147 adds the extended-timing profile. R295 says a no-speech tag can't stop text from reaching the stop pass. R108 stops playout on any near-end energy burst, even one the pre-gate rejects. R169 is the key backstop: a low-confidence or unintelligible span keeps the number under the R138 pending_sweep lock "until a human has listened to every low-confidence or unintelligible span for an opt-out". The whispered stop is therefore not lost, because a human hears it before any redial. R104 means a whisper can never commit. Whisper itself still deserves its own stratum and a gentler response.
- Verdict: R111's hypophonia stratum covers quiet voiced speech, not unvoiced whisper. R169's human review of low-confidence spans applies only to calls that end on unintelligible fallback, not to a callee who whispers and hangs up. R295 and the raw-ASR sweep help only if ASR emits text, which energy/voicing-based endpointing may never pass on for a whisper. With no whisper stratum, a systematic miss would not block release, and a whispered opt-out could leave the number dialable.
- Fix: Add a non-identifying unvoiced-speech detector ahead of the pre-gate so a whisper is never treated as silence or a timeout. End the call on a detected whisper with a fixed low-gain exit, count it as indirect_decline, and put the number under suppress-pending until a human has reviewed the span. Add a whispered-speech certification stratum with Tier A false-continue bounds.

### Round 353. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Anti-robocall tones played by a live callee (SIT "disconnected" tri-tone, TeleZapper-style) are taken as a network event, so a deliberate opt-out becomes a "bad number"
- Attack: The callee answers "Hello?", then plays the special-information tri-tone (a classic anti-telemarketer device or app) followed by "the number you have reached is not in service." Tones are out-of-band telephony events handled at the StateSmith root, which ends the call as intercepted/disconnected. No stop event is logged. Because outcomes don't write back reliably, the number is either redialed next cycle or marked invalid, and the RND/consent logic then treats a live, objecting subscriber as a vacated line. The SIT tone is a signal a human chose to send at the opening, meaning "remove me", the reason devices like TeleZapper existed. The design classifies all tones as network state and never as caller intent. Under "any reasonable means" revocation, the objection goes unrecorded, the frequency cap doesn't count it as a refusal, and the post-call sweep has no transcript to catch it.
- Defense: A SIT played after a human "Hello?" on an answered call is technically distinguishable from a network intercept. Real intercepts arrive as early media before answer, or with SIP failure codes. The design also fails closed on outcomes: R170 says every END state carries a writeback the R138 permit reads, two preamble hang-ups suppress the lead, and R113 suppresses when no transcript exists. The gap is fair: R35 routes tones to telephony handling with no rule separating a device-played tone from a carrier one, and a bad-number disposition could wrongly vacate the consent.
- Verdict: A device-played fake disconnect tone is built to fool dialers. It is not a clear statement to the caller that consent is revoked, so treating it as revocation by "reasonable means" is a stretch. Both outcomes in the attack are safe or bounded. Marking the number invalid stops calls, which is what the callee wants. Otherwise R170's END writeback, the two-hang-up suppression and the R138 permit govern any redial, and R73 alone decides reassignment.


### Round 354. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The COMMIT gate rejects the natural, enthusiastic yes, so the most willing buyers get re-asked and then pushed to fallback
- Attack: After READ_BACK the buyer says "Absolutely, let's do it, send it over." Not short, not a bare lexical affirm: closed re-ask. Buyer, puzzled: "I said yes, sure, why not." Contains "not" and a hedged "why not": polarity-ambiguous, conditional-shaped, excluded again. "Clarify once, then fallback": booked callback or warm transfer. Same for "go for it", "sign me up", "I don't see why not", "can't argue with that". The buyer hears a machine that can't take yes and cools off before the human calls. The gate was tuned only against false accepts (topics 154, 194, 339); nothing bounds false rejects. Real assent is elaborated, idiomatic and often carries negation words. The certification counts harm, not lost willing buyers, so the gate that ends the sale path is never measured on the outcome it exists to produce.
- Defense: The COMMIT gate doesn't parse an open-ended yes. Since R144, the commit question is an action-production prompt: "To have the contract sent, please say 'send the contract'". A willing buyer is told the exact words, so "Absolutely, let's do it, send it over" is one short prompt away from a clean commit, not a trap. "Why not" and "can't argue with that" are the hedged, polarity-ambiguous assents that R77 and R289 exist to keep out of a binding commit. A re-ask doesn't lose the buyer either: R239(3) sends it to a written proposal with cooling-off and a consult at a named time (R293). False rejects are not unmeasured. Fallback rate is a retirement condition (R36), drives throttling (R263), and the kept-contract metric falls whenever a willing buyer is lost.
- Verdict: The attack describes a yes/no commit question, but R144 already replaced it with an action-production prompt that tells the buyer the exact words to say, so a willing buyer is one short, explicit instruction away from a clean commit. "Why not" and "can't argue with that" are exactly the hedged assents that R77 and R289 are meant to keep out of a binding commit. A re-ask leads to a written proposal plus a booked consult (R239(3), R293) rather than losing the buyer, and fallback rate is bounded per state (R36) and throttles dialing (R263), so false rejects are measured indirectly.

### Round 355. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Read-first buyers: no path sends a written proposal before the verbal commit, so "email me the numbers, I'll sign tonight" ends the sale
- Attack: Buyer, persuaded: "Sounds good. Text me the proposal; my husband and I will read it tonight and sign." The graph reaches CONTRACT_SENT only through COMMIT, and COMMIT needs a spoken yes to the read-back. The buyer refuses to say yes to a contract unseen. The only exits are a re-ask, a booked consult or WRAP_UP. No "send written quote, no commitment" effect exists. Days later the callback hits voicemail; the lead decays. The design treats the oral yes as the gateway to paper, but a large share of high-value home-improvement buyers want paper first, which is also the cautious behaviour regulators encourage. The safest willing buyers are the ones the model-checked path structurally cannot convert.
- Defense: The premise is false. A "written proposal" is an existing outbox effect that the design uses all over the place: R56 (request_human), R152, R225 (no lender terms), R238, R239, R289, R290, R291. "My husband and I will read it tonight" is defers_decision(to=spouse). That triggers R243's SUPPORT_PERSON path: the pitch stops, the written proposal goes to the verified owner, and a human consult is booked at a time the buyer names. The husband is almost certainly a record owner, and R123 requires his own independent signature anyway, so paper first is the correct path. R232 keeps the price valid until quote_valid_until, so nothing decays from a price change. What the design refuses on purpose is sending a binding contract without READ_BACK, which is model-checked.
- Verdict: The premise is false. A written-proposal effect is used throughout the design (R56, R152, R225, R238, R239, R289 to R291), and "my husband and I will read it tonight" is defers_decision(to=spouse), which R243 routes to a written proposal plus a booked human consult. R232 holds the price until quote_valid_until, so read-first buyers have a real path to signing; only the binding contract waits for READ_BACK, which is deliberate.

### Round 356. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Audio-only READ_BACK of nine dense numeric categories exceeds listener working memory, so willing buyers stall, ask for repeats or drop
- Attack: READ_BACK speaks product, kW size, total cost, escalator %, named warranties, limitations, cancellation, lien/UCC and APR/term/payment: dozens of numbers over several minutes, no screen. Buyer: "Wait, what was the payment again? And the escalator?" Each repeat restarts or extends the read-back, spends turn and time budgets, and the call hits WRAP_UP. Buyers who don't ask just say "sure" without having retained the terms. Short-term memory holds about four chunks (Cowan 2001); a spoken list of 20-plus figures is not retainable. Fixed pre-rendered fragments can't summarise or pace to the listener. The required content alone makes the shortest compliant close long and fragile.
- Defense: Retention isn't the commit's safety basis. The signed document, the cancellation window that install waits out, and a survey-contingent price (R159) are. The read-back is still built for listeners. Each item is its own mandatory segment (R39). repair_request targets one fragment id with a repeat variant (R48), so "what was the payment?" replays one segment instead of restarting. Budgets count agent loops, not caller time (R293). R144 forces a teach-back in which the customer states payment and term (R15). R231 keeps multi-year figures out of the call. ACCESSIBLE_AUDIO gives shorter chunks (R238), and CAPTION_MODE sends a written SMS read-back (R237). A second repair, or too many digression cycles (R291), sends the buyer to a written proposal rather than a stalled call.
- Verdict: The read-back is already segmented (R39), each segment can be repeated on its own through repair_request (R48), a compact recap replays before the commit and a digression cap moves the buyer to a written proposal (R291), and budgets count agent loops rather than caller time (R293). Teach-back (R15, R144), year-one-only figures (R231), ACCESSIBLE_AUDIO (R238) and a written SMS read-back in CAPTION_MODE (R237) also exist. Retention isn't the only safety basis either, since the signed document, cancellation window and survey-contingent price (R159) back it.

### Round 357. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The e-sign link arrives as a cold text from an unknown number carrying a link and an OTP: the exact smishing pattern phones and the agent itself teach people to ignore
- Attack: Buyer says yes. An SMS from an unsaved 10DLC number: "Sign your agreement: https://… Code 482913." On iPhone, Filter Unknown Senders files it away silently, and iMessage disables links from unknown senders until the user replies. Android spam protection flags it. The buyer, told minutes earlier by the preamble's anti-phishing line to be wary, won't tap. WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE times out. Apple disables links in messages from unknown senders unless the user replies (MacRumors, Jan 2025: https://www.macrumors.com/2025/01/13/imessage-users-targeted-phishing-scam/). The FTC tells consumers not to click links in unexpected texts. The only signature channel is the one consumers are trained to distrust.
- Defense: The scenario isn't the design's message. R260(3) makes OTP templates link-free on a separate transactional short code. R272 puts contract delivery on its own registered campaign, with the brand name, owned domain only, no shorteners and STOP/HELP. Calls come from branded caller ID with A-level attestation (R59), and the lead was told the number at consent (R259). The R171 anti-phishing line lists exactly what we never ask for ("Social Security, bank or card numbers"), and a contract link isn't on that list. WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE runs while the buyer is still on the line: help_with_screen offers resend, wait or callback (R60). R120 offers paper or in-person signing, and R123 and R304 add consultant, notary and mail fallbacks.
- Verdict: The cited link-disabling applies to iMessage from unknown senders, while the design sends contract delivery and link-free OTPs on separate registered branded SMS campaigns (R260, R272) from a number the lead was told at consent (R259), and the anti-phishing line names only SSN, bank and card numbers (R171). The buyer is still on the call during WAITING_FOR_SIGNATURE, where help_with_screen offers resend, wait or callback (R60), and paper, mailed or consultant-assisted signing exist (R120, R123, R304), so a filtered text delays rather than kills the sale.

### Round 358. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Credit freezes block the lender's pull after the commit, and the design has no event, fragment or step to get them lifted
- Attack: Buyer commits to the financed option. The lender's application returns "file frozen" at Equifax/TransUnion/Experian. The buyer must lift three freezes on three bureau sites, with PINs they lost years ago. The agent can't see the lender screen, has no "credit freeze" event and can't advise. The envelope sits unsigned because the lender documents never generate. A cautious buyer (exactly the type who freezes) gives up. Freezes have been free since 2018 and are widespread: about 20% of Americans froze their credit after the Equifax breach (Wakefield/Krebs 2018: https://krebsonsecurity.com/2018/03/survey-americans-spent-1-4b-on-credit-freeze-fees-in-wake-of-equifax-breach/). The financed close has a mandatory third-party step the graph never models.
- Defense: A freeze can't strand a committed buyer, because the lender decision comes before COMMIT. R225: a financed COMMIT requires lender_verified rate, term and payment. The customer starts the lender's decision on a lender-hosted page, never by voice. A frozen file returns no lender_verified terms, so the call can commit only to the cash contract or ends with a written proposal. Lender status comes only from the API as a versioned enum (R60), so no envelope is generated on a missing decision. The agent never advises on the buyer's credit file (R7 data minimization; the R89 lint bans credit-consent requests). R232's quote_valid_until holds the price while the buyer lifts the freeze, and the lender page itself tells them how.
- Verdict: R225 already requires lender_verified terms from a lender-hosted soft-pull decision before any financed COMMIT, so a frozen file shows up before the commit, and the call can commit only to cash or end in a written proposal. Lender status comes only from a versioned API enum (R60), so no envelope is built on a missing decision, and R232 holds the price while the buyer lifts the freeze.

### Round 359. Winner: Designer
- Topic: A pending refinance, home purchase or HELOC collides with new solar debt and the UCC fixture filing; QUALIFY never asks
- Attack: Buyer is mid-refinance, closing in three weeks, and eager to lock solar too. QUALIFY asks about ownership, roof, shade and move horizon, not pending mortgage activity. The commit goes through; the solar loan inquiry and new debt appear on the mortgage lender's pre-closing credit refresh; the UCC fixture filing shows up in title. The refi underwriter suspends the file. The buyer rescinds solar to save the mortgage, or loses the rate lock. Mortgage lenders recheck for undisclosed debt and new liens before closing (Fannie Mae Selling Guide requirements on undisclosed liabilities). A willing, suitable buyer's sale fails because the suitability table lacks a timing input that decides whether the lien can exist.
- Defense: QUALIFY does ask. R208(1) is one neutral QUALIFY question: "Do you expect any change in ownership in the next few years, such as a sale, refinance, divorce or estate?" Any yes goes to NOT_SUITABLE, an honest exit. A buyer mid-refinance names refinance and exits before any quote. R208(3) has the lender's title pull check filings before the lien. Install waits out the longest cancellation window (R17), and the survey is scheduled inside it (R159). R216's cancel saga voids the lender, UCC and install legs without friction, so a buyer who discovers the conflict late loses nothing. Making R208 cover pending debt (HELOC, purchase) is a sharpening, not a missing input.
- Verdict: QUALIFY does ask. R208(1) is a neutral question about any expected change in ownership "such as a sale, refinance, divorce or estate", and any yes exits as NOT_SUITABLE before a quote. The lender title pull checks filings before the lien (R208(3)), install waits out the cancellation window (R17), and the cancel saga voids lender, UCC and install legs (R216), so a late-discovered conflict costs the buyer nothing.

### Round 360. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Two calculators: the read-back's payment and total come from the policy-table calculator, but the signed documents come from the lender's amortization engine
- Attack: READ_BACK states payment X at APR Y. The lender's documents compute from the actual first-payment date (odd-days interest), the funded amount including tax and fees, and an APR that assumes an autopay discount needing bank details the agent is banned from collecting. The signer sees $187.43, not the $184 heard, and a higher rate without autopay. The mismatch with "what the agent promised" reads as bait-and-switch, and the buyer stops signing. Certification pins the policy table, not the lender's engine. The read-back that gates the commit is guaranteed to be an approximation of the binding numbers, and every discrepancy lands at the moment of signature.
- Defense: There aren't two calculators for financed terms. R225 makes the financed read-back speak lender_verified rate, term and payment, a provenance class that only the lender API can write. The policy-table calculator never sets credit facts (writer allowlists, R5). R224 has every lender product row carry its full Reg Z payment schedule, and the no-prepayment payment is the payment owed. R309 makes the price all-in, including tax. R225 already says any envelope whose terms differ from the read-back terms gets a new quote hash and written re-disclosure. R23 binds the read-back to the config hash. A mismatch therefore can't reach the signer as a surprise, because the read-back must be redone first. Autopay can't be assumed, since no bank data is collected by voice (R45, R89).
- Verdict: There aren't two calculators for financed terms. R225 makes the financed read-back speak lender_verified rate, term and payment, only the lender API can write that provenance (R5), and any envelope whose terms differ from the read-back gets a new quote hash and written re-disclosure. R224 (full Reg Z schedule), R309 (all-in tax price) and R23 (read-back bound to config hash) mean a mismatch must be re-read before signing, not discovered at signature, and no bank data is taken by voice, so autopay can't be assumed silently (R45, R89).

### Round 361. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: The utility account holder isn't a record owner (late spouse, adult child, ex), so the interconnection paperwork has no valid signer
- Attack: Widow owns the home; the electric account is still in her late husband's name (or her son's). The sale commits, and the e-sign goes to record owners plus lender signers. The utility's interconnection application and agreement must be signed by the customer of record on the account. No envelope reaches that person, and an account transfer needs a separate utility process and possibly a new deposit. PTO stalls; install never completes. Utilities require the account holder's signature or authorization for net-metering interconnection. The design keys every signer on title and lender, never on the utility account, the third legal party the installed success outcome depends on.
- Defense: The design keys on the utility account holder already. R156(2): usage authorization must come from the utility account holder, and a mismatch with the record owner routes to a written authorization. R209: any savings figure requires bill_payer equal to a signing record owner, else no figure. R233 reads service_start_date for this account holder, and under 12 months means a written proposal only. The widow's case surfaces in QUOTE as no figure, so no same-call close occurs on numbers that can't be substantiated. Success is defined as PTO granted plus monitored production (R158), so a stalled interconnection is a Tier A harm signal ("paying, not operating"), not a hidden success. Install waits out the cancellation window, so nothing irreversible happens before PTO prerequisites.
- Verdict: The cited elements handle the utility account holder only for usage data (R156(2) authorization, R209 savings figure, R233 service history). That blocks a savings figure, but nothing stops a price-only close when the account holder isn't a record owner, and nothing makes the interconnection application a signed document. R158 only detects the stalled PTO after the fact as a "paying, not operating" harm, so the existing design lets a willing buyer close into an install that can't be energized.
- Fix: Add an interconnection_signer slot written only by the utility authorization API. If the account holder is not a signing record owner, block the same-call close, send a written proposal stating that a utility account transfer is required, and have a human consult handle the transfer. Make the utility interconnection application a required signer role in the envelope, and block install until the utility confirms the account holder has signed.

### Round 362. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Lead-record errors make genuine, willing owners fail the IDENTITY challenge-response, which sends them to WRONG_PARTY
- Attack: The lead form was filled by a vendor or by the owner's spouse: misspelled surname, old address, wrong birth year, "Bill" for William. The real owner answers truthfully. The challenge doesn't match: WRONG_PARTY or non-owner handling, no pitch. The owner says "that's me, they typed it wrong". There is no correction path that doesn't let an impostor do the same, so the code refuses. Purchased lead data is notoriously dirty. A challenge-response against it rejects true owners at a rate equal to the record error rate, before any sales conversation. The design has no audited measure of this false reject because WRONG_PARTY exits count as correct behaviour.
- Defense: The challenge doesn't run against the vendor's lead fields. R162 draws identity answers only from independent sources (public records, KYC vendor), never from partner lead fields, so a vendor's typo isn't the key. R146 uses a token-set name matcher across county records: an address match plus any surname-token match counts as VERIFIED. A partial match goes to clarify or a human, never to a WRONG_PARTY DNC. A release-blocking false-WRONG_PARTY stratum already exists, so false rejects are measured, not credited as correct. R195 makes the OTP to the number of record the real unlock, and that is an independent correction path an impostor can't reproduce. An owner who answers truthfully and gets the code is verified whatever the lead says.
- Verdict: Identity answers come only from independent records and KYC sources, never from partner lead fields (R162), so a vendor typo isn't the key. R146's token-set matcher accepts an address match plus any surname-token match, ignores first-name variants such as Bill and William, sends partial matches to clarify or a human rather than WRONG_PARTY, and already has a release-blocking false-WRONG_PARTY stratum. The OTP to the number of record (R195) gives true owners a correction path an impostor can't reproduce.

### Round 363. Winner: Designer
- Topic: NOT_SUITABLE counts as success, so false-negative suitability exits of good buyers are invisible to the metric and to certification
- Attack: A coarse suitability table: roof "about 15, maybe 18 years" goes to ROOF_FIRST; "a bit of shade from the oak" to NOT_SUITABLE; "might move in a few years" to exit. Each is an honest exit, scored as success, with no rebuttal allowed. Training and certification reward fast, confident exits as safe. Suitable, willing owners are turned away on rough guesses a site survey would have cleared. A success metric that credits exits equally with installs, plus a harm-only gate, makes over-exiting free. Conversion losses on suitable buyers are never labeled, audited or bounded, so the system drifts toward the cheapest "success".
- Defense: Exits on rough guesses are already blocked. R287(2): a NOT_SUITABLE outcome that rests only on a spoken slot needs an explicit lexical affirm at the critical-slot read-back. Where the spoken value conflicts with a permit or aerial record, it goes to ROOF_FIRST or a survey, never an exit. R211(3) cross-checks roof age against permit records and shade against aerial data. R230 computes shade from LIDAR, so "a bit of shade from the oak" isn't a verdict. The critical-slot read-back (R2) turns "about 15, maybe 18" into a confirmed value or a clarify. R151 bans optimizing variants on conversion in either direction. Over-exiting isn't free. The pass metric is the bot's own signed-and-kept rate (R20), and needless exits lower it.
- Verdict: The concrete rough-guess exits are already blocked: a NOT_SUITABLE resting only on a spoken slot needs an explicit lexical affirm, and a conflict with permit or aerial records goes to ROOF_FIRST or a survey, never an exit (R287). Roof age and shade are cross-checked against permits and aerial or LIDAR data (R211, R230), and the critical-slot read-back confirms or clarifies hedged values (R2). Over-exiting isn't free, because the pass metric is the bot's own signed-and-kept rate (R20) and variants are never tuned on conversion (R151).


### Round 364. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Speculation shaves the wrong latency: the endpointing silence timeout, not Needle or TTS, sets the reply gap, and Tier A states lengthen it
- Attack: Caller: "How long is the warranty?" The endpointer must see roughly 500-800 ms of silence before it declares end of turn, then the final ASR pass, GLOBAL and LOCAL passes and the cache-equality check run. The speculative audio is ready but sits unused until all of that finishes. In Tier A states (read-back, commit) the endpointer waits longer still. Every answer arrives one to two seconds after the caller stops. That is the "is anyone there?" range, so callers re-speak into the agent's reply. Across 10 languages, human turn gaps cluster at about 0-200 ms (Stivers et al. 2009, PNAS). Production endpointers default to hundreds of ms of trailing silence (Deepgram/Telnyx endpointing docs). Speculation removes compute time but cannot remove the silence wait that comes before the equality check. "Feeling human" can't happen by design, and the slowest gaps fall on the most important turns.
- Defense: The attack assumes a generic vendor endpointer at a fixed 500-800 ms. Ours is not that. R25 is "semantic endpointing": the incomplete() tool runs in every state, plus a dangling-word rule, with the "threshold short when complete". A turn that Needle scores complete on the prefix ("How long is the warranty?") ends on the short threshold, not the vendor default. R65 says timer values are "fit from ASR word timestamps in real calls and A/B tested", and R100 measures "per-state hit rate and p95 reply latency". The longer Tier A wait is deliberate: the design never promised 200 ms gaps on commit turns. It chose to be slower and safer there. Where the gap hurts, the measured p95 shows it per state.
- Verdict: The attack assumes a fixed vendor endpointer at 500-800 ms, but the design already has semantic endpointing (R25: incomplete() in every state plus a dangling-word rule, "threshold short when complete"), and timers fit from real calls and A/B tested (R65). After the endpoint, the remaining work is two passes of a ~26M model and a C transition. The longer Tier A wait is a deliberate safety trade, and R100 already measures per-state p95 reply latency.

### Round 365. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Cache misses land on objections and money questions, so the agent's delays fall exactly where human listeners read delay as evasion or bad news
- Attack: Routine turns ("yes, I own it") hit the cache, and the agent answers almost at once. "Wait, is there a lien on my house?" or "What happens if I sell?" are rare, so they miss the cache and are computed fresh with a visibly longer gap. Speculation also switches off under "rising confusion." So the agent hesitates only on the lien, cancellation and price questions. The caller hears a rep who stalls on the hard questions and decides the agent is hiding something, or that the answer is bad. Gap length carries meaning. Dispreferred responses come later, and listeners revise their expectations toward "no" or "trouble" as the silence grows (Kendrick & Torreira 2015; Bögels et al.). Hit-or-miss latency is a deterministic, involuntary prosodic signal that lines up with the compliance-critical topics. It was never reviewed as part of the approved message.
- Defense: A cache miss costs far less than the attack thinks. Lien, cancellation and price answers are fixed policy-table fragments: "every fact token is a table key", and R105 has us "pre-render every fact-bearing clip at build time with an audio hash". So a miss needs no TTS. It needs only the two passes of a ~26M encoder-decoder and a C transition. The fresh path is tens of milliseconds, not a hesitation a listener notices. Speculation is also off "in states where measured hit rate/p95 latency don't pay" (R100), so in those states every turn takes the same fresh path. Turning speculation off under rising confusion (R15) slows answers to every confused turn, not only to the money questions.
- Verdict: The existing design already removes the timing signal the attack relies on: R301 quantizes reply onset to a fixed post-endpoint delay bucket per state "whether speculation hit, missed or clarified", with a timing-indistinguishability CI test. A lien or cancellation question therefore can't be audibly slower than a routine turn in the same state. Fact answers are also pre-rendered at build time (R105), so a miss adds no TTS time.

### Round 366. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Safety exits are never pre-rendered: stop, cancel and request_human are rare, so they are never in top-k and always take the slowest path
- Attack: Speculation ranks the state's likely outcomes on the partial transcript and pre-renders the top-k, which are always LOCAL sales transitions. Caller: "Look, just take me off your list." The cache misses, the turn is computed fresh, and the opt-out confirmation plays after the longest gap in the call. Meanwhile the caller has said "Hello? Did you hear me?" or hung up. A hang-up means the confirmation and the outbox copy go out to someone who has left, and the next turn's state is undefined. Hit rate is optimized per state, so the cache structurally favors sales transitions and penalizes every GLOBAL safety event. The case that most needs a prompt acknowledgment (a revocation that must be honored, a person in distress) is the one guaranteed the worst latency. It also produces the "Hello?" overlap turns that the commit and stop logic handle worst.
- Defense: Safety exits don't go through the speculation cache. The stop path is "union of deterministic lexicon (incl. dialect patterns) and Needle GLOBAL". Under R24, Tier 1 phrases such as "take me off" mean "end immediately". The lexicon pre-empts sensitive events too (R19). Exit audio is a fixed fragment, certified and pre-rendered (R105), and is not rendered on demand. GLOBAL runs on every turn whatever the speculation state, and R192 proves that the root handler is the effective one for every state and GLOBAL event. A hang-up is "out-of-band telephony events handled at the StateSmith root", so the next state is defined, and R173's POST_CALL handler takes any late ASR. The DNC write and the outbox copy are meant to happen even if the caller has gone: a written confirmation isn't wasted on someone who hung up.
- Verdict: Under R301 the onset of a stop or cancel reply is the same fixed bucket as a cache hit, so safety exits are not "guaranteed the worst latency". Tier 1 stop phrases are a deterministic lexicon that ends the call immediately (R24), GLOBAL runs every turn, and hang-ups are root telephony events with a POST_CALL handler (R173), so the next state is defined. The DNC write and the written confirmation are meant to happen even after a hang-up.

### Round 367. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The longer Tier A endpoint wait after the closing question copies the "silent close" pressure tactic
- Attack: The agent asks the read-back confirmation question and then waits longer than anywhere else in the call. A hesitant owner pauses to think. The silence stretches, the social pressure to fill it builds, and they fill it with "...uh, yeah, okay I guess." That trailing "okay" is short, direct and lexical, so it passes COMMIT. The extra wait meant for safety has given the agent, at the decisive moment, the oldest pressure move in sales training: "after you ask for the order, shut up." A deliberate long silence at the close is a known high-pressure technique. People rush to fill gaps that pass about a second because silence signals trouble (Roberts et al. 2006; Kendrick & Torreira 2015). Fixed by graph, it is applied to every hesitant caller, and the agent can't say "take your time" because no event signals hesitation.
- Defense: The longer Tier A wait is the end-of-turn threshold, which applies after the caller has started speaking (R10, R25). It isn't a programmed silence after the question. The pre-answer pause is governed separately by R65's "PAUSE substate after the ask" and silence(duration_bucket) events. And "uh, yeah, okay I guess" can't close. R144 makes the commit "an action-production prompt ('please say send the contract'), not a yes/no". R77 requires "a narrow unambiguous set", and anything else gets a closed re-ask. That re-ask "ends on the safe option ('…, or not?')" (R239-1). And "any re-ask at COMMIT means no same-call close and a written proposal with cooling-off" (R239-3). Filling the silence buys a cooling-off period, not a sale.
- Verdict: The pressured "uh, yeah, okay I guess" cannot close the sale in the existing design: R144 requires the action phrase "send the contract", not a yes, R77 limits commits to a narrow affirm set, and any re-ask at COMMIT means no same-call close and a written proposal with cooling-off (R239-3). The silence may make the caller uncomfortable, but it cannot turn that discomfort into a contract.

### Round 368. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: The agent never backchannels, so on a phone line its silence during long caller turns sounds like a dropped call
- Attack: A caller explains their roof history for 25 seconds. On the phone there are no visual cues, and the agent has no "mm-hm" fragment: it only labels finished turns. After about eight seconds of nothing the caller stops: "Hello? You still there?" That splits their account into fragments. The endpointer closes a turn mid-story, speculation resets, and "you still there?" becomes a LOCAL turn (small_talk or unclear). The remainder of the roof story arrives as a new turn the agent answers out of context. Telephone talk depends on regular verbal backchannels, because listeners use them to show the channel is alive. Speakers who get none falter, repeat themselves and check the line. The design's own vocabulary treats backchannels only as a caller-side risk. It has no way to give a continuer without "talking over the caller", so long, important turns (the ones that matter for suitability) get broken up.
- Defense: The scenario assumes the story gets chopped. It doesn't. R25 holds the turn open for "~2-3s when incomplete", so mid-story pauses don't close it. R44's long-turn pipeline ("deterministic segmenter -> per-segment Needle -> merger with self-repair rules") is built for exactly this 25-second history, and it ends with a "summary read-back after multi-fact turns" that rejoins anything the caller split. Long turns are "a separate Tier A stratum" with speculation skipped, so no reset races happen. Even so, there is a real gap: the agent has no continuer of its own.
- Verdict: The defense agrees that the agent has no continuer, and its fix (continuers, a channel_check event) is entirely new. The existing pieces don't close the gap. The R44 merger joins segments inside one turn, but a caller's "you still there?" ends that turn, so the rest of the story arrives as a new turn. Worse, R107 treats repeated "hello?" as a media fault and hangs up. On a phone line, 20+ seconds of total silence from the listener reliably makes callers check the line, which breaks up the suitability answers that matter most.
- Fix: Add a certified continuer set ("mm-hm", "I see", "go on", no assent words) that plays only in intra-turn pauses scored non-final by incomplete(), after about 6 s of caller speech, never over voiced frames and never in AWAIT_CONFIRM. Add a channel_check event that answers "Yes, I'm here, go ahead" and keeps the long turn open so the R44 merger joins both sides. Exclude channel_check from the R107 "hello?" counter, and add a fragmentation-rate bound on a long-narrative stratum.

### Round 369. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Barge-in and backchannel are not told apart during agent playback, so habitual "mm-hm"s chop every fragment
- Attack: A caller who backchannels heavily says "mm-hm", "right", "yeah" every few seconds, as many speakers do (Japanese aizuchi, call-and-response styles, older speakers). Each one is voiced energy while the agent is playing. If that counts as barge-in, playback stops, the speculation cache is thrown away, and mandatory segments, which count only on uninterrupted playback, restart. The preamble and READ_BACK never finish. If it doesn't count, a real "wait, stop" said with the same short shape is ignored until the fragment ends. The design says "Barge-in discards" but defines no rule for what counts as barge-in over agent speech. Either threshold fails a group systematically. Japanese conversation has backchannels roughly three times as often as English (Maynard). The failure falls by culture and dialect, the very strata the per-group release blockers exist to protect.
- Defense: The design does define barge-in, contrary to the attack. R108: "any near-end energy burst during agent playback … stops playout and enters PAUSE". R14 then classifies the residue: "Short affirmations overlapping agent speech = backchannel, no event". So "wait, stop" can never be ignored: every burst yields, and stop-adjacent residue "triggers the R24 check question at once". The safe horn is the one we chose. The cost lands on heavy backchannelers, whose mandatory segments replay (R39). That costs time but never harms them, and the per-group strata exist to measure exactly that cost.
- Verdict: The design does define barge-in, but that rule produces the attack's first horn: R108 stops playout on any near-end burst and says "Sorry, go ahead", and R39 replays an interrupted mandatory segment from the start. A caller who says "mm-hm" every few seconds can therefore stop the preamble or READ_BACK from ever completing, and the agent keeps apologising. That is a systematic failure for heavy-backchannel dialects and age groups, the very groups the per-group bounds protect. The defense's fix (duck-then-decide) is new.
- Fix: Duck-then-decide: a burst during playback ducks audio and holds it for up to about 400 ms while the lexicon classifies the residue. A short backchannel with no stop-adjacent token resumes from the current clause and keeps the delivered flag. Anything else stops playout and goes to PAUSE as now. After 3 backchannel overlaps, switch to short chunks with pauses (the R238 profile). Backchannel overlaps don't count toward the R152 barge-in signal. Add a high-backchannel stratum with per-group completion and replay bounds as release blockers.

### Round 370. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Simultaneous-start collisions: after a gap, the caller and the agent start together, both stop, and the agent restarts its fragment from the top
- Attack: After a cache miss the agent begins its reply 1.2 s late, just as the caller starts "So what about—". Barge-in stops the agent. The caller, being polite, also stops at the overlap. Now there is mutual silence. The endpointer sees a three-word fragment, Needle labels it unclear, and the agent replays its earlier reply from the start, again overlapping the caller's restart. Three rounds of "sorry, go ahead" follow, each a turn that counts against the clarify-once budget, and the call falls back to a callback nobody asked for. Overlap resolution is a two-party protocol. Humans settle it with fine-grained timing and by one party "dropping out" mid-word, then letting the other go first. The design has only one rule (the agent yields and discards) and no "you first" fragment. Its restart-from-start playback makes the collision more likely to repeat, and each collision costs budget and pushes the call toward fallback.
- Defense: The "you first" fragment exists. R108's yield enters PAUSE with "Sorry, go ahead", and PAUSE is listed among the "internal transitions" (small_talk, acknowledge, side_talk, HOLD, PAUSE). So a collision doesn't spend the clarify budget. Restart-from-top applies only to mandatory segments (R39). An ordinary reply resumes "at the current question (re-read it)" (R24 resume window). Budget counters (R69) end a pathological loop in WRAP_UP, not in an endless round of apologies. Admittedly, the third-round collision and the "unclear" label on a truncated three-word start are real.
- Verdict: The "you first" move the attack says is missing already exists: R108 yields into PAUSE with "Sorry, go ahead", and PAUSE is an internal transition that doesn't spend the clarify budget. A truncated "So what about—" ends on a dangling word, so R25's incomplete() holds the turn open for 2-3 s and the caller's restart joins the same turn instead of being labeled unclear. Ordinary replies resume at the current question (R24), and R69 budgets bound any loop.

### Round 371. Winner: Designer
- Topic: A barge-in on a half-played question binds the caller's answer to a question they never fully heard, especially "A or B" questions
- Attack: QUALIFY: "Do you own the home, or are you currently renting with—" The caller cuts in at "home": "Yeah." Playback stops, but the turn is still answered against the state's question id, so ownership = yes. The same happens with "Would you like the 8 kilowatt system, or the smaller 6 with—", where "yeah" binds to the first option. The commit-time ban ("no commit tools while agent audio plays") doesn't apply because playback stopped before the caller answered. The slot was grounded on the word "yeah". Callers answer early at projected completion points. An alternative question projects completion after the first alternative, so a "yes" there answers a yes/no question that was never asked. The design records which fragment was asked, not how much of it played. Provenance says "spoken", but the caller never heard the alternative they rejected.
- Defense: The binding the attack describes can't happen. R14: "AWAIT_* entered only when the question finishes playing", with "Separate *_SPEAKING states while agent audio plays". A "Yeah" at "home" overlaps agent speech, so it is "backchannel, no event", and R25 says "Overlapped text within agent playback window excluded". R106 extends that window by the path delay, and answers in the guard band "count as overlap and get a closed re-ask". If the caller keeps talking, R108 yields to PAUSE and the question is re-read whole (R24 resume). Qualification-critical slots are Tier A, and R287 requires "an explicit lexical affirm at the critical-slot read-back". System size comes from the calculator, and every commit passes READ_BACK.
- Verdict: R14 says AWAIT_* states are entered only when the question finishes playing, and an affirmation overlapping agent speech is a backchannel with no event. R25 excludes overlapped text and R106 extends that window by path delay, with a closed re-ask in the guard band. A "Yeah" at "home" therefore can't write the ownership slot, and system size comes from the calculator and goes through READ_BACK anyway.

### Round 372. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Trail-off turns ("but...", "so...", "I mean...") are finished turns, but dangling-word endpointing treats them as incomplete(), so caller and agent both wait
- Attack: Read-back. Caller: "Well, it sounds good, but..." and stops: a polite refusal that leaves the floor to the agent. The turn ends on a dangling conjunction, so incomplete() fires and the endpointer waits longer, as Tier A states do. The caller is waiting too. After several seconds of silence the caller fills the gap with "...yeah," which joins the turn or starts a new one. "It sounds good ... yeah" now reaches a commit-shaped decision instead of the implied no. Turn-final "but" and "so" are recognized floor-yielding devices in conversation (studies of trail-off conjunctions; Walker 2012). They do the most work in indirect refusals. Treating them as syntactically unfinished guarantees dead air at exactly the moment the caller is softly declining, and it invites the filler that the lexical COMMIT gate accepts.
- Defense: "It sounds good, but..." doesn't end in a commit. R144 requires the caller to produce "send the contract", and a late "yeah" isn't that. A turn with "but" is neither "short, direct" nor free of conditions, so it gets a closed re-ask. That re-ask ends on "…, or not?" (R239-1), and any re-ask at COMMIT means "no same-call close" (R239-3). Under R289, a hedge carrying a condition becomes condition_stated, which also blocks the close. After the incomplete() wait ends, Needle labels the trailing turn, and R145's indirect_decline class ("high-context indirect refusals") is trained on exactly this kind of refusal. So the soft no is honoured, just two seconds late.
- Verdict: The harm the attack describes, a filler "yeah" being accepted as a commit, is blocked: R144 needs the caller to say "send the contract", R77 rejects conditional and hedged turns, and any re-ask at COMMIT means no same-call close (R239-3). R145 indirect_decline and R289 condition_stated also catch "sounds good, but...". What's left is a couple of seconds of dead air before a soft no is honoured, which is a cost to the call's feel, not a broken safeguard.

### Round 373. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The "speculation on/off gives bit-identical committed traces" CI check is open-loop, so it cannot see how reply timing changes what the caller says next
- Attack: CI replays recorded caller audio twice, with speculation on and off, and gets identical traces. In live calls, speculation changes when the agent starts speaking, and that changes the caller. With speculation off the reply comes 900 ms later, so the caller adds "...and is that with the battery?" or says "Hello?" The audio, the endpoint, the event set and the path are all different. The check passes because recorded audio can't react to the agent's timing. Turn-taking is closed-loop: response latency causally shapes the next turn (Stivers 2009; Kendrick & Torreira 2015). Bit-identical replay over fixed audio proves only that the computation is the same, never that the behavior is safe. The certification claim that speculation is a "cache only" with no behavioral effect is untestable by the stated CI. It needs live, timing-stratified audited calls, which the harm gate doesn't break out by speculation mode.
- Defense: R64's CI check claims isolation, not behavioural equivalence. It proves that "live slots and backtrack history" are "written only on commit", so a guess can't leak into state. The closed-loop safety claim never rested on it. It rests on the release gate: a "per-call harm bound measured only on real calls labeled by humans listening to audio", with "continuous audited sampling with per-state drift alarms". Real calls already include whatever timing speculation produces, closed loop included. R100 measures p95 latency per state and turns speculation off where it doesn't pay. R65's timers are "A/B tested" on live calls. The attack asks for live timing evidence, and the design already gathers it.
- Verdict: The bit-identical CI test (R64) claims only that speculation can't leak into state. The closed-loop safety claim rests on the audited real-call harm gate, which already includes whatever timing speculation produces. The attack's scenario (speculation off makes the reply 900 ms later) is also largely closed by R301, which releases reply onset at the same fixed per-state bucket whether speculation hit, missed or was off.


### Round 374. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The symmetric near-tie rule treats a stop-vs-continue tie like any other tie, so a probable opt-out gets "could you say that again?" instead of a stop
- Attack: Over a noisy line the caller says "yeah no, I'm done, just quit calling here." Lexicon misses it ("quit calling" isn't listed, and ASR writes "quick calling"). GLOBAL scores stop 0.46, acknowledge 0.41: a near-tie, so clarify. Agent: "Sorry, could you say that again?" The caller, annoyed, says "I said I'm good." That now reads as acknowledge or polite decline, and the pitch continues. Stop is caught later in the post-call sweep, if at all. Clarify-on-margin is cost-blind: a missed stop and a wrong roof size are not equal errors. Under 47 CFR 64.1200(a)(10) (2024 order), revocation by "any reasonable means" must be honored, and "quit" is a listed per-se revocation word. Asking the caller to repeat a likely revocation means continuing a sales call after revocation. A Bayes decision rule would choose stop on ties.
- Defense: The scenario uses the wrong rule. Top-1/top-2 clarify applies to LOCAL. GLOBAL is multi-label (R189): "each safety event gets its own calibrated threshold and every event above it fires." There's no argmax tie, so stop at 0.46 fires whenever it clears its own threshold. R133 sets that threshold to hit a target false-continue rate, which makes the rule cost-weighted, not symmetric. Even below threshold, the follow-up "I said I'm good" gets R145 indirect_decline: "Would you prefer we don't call again?" Anything except an explicit time request suppresses the lead. R108 sends stop-adjacent tokens ("calling") straight to the R24 check question. R169 and the post-call sweep hold the number until a human reviews it. The attack is right on one point: a generic "say that again?" should never be the reply to stop mass.
- Verdict: The scenario assumes GLOBAL is an argmax with a stop-vs-acknowledge tie, but R189 (in design.md) made GLOBAL multi-label with a per-event calibrated threshold, and acknowledge is not a GLOBAL event, so no stop tie exists there. R133 sets those thresholds against a target false-continue rate, which is already the cost-weighted rule the attack asks for, and R145 indirect_decline, R190's safety-mass veto, R66 sweep and R169 lock back it up.

### Round 375. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The clarify re-ask produces hyperarticulated repetition that the pinned ASR recognizes worse, so "clarify once" makes the second try less likely to succeed than the first
- Attack: The ASR confidence pre-gate rejects "fifteen years." The agent re-asks. Like most people correcting a machine, the caller slows down, separates the syllables, and stresses the vowels: "FIF... TEEN... YEARS." The telephony ASR, trained on conversational speech, misreads the stretched words or scores them lower. The second try fails too, so the call goes to the callback or human fallback. Accented and older speakers hyperarticulate the most. Oviatt et al. (JASA 1998, CHAM model) show users hyperarticulate after recognition errors: longer duration, more pauses, more careful articulation. This shifts the input away from the recognizer's training distribution and raises error rates. The design's one retry is drawn from a worse distribution than the original turn, and nothing in training covers it, since synthetic "ASR noise" doesn't model error-recovery speech. https://faculty.washington.edu/levow/papers/JASA98.pdf
- Defense: The design never assumes the retry comes from the same distribution. R20 certifies on the agent's own on-policy calls, so every real post-clarify turn, hyperarticulated ones included, is in the human-audited gold and counts toward the bound. R277 fits the confusion model on real audited calls rather than LLM-made noise. Numbers already have a non-acoustic route: R26 confirms Tier A numbers digit-wise, and R111/R121 offer DTMF and SMS/web paths. When the second try fails, the failure is bounded: R111 counts it as an access failure, R121 sends it to a human, and per-group fallback and false-unintelligible rates block release. The attack does identify an unmeasured stratum: the second try is its own distribution and should be measured as one.
- Verdict: R20 certifies on the agent's own on-policy calls and R277 fits its confusion model on real audited calls, so post-clarify speech is in the measured data, not assumed away. A failed second try lands in R111/R121 access routing (DTMF, text/web, human) with per-group fallback rates as release blockers, which is a safe, bounded outcome rather than a harm.

### Round 376. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Model uncertainty is billed to the caller: Needle's own near-ties and clarifies are indistinguishable from caller confusion, so they drive "rising confusion" and the vulnerability counter
- Attack: A lucid owner speaks African American English. Needle, under-trained on that dialect, near-ties three times in QUALIFY. Each clarify is logged as a "confusion" signal. Speculation turns off ("rising confusion"), replies get slower, the vulnerability counter crosses its threshold, and the call goes to NO_SALE_SAFE_EXIT. The CRM now flags this caller as vulnerable or confused. Another caller, who really is confused but whose speech is fluent and in-distribution, never triggers a clarify and gets the close. The gates see one signal, a low margin, that mixes up epistemic uncertainty (the model's) with caller state (aleatoric). The deterministic counters count the model's ignorance as evidence about the person. This produces both errors at once: lucid out-of-distribution speakers are labeled vulnerable and turned away, and truly confused in-distribution speakers sail through.
- Defense: R111 already removes most of this: "Exclude unintelligible, low-ASR-confidence and pure-ASR-quality unclear events from the vulnerability counter." It also makes per-group false-vulnerability and fallback rates release blockers. A model that near-ties three times on AAE gives that stratum a high false-vulnerability rate, and the release fails. R275 runs matched-pair SAE/AAE tests and bars teacher labels for any stratum with disparity. The CRM never sees the flag: R203's handoff schema "never carries the vulnerability counter," and the flag only routes. The truly confused fluent caller still hits R15 teach-back, the R144 action-production commit, R152 overcommitment signals and R239's echo filter, none of which depend on margin. The remaining gap is near-tie clarifies, which R111 doesn't name.
- Verdict: R15's vulnerability counter counts unclear()/clarify events, and R111 only excludes unintelligible, low-ASR-confidence and pure-ASR-quality unclear events; a Needle margin near-tie on clean AAE audio is none of those, so model ignorance still drives a lucid caller to NO_SALE_SAFE_EXIT with redial suppressed. Per-group false-vulnerability blockers (R111) and R275 matched pairs catch this only in aggregate at release, and the defense itself names the near-tie gap; the CRM-flag part of the attack is wrong (R203), but the per-call conflation stands.
- Fix: The vulnerability counter takes only caller-content signals (confused(), contradictions between committed slots, wrong_party_reference, R152 overcommitment). Margin near-ties, OOD-floor unclear and grounding misses go to a separate model_uncertainty counter that routes to a human or ACCESS_PATH as an access failure, never to NO_SALE_SAFE_EXIT, never suppresses redial and never disables speculation as a "confusion" signal.

### Round 377. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The "live error bound" can only run on real-time confidence proxies (clarify and near-tie rates), and those proxies reward confidently wrong states and punish careful ones
- Attack: Human audit labels arrive days later, so the live per-state error bound has to use what is available in real time: clarify rate, near-tie rate, fallback rate. After a release, state QUOTE becomes overconfident on "fifty vs fifteen." It almost never clarifies and looks healthy. State IDENTITY becomes more cautious, clarifies more, trips its bound, and goes to fallback. Ops reads this as "QUOTE is our best state." Its errors only show up weeks later in audits. This is Goodhart's law in the safety loop. Confident errors are by definition the ones that produce no uncertainty signal. A bound computed from self-reported uncertainty systematically clears the dangerous failure mode and penalizes the safe one. "Live" bounds built on the model's own confidence cannot detect a calibration collapse, the very failure they exist to catch.
- Defense: The live monitors are not just self-reported confidence. R132 also monitors per-state event-mix shift, lexicon-vs-Needle disagreement and hang-up-after-turn rate, all with CUSUM. CUSUM is two-sided, so a release that suddenly cuts QUOTE's clarify rate is itself a shift alarm and triggers a 100% audit surge. Several gates don't depend on Needle's confidence: the grounding gate, R186's role-binding and negation cross-check, R211's permit and aerial cross-checks, and R72/R156, under which QUOTE figures come only from verified_usage. A calibration collapse must also get through pre-release gates first: R3 gold replay, sealed holdouts and the R133 calibration split. R324 makes any rise in fallback rate a blocker, so ops can't read "cautious" as "bad" without that being audited. Overconfidence does need its own named signal.
- Verdict: The live monitors are not only self-reported confidence: R132 runs CUSUM on event-mix shift, lexicon-vs-Needle disagreement and hang-up-after-turn rate with audit surges, and a release-induced overconfidence on fifty/fifteen must first pass R3 human-audited gold replay, sealed holdouts and the R133 calibration split, plus R26 digit-wise confirmation of Tier A numbers. Confident errors are caught by label-based pre-release gates and continuous audited sampling, not by clarify rates.

### Round 378. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: The gate thresholds themselves (margin, softmax floor, ASR-confidence cutoff, vulnerability count, call budgets) aren't listed in the content-hashed certified bundle, so they can be changed without recertifying
- Attack: The bundle hashes graph, weights, fragments, tool lists, ASR and policy table. Conversion drops 8% after a release because clarifies are up. A growth engineer lowers the near-tie margin from 0.15 to 0.08 and the ASR-confidence floor from 0.6 to 0.45 in runtime config. The hash, certificate and replay are all unchanged. Clarifies fall, commits rise, and confidently wrong stops and commits pass through under a certificate measured at the old operating point. Every error rate in the certificate is conditional on a threshold, because thresholds set the point on the risk-coverage curve. If the thresholds aren't part of the certified artifact, the certificate certifies a model, not a decision rule. Bit-identical replay with the new config reproduces the new behavior faithfully, so replay can't flag the change either.
- Defense: The design treats thresholds as certified outputs. R133 re-sets "margin, OOD and clarify thresholds" on a held-out calibration split each release to hit target false-stop and false-commit rates, so a threshold is a release artifact. R273 logs "mask/threshold version" on every turn's decision record, so a change shows up in every audited trace. R328 already puts alarm thresholds in "the content-hashed certified bundle," and changing one is a release with two-person sign-off. R324 names this exact move: "lowering the clarify threshold to shed hard calls fails release." R184 replays margin decisions bitwise. The final-plan bundle list doesn't name the gate thresholds, though, so that should be stated explicitly.
- Verdict: R133 re-sets thresholds per release and R273 logs the threshold version, but nothing in design.md puts the gate thresholds into the content-hashed bundle or certificate key: R53/R3 key on weights, runtime, hardware and facts table, and R328 covers only alarm thresholds. A runtime config change therefore leaves the certificate valid while moving the operating point it was measured at; logging detects it later, it does not prevent it.
- Fix: Make every gate parameter (margin, softmax/OOD floor, ASR and word-confidence floors, veto threshold, vulnerability counts, call budgets, endpointing timers) a compile-time constant in the generated C, included in the bundle hash and certificate key, with no runtime config path and a build check that fails if any guard reads a non-hashed value.

### Round 379. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The ASR-confidence pre-gate scores whole utterances, so a confident average hides the one low-confidence word that decides the turn
- Attack: "Yeah, the house is in my name and my wife's, and we can't do anything over a hundred a month." Ten clear words, one garbled: "can't" comes out as "can" with word-confidence 0.31. The utterance's mean confidence is 0.88 and passes the pre-gate. Needle sees "we can do anything over a hundred a month," grounds budget > 100, and is confident. No gate re-asks, because the gate measures how clear the turn was, not how clear its pivotal token was. Utterance-level confidence is an average. Negations, digits and modals are short, unstressed, and carry the most information. A gate keyed to mean confidence approves exactly the turns where one critical word is a guess among many clear ones. The grounding gate then confirms the misheard word, since it appears in the "final ASR text."
- Defense: Several existing gates work below utterance level. R186's extractor tags negation scope per token, and a disagreement with Needle's binding goes to clarify. R104 already checks commit tokens one by one against ASR word timestamps. R26 confirms Tier A numbers digit-wise. R273 keeps the ASR n-best per turn. The pivotal error here also does limited harm: R72 says "spoken amounts only fill coarse routing bands," and binding terms come only from the price table, verified_usage and the READ_BACK plus action-production commit (R144). A misheard budget can't create a contract. The attack is right that the pre-gate averages, so a single low-confidence pivotal token should trigger a re-ask on its own.
- Verdict: A garbled "can't" can mis-set a budget slot, but R72 keeps spoken amounts in coarse routing bands only, R2 reads back every qualification-critical slot before a transition depends on it, and binding terms come only from the price table, verified usage and READ_BACK plus the R144 action-production commit. So one misheard pivotal word becomes a caught correction or a band misroute, not a contract harm.

### Round 380. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Fallback to a booked callback sends the caller back into the same uncertain state with the same weights, which creates a clarify-fail-callback loop that burns frequency caps and consent
- Attack: QUALIFY can't parse a caller's answers about roof ownership (an accent plus a mixed-title situation), and after one clarify it falls back to "We'll call you back." The callback is placed by the same bundle, which runs the preamble and identity again and reaches the same QUALIFY turn. It hits the same near-tie, clarifies once, and books another callback. After three calls the caller is angry, hasn't refused anything, and hasn't been told that a machine can't understand them. "Deterministic fallback" is only a fallback if it changes something. A callback to the same deterministic agent is a retry with no new information, and determinism guarantees the same failure. Nothing records "this caller failed state X," so the frequency caps and repeat-call harassment rules (TSR 310.4(b)(1)(i)) are spent on a loop the design itself creates.
- Defense: R169 already rules out this loop: "Any call that ends on unintelligible fallback books nothing." R121 says that a call with repeated unintelligible turns but clear speech "is never given an AI callback, and its lead is flagged human-only." R111 routes repeated re-asks to the ACCESS_PATH (DTMF, SMS/web or a human with extra time). R293 sends budget exhaustion to request_human, never a generic follow-up. R284 forbids any callback after a hostile turn, and R145 suppresses on defers. So the second call in the scenario goes to a person, not to the same bundle. The gap is that a near-tie fallback (not unintelligible) isn't explicitly tied to a lead-level memory.
- Verdict: R169 and R121 stop the loop only for unintelligible fallback; a near-tie clarify-then-fallback in QUALIFY can still end in a booked callback by the same bundle, and nothing in design.md records per lead which state failed or bars the AI from re-entering it. Frequency caps (R41) and the R284 hostility exit only end the loop after the caller has been called repeatedly and angered, which is the harm.
- Fix: Write a lead-level failed_state record (state id plus bundle hash) on any clarify-then-fallback; the R138 dial permit refuses any AI redial that would re-enter that state under the same or an equivalent bundle, so follow-up is only a human, DTMF/SMS self-serve or a written proposal, and the fallback fragment says plainly that a person will follow up.

### Round 381. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: The slot store has no UNKNOWN value, so a caller's honest "I don't know" about roof age, shade or move horizon is forced into a typed value, a clarify loop, or a guard default
- Attack: QUALIFY: "How old is your roof?" The caller: "No idea, we bought the place six years ago, it was here then." Grounding finds "six years" in the text, so roof_age=6. Or nothing grounds, the agent clarifies once ("about how many years?"), and the caller guesses "maybe ten?", which the rising pitch excludes only at COMMIT, not here. Either way the suitability table receives a crisp number and returns a confident SUITABLE. Every uncertainty gate in the design is about whether the machine understood the caller, not whether the caller knows the answer. Two-valued slots plus pure-code guards can't represent a fact that is understood but unknown. So the suitability exit, the "honest" path, runs on fabricated precision, and the provenance tag "spoken" makes a guess look like a verified fact in the audit.
- Defense: The scenario's binding fails under existing gates. R186 tags each number with its nearest slot cue, and "six years" binds to "bought the place," not "roof," so the disagreement goes to clarify. R211 cross-checks roof age against permit records, R71 sends uncertain roofs to ROOF_FIRST ("inspection before any quote"), and R2 reads critical slots back before any transition depends on them. The design already uses unknown values elsewhere: R159 (unknown panel amperage means "price subject to survey" with ranges), R227/R319 (unknown means no figure) and R341 (unknown category means human review or NOT_SUITABLE, "never a default"). The attack is right that qualification slots need a caller-unknown value of their own.
- Verdict: R186 does stop the specific "six years" misbinding, but the existing slot model has no caller-unknown value: after "no idea" the design can only clarify for a number, so the caller's guess is stored as a crisp value with "spoken" provenance or the call falls to fallback. The UNKNOWN handling the defense cites (R159, R227, R319, R341) covers other slots and table enums, not QUALIFY answers, and R211 permit cross-checks only help where a record exists.
- Fix: Add a dont_know(slot) event and an UNKNOWN value for every QUALIFY slot, a caller_estimate provenance for hedged answers, and mandatory suitability-table rows for UNKNOWN and near-cutoff estimates that resolve via permit/aerial records to ROOF_FIRST or NOT_SUITABLE (build check enforces the rows); no clarify asks for a number after the caller says they don't know.

### Round 382. Winner: Designer
- Topic: One-hot training labels from the big model and from single annotators teach Needle that no utterance is ambiguous, so the near-tie rule rarely fires on genuinely ambiguous turns
- Attack: "I mean, I guess it makes sense" occurs thousands of times in synthetic data, each time with one hard label (acknowledge, or affirm, depending on the generator's prompt). Real annotators disagree 50/50 on it, but the training target is a one-hot vector, never 0.5/0.5. Needle learns to output 0.97 on whichever label dominates its sources.
