At COMMIT-adjacent states the margin gate sees 0.97 vs 0.02 and never clarifies on the turns humans find most ambiguous. Calibration has to be learned from label distributions, not imposed by thresholds. Peterson et al. (ICCV 2019, CIFAR-10H) show that training on human label distributions instead of hard labels improves calibration and robustness. The design trains on single labels per example and then trusts a softmax margin to show ambiguity it was trained to erase. https://arxiv.org/abs/1908.07086
- Defense: The design doesn't rely on the margin alone to surface ambiguity. R77 gives ambiguity its own audio-informed ambiguous_polarity label class, which maps to clarify, so "I guess it makes sense" is trained toward a class that is explicitly ambiguous. R133 fits temperature scaling on a real audited split. R131 measures inter-rater agreement with two auditors, and R332 escalates codebook-ambiguous items. COMMIT can't fire on that utterance regardless: R144 requires an action-production phrase ("say 'send the contract'"), and R9 takes only a short lexical affirm. Hedges like this one are excluded. The point about hard labels is fair as a training improvement.
- Verdict: Ambiguity is not left to the margin alone: R77 trains an explicit audio-informed ambiguous_polarity class mapped to clarify, R133 calibrates on a real audited split, and an utterance like "I guess it makes sense" cannot commit anyway because R144 requires an action-production phrase and the COMMIT gate takes only a short lexical affirm. The overconfident hard-label harm the attack describes has no commit path.

### Round 383. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Explicit confirmation ("Did you say fifteen?") turns misrecognitions into "customer-confirmed" facts, because callers accept wrong echoes and the "yes" then becomes the provenance
- Attack: Grounding is uncertain on the monthly-bill amount, so the agent asks "Just to confirm, that's two hundred fifty a month?" The caller said "a hundred fifty." They half-hear the agent's TTS on a speakerphone and say "yeah." The closed binary re-ask accepts it. The slot now carries provenance "spoken" plus a confirmation record. Savings and system size are computed from $250, and in any later dispute the log shows the customer confirmed it. The uncertainty-resolution step has its own acceptance bias. People say "yes" to echoes they didn't fully process, especially for numbers, and the binary re-ask has no third option such as "no, it's...". Each confirmation creates audit evidence that blames the customer for the machine's misrecognition, which makes confidently wrong slots both more likely to survive and harder to challenge.
- Defense: The scenario's harm can't happen. R72 says "spoken amounts only fill coarse routing bands," and savings wording requires calculator_output. R156 says "the only writer for verified_usage is the utility data API or that extractor." Savings and size are never computed from $250 the caller said. R239's re-asks end on the safe option ("…, or not?"), and its echo filter discounts answers that match the prompt. R26/R22 confirm numbers digit by digit. R273 keeps the ASR n-best, so a dispute shows the original "one fifty" hypothesis, and the log doesn't blame the customer. Echo-confirmation as provenance is a real weakness for spoken bands and should change.
- Verdict: The scenario's harm cannot occur: R72 confines spoken amounts to coarse routing bands and R156 makes the utility API or bill extractor the only writer for verified_usage, so savings and size are never computed from a caller-confirmed $250. R239's safe-option re-asks and echo filter, R26/R22 digit-wise confirmation and R273's n-best decision record limit both the acceptance bias and the blame-the-customer audit trail.


### Round 384. Winner: Designer
- Topic: State ADAD statutes (California PUC 2874) require a live, unrecorded natural-voice announcement and consent before any recorded message plays
- Attack: Dialer calls a San Diego homeowner with valid FCC-style written consent naming the seller and AI voice. Callee answers; OPENING plays a pre-rendered, human-approved fragment, then the compiled PREAMBLE. No human ever speaks. The callee never hears a natural voice ask "may I play a recorded message?", and no business address is stated. Same pattern on Indiana and Illinois numbers. A plaintiff's firm files a class action on every California call since launch. Every utterance is a certified pre-rendered fragment, i.e. a recorded message played by an automatic dialing-announcing device. Cal. Pub. Util. Code 2874 lets an ADAD run only after an unrecorded natural-voice announcement stating name, address and phone number and asking consent. The no-human design can't satisfy it, and the profile models only TCPA consent. (law.justia.com/codes/california/code-puc/division-1/part-2/chapter-10/article-1/section-2874)
- Defense: A California, Indiana or Illinois number only reaches the dialer through a certified regulation profile. R305 says "any candidate jurisdiction without a certified regulation profile is a hard no-dial", and R265 has outside counsel sign every mandatory preamble fragment per profile. R331's traceability matrix makes every guard cite a profile row, uses union of obligations, and runs a satisfiability check that sends unsatisfiable mixes to lead-gen or a human. An ADAD live-intro duty the graph can't meet is exactly that kind of unsatisfiable mix. The ADAD row is missing, though. This is a profile-authoring gap, not an architectural one: the R333 human channel already exists.
- Verdict: The attack misreads the statute. Cal. PUC 2873 lets an ADAD run "pursuant to a prior agreement ... whereby the person called has agreed ... to receive such calls ... or as specified in Section 2874", so prior consent and the 2874 live intro are alternative paths, and 2872(f) excludes non-random dialing to people who asked for the call. The existing R8/R74/R197 per-number written-consent gate (no random or sequential dialing) already puts these calls on the consent path, and Indiana and Illinois also allow consented recorded messages. The Designer's admission that an ADAD row is missing doesn't change the outcome on the law.

### Round 385. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Every artificial-voice call must state the seller's callback telephone number (47 CFR 64.1200(b)(2)); the preamble has no such segment
- Attack: The compiled PREAMBLE states company, sales purpose, AI designation, recording notice, opt-out sentence, ADMT notice and anti-phishing line. It gives no phone number. The number is spoken only if the caller asks (request_caller_identity). The callee hangs up after the preamble, or the call ends at NOT_SUITABLE, without ever hearing a number. A TCPA plaintiff counts every completed call. 64.1200(b)(1)-(2) require every artificial or prerecorded voice message to state the entity's identity at the start and, during or after the message, a callback number that is not the dialer's. This is an affirmative per-call duty, not an on-demand one. The design treats contact info as a request-triggered fixed answer, so every call that ends without the request is a $500-$1,500 violation.
- Defense: The number isn't hidden. R259 tells the lead the calling number at consent capture, and the branded A-attested caller ID shows it. R84's request_contact_info gives it as a fixed answer at any moment. R75 already treats (b)-paragraph content as profile-mandated preamble segments with a position lint, and R331 forces every 64.1200 subsection to map to a guard. So (b)(2) is one missing segment key in a mechanism built for it, and it needs no new architecture. Fair point, though: an on-demand answer does not meet an affirmative per-call duty.
- Verdict: 47 CFR 64.1200(b)(2) requires every artificial or prerecorded voice message to state a callback number other than the dialer's, during or after the message, on every call. The existing design gives the number only on request (R84), through caller ID, or at consent capture (R259), and none of those satisfies the per-call spoken duty. R75's profile-segment mechanism is real, but the segment is absent, and the defense says so.
- Fix: Add a callback_number policy key: a staffed line, not the dialed ANI, that accepts DNC requests and is live-tested daily. Make it a profile-mandated preamble segment (counted only on uninterrupted playback) and repeat it in every agent-initiated END fragment. Add a model-checked invariant that no path from DISCLOSED reaches an agent hang-up without a playout mark on it, proven per call through the R116 render log.

### Round 386. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Aggregate social proof computed over success-labeled customers is survivorship-filtered review data under the FTC's 2024 Consumer Reviews and Testimonials Rule
- Attack: Policy-table row: "9 in 10 of our customers say they're happy with their savings", with a substantiation id pointing to the post-install survey. That survey goes only to "success" accounts: installed, not rescinded, no complaint. Cancelled, disputed and complaining customers are never asked. The agent states the figure on thousands of calls. An FTC staffer requests the substantiation file. The success label filters the sample by satisfaction, so the stat is review gating by design. 16 CFR Part 465 (effective Oct 21, 2024) and Endorsement Guides 255.2 treat misrepresenting reviews and selective solicitation or suppression as deceptive. Civil penalties apply per violation, i.e. per call that states the claim. A substantiation id doesn't fix a biased population.
- Defense: R92 limits social proof to audited aggregate policy-table facts with a substantiation id. R94 puts testimonials under endorsement-disclosure rules. R199 makes any quantifier ("9 in 10") require a table key, and R326 juxtaposition lint and the counsel-signed path book judge net impression. R222 requires a legal attestation before a row can be cited. A survey sent only to success accounts was never sanctioned. R163 makes the post-install survey a labeling feed, not a marketing sample, so reusing it as a claim is a row-authoring error. Still, nothing checks the sampling frame mechanically.
- Verdict: Social proof can only be an audited aggregate policy-table fact with a substantiation id (R92), any quantifier needs a table key (R199), and counsel judges net impression through the path book (R326). Under FTC substantiation doctrine a survivorship-filtered sample doesn't substantiate "9 in 10 of our customers", so the scenario needs the audit itself to fail. Also, R163's post-install survey is a labeling feed, and the design names no satisfaction row. R222's attestation covers warranty and terms rows, not social proof, so citing it overstates the defense, but the remaining controls are enough.

### Round 387. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: FTC Holder Rule: seller-arranged solar loans must carry the Holder notice, which turns every certified spoken claim into a defense against the lender
- Attack: The agent quotes financing, READ_BACK names the warranties, and the lender's e-sign package goes out. The lender's form omits the 16 CFR 433.2 Holder notice, or caps recovery at amounts paid. The seller takes milestone funding anyway. Two years later the system underperforms the certified savings fragment. The borrower stops paying, and the lender sues. Referring buyers to an affiliated lender makes the seller's acceptance of loan proceeds without the Holder notice an unfair practice. The FTC's 2022 advisory opinion says recovery caps are unlawful. READ_BACK and the commit gate never check the lender document for it. With the notice, every spoken savings or warranty misstatement becomes a claim against the loan, which widens exposure to whole portfolios.
- Defense: This was already fixed. R242 requires every e-sign clause to be a policy-table key, and its material list names "assignment plus the FTC Holder Rule notice". A template with an untagged clause, or a template/READ_BACK version mismatch, fails release. R17 puts the lender's financing disclosures in the e-sign packet, and R217 admits a lender program only after profile review. Wider exposure is what the Holder Rule intends. Our defense is that the spoken claims are certified and true: savings come from R230 modeled_production with a shortfall tolerance, and warranties need R161 backing instruments. A recovery cap clause isn't an explicitly linted pattern yet.
- Verdict: R242 lists the Holder notice as a material clause of the seller's e-sign template, but nothing checks that the lender's own credit contract carries the verbatim 16 CFR 433.2 notice. Nothing bans a recovery-cap clause either, which the FTC's 2022 advisory opinion calls unlawful. R217 admits lender programs on draw-schedule grounds only, and R154 checks amounts, not clauses. So the seller can accept loan proceeds under a defective lender form, and the defense concedes the cap gap.
- Fix: Hash each admitted lender loan-document version into the bundle. Admit a lender program only if the verbatim 433.2 notice is present and no recovery-cap pattern appears (extend the R222 lint to lender docs). At runtime, refuse CONTRACT_SENT and accept no milestone funding unless the lender envelope's document hash matches an admitted version.

### Round 388. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Credit-filtered lead lists and pre-call lender soft pulls are FCRA consumer reports used for marketing without a firm offer of credit
- Attack: The lead vendor sells "homeowners, FICO 680+, high bill" segments. Or the lender soft-pulls the lead file overnight so lender_verified slots pre-populate the financing menu. The agent then calls and pitches financing with no firm offer of credit and no FCRA prescreen opt-out notice. A consumer who opted out of prescreening at optoutprescreen gets called anyway. Using consumer-report data to pick whom to solicit is permissible only as prescreening tied to a firm offer of credit, with the 615(d) opt-out notice (FCRA 604(c)). The pre-dial gate checks consent, DNC and the RND, but not how the lead was selected or prescreen opt-outs. The lint bans the word "pre-qualified", not the underlying practice. FCRA brings willful-violation statutory damages and class actions.
- Defense: The overnight soft-pull scenario contradicts the design. R225 says the customer starts the lender's soft-pull decision "through an outbox link to a lender-hosted page, never by voice", and lender_verified slots have writer allowlists. R7 data minimization gives the agent "no account/income/credit". R73 says "credit/prequal never spoken", and R46 gives every caller the same menu. Credit-filtered lead purchases are a lead-sourcing gap, but the controls are already in place: R268 checks each lead's source id at dial and screens vendors, and R246 requires vendor attestations.
- Verdict: The overnight soft-pull path contradicts the design. R225 makes the soft pull customer-initiated on a lender-hosted page, R7 holds no credit data, and R90 accepts a credit band only with lender-prequal provenance, so the agent never selects or pitches on credit. Leads are PEWC opt-in leads with per-lead source ids (R268) and vendor attestations (R246), so any prescreen exposure from a vendor's credit-filtered segment is a sourcing-contract matter, not something the call path creates.

### Round 389. Winner: Designer
- Topic: TSR external-upsell rules: ROOF_FIRST, partner roofing, batteries and lead-gen handoffs to partner installers are a second seller in the same call with no new seller disclosure
- Attack: QUALIFY returns ROOF_FIRST and the agent books a reroof quote with a partner roofing company. Or, in a lead-gen state, the agent warm-transfers to a partner installer. Or a battery or monitoring plan comes from a third-party provider. The preamble named only the solar seller. No fragment names the new seller, states the upsell's sales purpose, or gets separate express informed consent before data passes over. TSR 310.4(d) and 310.2 treat an external upsell as a new solicitation: the seller's identity, the purpose and the nature of the goods must be disclosed promptly at that point. Passing billing or account data to the second seller also needs express informed consent (310.4(a)(7)). The design has one seller identity, compiled once. It has no external-upsell state, so handoffs disclose nothing.
- Defense: ROOF_FIRST, per R71, means "inspection before any quote", not a sale of a partner's reroof. R322 names the licensee and licence number per line item and splits the contract when another trade's licensee is involved. R318 restricts third-party battery programs to post-PTO outbox enrollment "under the program's own terms and consent". R321 bans recurring services from voice. Partner data flow is gated by R253 (notice playout-marked, no opt-out), R251 GLBA and R203's allowlisted handoff schema with no billing fields, and R45 means billing data never exists anyway. The one real gap is that a lead-gen warm transfer has no spoken second-seller disclosure.
- Verdict: The design has no in-call second-seller sale. ROOF_FIRST is "inspection before any quote" (R71), warm transfers go to the seller's own staffed queue (R56/R333), third-party battery programs are post-PTO outbox enrollments on the program's own terms (R318), recurring services are barred from voice (R321), and no payment data exists to pass on (R45). Partner data flows are gated by R253, R251 and R203. The "lead-gen warm transfer to a partner" gap the defense concedes isn't a path in the design.

### Round 390. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The opt-out confirmation text is narrowly limited by the FCC's 2024 revocation rules (one text, within 5 minutes, no marketing), and the outbox can't meet that
- Attack: The caller says "stop calling me." GLOBAL stop fires, and the fixed answer plus "outbox copy" go to the outbox. The outbox drains only on committed turns, so it sends after the hang-up commits, sometimes hours later after a vendor backlog. The text includes the full written DNC policy, the company tagline and a "we're sorry to see you go" link. A second copy goes out on the DNC-policy request. 64.1200(a)(12) (effective April 11, 2025) allows only one confirmation text after a revocation. It must be sent within five minutes, must only confirm the revocation, and must carry no marketing or persuasion. Anything else is a fresh unconsented message to a number that just revoked consent. Outbox timing and fragment-assembled SMS content are unmodeled against this, so each late or branded text creates a new TCPA claim.
- Defense: Most of this is already blocked. R272 certifies SMS templates with a lint against promotional wording, so taglines and "sorry to see you go" links can't render. R342 re-reads the revocation ledger at send time and drops every marketing intent in a stop turn, so "compliance confirmations alone pass". R261 gives every message a [not_before, not_after] window and never sends session-bound items late. R260 has priority classes, and R196 deduplicates by idempotency key. What's missing is a 5-minute cap and a one-message limit specific to revocations.
- Verdict: The scenario's content violations can't render. SMS templates are certified with a lint against promotional wording (R272), the drain re-reads the revocation ledger and drops every marketing intent in a stop turn (R342), and sends are deduplicated (R196) and windowed (R261). Under the FCC's revocation order, a confirmation sent after five minutes isn't per se unlawful (the sender must show the delay was reasonable), and a DNC policy the caller asked for isn't an unsolicited message.

### Round 391. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: State mini-TCPA consent-form elements (Florida FTSA 501.059, Oklahoma, Maryland) aren't met by a consent check that only verifies "seller + AI voice"
- Attack: The pre-dial gate confirms the lead's written consent names the seller and AI voice. The Florida lead's form never says the person authorizes calls "using an automated system for the selection or dialing of telephone numbers or the playing of a recorded message". It doesn't state that signing isn't a condition of purchase, and it doesn't list the authorized number. The dialer places the call. A Florida FTSA firm files with $500-per-call statutory damages. Mini-TCPAs define consent by specific required elements and technology wording, and each carries a private right of action. The FCC's 2024 AI-voice ruling doesn't satisfy Florida's "playing of a recorded message" wording, and the design's speech is recorded fragments. The gate checks presence of consent, not per-state form content, so "most restrictive wins" is never applied to the consent text.
- Defense: The premise is wrong. The gate checks more than "seller + AI voice". R8 already requires "not a condition of purchase" and "state mini-TCPA rules as facts rows". R74 keys the artifact to a disclosure version, and R269 stores the "rendered form page and disclosure text hash" in seller WORM, so the exact wording a Florida lead saw is available at dial time. R197 double opt-in proves the authorized number, and R331 replaced "most restrictive" with union of obligations. What's missing is a per-state element check against that stored text.
- Verdict: R8 checks seller, AI/artificial voice and not-a-condition, and it names "state mini-TCPA rules as facts rows", but nothing compares the stored disclosure text (R269 hash) with each state's required consent elements. Florida's FTSA defines consent by authorization of an "automated system ... or the playing of a recorded message", and a consent naming only "AI voice" may not meet that. The defense concedes the per-state element check is missing, and the FTSA carries a $500-per-call private right of action.
- Fix: Add consent_form_element rows to each profile layer (FL 501.059 technology wording, authorized number, not-a-condition, and the OK and MD analogues). Have a counsel-maintained registry classify each consent disclosure-text hash against every layer's elements, and make the R138 dial permit require the hash to be approved for every candidate jurisdiction (unknown hash = no dial).

### Round 392. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: Drip and partitioned pricing: quoting per-watt or monthly figures before the all-in total violates Massachusetts 940 CMR 38 and California SB 478
- Attack: QUOTE says "about $X a month" or "$Y per watt", then talks savings, and states the total cost only at READ_BACK several turns later. Permit, interconnection-application, HOA-review and dealer fees appear only in the e-sign packet. A Massachusetts homeowner hears the monthly figure first. The AG's office receives the recording in a complaint. Massachusetts junk-fee rules (940 CMR 38, compliance from Sept 2, 2025) require the total price, with all mandatory fees, to be disclosed clearly whenever a price is first presented. California Civ. Code 1770(a)(29) (SB 478) bans prices that exclude mandatory fees. The design orders disclosures by state for READ_BACK only, so the first price spoken is a partial one by construction. (mass.gov/news/ag-campbell-releases-junk-fee-regulations-to-help-consumers-avoid-unnecessary-costs)
- Defense: Totals already lead in the design's architecture. R85 makes total cost a mandatory READ_BACK item. R154 says "a financed quote's total is the lender contract price" and requires the envelope amount to equal the lender amount. R309 states the contract price "as all-in" including local taxes. R32 allows only non-triggering financing language before a quote, and R231 limits savings to year one. Massachusetts and California also carry R47-style lead-gen rows in many territories. Fair point, though: the ordering of the first spoken price and the mandatory fees left in the envelope aren't constrained by any lint or invariant.
- Verdict: R85 and R309 make the total all-in at READ_BACK and in the contract, but nothing controls which price figure is spoken first in QUOTE. Nothing prevents a monthly or per-watt figure before the total, or mandatory fees that appear only in the envelope. Massachusetts 940 CMR 38 requires the total with mandatory fees whenever a price is first presented, and the defense concedes no lint or invariant constrains this ordering.
- Fix: Type fee rows as mandatory or optional, and add a calculator build check that folds every mandatory fee into total_price (nothing mandatory may appear only in the envelope). Add a fragment-sequence invariant that the first price-typed fill in any call is total_price, with monthly figures allowed only inside the same fragment, and ban per-watt keys from voice in every profile.

### Round 393. Winner: Breaker
- Topic: New York's Algorithmic Pricing Disclosure Act: a price computed from the caller's personal data needs the statutory "set by an algorithm" disclosure, and no fragment has it
- Attack: A New York homeowner's quote comes from the calculator over verified usage, address, roof answers and lender_verified credit tier. The resulting total and monthly payment differ from a neighbor's for the same panel count. The agent speaks the price with no algorithmic-pricing notice. The NY AG, which issued a consumer alert when the law took effect in November 2025, opens an inquiry. NY GBL 349-a requires a clear disclosure, "THIS PRICE WAS SET BY AN ALGORITHM USING YOUR PERSONAL DATA", next to any price set by an algorithm using consumer personal data, with AG penalties per violation. The calculator is exactly that. Fragment lints and the policy table have no such row, and certificates don't expire on new statutes. (kelleydrye.com/viewpoints/blogs/ad-law-access/new-yorks-algorithmic-pricing-disclosure-law-takes-effect; ag.ny.gov consumer alert)
- Defense: The quote is not person-priced. R46 maps configuration to price, "all options to all", with prohibited-basis tags. R288 has one pricing authority and price gaps across arms counted as Tier A, and matched-pair tests prove that equal configurations get equal prices. Usage sets system size, not unit price. The credit tier affects only lender terms, and the lender sets those. R90 already gives an ADMT pre-use notice. Personal data still drives the total, though, and New York counsel may read GBL 349-a to cover it.
- Verdict: GBL 349-a defines personalized algorithmic pricing very broadly: "pricing that fluctuates dependent on conditions" set by "a computational automated process that uses a set of rules", using data "reasonably linked" to a consumer. Its express carve-out for rideshare mileage pricing shows that rule-based calculators fall inside it otherwise. The calculator total depends on this household's usage, roof geometry and rooftop tax address (R230, R309), so even a fixed per-configuration price table (R46) doesn't keep the total outside the statute. No fragment carries the disclosure, and certificates expire only on a cited row change, not on a new statute. The GLBA exemption reaches only financing activity.
- Fix: Add an algorithmic_pricing_disclosure key with the verbatim statutory text to the NY layer, with a co-occurrence lint that places it in the same fragment as every calculator_output price fill, spoken and written. Add a legislative-change monitor with an attestation effective_until date per profile layer, so a new statute or a lapsed re-attestation drops the layer to lead-gen (no price) until counsel re-attests.


### Round 394. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Teacher-student capacity gap: a frontier teacher is the wrong teacher for a 26M student, and hard event labels throw away what little it could pass on
- Attack: The build phase labels every turn with the big model's single top event. Needle is finetuned on those one-hot labels. On QUALIFY turns like "we might be moving, depends on my wife's job", the teacher's decision rests on discourse reasoning a 26M encoder cannot represent. Needle fits the label surface instead ("moving" -> move_horizon slot). Adding more teacher data and a stronger teacher in later releases does not lift Needle's accuracy on these turns; it plateaus or drops. Distillation degrades when the teacher-student gap is too large: a bigger, more accurate teacher can produce a worse small student (Cho & Hariharan, ICCV 2019; Mirzadeh et al., "Teacher Assistant" KD, AAAI 2020). The plan has no intermediate teacher and no soft targets, and it assumes "more big-model data" converges. Needle's ceiling is set by its capacity, not by data volume.
- Defense: The design never assumes "more big-model data converges". Release is gated only on real-call, human-audited bounds (R2), so a state whose accuracy plateaus simply stays on deterministic fallback; it does not ship. Needle's job is also cut down to what 26M can do: one numeric slot per QUALIFY question (R186), exact-entity grounding (R185), role-binding and negation cross-check (R186), and every qualification-critical slot is read back and confirmed (R2 critical-slot read-back), so "moving" mapped to move_horizon gets corrected aloud. The ownership-change question is neutral and "any yes -> NOT_SUITABLE" (R208). Teacher labels are barred wherever teacher-vs-human disagreement is high (R275). Discourse reasoning lives in code (condition_stated, R289; defers_decision, R15), not in Needle.
- Verdict: The design never relies on more teacher data converging. Release is gated only on human-audited real-call bounds (R2), and a state that plateaus stays on deterministic fallback, which costs money but causes no harm. The attack's own example is handled by existing elements: one numeric slot per QUALIFY question (R186), the neutral ownership-change question where any yes leads to NOT_SUITABLE (R208), condition_stated (R289) and critical-slot read-back (R2). All of these are in design.md.

### Round 395. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Retiring the big model deletes the second opinion that drives audit sampling and DAgger, so post-retirement audits are random-only and lose almost all detection power
- Attack: Before retirement, the audit pulls "all disagreements" between Needle (shadow) and the big-model stand-in, plus random agreements. Most harms surface as disagreements. State QUOTE retires. Now only Needle labels the turn, so no disagreements exist. Audit falls back to random sampling at a fixed budget. A new failure mode (a rescind phrasing after a tariff headline) appears at 1 in 20,000 calls; random sampling of a few hundred calls a week effectively never draws it. Disagreement-triggered review is the design's main way to find errors cheaply. It needs a second classifier, which retirement removes by definition. DAgger also requires an expert to label the learner's new states (Ross et al., AISTATS 2011). After retirement there is none, so the "measure, then fix" loop runs blind just when the model is alone.
- Defense: Retirement removes the big model from the call path only. R36 says "after retirement big model is offline-only", so it still exists as an offline second opinion. Label-free monitors run on 100% of traffic: lexicon-vs-Needle disagreement, near-tie and clarify rate, out_of_scope cluster growth, hang-up-after-turn, each with CUSUM and an automatic audit surge (R132). The deterministic stop/cancel lexicon is a permanent second classifier: the post-call sweep re-runs lexicon + GLOBAL on every transcript (R66), extended to cancel intents (R189). Rescinds also arrive out of band through the revocation intake bus (R115), complaint feeds (R271), and capture-recapture estimation across human, sweep and complaint channels (R257). R97 adds extra audits where models agree confidently.
- Verdict: The premise that audits fall back to random sampling only is false in the existing design. R132 runs label-free monitors on 100% of traffic, including lexicon-vs-Needle disagreement, hang-up-after-turn and out_of_scope growth, and any alarm triggers an audit surge. Missed rescinds also surface through the revocation and complaint intake (R115, R271), the post-call sweep with the cancel lexicon (R66, R189) and capture-recapture (R257). DAgger keeps an expert, because human audit (R20, R33) and the offline-only big model (R36) both remain.

### Round 396. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Privileged-information teacher: the big model labels turns using whole-call context that Needle never sees, so identical Needle inputs carry contradictory labels
- Attack: In the build phase the stand-in classifier sees the full transcript. "Yeah, that's fine" is labeled affirm after a price read-back where the caller asked for it, acknowledge after a long monologue, and request_human where the caller earlier said "only if my son's on". Needle's input is the current turn plus state tools only. Training sees the same (state, text) pair with three labels and learns the average, a near-tie, which then clarifies or flips depending on the release. Imitating an expert that acts on information the learner cannot observe causes an "imitation gap" that more data cannot close (Weihs et al., NeurIPS 2021, "Bridging the Imitation Gap"). This is not a cross-turn runtime bug. The training targets themselves are inconsistent for Needle's observation space, which caps attainable accuracy on exactly the high-stakes short answers.
- Defense: Cross-turn context is not supposed to live in Needle. It lives in the slot store and the state. Commit needs a lexical affirm bound to the instance-scoped read-back question id (R87, R337), given as an action-production phrase (R144), so "yeah, that's fine" after a monologue cannot commit. "Only if my son's on" is captured when spoken, as condition_stated (R289) or defers_decision -> SUPPORT_PERSON (R243), and code blocks the close. The big model is "only a stand-in classifier on the same schema/templates" (R54). Training items store only the utterance + target event, with state context re-rendered from the graph (R279). The context-only probe (R274) checks the related leakage. Tier A gold is labeled by humans, blind (R281).
- Verdict: Contradictory labels on short answers produce near-ties, and near-ties route to clarify (R53, R77). That fails safe. The high-stakes cases do not depend on Needle's context. A commit needs a lexical affirm bound to the instance-scoped read-back qid (R87, R337) and an action-production phrase (R144), and conditions such as "only if my son's on" are captured when spoken (R289, R243). An accuracy ceiling therefore lowers throughput but does not create harm, and the real-call gate measures it.

### Round 397. Winner: Designer
- Topic: RLHF-tuned generators collapse paraphrase diversity, so synthetic "coverage" is dense around fluent typical phrasings and empty where real callers talk
- Attack: The big model is asked for 5,000 paraphrases of "not interested" with noise and dialect transforms. Outputs cluster: "I'm not really interested right now", "No thanks, I'm all set", polite, complete sentences. Real callers say "nah, we good", "uh-uh", "ain't doin' that", or a sigh plus "what". Coverage dashboards show thousands of examples per branch. The real mass sits in short, ungrammatical fragments, the region with the fewest training points. RLHF measurably reduces the output diversity of LLMs compared with supervised models (Kirk et al., ICLR 2024). Asking for more samples gives more of the same modes, not new regions. "Covers every branch" counts examples per label, not how much of the input space the examples span.
- Defense: The design does not rely on RLHF paraphrase for real-caller coverage. Dialect variation comes from rule-based Multi-VALUE transforms (R93), which keep the source label (R275), with an AAE/Spanglish allowlist that is exempt from quarantine (R130). Noise comes from TTS + pinned ASR and from an empirical confusion model fitted on real calls; LLM-written ASR errors are banned (R277). Real on-policy build-phase calls are the core data and are never replaced (R20, R33). Human-written seeds supplement them (R97). The surface-only artifact probe (R127) catches style shortcuts. Coverage does not certify: synthetic data never counts toward the gate (R2), and per-dialect and worst-acoustic-cluster real bounds are release blockers (R26, R131, R255). The Tier 1 stop lexicon covers short forms deterministically (R24, R93).
- Verdict: Coverage in the existing design does not come from RLHF paraphrases. Dialect variation comes from rule-based Multi-VALUE transforms (R93), noise from TTS plus the pinned ASR and an empirical confusion model, with LLM-written ASR errors banned (R277), and the core data is real on-policy calls that are never replaced (R20, R33). Synthetic counts never certify (R2), and per-dialect and worst-cluster real bounds block release (R131, R255), so a training-coverage gap shows up as a failed gate rather than shipped harm.

### Round 398. Winner: Designer
- Topic: A 26M model cannot hold the domain's entity knowledge (utilities, CCAs, co-ops, roof materials, lenders), and the grounding gate checks only that a string was said, not that it maps to the right key
- Attack: QUALIFY asks "who's your electric company?" The caller says "Oncor, well, TXU bills me." Needle sets utility=TXU (a retailer, not the wires utility). Another caller says "the co-op", "MCE", or "it's a TPO roof". Needle snaps each to the nearest seen entity or roof type. Each value appears in the ASR text, so grounding passes. Wrong tariff and suitability rows are chosen deterministically from there. The U.S. has roughly 3,000 electric utilities plus retail providers and CCAs (EIA). Memorizing that mapping and its aliases is world knowledge, which scales with parameters. A 26M model has little room for it. Presence-in-text grounding validates the span, not the canonical key, so confident mis-mapping passes every gate.
- Defense: Spoken entities never choose tariff rows. The calculator is keyed by (delivery utility, generation provider, schedule). Those come only from the utility authorization API or the bill extractor (R156, R227, R212 tariff_class), so "TXU" vs "Oncor" can't select a wrong tariff, and an unknown provider means no figure (R227). The bill-reconciliation gate must reproduce actual billed amounts or no figure is given (R228). Roof suitability comes from roof/shade data lookup and LIDAR geometry (R79, R230), with ROOF_FIRST inspection before any quote (R71). Grounding requires exact equality with one complete extracted entity (R185). Utility is a qualification-critical slot that gets read back (R2).
- Verdict: The attack's chain from spoken entity to wrong tariff row does not exist. Calculator rows are keyed by delivery utility, generation provider and tariff_class, which only the utility API or the bill extractor can write (R156, R212, R227). The bill-reconciliation gate must reproduce actual billed amounts or no figure is given (R228). Roof suitability comes from roof data and LIDAR lookups plus a ROOF_FIRST inspection (R79, R230, R71), and utility is a read-back critical slot (R2).

### Round 399. Winner: Designer
- Topic: PII scrubbing of seed transcripts deletes the very spans Needle must learn to extract as slots, and LLM-filled surrogates don't look like real ones
- Attack: Seed calls are scrubbed: names, addresses, dollar amounts, bill figures and dates become [NAME], [ADDR], [AMOUNT], or big-model surrogates ("John Smith", "$150", "123 Main St"). Needle learns slot extraction on round numbers, Anglo names and tidy formats. Live, a caller says "bill's like two-eighty-seven, two-ninety in summer" or gives a hyphenated surname. Needle extracts "two-ninety" for the bill, or nothing, at rates the synthetic eval never showed. Scrubbing is applied to exactly the label-bearing spans, so the real distribution of slot values never reaches training. Generated surrogates follow the generator's priors (common names, round values), not the callers'. It is a train/serve skew created by the privacy step, and it hits the slots that drive pricing and identity.
- Defense: The slot values that drive pricing and identity are not learned from scrubbed spans. Numbers go through a deterministic normalizer and "Needle never aggregates numbers" (R43). Spoken amounts only fill coarse routing bands (R72). Savings come only from verified_usage or a bill extractor (R61, R156). The attacker's own example, "two-eighty-seven, two-ninety in summer", is exactly R185: "a turn with two candidate values for one slot (summer/winter) goes to clarify(slot) naming the period." Identity uses a deterministic challenge-response match (R73) with a token-set name matcher for hyphenated and compound surnames (R146). Certification uses real gold utterances (R7, R2), so this skew is measured.
- Verdict: The slots that drive pricing and identity are not learned values that Needle passes through. Numbers go through a deterministic normalizer and extractor (R43, R61), spoken amounts only fill coarse routing bands (R72), and savings use verified usage only (R156). The attack's own "two-eighty-seven, two-ninety" example is the R185 two-value clarify case, and identity uses a deterministic challenge-response with a token-set matcher for compound and hyphenated names (R73, R146). Certification on real gold (R2, R7) would expose any remaining skew.

### Round 400. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Margin and softmax thresholds are tuned on synthetic held-out data, and a small model's calibration collapses under shift, so "clarify" fires least where Needle is most wrong
- Attack: Per state, the clarify margin is set to give 3% clarifies at 99.9% accuracy on held-out synthetic turns. On real calls (terse, noisy, novel phrasings), Needle stays confident: margins are wide on wrong labels. The clarify rate on real traffic is lower than on synthetic data while the error rate is several times higher. The deterministic gates trust a confidence signal whose meaning changed when the inputs moved. Calibration measured in-distribution does not survive dataset shift. Ovadia et al. (NeurIPS 2019) show that calibration from post-hoc methods like temperature scaling degrades sharply under shift, and confidence stays high on wrong predictions. Every uncertainty gate in the design inherits thresholds fitted to the wrong distribution.
- Defense: The thresholds are not fitted on synthetic data. R133: "each release runs a calibration step on a real, audited calibration split", with temperature scaling plus label-shift correction to live priors. Margin, OOD and clarify thresholds are re-set to target false-stop and false-commit rates. The OOD score is pre-mask off-schema mass (R125). Commit does not trust Needle confidence at all: it is lexical (R87), needs voicing evidence (R104), an action-production phrase (R144) and a safety-mass veto (R190). Critical slots are read back (R2). Under shift, clarify and near-tie rate, event mix and off-schema mass are CUSUM-monitored on 100% of traffic, with audit surge and Tier A fallback (R132).
- Verdict: The premise is wrong, because thresholds are fitted on a real audited calibration split with label-shift correction to live priors (R133), not on synthetic data. Commit does not trust Needle's confidence at all: it is lexical, voicing-gated and action-phrase based, with a safety-mass veto (R87, R104, R144, R190). Further drift under shift is caught by 100% label-free CUSUM monitors on clarify rate, near-tie rate and off-schema mass (R132).

### Round 401. Winner: Designer
- Topic: The build-phase data captures callers adapting to big-model latency, a conversational rhythm that disappears once fast Needle runs
- Attack: During the build phase, the live stand-in classifier adds 700-1500 ms per turn. Callers adapt: "hello?", repeating themselves, talking over the gap, shorter answers. DAgger data and the shadow labels are full of these patterns. After retirement Needle answers in about 100 ms with pre-rendered audio. Callers now produce longer, more complex turns and different turn-taking. The state-by-state certification evidence was gathered on a different conversation. A response gap of about 700 ms or more is perceived as a problem and changes what people say next (Roberts et al., on silence after requests; turn-taking norms around 200 ms, Stivers et al., PNAS 2009). The teacher's latency shaped the training distribution. Removing it is a deployment-time covariate shift that the build data cannot represent.
- Defense: The certification evidence is not gathered at the teacher's latency. R124: "Whole-trace Tier A/B bounds and rule-of-three counts come only from calls where Needle actually drove the transitions", through a Needle-driven canary cohort with 100% human audit before any wider ramp. The confirmatory sample is collected fresh after promotion (R126). Silence and endpointing timers are fit from real ASR timestamps and A/B tested (R65, R25). Long turns have their own segmenter and Tier A stratum (R44). "Hello?" maps to repair_request (R48). Label-shift correction re-fits to the event priors of the live deployment (R133). Event-mix and hang-up drift on the new rhythm triggers audit surges (R132).
- Verdict: R124 already requires that whole-trace bounds and rule-of-three counts come only from calls where Needle drove the transitions, in a canary with 100% human audit. Certification evidence is therefore gathered at Needle's own latency, and the fresh confirmatory sample (R126) is collected after promotion. Timers are fitted on real ASR timestamps and A/B tested (R65), and event-mix drift triggers audit surges (R132).

### Round 402. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Label-by-construction: generated hard cases drift off the event they were requested for, and synthetic data underperforms most on subjective boundaries like affirm vs acknowledge vs unclear
- Attack: The generator is asked for "ambiguous-but-still-affirm" commits and "soft stop" requests. It writes "Sure, I guess we could look at it" tagged affirm and "I'm kinda busy, maybe not" tagged stop, labels taken from the prompt, not from a reading of the output. Many such outputs are really hedges. Needle learns that hedges are commits and that vague deferrals are opt-outs. No human reads the millions of boundary items. Li et al. (EMNLP 2023, "Synthetic Data Generation with LLMs for Text Classification") find that synthetic training data helps least, and hurts most, on high-subjectivity tasks. Labeling by request puts the noisiest labels exactly on the decision boundaries the safety gates depend on.
- Defense: Label noise on hedges cannot produce a commit. "Sure, I guess we could look at it" fails the lexical commit gate (R87), the short-direct-answer check (R81) and the action-production prompt "please say 'send the contract'" (R144), whatever Needle learned. Hedges have their own classes: ambiguous_polarity -> clarify (R77) and indirect_decline with a fixed closed question (R145). Mislabeling a soft deferral as stop is conservative: a false stop is "a business cost and not a harm" (R254). Tier A gold is human-labeled blind (R2, R281). Counterfactual minimal pairs and the artifact probe target this boundary (R127). Generation is prompted by target event with a balanced mix (R274), and teacher labels are checked against humans (R275).
- Verdict: Mislabeled hedges cannot produce a commit, because the commit path is lexical, requires a short direct answer and an action-production phrase, and is independent of Needle's label (R87, R81, R144). A soft deferral mislabeled as stop is conservative, a business cost and not a harm (R254). Hedges have their own classes, ambiguous_polarity and indirect_decline (R77, R145), and Tier A gold is human-labeled blind (R281).

### Round 403. Winner: Designer
- Topic: Underspecification: training runs with equal offline scores behave differently on real callers, so which weights enter real-call audit is a coin flip the synthetic suite can't call
- Attack: Each release retrains on the union of all data. Five seeds score the same on synthetic branch suites and red-team sets. One seed misses "take me off" phrasings in one dialect 4x more often. Another flips 2% of near-tie commit turns. Engineering picks seed 3 by the suite. Real-call audit then needs months of traffic to separate it from seed 5, and the next release is a fresh draw. D'Amour et al. (JMLR 2022, "Underspecification Presents Challenges for Credibility in Modern ML") show that models with identical held-out performance diverge widely under shift and stress tests. Synthetic evaluation cannot rank them. Rare safety classes, measured at 1e-5, show the most seed-to-seed churn.
- Defense: A seed draw can't slip into production on synthetic scores. The certificate is keyed on the weights hash (R53), so each new seed is a new artifact. It goes through shadow, then a Needle-driven canary with 100% audit (R124), and a fresh confirmatory sample used once (R126). Training is seeded and deterministic (R280), and frozen real gold is replayed every build (R3). The safety tails don't depend on the seed: stop is the union with a deterministic lexicon (R66), and "take me off" is literally in Tier 1 (R24). Commit is lexical (R87). Dialect matched-pair tests are release blockers (R275, R93). Monotone patches inherit certificates (R254), and GLOBAL/LOCAL weights are separate (R283).
- Verdict: A seed cannot reach production on synthetic scores. Each weights hash is a new certificate (R53) that must pass shadow, a 100% audited Needle-driven canary and a fresh confirmatory sample (R124, R126), and frozen real gold is replayed on every build (R3). The safety tails are largely seed-independent: stop is the union with a deterministic lexicon including dialect patterns (R66, R93, R24), commit is lexical (R87), and dialect matched-pair tests are release blockers (R275).
